NIHĀYAT AL-ḤIKMA
Sayyid Muḥammad Ḥusayn al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī
English working translation · revised 11 November 2026
Coverage: 136/136 reading units, including 130 numbered chapters.

Author’s Introduction

N-INTRO · Remarks Serving as an Introduction to This Discipline
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 7–10
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=7&type=c_fbook

In the name of God, the All-Merciful, the Ever-Merciful.

Praise belongs to God, Lord of the worlds, and blessings and peace be upon our master Muhammad and his pure family.

We human beings are things that really exist. Alongside us are other existent things that may act upon us or be affected by us, just as we act upon them or are affected by them.

There is air that we breathe, food that nourishes us, dwellings that we inhabit, earth upon which we move, a sun by whose light we see, stars by which we find our way, animals, plants, and other things.

There are things that we see, others that we hear, others that we smell, others that we taste, and still others of various kinds.

There are things that we pursue or flee from; things that we love or hate; things that we hope for or fear; things that our natures desire or recoil from; and things that we want in order to remain in a place, to move from or to a place, to obtain pleasure, to avoid pain, to escape something unwelcome, or to accomplish other purposes.

All these things of which we are aware—and perhaps others of which we are not aware—are no mere nullity, for they really exist and are established in reality.

Nothing pursues anything except because it is an external entity and a real existent, or leads to one, rather than a mirage-like illusion.

We therefore cannot doubt that existence is there, or deny reality absolutely, unless we obstinately oppose the truth by denying it or professing doubt about it. If anything of this sort occurs, it occurs in words alone.

Each of us, like every existent that lives through knowledge and awareness, constantly regards himself as a real existent with real effects. He encounters nothing other than himself except insofar as it has some share in reality.

Yet, just as we do not doubt this, we also do not doubt that we sometimes err, taking what does not exist to exist, or the reverse. Early human beings affirmed things and held views that we deny today, while we hold their contradictories; one of the two views must be mistaken. There are also errors that afflict us every day: we affirm existence of what does not exist and deny it of what really does exist, and then discover that our judgment was mistaken. Hence the need arises to investigate existent things and distinguish them, through the established properties of existence, from what does not exist. This investigation must remove doubt and yield certainty, for it is this kind of investigation that guides us to real things themselves insofar as they are real.

In other words, it must be an investigation in which we restrict ourselves to demonstration. Among syllogisms, the demonstrative syllogism is the one that yields a certain conclusion, just as, among beliefs, certainty is the belief that discloses reality as it is.

If we undertake this kind of investigation, we can conclude through it that such-and-such exists and such-and-such does not exist.

But investigating particulars lies beyond our capacity. Moreover, demonstration does not apply to the particular insofar as it changes and passes away.

For this very reason, in this kind of inquiry we turn to investigating the condition of the existent universally, and thereby learn the conditions of the existent without qualification insofar as it is universal.

Since it is impossible for an existent to be characterized by conditions that do not exist, these conditions are confined to determinations coextensive with the existent insofar as it is existent—such as unrestricted externality, general unity, and universal actuality, which are coextensive with the existent without qualification—or to conditions narrower than the existent without qualification, but which, taken together with their opposites, are coextensive with it. Examples are our statements, “The existent is either external or mental,” “The existent is either one or many,” and “The existent is either actual or potential.” As you see, none of these lies outside existence without qualification.

The totality of these inquiries is what we call “philosophy.”

What has preceded makes the following points clear.

First, philosophy is the most general of all sciences because its subject is the most general of subjects: the existent, which embraces everything.

Thus all the sciences depend upon it for establishing their subjects.

Philosophy, however, depends upon none of the sciences for establishing its own subject. Its subject is the existent in general, of which we have a primary conception and whose existence we likewise affirm primarily, because existence is its very self.

Second, since its subject is the most general of things, and nothing outside it has any established reality, the predicates affirmed in philosophy are either identical with the subject—as when we say, “Every existent, insofar as it is existent, is one,” or “is actual”—or are narrower than the subject without being other than it. Although the one differs conceptually from the existent, it is identical with it in instance; if it were other than it, it would be null in itself and could not be established of the existent. The same holds of the actual. An example of the second kind is our statement, “The cause exists.” Although the cause is narrower than the existent, causality is not an aspect outside existence in general, for otherwise it would be null.

Such propositions, together with their opposites, return to propositions with a disjunctive predicate whose alternatives are coextensive with existence in general, as when we say, “Every existent is either actual or potential.” Most philosophical questions therefore proceed by division: dividing the existent into necessary and contingent; dividing the contingent into substance and accident; dividing substance into immaterial and material; dividing the immaterial into intellect and soul; and so forth.

Third, the propositions of philosophy are formulated by reversing the direction of predication. Our statements, “The necessary exists” and “The contingent exists,” mean, “Existence is necessary in some instances and contingent in others.” Our statement, “Necessity is either intrinsic or through another,” means, “The necessary existent is divided into what is necessary in itself and what is necessary through another.”

Fourth, since this discipline has the most general subject of all disciplines, and nothing whatsoever falls outside its subject and the predicates that refer back to it, no end external to it can be conceived for whose sake the discipline is pursued.

Knowledge of philosophy is therefore sought for its own sake. It is not sought for the sake of something else, serving as an instrument for reaching another end in the manner of the instrumental disciplines. Nevertheless, benefits do follow from it.

Fifth, the fact that its subject is the most general of things entails that it cannot be the effect of anything outside it, since there is nothing outside it. It therefore has no cause.

The demonstrations employed in philosophy are consequently not demonstrations of the reason why.

As for demonstration of the fact, it has been established in the Book of Demonstration in logic that proceeding from an effect to its cause does not yield certainty. Philosophical inquiry is therefore left only with that kind of demonstration of the fact which relies upon general concomitances, proceeding from one of two general concomitants to the other.

[translation] “Demonstration of the reason why” renders burhān al-limm; “demonstration of the fact” renders burhān al-inn. The latter here includes inference through general concomitances, not only inference from effect to cause.

The General Properties of Existence

N-I-01 · Chapter One: Existence Is Shared in Meaning
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 12–14
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=12&type=c_fbook

Existence, as a concept, is shared in meaning: it is predicated with one meaning of everything of which it is predicated.

This is evident when we consult the mind as we predicate it of things or deny it of things, as in our statements, “The human being exists,” “The plant exists,” “The sun exists,” “The conjunction of contradictories does not exist,” and “The conjunction of contraries does not exist.”

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified—put it well when he said, “That the concept of existence is shared among quiddities is close to being a primary truth.”

Thus the statement of some people that “existence is shared only as a word, and in each quiddity of which it is predicated it means that quiddity” is an absurd assertion.

It is refuted by the consequence that all simple existential propositions would lose their informative value: for example, “The Necessary exists,” “The contingent exists,” “Substance exists,” and “Accident exists.”

Moreover, one may be uncertain whether a thing exists or does not exist while knowing its quiddity and meaning, as when we ask, “Does coincidence exist or not?”

Likewise, one may be uncertain of a thing’s quiddity while being certain of its existence, as when we ask, “Is the existing human soul a substance or an accident?” Uncertainty about one of two things, together with certainty about the other, establishes that they are different.

Equally absurd is the view attributed to some that “the concept of existence is shared only as a word between the necessary and the contingent.”

This has been refuted as follows. Either we intend a meaning by the existence that we predicate of the Necessary, or we do not. The second alternative entails the suspension of any meaningful affirmation. On the first alternative, either we mean what we mean when we predicate existence of contingent things, or we mean its contradictory. On the second of these alternatives, we would be denying existence of Him when affirming existence of Him—exalted is He above that. On the first, what was required is established: the concept of existence is shared in meaning.

The truth, as some investigators have stated, is that the claim that existence is shared only as a word arises from confusing the concept with the instance. The judgment of difference applies to the instance, not to the concept.

[translation] “Shared in meaning” renders mushtarak maʿnawī: the concept has one meaning across its predications. This does not by itself assert that its external instances are identical in every respect.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

N-I-02 · Chapter Two: The Primacy of Existence and the Derivative Character of Quiddity
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 14–23
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=14&type=c_fbook

Existence is primary, rather than quiddity: it is the concrete reality that we affirm as a matter of necessity.

Once we have settled the basic issue of doubt and sophistry and affirmed that which is primary—the reality of things—our first return to things reveals them as different and distinct, each negated of the others, while they all agree in excluding the unreality that the sophist had entertained. Thus we find among them an existent human being, an existent horse, an existent tree, an existent element, an existent sun, and so forth. They have quiddities predicated of them, through which they differ from one another, and existence predicated of them, with one meaning shared among them.

Quiddity differs from existence, since what is peculiar to each differs from what is shared. Moreover, quiddity in itself does not refuse either the predication or the negation of existence. Were it identical with existence, existence could not be negated of it, since a thing cannot be negated of itself. The aspect of quiddity that we find in things therefore differs from the aspect of existence that we find in them. Since each of these things has only one reality, one of these two aspects—quiddity and existence—corresponds to its reality and actuality. This is what is meant by primacy. The other aspect is intellectually derived from the primary aspect, and reality is attributed to it incidentally.

Since each thing attains reality only when existence is predicated of it and it is qualified by existence, existence is that which corresponds to the reality of things.

Quiddity, however, has reality when qualified by existence and is null in its essence when existence is negated of it. In itself, therefore, it is not primary; it acquires actuality only incidentally to existence.

We have thus established that existence is primary and quiddity derivative, as the Peripatetics maintained. That is, existence exists intrinsically, and quiddity exists through it.

This disposes of the following objection to the primacy of existence: if existence were realized among concrete things, it would be existent, since realization is existence. Existence would therefore have an existence; transferring the discussion to that existence, and so on, would produce an infinite regress.

The reply is that existence is existent, but intrinsically, not through an additional existence. Existence is, in other words, identical with being existent. Quiddity differs in that the aspect of its essence differs from the aspect of its existence.

As for the claim that, in customary linguistic usage, “existent” is applied only to something possessing an essence that serves as the subject of existence—and that existence itself consequently is not existent—even if correct, this is a matter of linguistic convention or prevalent usage. Realities do not follow the usage of words. As already explained, existence has a concrete reality that is itself established for itself.

Bahmanyār says in al-Taḥṣīl: “In sum, the reality of existence is nothing other than that it is among concrete things. How could that whose reality is this fail to be among concrete things?” End of quotation.

The following difficulty is also disposed of: if existence is existent intrinsically, contingent existences must be necessary in themselves. For existence being existent intrinsically entails that it cannot be negated of itself, since a thing cannot be negated of itself; and by the necessary in itself we mean only that whose nonexistence is impossible on account of itself.

The reply is that the criterion for a thing's being necessary in itself is not that its existence is its very essence. Rather, it is that its existence is required by its essence without need of anything else. Every contingent existence, while existent in itself, needs something else and is bestowed by it. It is like a particle's relational meaning: it is itself, yet it becomes a complete concept only through subsisting in another.

This will be explained further in the discussions to come.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn says in al-Asfār: “The meaning of the Necessary's existence through itself is that it is required by its essence without need of an agent or a recipient. The meaning of existence being realized through itself is that, once it occurs—whether through itself, as in the Necessary, or through an agent—its realization does not require a further existence through which it subsists, unlike what is other than existence.” End of quotation.

Another objection is also disposed of: if existence is existent intrinsically, whereas quiddity is existent through something else, namely existence, the concept of existence would be shared between what exists through itself and what exists through another. The argument's assumption that existence is shared in meaning among existents, rather than merely shared in name, would then not hold.

The reply is that this confuses concept with instance. The difference mentioned is a difference of instances, not of concept.

What has preceded shows the falsity of the view that quiddity is primary, a view attributed to the Illuminationists.

According to them, quiddity is primary when it is such that existence can be abstracted from it, although in itself it is derivative, and the existence abstracted from it is derivative as well.

The answer is that a derivative quiddity's becoming primary and possessing a concrete reality through the abstraction of a derivative concept of existence would be a transformation of its very nature, which is necessarily impossible.

Also shown to be false is the position that existence is primary in the Necessary and quiddity primary in the contingent. Al-Dawwānī adopted this position, explaining that, as the insight of the metaphysical sages requires, existence is one individual concrete reality, namely the Necessary, exalted is He, and contingent quiddities acquire actuality through a certain relation to it. “Existent” is thus applied to Him, exalted is He, in the sense that He is existence itself, and to contingent quiddities in the sense that they are related to existence, which is the Necessary.

The reply is that if this relation entails the supervening of a concrete reality upon quiddities, that reality is existence. For an actualized quiddity has only the two aspects of quiddity and existence; if primacy is not attributed to quiddity, it belongs to existence. If the relation entails nothing, and quiddity's condition is the same before and after the relation, its acquiring actuality through that relation would be an impossible transformation of its nature.

The primacy of existence and the derivative character of quiddity have the following consequences.

First, whatever is predicated of the aspect of quiddity is predicated only through existence.

Existence is a qualifying aspect in every quiddative predication. For quiddity in itself is null and perishing, possessing nothing; its essence and essential constituents are therefore established for it through existence.

When the intellect considers quiddity as such, it is indeed nothing but itself, neither existent nor nonexistent. Yet existence's exclusion from it under this consideration—meaning that existence is not included in its definition—does not prevent existence from being predicated of it outside its definition and as supervening upon it. It therefore has some establishment however it is supposed.

Likewise, the concomitants of its essence—the concomitants of quiddity, such as the concept of quiddity that supervenes upon every quiddity and the evenness that supervenes upon the quiddity of four—are established for it through existence, not through its essence.

This makes clear that a concomitant of quiddity is in truth a concomitant of both external and mental existence, as al-Dawwānī maintained.

Likewise, the concomitants of mental existence, such as being a species in the case of the human; the concomitants of external existence, such as coldness in the case of snow; and predicates that are not concomitants, such as writing in the case of the human: all of these are through existence.

This also shows that existence is among the concomitants of quiddity that lie outside its essence.

Second, existence is not qualified by any of the determinations of quiddity, such as universality and particularity; being a genus, species, differentia, proprium, or common accident; or substantiality, quantity, quality, and the other accidental categories. All these determinations supervene upon quiddity either insofar as it applies to and is true of something—as “human” applies to and is true of Zayd, ʿAmr, and the other individuals—or insofar as it falls under something, as individuals fall under species and species under genera. Existence, which is intrinsically concrete reality, admits neither application to something nor inclusion under something, neither truth of something nor predication, nor anything resembling these meanings. The concept of existence does, however, admit being true of many and being shared, like other concepts.

It follows that existence is coextensive with individuality.

It also follows that existence has no like. For a thing's like is what shares its specific quiddity, and existence has no specific quiddity.

It further follows that existence has no contrary. As will be explained, two contraries are two positive things that succeed one another in a single subject, fall under one proximate genus, and differ to the utmost degree. Existence has neither a subject nor a genus, nor does it stand in contrariety to anything.

Third, existence cannot be part of something. For if the other part and the whole composed of the two are existence itself, a thing's being part of itself has no meaning. If one or both are other than existence, they are null in themselves, since nothing other than existence is primary; there is therefore no composition.

The same argument establishes that existence has no parts, and also shows that existence is simple in its essence.

Fourth, the attributes and predicates that truly attach to existence are not outside its essence. Were they outside it, they would be null.

Fifth, the existent, insofar as it is qualified by existence, admits a kind of division into what is existent intrinsically and what is existent incidentally. Existence is existent intrinsically in the sense that it is identical with itself. Quiddity is existent incidentally: it is not qualified by existence when considered solely in respect of its own essence, although it truly exists through existence, in contrast to that which does not exist through existence.

Sixth, existence supervenes upon quiddity. This means that the intellect can strip quiddity of existence and understand it by itself, without considering its existence. Existence is therefore neither identical with it nor part of it. Evidence for this includes the possibility of negating existence of quiddity, the need for proof that it is qualified by existence, and its equal relation in itself to existence and nonexistence. Were existence identical with quiddity or a part of it, none of these would be correct.

As you have learned, this difference is intellectual; it does not conflict with the unity of quiddity and existence externally and mentally. There is only one reality, namely existence, because existence is primary and quiddity derivative. Different quiddities differentiate existence in a certain way, without anything being added to existence. This is the meaning of their statement, “Quiddities are modes of existence.” The distinction, separation, and difference in effects among existent quiddities reduce to this difference; this is the meaning of their statement, “Quiddities are the limits of existence.” The essence of each existent quiddity is a limit that its existence does not exceed. This entails negations equal in number to the existent quiddities outside it. The existent quiddity of a human, for example, is a limit of that human's existence, which does not pass beyond it to another: the human is not a horse, not a cow, not a tree, not a stone, and so on through the existent quiddities distinct from the human.

Seventh, the establishment of each thing—whatever kind of establishment is supposed—is due solely to an external existence there that intrinsically excludes nonexistence. Thus judgments true in the order of things themselves, which have no corresponding object either externally or mentally, have a corresponding object established with a certain derivative establishment, following real existents.

To explain: some true judgments have a corresponding object externally, such as “The human exists” and “The human writes.” Others have a corresponding object mentally, such as “The human is a species” and “Animal is a genus.” Still others have a corresponding object to which they conform, but it exists neither externally nor mentally. Examples are “The nonexistence of the cause is a cause of the nonexistence of the effect” and “Nonexistence is null in itself.” Nonexistence has no realization externally or mentally, nor do its determinations or effects. Propositions of this kind are considered to conform to the order of things themselves, nafs al-amr. For when the intellect affirms that the existence of the cause is a cause of the existence of the effect, it must affirm that the effect ceases if its cause ceases. This is the nonexistence of the cause's being a cause of the nonexistence of the effect. Yet nonexistence has no realized instance externally or mentally, since whatever occurs in either has existence.

In light of the preceding discussions, what should be said is that the primary is real existence: it is existence, and every real determination belongs to it.

Then, since quiddities are manifestations of existence to minds, the intellect necessarily extends its consideration by considering existence as belonging to them and predicating it of them. The concept of existence and establishment thereby comes to be predicated of existence, quiddity, and the determinations of both.

The intellect then necessarily makes a second extension, predicating establishment and realization without qualification of every concept that it must consider in consequence of existence or quiddity—such as the concepts of nonexistence, quiddity, potentiality, and actuality—and then affirming their determinations.

The domain that the intellect supposes for establishment and realization without qualification in this last sense is what we call “the order of things themselves.” It encompasses true mental and external propositions and what the intellect affirms without a corresponding object mentally or externally. Matters belonging to this order are, however, intellectual concomitants of quiddities, established through their establishment.

A continuation of this discussion will come before you, God willing.

It has been said that “the order” in “the order of things themselves” means the world of command, a universal intellect containing the forms of all intelligibles. A proposition's conformity to the order of things themselves would then mean its conformity to the intelligible form present in that intellect.

The difficulty is that the discussion is transferred to the intelligible form present in it. That form too is an intelligible form requiring a corresponding object beyond itself to which it conforms.

It has also been said that “the thing itself” is what is meant, the explicit noun being put in place of a pronoun. For example, nonexistence's being null in itself in the order of things themselves would mean its being so in itself.

The difficulty is that what has no corresponding object externally or mentally has no selfhood to which it and its determinations could conform.

Eighth, thingness is coextensive with existence. What has no existence has no thingness; the nonexistent, insofar as it is nonexistent, is not a thing.

The Muʿtazilites are reported to hold that nonexistent contingent quiddities have thingness in nonexistence, and that between existence and nonexistence there is an intermediary that they call a “state.” They define it as an attribute of an existent that is neither existent nor nonexistent, such as being capable of laughter or writing in the case of the human. They deny, however, an intermediary between negation and affirmation: the negated is the impossible, while the affirmed comprises the necessary, the existent contingent, and the nonexistent contingent. A state is that which is neither existent nor nonexistent.

These are claims rejected by plain reason. They resemble terminological stipulations more than scientific considerations, and it is better to pass over their discussion.

Ninth, the reality of existence, insofar as it is the reality of existence, has no cause beyond itself. Its concrete identity, intrinsically primary, existent, and excluding nonexistence, does not depend for its realization on anything outside this reality, whether as a complete or a partial cause. This is because it is primary and whatever lies beyond it is null. There is no objection, however, to some degrees of this reality depending on others, as contingent existence depends on necessary existence and some contingents depend on others. From this it becomes clear that demonstration of the reason why has no application in divine philosophy, which investigates the determinations of the existent as existent.

Tenth, since the reality of existence is identical with the aspect through which effects follow, it is identical with externality. It therefore cannot enter the mind and become mental, with its effects no longer following, since that would entail an impossible transformation of its nature.

As for mental existence, which will be established, God willing: insofar as it excludes nonexistence from itself, it is an external existence upon which effects follow. It is counted as mental, without the effects following, only in comparison with the external instance corresponding to it.

It is thus clear that the reality of existence has no intellectual form, unlike quiddities existing externally, which do have intellectual forms.

It is also clear that the relation of the concept of existence to external existences is not the relation of a universal quiddity to its external individuals.

What has preceded also shows that a concept is a quiddity only if it has an external individual that it constitutes and upon which its effects follow.

[translation] Iʿtibārī is rendered “derivative” where quiddity is contrasted with primary existence; the text does not thereby deny that quiddities truly exist through existence. “The order of things themselves” renders nafs al-amr, whose scope the author explains in the seventh consequence.

The 1428 AH revised edition prints «ولو كانت عين الوجود لم يجز أن يسلب عنها» and explicitly records the earlier reading in footnote 1 (vol. 1, p. 19), calling the printed version correct. The emended reading makes 'existence' the predicate that could not be negated of a quiddity identical with it. It agrees with the immediately following examples of individuals under species and species under genera.
