N-I-03 · Chapter Three: Existence Is a Graded Reality
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 23–27
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=23&type=c_fbook

There is no doubt that concrete external identities are characterized by multiplicity: sometimes because this is a human being, that is a horse, and another is a tree, and so forth; and sometimes because this is actual and that is potential, this is one and that is many, this is originated and that is eternal, this is contingent and that is necessary, and so on.

What we presented in the preceding chapter established that multiplicity in the first respect—quiddative multiplicity—exists externally incidentally through existence, while existence is characterized by it incidentally through quiddity, because existence is primary and quiddity is derivative.

Multiplicity in the second respect, however, is what belongs to existence through the divisions that occur in existence itself, such as its division into necessary and contingent, one and many, actual and potential, and the like. It was established in the preceding chapter that existence is simple and that there is nothing other than it.

It follows that this multiplicity is constitutive of existence, in the sense that it is within existence and does not stand outside it. Otherwise it would be a part of existence, whereas existence has no part, or a reality outside existence, whereas there is nothing outside existence.

Existence therefore has multiplicity within itself. Is there, then, an aspect of unity to which this multiplicity returns without being abolished by returning to it, so that the reality of existence is many while being one, and one while being many? In other words, is it a graded reality with different degrees, in each of which that by which it is distinguished returns to that which it shares with the others, as has been attributed to the ancient Persian sages? Or does it have no aspect of unity, so that existence amounts to realities entirely disparate in their essences, each distinguished from the others by its entire simple essence, rather than by a part or by something external, as has been attributed to the Peripatetics?

The truth is that it is one reality while being many. For we abstract from all its degrees and instances the single, self-evident, general concept of existence; and it is impossible to abstract one concept from many instances insofar as they are many and do not return to any unity.

This makes clear that existence is a graded reality with different degrees. The example used for it is the reality of light, as understood by ordinary understanding: a single reality possessing degrees that differ in intensity and weakness. There is, for example, strong, intermediate, and weak light. The strong degree is not light together with something additional to luminosity, nor does the weak degree lack anything of the reality of light or mix with darkness, which is the absence of light. None of its differing degrees adds anything to the shared reality of light or lacks anything of it. Each is simply light in a particular, simple degree, neither composed of parts nor joined by any addition; and it is distinguished from the others by its very essence, which is the luminosity they share.

Light is thus one simple reality, multiple in its very unity and unified in its very multiplicity. Likewise, existence is one reality possessing degrees that differ in intensity and weakness, priority and posteriority, height and lowness, and other such respects.

Several matters follow from what has preceded.

First: one degree of existence is distinguished from another only by its very simple essence, in which that which is shared is identical with that by which it is distinguished. This does not prevent the intellect from attributing existential distinction to the aspect of multiplicity in existence rather than to the aspect of unity, or from attributing what is shared and the affinity between them to the aspect of unity.

Second: the degrees of existence possess absoluteness and restriction relative to one another, because they differ in intensity and weakness and the like. If we suppose two degrees of existence, one weak and one intense, a comparison and relation between them necessarily arises. The weak degree, by its character, does not include some of the perfection possessed by the intense degree; but there is no perfection in the weak degree that the intense degree does not possess. The weak degree is therefore as though composed of possession and lack: its essence is restricted by the absence of some of the perfection in the intense degree. You may, if you wish, say that it is “limited.” The essence of the intense degree, however, is absolute and unlimited in relation to the weak degree.

If we suppose another degree above the intense one, the relation of the intense degree to this degree supposed above it is like the relation of the degree below it to itself. The intense degree becomes limited in relation to what is above it, just as it was absolute in relation to what is below it. The same applies to the degrees proceeding upward, until one stops at a degree above which there is no degree. This is absolute, without being limited except in that it has no limit.

The reverse applies when we take a weak degree and consider it in relation to what is weaker than itself, and so on, until we arrive at a degree of perfection and actuality that possesses no actuality except the actuality of having no actuality.

Third: everything that has preceded makes clear that the ordered degrees of existence have limits, except for the highest degree, which is limited in that it has no limit.

It is evident that these limits, inseparable from the negations, nonexistences, and lacks that we affirm of the degrees of existence even though those degrees are primary and simple, arise only from the inadequacy of expression. Otherwise, nonexistence is the contradictory of existence, and it is impossible for it to penetrate the degrees of its contradictory.

This meaning—the entry of nonexistences into the limited degrees of existence, and their non-entry, which amounts to sheer existence—is a kind of simplicity and composition in existence different from the simplicity and composition spoken of technically elsewhere, namely simplicity and composition with respect to external, intellectual, or imagined parts.

Fourth: the further a degree descends, the more numerous its limits become and the narrower its existence; and the further it ascends and approaches the highest degree, the fewer its limits and the broader its existence. At the highest degree, it includes every existential perfection without limitation and is absolute without end.

Fifth: existence has two extremes in respect of intensity and weakness. This is what follows from saying that existence is a graded reality.

Sixth: existence, with the breadth and expansiveness belonging to its reality, is specified by its simple concrete reality; it is specified by a degree among its differing simple degrees, in which that by which it is distinguished returns to that which it shares with the others; and it is specified by the quiddities that arise from it and delimit it. It is evident that specification in either of the first two ways belongs to existence intrinsically, whereas specification in the third way belongs to it incidentally through quiddities.

[translation] “Graded reality” renders ḥaqīqa mushakkaka: the degrees share the very reality by which they differ. The “ancient Persian sages” are the Fahlawiyyūn named in the Arabic.

[Author’s footnote · restored from printed editions] Since a limit signifies negation, negating a limit is a negation of negation, which is affirmation. It therefore amounts to the purity of existence, that is, sheer existence.

[Author’s footnote · restored from printed editions] By the breadth and narrowness of existence is meant a degree’s possession of more or less perfection.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: أنّ ينسب العقلُ → أن ينسب العقلُ. Remove the inappropriate shadda in أن before the subjunctive verb; this is a vocalization repair, not a substantive reading change. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 19; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 35

N-I-04 · Chapter Four: Some Judgments Concerning Nonexistence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 28–30
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=28&type=c_fbook

It has already been explained that nonexistence has no thinghood: it is sheer perishing and nullity.

It follows that there is no differentiation in nonexistence. For two things differ either through their entire essences, as do two species under two different categories; through a part of their essences, as do two species under one category; or through something that occurs to their essences, as do two individuals of one species. Nonexistence, however, has no essence.

Nevertheless, nonexistence is sometimes related to existence, thereby acquiring a share of existence, which is accompanied by a kind of differentiation. Thus the nonexistence of sight, which is blindness, differs from the nonexistence of hearing, which is deafness; and the nonexistence of Zayd differs from the nonexistence of ʿAmr.

In this way the intellect attributes causality and being an effect to nonexistence, corresponding to what existence possesses in this respect. Thus it is said, “The nonexistence of the cause is a cause of the nonexistence of the effect.” Here nonexistence is related to the cause and the effect, and the two nonexistences become differentiated. The nonexistence of the effect is then made dependent on the nonexistence of the cause, just as the existence of the effect depended on the existence of the cause. This is a kind of figurative expression whose actual significance is to indicate the dependence obtaining between the two existences.

Nonexistence qualified by any qualification—such as essential nonexistence, temporal nonexistence, and eternal nonexistence—is analogous to relational nonexistence. In all these cases the concept of nonexistence is conceived, and an instance is posited for it just as for other concepts. The concept is then qualified, so that the instance becomes differentiated; and a judgment appropriate to it is made concerning that instance on the basis of its supposed subsistence. One example is considering the nonexistence of nonexistence in opposition to nonexistence, just as the nonexistence opposed to existence is considered in opposition to existence.

This resolves the objection concerning the consideration of the nonexistence of nonexistence: nonexistence related to nonexistence is a species of nonexistence, yet, insofar as it removes the nonexistence to which it is related, it stands opposed to that nonexistence by the opposition of contradiction; and being a species and being an opposite can never go together.

The resolution, as Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may God have mercy on him—explained, is that the respects differ. Insofar as the nonexistence of nonexistence is a concept more specific than unrestricted nonexistence, with nonexistence included in it, it is a species of nonexistence. But insofar as the nonexistence to which it is related has a supposed subsistence, which the related nonexistence removes as one contradictory removes the other, the related nonexistence stands opposed to it.

The same consideration resolves an objection raised against their statement, “Nothing can be asserted of the absolutely nonexistent.” The objection is that the proposition contradicts itself: it indicates that nothing can be asserted of the absolutely nonexistent, yet this itself is an assertion about it.

The answer is that nothing can be asserted of the absolutely nonexistent insofar as it is sheer nullity in reality. But insofar as its concept has a certain mental subsistence, it can be asserted of it that nothing can be asserted of it. The respects are therefore different.

In other words, nothing can be asserted of what is absolutely nonexistent by common predication; but of what is absolutely nonexistent by primary predication it can be asserted that nothing can be asserted of it.

The foregoing also removes the suspicion of contradiction from several other propositions. For example, we say, “The particular is particular,” whereas it is itself a universal that applies to many things. We say, “The conjunction of contradictories is impossible,” whereas it is itself possible and exists in the mind. And we say, “A thing either subsists in the mind or does not subsist in it,” whereas what does not subsist in the mind does subsist in it, since it is something understood and exists with a mental existence.

The particular is particular by primary predication, and a universal applying to many things by common predication. The conjunction of contradictories is possible by primary predication and impossible by common predication. What does not subsist in the mind does not subsist in it by primary predication, and does subsist in it by common predication.

[Later editor’s philosophical objection · not authorial text] The 1428 editor disputes the concluding formulation about the conjunction of contradictories. He distinguishes qualifying the subject as concept or instance from qualifying the predication itself. On his account, the concept is a possible mental existent, whereas a supposed external instance is impossible. Both the 1404 print and the later edition retain the author’s wording in the main text; this note reports a philosophical objection, not an attested textual replacement.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

N-I-05 · Chapter Five: There Is No Repetition in Existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 30–33
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=30&type=c_fbook

The concrete identity of every concrete existent is its existence, in accordance with what has already been established concerning the primacy of existence. A concrete identity by its very essence refuses to apply to many things; this is individuation. Individuality therefore belongs to existence intrinsically.

If two existences were posited for one existent, its one concrete identity would be many while being one. This is impossible.

A similar argument shows that it is impossible for two things exactly alike in every respect to exist. Positing two like things necessarily entails a distinction between them, whereas positing likeness in every respect entails the absence of any distinction between them. This involves the conjunction of contradictories, which is impossible.

In general, it is impossible for one existent to exist with more than one existence, whether the two existences, for example, occur at one time with no intervening nonexistence, or are separate with nonexistence intervening between them. The difficulty—the necessary identity together with the supposed duality—is the same in both cases.

It may be said: “The second existence differs from the first because it is preceded by nonexistence after existence, whereas the first is not. This suffices to establish duality without impairing identity, since the distinction is through nonexistence.”

This is rejected because nonexistence is sheer nullity, in which there is neither multiplicity nor differentiation. There is no essence within it characterized by nonexistence to which existence subsequently attaches after that characterization has ceased.

It has already been explained that all this is an intellectual consideration aided by the estimative faculty, which relates nonexistence to a positive possession, so that nonexistence becomes numbered and multiplied as those positive possessions multiply.

The real meaning of a thing's existence being preceded and followed by nonexistence—in general, of nonexistence surrounding it before and after—is that its existence is confined to one circumstance of reality and falls short of extending over the other concrete circumstances. It does not mean that the thing has a real existence in one circumstance of reality while nonexistence has an actual subsistence extending over the other circumstances, sometimes coming upon existence, driving it out of its abode, and occupying that abode itself. This would confer primacy on nonexistence and entail the conjunction of contradictories.

The result is that the distinction of the second existence is a distinction made by the estimative faculty and does not entail a real distinction. If it did, it would entail separation between the two existences and identity would be abolished.

It may be said: “Why should it not be possible for an agent that gives existence to bring something into existence, after which it ceases to exist, while an intelligible form of it in its individuality remains with that agent or with some of the higher principles? The thing could then exist a second time in accordance with what was known, with the intelligible form preserving unity and identity between the two existences.” The answer is that the second existence, however it is posited, is an existence after an existence. Its otherness and separation from the first existence, insofar as it is after that existence, are necessary; and identity and otherness can never go together.

This established impossibility of the repetition of a thing's existence with intervening nonexistence is what is meant by their statement, “Restoring the nonexistent as numerically the same thing is impossible.” The Shaykh regarded the impossibility of restoring the nonexistent as numerically the same thing as self-evident.

They have put forward arguments for this which, if the issue is self-evident, serve as reminders.

One is that, if an existent at one time could cease to exist for a time and then exist as numerically the same thing at another time, nonexistence would have to intervene between the thing and itself. This is impossible, since it entails the existence of the thing at two times with nonexistence intervening between them.

Another is that, if a thing could be restored as numerically the same thing after ceasing to exist, it would be possible to bring into existence initially something like it in every respect. This is impossible.

The entailment holds because the thing restored as numerically the same thing and what is like it in every respect are two like things, and like things have the same ruling concerning what is and is not possible. If it were possible to bring it into existence as numerically the same thing a second time by restoration, it would be possible to bring its like into existence initially.

The consequent is impossible because the coexistence of two like things in existence entails the absence of any distinction between them, whereas they are two distinct things.

Another argument is that restoring the nonexistent as numerically the same thing entails that the restored thing is the initial thing. For the supposition of numerical identity entails that the restored thing is the initial thing in essence and in all its individuating characteristics, including time. Thus the restored thing becomes initial, and the respect of restoration is identical with the respect of initial origination.

Another is that, if restoration were possible, the number of returns would reach no determinate limit at which it stopped. There is no difference between the first return, the second, the third, and so on without end, just as there would be no difference between what is restored and what is initially originated. Yet a determinate number is one of the necessary concomitants of the existence of an individuated thing.

A group of theologians held restoration to be possible, on the grounds that the resurrection proclaimed by the true revealed laws is a restoration of the nonexistent.

They argued that, if restoring the nonexistent as numerically the same thing were impossible, this would be due either to its quiddity, or to something necessarily accompanying its quiddity—in which case it would not have existed initially—or to something separable, in which case the impossibility would cease when that thing ceased.

The reply is that the impossibility is due to something necessarily accompanying its existence, not its quiddity.

As for what the true revealed laws proclaim, gathering and resurrection are a passage from one mode of being to another, not an act of bringing into existence after annihilation.

[translation] “Numerically the same” renders bi-ʿaynihi: the question concerns the restoration of an individual in its very identity, rather than the production of something qualitatively similar.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: هويتّه العينيّة → هويّته العينيّة. Move misplaced shadda from ت to ي in identity; the consonantal text is unchanged. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 22; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 43

Adopted: فالشخصيّه → فالشخصيّة. Restore final tāʾ marbūṭa, corroborated by both witnesses. Grade: orthographic-transcription-repair; consonantal-reading-or-standard-spelling. Source: 1404 pp. 23; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 43

Adopted: حيثيّه الإبتداء → حيثيّة الإبتداء. Restore final tāʾ marbūṭa, corroborated by both witnesses. Grade: orthographic-transcription-repair; consonantal-reading-or-standard-spelling. Source: 1404 pp. 24; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 45


Independent and Linking Existence

N-II-01 · Chapter One: The Division of Existence into Independent and Connective Existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 37–39
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=37&type=c_fbook

An existent is divided into that whose existence is in itself, which we call “independent,” “predicative,” or “self-standing existence,” and that whose existence is in another, which we call “connective existence” (al-wujūd al-rābiṭ).

This is because there are propositions about external reality whose subjects and predicates apply to external reality, such as “Zayd is standing” and “The human being laughs.” There are also qualifying compounds drawn from these propositions, such as “Zayd’s standing” and “the human being’s laughter.” Between their terms we find something that we call a relation or connection. We do not find it in the subject alone, in the predicate alone, between the subject and something other than the predicate, or between the predicate and something other than the subject. There is, therefore, something existent in addition to the subject and predicate.

It does not have an essence separate from the two terms, such that it would be a third alongside them and separate from them as each is separate from the other. Otherwise it would need one connection linking it to the subject and another linking it to the predicate. What was supposed to be three would then be five; these five would need four further connections, becoming nine; and so on. The parts of the proposition or compound would thus regress without end, although they are confined between two limits. This is impossible.

It is therefore existent in the two terms and subsists through them: it is not outside them, without being identical with them, a part of them, identical with either one, or a part of either one; nor is it separate from them. The two terms in which it exists are otherwise than it in this respect.

It has thus been established that some existents have existence in themselves—these are independent—and some have existence in another—these are connective.

From what has been said, it has become clear that for the relation to mediate between the two terms means that its existence subsists through the two terms and connects them.

Several points follow.

First, the domain in which connective existence is realized is the domain in which the existence of its two terms is realized, whether that domain is external reality or the mind. This follows from the nature of connective existence, which is not outside the existence of its two terms. The domain of the existence of each of them is precisely the domain of its existence. An external relation is realized only between two external terms, and a mental relation only between two mental terms.

The rule is that the existence of the two terms is of the same order as the existence of the relation obtaining between them, and conversely.

Second, the realization of connective existence between two terms entails a kind of existential unity between them. This is because it is realized in them, is not distinct from them in essence, and is not outside them. Its individual unity therefore calls for a kind of unity between them, whether there is predication, as in propositions, or no predication, as in other compounds. None of these cases is without a manner of unity.

Third, propositions involving primary predication, such as “The human being is a human being,” have a connection only through mental consideration.

The same holds for simple existential propositions, such as “The human being exists,” since it makes no sense for a connecting relation to be realized between a thing and itself.

Fourth, no connection is realized from nonexistence, since it has no thinghood and contains no distinction.

It follows that affirmative propositions in which one or both terms are nonexistence, such as “Zayd is nonexistent” and “A partner of the Creator is nonexistent,” contain no connective nonexistence. It makes no sense for a nonexistence to subsist through two nonexistences, or through an existence and a nonexistence, when it has no thinghood and contains no distinction—except, of course, through mental consideration.

Negative propositions, such as “The human being is not a stone,” are similar: they contain no connective nonexistence except through mental consideration.

Fifth, connective existences have no quiddity. Quiddities are what is said in answer to “What is it?” and are therefore independent in their conceptual content, whereas connective existences have no concept that is independent in its conceptual content.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: جزءَ هما → جزءَهما. Join attached dual pronoun; early p.28 and later p.50 print one word. No change of meaning. Grade: spacing-or-punctuation-normalization; no-substantive-reading-change. Source: 1404 pp. 28; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 50

N-II-02 · Chapter Two: How Connective and Independent Existence Differ
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 39–40
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=39&type=c_fbook

Is the difference between independent and connective existence a difference in kind, or is it not? That is, can connective existence, which has a dependent meaning, shed this character and become an independent meaning when attention is directed to it independently, after having had the meaning of a particle, or can it not? The correct position is the latter alternative: the difference is not one of kind. For, as will be explained in the discussions of cause and effect, an effect’s need for its cause is rooted in its very essence. This entails that it is need itself and that its essence subsists through the existence of the cause, having no independence apart from it in any respect. It follows that the existence of every effect—whether it is a substance or an accident—is connective when considered in relation to its cause, although, when considered in itself and when such effects are compared with one another, it is a substance or an accident existing in itself. Thus the difference between connective and independent existence is not a difference in kind such that the nonindependent concept abstracted from the connective could not be transformed into the independent concept abstracted from the independent.

Several points follow from the foregoing.

First, a concept’s independence or nonindependence in its conceptual content follows the existence from which it is abstracted. In itself, it has only indeterminacy.

Thus the determinations of substances and accidents are substantial and accidental quiddities when compared with one another and considered in themselves; when compared with the First Principle—blessed and exalted is He—they are existential connections. Considered in themselves, apart from their existence, they are neither independent nor connective.

Second, some connective existences subsist through a single term, such as the existence of an effect in relation to its cause, just as some subsist through two terms, such as the existences of other relations and correlations.

Third, the order of existence contains only one independent existence, namely the Necessary Existent—exalted is His name. The rest are connections, relations, and correlations.

[translation] The Arabic “the latter” is read with the chapter’s initial question and its explicit conclusion: the distinction is not a difference in kind. “The meaning of a particle” refers to a meaning dependent on its terms, like that of a grammatical particle, rather than an independently apprehended concept.

[Textual emendation · 1428 AH editor] At N-II-02, the 1417 AH base reads «كان جوهراً أو عرضاً موجوداً في نفسه رابطاً بالنظر إلى علّته». The revised 1428 AH editor explicitly reports that all consulted copies include «موجوداً في نفسه», but omits it as redundant and misleading: the effect is considered ‘in itself’ only relative to itself, not absolutely while considered as dependent on its cause. This reader follows that printed editorial deletion in Arabic and English, preserving the earlier wording here. The change is an editor's conjectural correction, not a manuscript-attested omission.

[Textual correction · source comparison] The 1404 print reads «المتبدّل إلى المفهوم المستقل»; the 1428 edition reads «التبدّل إلى المفهوم المستقلّ». No dedicated later variant note is claimed for this difference. The reader adopts «التبدُّلَ إلى المفهوم المستقلّ». Object of يقبل must be the verbal noun transformation; current early reading adds a mīm and vocalizes a participle. Later edition silently reads the grammatically coherent verbal noun. English already renders this sense. This is printed/digital edition evidence, not manuscript certification.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: المتبدَّلَ إلى المفهوم المستقلّ → التبدُّلَ إلى المفهوم المستقلّ. Object of يقبل must be the verbal noun transformation; current early reading adds a mīm and vocalizes a participle. Later edition silently reads the grammatically coherent verbal noun. English already renders this sense. Grade: unfootnoted-later-edition-reading; contextual-grammatical-justification. Source: 1404 pp. 30; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 53

N-II-03 · Chapter Three: The Division of Existence in Itself into Existence for Itself and Existence for Another
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 40–41
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=40&type=c_fbook

An existent in itself is divided into that whose existence is for itself and that whose existence is for another.

By a thing’s existence being for another, we mean that its existence in itself—the existence that excludes nonexistence from its quiddity—is itself also what excludes nonexistence from something else. It does not exclude the nonexistence of that other thing’s quiddity and essence, for otherwise a single existent would have two quiddities, which is impossible. Rather, it excludes a nonexistence additional to that other thing’s quiddity and essence and having a certain concomitance with it. Knowledge, for example, by its existence excludes nonexistence from its own quiddity, while that very same existence excludes ignorance, which is a certain nonexistence, from its subject.

The proof that this division, namely existence for another, is realized is provided by the existences of accidents. Each, just as it excludes nonexistence from its own quiddity, excludes from its subject a certain nonexistence additional to the subject’s essence.

The same applies to immanent specific forms. They have a certain presence in their matters through which they exclude from those matters, not the nonexistence of their essence, but a substantial deficiency whose exclusion perfects them. This is what is meant by a thing’s existence being for another and being attributive.

Opposed to this is that whose existence excludes nonexistence only from its own quiddity. This is existence for itself, as in complete substantial species, such as the human being, the horse, and others.

It has thus been established that existence in itself is divided into that whose existence is for itself and that whose existence is for another. This was what was required.

What has been said also makes clear that the existence of accidents belongs to the modes of the existence of the substances that are their subjects. Likewise, the existence of immanent forms is not separate from the existence of their matters.

It also makes clear that the concepts abstracted from attributive existences, which are attributes of their subjects, are quiddities neither of those existences nor of their subjects. A concept abstracted from an existence is its quiddity only if the existence from which it is abstracted excludes nonexistence from that concept itself; attributive existence does not exclude nonexistence from the very concept abstracted from it. For example, the existence of blackness in itself excludes nonexistence from blackness itself, so blackness is its quiddity. But this existence, insofar as it makes a body black, excludes a nonexistence neither from blackness in itself nor from the quiddity of the body characterized by it. Rather, it excludes it from an attribute with which the body is characterized and which lies outside its essence.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: فإنّ لها نوعُ حصول → فإنّ لها نوعَ حصول. Correct case of delayed اسم إنّ; not a consonantal emendation. Both witnesses have نوع حصول without this erroneous nominative. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 31; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 54


Mental and External Existence

N-III-01 · Chapter: The Division of Existence into Mental and External Existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 45–52
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=45&type=c_fbook

The well-known position of the philosophers is that these quiddities which exist externally and upon which their effects follow have another existence in which those very external effects do not follow upon them, although other effects, different from their external effects, do follow upon them.

This kind of existence is what we call “mental existence.” It is our knowledge of the quiddities of things.

Some have denied mental existence. Some of them hold that knowledge is only a kind of relation between the soul and the external object known. Others hold—and this view has been attributed to the ancients—that what is present in the mind when things are known consists of likenesses representing them, as a statue represents that of which it is a statue, although the two differ in quiddity. Yet others affirm likenesses which differ from their objects without representing them. On this view the soul makes an error, but an orderly error which does not disrupt human life: as if a person were supposed always to see redness as greenness and always to assign the effects of redness to what he sees as greenness.

The demonstration of mental existence is that we conceive these things which exist externally—such as a human being and a horse—with the characteristics of universality and purity, and judge them to have these characteristics. We have no doubt that what we thus conceive has some established presence within the sphere of our awareness and that we judge it to have these characteristics. It therefore exists with some existence. Since it does not exist externally with these characteristics, for externally it has the characteristics of individuality and admixture, it exists in another sphere in which its external effects do not follow upon it. We call this sphere “the mind.”

We also conceive privative things which do not exist externally, such as absolute nonexistence, the absolutely nonexistent, the conjunction of contradictories, and the other impossibilities. They have some established presence for us, since they are characterized by positive judgments: they are distinguished from other things, they become present to us after having been absent from us, and so forth. Since this is not an external established presence, for they are nonexistent externally, it is in the mind.

We have no doubt that everything we apprehend intellectually is of one kind. Thus, just as things have an external existence possessing external effects, they have an existence in the mind in which those external effects do not follow upon them, although effects other than their particular external effects do follow upon them.

If what we apprehend intellectually of things were identical with what is external, as the proponent of the relational theory holds, it would be impossible to apprehend intellectually what is not external, such as nonexistence and the nonexistent, and no error would occur in knowledge.

If what exists in the mind were a likeness of the external thing, related to it as a statue is to that of which it is a statue, identity in quiddity would be lost and sophistry would follow, since our cognitions would become instances of ignorance. Moreover, actually passing from a representation to what it represents depends upon prior knowledge of what is represented, whereas knowledge of what is represented is supposed to depend upon the representation.

If every cognition were mistaken in disclosing what lies beyond it, sophistry would follow, and this would lead to contradiction. For the claim that every cognition is mistaken also requires this universal cognition itself to be mistaken and therefore false. Its contradictory would then be true: some cognition is correct.

It has therefore been established that quiddities have a mental existence in which their effects do not follow upon them, just as they have an external existence in which their effects follow upon them. This makes clear the division of the existent into external and mental.

Several matters become clear from what has preceded.

First: a mental quiddity does not fall under, and is not included in, the category under which it fell when it was external and its effects followed upon it. It has only the concept of that category. Thus, although the mental human being is a substance, a body, growing, sentient, moving by will, and rational, it is not, insofar as it is a substance, a quiddity existing without being in a subject, nor, insofar as it is a body, something possessing three dimensions. The same applies to the other parts of the definition of the human being. It possesses only the concepts of the genera and differentiae in its definition, without the external effects following upon it—by which effects we mean first and second perfections. Falling under and being included in a category means nothing other than that its external effects follow upon the thing. Otherwise, if the mere applicability of a category's concept to something were sufficient for its inclusion under it, the category itself would be included under itself because it is predicated of itself, and it would be an individual of itself. This is the meaning of their statement: “Mental substance is substance by primary predication, not by common predication.”

The logicians' division of individuals into mental and external individuals is based upon a loose manner of speaking intended to facilitate instruction.

What has preceded resolves an objection raised against the doctrine of mental existence. According to this doctrine, essential constituents are preserved. Thus, when we apprehend substance intellectually, it is substance in view of the preservation of its essential constituents. Yet the very same thing is an accident, since it subsists in the soul as an accident subsists in its subject. It would therefore be, in itself, both substance and accident; and the impossibility of this is evident.

The resolution is that what is impossible is for one thing to be both substance and accident by common predication. Intellectually apprehended substance is substance by primary predication and accident by common predication. There is therefore no impossibility.

A second objection is that the doctrine of mental existence requires intellectually apprehended substance to be substance, in view of the preservation of its essential constituents, while knowledge, according to the philosophers, belongs to the qualities of the soul. What is intellectually apprehended of substance would therefore be included both under the category of substance and under the category of quality. This is impossible, since it would make its essence contradictory, the categories being distinct in their entire essences. Likewise, if we apprehend quantity intellectually, for example, the intelligible form would be included under both the category of quantity and the category of quality, which is impossible. Likewise, if we apprehend a visible quality intellectually, for example, it would be included under two species of the category of quality: sensible quality and quality of the soul.

The resolution is that it is a quality of the soul by common predication and is therefore included under that category. The other categories or their species, however, are predicated of it by primary predication, and this does not constitute inclusion under them at all.

A third objection is that the doctrine of mental existence requires the soul to be hot and cold together, square and triangular together, and similarly with other opposites, when it conceives these things. For by something hot, cold, square, or triangular we mean only that in which these meanings—which exist for another and qualify it—have occurred.

The resolution is that the criterion for a thing's existence being for another and for its qualifying that other is common predication. What exists in the mind of coldness, heat, and the like is such by primary predication, rather than common predication.

A fourth objection is that this doctrine requires one thing to be both universal and particular, which is evidently false. The implication is explained as follows. An intellectually apprehended human being, for example, is universal insofar as the intellect permits it to be true of many. The very same thing is particular insofar as it is an existent subsisting in one individual soul, through which it is distinguished from other things. It is therefore both universal and particular.

The resolution is that the respects differ. An intellectually apprehended human being, for example, is universal insofar as it is considered in relation to the external world, and particular insofar as it is a quality of the soul, subsisting in the soul without being considered in relation to the external world.

A fifth objection is that we conceive intrinsic impossibilities, such as a partner to the Creator, the negation of a thing of itself, and the conjunction of contradictories or the absence of both of them. If things themselves were present in the mind, this would entail the established presence of intrinsic impossibilities.

The resolution is that what is established in the mind consists only of their concepts by primary predication, not their instances by common predication. What is conceived of a partner to the Creator is a partner to the Creator by primary predication. By common predication, however, it is a possible existent and a quality of the soul, caused and created by the Creator.

Second: since mental existence is, in itself, considered in relation to the external world, it is intrinsically representative of what lies beyond it. It is therefore impossible for a thing to have a mental existence without having an external existence, either actual—as with real quiddities abstracted from external existence—or posited—as with concepts which are not quiddities and which the mind fashions by drawing in some way upon its intelligibles. It conceives the concept of nonexistence, for example, and posits for it some established presence, which it represents through the concept it has conceived. In general, the function of mental existence is to represent what lies beyond it, without the effects of what is represented following upon the representation.

This does not conflict with the occurrence of the representation's own particular effects insofar as it has the quiddity of quality. Nor does it conflict with what will be explained later: that cognitive forms are, without exception, immaterial. For the occurrence of the effects of a quality of the soul, and likewise immateriality, are judgments concerning cognitive forms in themselves. Representation and the absence of the effects, however, are judgments concerning them in relation to the external world and insofar as they constitute the mental existence of a certain external quiddity.

This resolves an objection raised against the proponents of mental existence: we conceive the earth in its vastness, with its plains, deserts, and mountains, and the sky surrounding it, with its distant reaches, and the stars and planets, with their enormous distances. The presence of these immense magnitudes in the mind—that is, their being imprinted in a part of a nerve or a part of the brain—would be the imprinting of something large in something small, which is impossible. The proposed reply that “the substrate in which the forms are imprinted is divisible without end” is of no avail. A palm cannot contain a mountain, even if it is divisible without end.

The resolution is that the truth, as will be explained later, is that particular cognitive forms are not material. They are instead immaterial with an imaginal immateriality in which the effects of matter—dimensions, colors, and shapes—are present, without matter itself. Being imprinted is a property of matter, and there is no imprinting in what is immaterial.

This also resolves another objection. Sensation and imagination, as natural scientists have explained, occur through the presence in the sensitive organs of the forms of bodies, with their external relations and particular characteristics, and through their transmission to the brain. These organs also modify the forms in accordance with their own particular natures. A human being arrives at the particular characteristics of their magnitudes, dimensions, and shapes through a kind of comparison among the parts of the form present to him, as they have explained in detail in its proper place. It is clear that these forms, present and imprinted with their particular characteristics in a material substrate, differ from the external quiddities. There would therefore be no justification for affirming mental existence and the presence of the external quiddities themselves in minds.

The resolution is that their account of material action and affection when knowledge of particulars occurs is correct in its own domain. These imprinted forms, however, are not what is known in itself. They are material things which prepare the soul and dispose it for the presence to it of external quiddities in immaterial imaginal forms. This follows from what will become clear concerning the immateriality of knowledge without exception. You have also learned that the claim that the forms in sensation and imagination differ from the very things whose forms they are in the external world cannot be separated from sophistry.

Third: since the real quiddities upon which their effects follow externally are the very ones that enter minds without their external effects following upon them, suppose there were something the very character of whose essence was identical with its being external and with the occurrence of effects—as is the case with concrete existence itself and its attributes subsisting in it, such as potentiality and actuality, unity and multiplicity, and the like. Its presence itself in the mind would be impossible. Likewise, if we suppose something the very character of whose posited essence is nullity and the absence of effects—as is the case with absolute nonexistence and what reduces to it—its entry into the mind would be impossible.

Thus, the reality of existence and everything whose essential character is that of existence, and likewise absolute nonexistence and everything whose posited essential character is that of nonexistence, cannot enter the mind in the way real quiddities enter it. This is what their statement means: “Intrinsic impossibilities have no correct form in minds.”

How the concept of existence and of that by which it is characterized, and the concept of nonexistence and of what reduces to it, are abstracted will be explained, God willing, in the discussions of intellect, the intellective subject, and the intelligible.

[translation] “Imaginal” renders mithālī: possessing such features as dimensions and shapes while being free of matter, as the author explains here.

The reader adopts «ذا التمثال». حاكى takes its represented object directly; the later editor explicitly corrects the preposition/genitive construction to the direct object. The Modalities of Propositions

N-IV-PRE · Stage IV: Necessity, Impossibility, and Possibility, and Their Restriction to Three
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 53–53
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=53&type=c_fbook

The primary aim here is to explain the division of the existent into the necessary and the possible, and to examine the properties of each. The examination of the impossible and its properties is subsidiary and intended only secondarily.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

N-IV-01 · Chapter One: Every Concept Is Either Necessary, Possible, or Impossible
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 55–62
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=55&type=c_fbook

Whatever concept we suppose and then relate existence to, either existence belongs to it necessarily, which is necessity; or existence is necessarily excluded from it—that is, nonexistence belongs to it necessarily—which is impossibility; or neither existence nor nonexistence belongs to it necessarily, which is possibility.

As for the supposition that existence and nonexistence might both belong to it necessarily, the slightest attention dismisses it. Every supposed concept, then, is either necessary, impossible, or possible.

This is a genuine disjunctive proposition, obtained from two divisions each turning upon affirmation and negation. We say: “For every supposed concept, either existence belongs to it necessarily or it does not. In the latter case, either nonexistence belongs to it necessarily or it does not. The first is the necessary, the second the impossible, and the third the possible.”

The definition of the three modal statuses supplied by this division is that a thing’s necessity consists in its existence belonging to it necessarily; its impossibility consists in its nonexistence belonging to it necessarily; and its possibility consists in the negation of both necessities in its regard. The necessary is that whose existence is necessary; the impossible is that whose nonexistence is necessary; and the possible is that whose existence and nonexistence are neither of them necessary.

These are all verbal definitions, explanations of a name that serve to draw attention to its meaning, rather than real definitions. Necessity and nonnecessity are clear, self-evident meanings that are impressed upon the soul primitively: they are known through themselves, and other things are known through them. For this reason, anyone who has tried to give them a real definition has produced circular definitions: defining the possible as “that which is not impossible”; defining the necessary as “that from the supposition of whose nonexistence an impossibility follows,” or “that whose nonexistence is impossible to suppose”; defining the impossible as “that which must not be”; and so on.

Of these three modal statuses, those primarily investigated in this discipline, which investigates the existent insofar as it is existent, are necessity and possibility, as was indicated earlier. These are two attributes by which the existent is divided in a primary division, with regard to the relation of its existence to itself.

This disposes of an objection to taking possibility as an established attribute of the possible, corresponding to necessity as an established attribute of the necessary. The objection runs as follows. As the preceding division has shown, possibility is the negation of necessary existence and the negation of necessary nonexistence. These are two negations, even if they are expressed in such a phrase as “the negation of the two necessities.” How, then, can possibility be a single attribute qualifying the possible? Granted that it amounts to the negation of the two necessities and is a single negation, it is nevertheless, as the division shows, a simple negation, not an affirmation with a negative predicate. What, then, does it mean for the possible to be qualified by it externally, when qualification requires the predicate to be made negative? Thus, when they begin to explain the properties of possibility—such as its inseparability from quiddity, its being the cause of the need for a cause, and so forth—they are obliged to express it as the nonnecessity of existence and nonexistence, or as the equal relation of quiddity to existence and nonexistence.

The answer is that a proposition with a negative predicate is equivalent to a simply negative proposition when the subject exists. The subject exists in our statements, “Some existents are neither necessary in existence nor necessary in nonexistence,” and, likewise, “Quiddity as such is neither necessary in existence nor necessary in nonexistence.” Accordingly, affirmation with a negative predicate and simple negation are equivalent in the case of possibility.

Furthermore, this negation has a relation to necessity and to its subject, of which the two necessities are denied. Through this relation it is distinguished from other negations and becomes a relative nonexistence, possessing a share of existence and effects consequent upon it. This holds even though the intellect first apprehends it in the form of simple negation, just as it first apprehends blindness, which is a relative nonexistence, in that form.

Several matters follow from the foregoing.

First: the subject of possibility is quiddity. A thing can be qualified by the nonnecessity of existence and nonexistence only if, in itself, it is devoid of both existence and nonexistence. This is nothing other than quiddity as such. Every possible thing, therefore, has a quiddity. This clarifies the meaning of their statement: “Every possible thing is a composite pair: it has a quiddity and an existence.”

Calling existence other than that of the intrinsically necessary “possible,” and naming it “possible existence,” belongs to another technical usage of possibility and necessity. In this usage, possibility and necessity mean intrinsic poverty and intrinsic self-sufficiency. What is meant is not the negation of the two necessities or an equal relation to existence and nonexistence, since that is inconceivable with regard to existence itself.

Second: possibility is inseparable from quiddity. If it were not inseparable from it, quiddity could be devoid of it and would then be necessary or impossible. It would thus be, in itself, existent or nonexistent. Yet quiddity as such is neither existent nor nonexistent.

Its being inseparable from quiddity means that merely supposing quiddity as such suffices for its qualification by possibility, without the need for anything additional. What is meant is not inseparability in the technical sense, in which the thing from which a concomitant is inseparable is a cause requiring that concomitant to be realized and to belong to it. At the level of quiddity as such there is no requirement, whether affirmative or negative.

It should not be objected: “For the negation of the two necessities to hold at the level of quiddity’s very essence entails that possibility enters into the essence of the thing, and this is plainly false.” We reply that a predicate enters into the essence only if its predication is primary predication, whose criterion is conceptual identity, rather than common predication, whose criterion is identity in existence. The predication of possibility and the other concomitants of quiddities of quiddity as such is common predication, not primary predication.

Third: possibility exists among concrete things through the existence of its subject. It is neither a purely intellectual consideration with no form among concrete things, as some have maintained, nor something existing externally through a separate, independent existence, as others have maintained.

That it exists among concrete things through the existence of its subject follows from its being, in the division, a counterpart of the necessary, whose necessity of existence is among concrete things. The absence of necessity, which is possibility, is therefore also among concrete things. Since it is posited in a division requiring the thing divided to be qualified by each of the divisions, it has the sense of a positive attribute by which its subject is qualified. It is thus a negative meaning possessing a share of existence, and quiddity is qualified by it among concrete things.

Since quiddity is qualified by it among concrete things, it has existence among them in the manner of relative nonexistences: negative attributes qualifying the things to which they belong and existing through their existence. In truth, the effects consequent upon possibility are the absence of the effects of necessity, such as the purity of existence, simplicity of essence, self-sufficiency with respect to another, and so forth.

This explanation makes clear the falsity of the view that “possibility is one of the purely intellectual considerations having no form either externally or mentally.” For it is clear that the necessity of an existent’s existence is something whose domain is external reality, and that it has positive external effects.

The view that “possibility has a separate, independent existence externally” is likewise false. For it is clear that possibility is one negative meaning shared by quiddities and established through their being established in themselves: the negation of the two necessities. There is no sense in nonexistences existing through a separate, independent existence. Moreover, if possibility existed among concrete things through a separate, independent existence, it would be either intrinsically necessary, which is self-evidently false, or possible. In the latter case, its establishment would lie beyond the establishment of quiddity and would not be sufficiently accounted for by quiddity’s being established in itself. It would therefore be through another; and the impossibility of possibility through another will be explained.

They have offered several arguments for that view. The most plausible is that, if the possible were not possible among concrete things, it would be either necessary or impossible among them. The possible would thus be necessarily existent or necessarily nonexistent, and this is absurd.

The answer is that qualification by an attribute among concrete things does not require that attribute to be realized among them through a separate, independent existence. It is enough for it to exist through the existence of what it qualifies. Possibility is one of the secondary philosophical intelligibles: its occurrence as a predicate is in the mind, while qualification by it is external, and such intelligibles exist externally through the existence of their subjects.

It is evident from the foregoing that possibility is one shared meaning, like the concept of existence.

A further point: the divisions of necessity

Necessity is divided into eternal necessity, in which the predicate belongs to the subject necessarily on account of its essence, without any restriction or condition, even that of existence. This is confined to the case in which the subject’s essence is sheer self-subsisting existence, unmixed with nonexistence and unlimited by quiddity. This is necessary existence—exalted and holy is He—with regard to the attributes by which He is qualified and which are identical with His essence.

Another division is essential necessity, in which the predicate belongs to the subject necessarily on account of its essence, with existence but not through existence. For example, we say, “Every human being is necessarily an animal.” Animality is essential to the human being and belongs to the human being necessarily so long as the human being exists, and with existence. Without existence, the essence would be null: there would be neither human being nor animal.

Another is descriptive necessity, in which the predicate belongs to the subject necessarily on account of a description of the subject. For example: “Every writer necessarily moves their fingers so long as they are writing.” Another is temporal necessity, which in one respect reduces to descriptive necessity.

Another further point: the divisions of possibility

The meaning of possibility explained above is the one investigated in these discussions. It is one of the three modalities, one of which is present in every proposition.

In ordinary usage, possibility was employed to mean the negation of necessity on the opposite side, which entails the negation of impossibility on the corresponding side. In an affirmative proposition it is true where the corresponding side is necessary, as in “A writer possibly moves their fingers,” or where necessity is denied of it, as in “A human being possibly moves their fingers.” In a negative proposition it is true where the corresponding affirmative side is impossible, as in “A writer is possibly not motionless in their fingers,” or where necessity is denied of it, as in “A human being is possibly not motionless in their fingers.”

Possibility in this sense is broader in its range of cases than possibility in the preceding sense—that is, the negation of the two necessities—and than either necessity or impossibility. It is not broader conceptually, since there is no common concept uniting the modalities. The philosophers then transferred the term specifically to the negation of necessity on both sides. They called this “special possibility” or “possibility in the special sense,” and called ordinary usage “general possibility” or “possibility in the general sense.”

Sometimes “possibility” is used to mean the negation of essential, descriptive, and temporal necessities. This is narrower than special possibility and is therefore called “more special possibility.” An example is “A human being is possibly a writer.” Human quiddity does not require writing, whether on account of its essence, on account of a description, or at a time specified in the proposition.

Sometimes “possibility” is used to mean the negation of all necessities, even necessity conditional upon the predicate. This concerns future matters in which neither affirmation nor negation has become determinate. Necessity is denied of them even with regard to the predicate, affirmatively and negatively. This consideration belongs to the simple, ordinary outlook, which is characteristically ignorant of future events because it does not encompass their causes and grounds. Otherwise, every supposed matter, in its own circumstances, has either existence and necessity, or nonexistence and impossibility.

Sometimes “possibility” is used to mean dispositional possibility. This is an existential attribute belonging to the qualities that subsist in matter, through which matter receives different actualities. It differs from special possibility in being an existential attribute that admits of strength and weakness, and of proximity to and remoteness from actuality. Its subject is existing matter, and it disappears from matter with the existence of that for which the matter was disposed. Special possibility, by contrast, is an intellectual meaning admitting neither strength and weakness nor proximity and remoteness. Its subject is quiddity as such, and it never departs from quiddity, whether quiddity exists or does not exist.

Sometimes “possibility” is used to mean that a thing is such that supposing its occurrence entails no impossibility. This is called “possibility of occurrence.”

Sometimes “possibility” is used to mean the dependence of the existence of an effect upon the existence of its cause, and its constitution through that existence—specifically, the intrinsic poverty of possible existence in relation to necessary existence, majestic and exalted is He. This is called “possibility as poverty” and “existential possibility,” in contrast to quiddative possibility.

Another further point

The three modalities mentioned are not restricted to propositions whose predicate is existence. One of them intervenes between any supposed predicate and any supposed subject to which it is related. Philosophy, however, treats them only insofar as they intervene between existence and its essential accidents, since its subject is the existent insofar as it is existent.

[translation] “Simple negation” renders salb taḥṣīlī; “affirmation with a negative predicate” renders ījāb ʿudūlī. The distinction is between denying a predicate and affirming a predicate that itself contains negation. “Special possibility” means the absence of necessity on both the affirmative and negative sides.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

N-IV-02 · Chapter Two: The Division of Each of the Three Modalities into What Is Intrinsic, What Is Through Another, and What Is Relative to Another, with an Exception for Possibility
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 63–66
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=63&type=c_fbook

Each of these three modalities is divided into what is intrinsic, what is through another, and what is relative to another, with an exception for possibility: there is no possibility through another.

By “intrinsic” is meant that positing the essence, while disregarding everything other than it, suffices for its characterization by the modality. “Through another” means that positing it in this way does not suffice; rather, the characterization depends on another’s bestowal and necessitation. “Relative to another” means that the characterization is considered in relation to another, in virtue of a requirement on its part, “requirement” here being broader than causal necessitation.

Intrinsic necessity is exemplified by the necessity of existence belonging to the essence of the Necessary Existent—exalted is He—for His essence through His essence. Necessity through another is exemplified by the necessity of existence that accrues to a possible being from its complete cause. Intrinsic impossibility is exemplified by the necessity of nonexistence belonging to intrinsically impossible things that, owing to their supposed essences, cannot admit existence: for example, the conjunction of contradictories, their joint exclusion, and the negation of a thing of itself. Impossibility through another is exemplified by the necessity of nonexistence that accrues to a possible being from the absence of its cause. Intrinsic possibility is a thing’s being such that, in itself and disregarding everything other than it, both the necessity of existence and the necessity of nonexistence are denied of it.

Possibility through another, however, is impossible, as was indicated earlier. For if possibility through another accrued to a thing from an external cause that necessitated it, the thing considered in itself, disregarding everything else, would be either intrinsically necessary, intrinsically impossible, or intrinsically possible, since, as established, the division into these three is exhaustive. On either of the first two alternatives, the accession of possibility from outside would entail a transformation of its intrinsic status. On the third alternative, namely, its being intrinsically possible, either it would be such that, if we supposed the external cause to be removed, the thing would remain in its former state of possibility, or it would not. In the former case, the other would have no effect on it, since the other’s existence and nonexistence would be alike in this respect, although it was supposed to have an effect. This is a contradiction. If it did not remain possible, it would not be intrinsically possible, although it was supposed to be so. This too is a contradiction.

This is so if what is intrinsic and what is through another are one possibility that is both intrinsic and through another. If they are supposed to be two possibilities, one intrinsic and one through another, then one thing, in one and the same respect, would have two possibilities for a single existence. This is plainly untenable, just as the realization of two existences for one thing is untenable.

Moreover, the supposition of possibility through another supposes an external cause that necessitates possibility, and this amounts to the joint exclusion of contradictories. For the other that confers possibility—which is the nonnecessity of existence and nonexistence—can confer it only by removing the cause that necessitates existence and removing the cause that necessitates nonexistence, the latter being the absence of the cause that necessitates existence. Its conferral of possibility is thus complete only if it removes both the existence and the nonexistence of the cause that necessitates existence. This involves the joint exclusion of contradictories.

Necessity relative to another is exemplified by the necessity of a cause when it is considered relative to its effect, owing to a requirement on the effect’s part: by existing, the effect permits nothing other than that its cause should exist. It is also exemplified by the necessity of an effect when it is considered relative to its complete cause, owing to causal necessitation on the cause’s part: by existing, the cause permits nothing other than that its effect should exist. Another example is the necessity of either of two correlatives when it is considered relative to the existence of the other.

The general criterion is that the two things compared should stand in the relation of cause and effect, or should be effects of one cause. Without a causal connection between them, neither would depend on the other, and the establishment of one would not require the establishment of the other.

Impossibility relative to another is exemplified by the impossibility of the complete cause’s existence when it is considered relative to the nonexistence of its effect, owing to a requirement; by the impossibility of the effect’s existence when it is considered relative to the nonexistence of its cause, owing to causal necessitation; and by the impossibility of the existence of either of two correlatives when considered relative to the nonexistence of the other, or of its nonexistence when considered relative to the other’s existence.

Possibility relative to another is the condition of a thing when it is considered relative to something that requires neither its existence nor its nonexistence.

The general criterion is that the two should neither stand in the relation of cause and effect nor be effects of a third thing.

There is no possibility relative to another between two existing beings. For the thing compared is either the intrinsically Necessary Existent considered relative to a possible being, or the reverse, and these stand in the relation of cause and effect; or it is a possible being considered relative to another possible being, and both ultimately terminate in the intrinsically Necessary Existent.

The intrinsically Necessary Existent does, however, have possibility relative to another when considered relative to another supposed Necessary Existent, or to the effects created by that other. For the two do not stand in the relation of cause and effect, nor are they effects of a third thing.

Analogous to two supposed intrinsically necessary beings are two intrinsically impossible things when either is considered relative to the other, or relative to what the other entails.

There is likewise possibility relative to another between the intrinsically Necessary Existent and a possible being that does not exist because some of the conditions of its existence are absent. Its nonexistence is an effect of the absence of its complete cause; on this supposition, the intrinsically Necessary Existent is one of that cause’s components and does not necessitate the supposed possible being. The intrinsically Necessary Existent therefore has possibility relative to it, and conversely.

What has preceded establishes, first, that the intrinsically necessary cannot be necessary through another or impossible through another. Likewise, the intrinsically impossible cannot be impossible through another or necessary through another.

It also establishes that everything necessary through another is possible, and likewise that everything impossible through another is possible.

Second, if two intrinsically necessary beings were supposed, no relation of entailment would obtain between them. Such a relation obtains only between two things of which one is the cause of the other, or which are both effects of a third cause. Being an effect has no place in an intrinsically necessary being.

[translation] “Requirement” renders istidʿāʾ, which the author expressly distinguishes from the narrower causal necessitation, iqtiḍāʾ. “Relative to another” renders bi-l-qiyās ilā al-ghayr and is distinct from “through another,” bi-l-ghayr.

The later editor expressly identifies his preferred reading in a variant note. The reader retains «فإفادتها الإمكانَ لا تَتمّ إلاّ برفعها»; «فإفادته الإمكانَ لا تَتمّ إلاّ برفعه» is recorded as an editorial alternative. Record both readings and the explicit p.84 n.1 note. This is printed/digital edition evidence, not manuscript certification.
