N-IV-03 · Chapter Three: The Quiddity of the Intrinsically Necessary Existent Is Its Existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 66–70
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=66&type=c_fbook

The quiddity of the intrinsically necessary existent is its existence: that is, it has no quiddity beyond the existence proper to it.

The proposition is evident in light of what was established previously, that possibility is inseparable from quiddity. Every quiddity is therefore possible. By contraposition, whatever is not possible has no quiddity. Accordingly, what is intrinsically necessary has no quiddity beyond its necessary existence.

Nevertheless, they have also offered arguments for this.

The strongest is that, if what is intrinsically necessary had a quiddity beyond the existence proper to it, its existence would be additional to that quiddity and accidental to it. Everything accidental has a cause. Its existence would therefore be caused either by its quiddity or by something else. The second alternative—being caused by another—contradicts intrinsic necessity of existence. The first—being caused by its quiddity—requires its quiddity to precede its existence in existence, because a cause must necessarily precede its effect in existence. If this prior existence were identical with the subsequent existence, a thing would precede itself, which is impossible. If it were other than it, one quiddity would exist through more than one existence, the impossibility of which has already been established. Moreover, we can transfer the discussion to the prior existence, and an infinite regress follows.

It has been objected that its quiddity might be a cause necessitating its existence, preceding it by quiddity, just as the parts of a quiddity are causes of its constitution and precede it by quiddity, rather than by existence.

The reply is that an effect necessarily depends, in its mode of existence, on the existence of its cause. The cause’s priority is therefore in the very mode in which the effect is established, except that the cause has it more intensely. If the effect is established externally, its cause precedes it in external existence; and if it is established mentally, the same applies.

Since the existence of what is necessary through itself is real and external, if it had a quiddity that was a cause necessitating its existence, its quiddity would have to precede it in external existence, rather than in quidditative establishment. The difficulty thus remains.

Another argument: considering any quiddity in itself, the intellect allows that it may have, beyond the individuals of it that exist, further individuals without end.

Any individual that has not been realized is therefore impossible through another. If it were intrinsically impossible, no individual of that quiddity would ever be realized.

If, then, this thing possessing a quiddity is supposed to be intrinsically necessary, its quiddity will be a universal having, beyond its individuals that exist externally, nonexistent individuals whose existence is admissible when the quiddity itself is considered. They are impossible only through another. It is known that impossibility through another cannot coexist with intrinsic necessity. It has already been established that everything necessary through another or impossible through another is possible. Thus the intrinsically necessary existent has no quiddity beyond its proper existence.

It has been objected: why may the intrinsically necessary existent not have an existential reality that is not additional to its essence but identical with it, which the intellect then analyzes into existence and a particular, individual, nonuniversal subject of that existence, this subject being its quiddity?

The reply is that the argument rests on the true position that individuation is through existence alone, as will be explained in the discussions of quiddity.

The foregoing has thus shown that what is intrinsically necessary is an existential reality without any quiddity to delimit it. It is necessary in existence through its very essence, without needing an additional causal or qualifying aspect. This is eternal necessity.

It was established in the first stage that existence is an objective, graded reality with different degrees. Each of its degrees possesses the existential perfection belonging to what is below it, sustains what is below it, and is sustained by what is above it, while lacking some of the latter’s perfection. This is deficiency and need. The exception is the highest of the degrees, which possesses every perfection and lacks none, by which every degree is sustained, and which is not sustained by anything beyond its own essence.

On the doctrine of gradation, the necessary reality thus corresponds to the highest degree: beyond it there is no degree to delimit it; there is no perfection in existence that it lacks; no deficiency or nonexistence mingles with its essence; and no need restricts it. The negative attributes inseparable from it reduce to the negation of negation and the absence of deficiency and need, which is affirmation.

This disposes of several objections that have been raised against denying quiddity to what is intrinsically necessary.

One is that the reality of what is intrinsically necessary does not equal the reality of anything else, because the reality of anything else entails possibility, whereas its reality excludes it. Its existence, however, equals the existence of the possible in being existence. Its reality must therefore differ from its existence; otherwise the existence of every possible thing would be necessary.

Another is that, if the existence of what is intrinsically necessary were devoid of quiddity, this description would belong to it either through itself or through another. If through itself, the existence of every possible thing would be necessary, because existence is shared, and this is impossible. If through another, it would need another, entailing possibility, contrary to the supposition.

Another is that what is intrinsically necessary is the principle of possible things. If it is devoid of quiddity, then, if it is a principle through itself, every existence must likewise be a principle. Every possible thing would consequently be a cause of itself and its causes, which is plainly impossible. If it is a principle through its existence together with the qualification of being devoid of quiddity, the first principle must be composite; indeed, it must be nonexistent, because one of its two parts—being devoid of quiddity—is privative. If it is a principle on condition of being devoid of quiddity, every existence could be the principle of every existence, although this judgment fails to apply to it because it lacks the condition, namely, being devoid of quiddity.

Another is that, if what is necessary through its essence is being in concrete reality itself—that is, unrestricted being—every existent must be necessary. If it is being together with the qualification of being devoid of quiddity, the necessary existent must be composite, although that qualification is a privative meaning and cannot be a part of the necessary existent. If it is being on condition of being devoid of quiddity, the intrinsically necessary existent will not be necessary through its essence. If it is other than being in concrete reality, then, if it is without being, it must not be existent, since an existence without being is inconceivable. If being is internal to it, composition follows. All the preceding consequences are plainly false. If being is external to it, its existence is external to its reality, which is what was to be proved. These are among the objections.

The reason they fail is that the existence taken in them is either the general, self-evident concept, which is an intellectually considered meaning distinct from necessary existence, the latter being an objective reality proper to the necessary existent; or it is a shared, univocal universal nature whose instances are equal. Objective existence, however, is a graded reality with different degrees, the highest of which is the existence proper to what is intrinsically necessary.

Moreover, being devoid of quiddity is not a privative description. It means the negation of a limit, which is a negation of negation and returns to affirmation.

It has also become clear that the necessity of existence belonging to what is intrinsically necessary is eternal necessity, rather than essential or descriptive necessity. For one kind of necessity is eternal: the necessity of a predicate’s belonging to a subject through the subject itself, without any qualification or condition, as in our statement, “The necessary existent necessarily exists.”

Another kind is essential necessity: the necessity of a predicate’s belonging to the essence of a subject with existence, rather than through existence. This holds whether the subject’s essence causes the predicate, as in our statement, “Every triangle necessarily has its three angles equal to two right angles,” for the quiddity of the triangle causes this equality when it exists; or whether the subject’s essence does not cause the predicate’s belonging to it, as in our statement, “Every human is necessarily human,” or “necessarily animal,” or “necessarily rational.” For the necessity of a thing’s belonging to itself means its inseparability from itself while it exists, without its essence being a cause of itself.

Another kind is descriptive necessity: the necessity of a predicate’s belonging to a subject by virtue of its description, with existence, rather than through existence, as in our statement, “Every writer necessarily moves his fingers for as long as he is writing.” This has already been indicated.

[translation] In the chapter heading, inniyya denotes existence or the fact of being. “Essential necessity” (ḍarūra dhātiyya), as a classification of propositions, is distinguished here from the eternal necessity of the intrinsically necessary existent.

N-IV-04 · Chapter Four: What Is Intrinsically Necessary in Existence Is Necessary in Existence in Every Respect
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 70–74
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=70&type=c_fbook

What is intrinsically necessary in existence is necessary in existence in every respect.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn, may God have mercy upon him, said: “What is meant by this is that there is no aspect of possibility in the Necessary Existent. Everything that is possible for it in the sense of general possibility is necessary for it.

“One consequence of this property is that it has no state awaiting realization: the former is a principle from which this latter judgment follows.

“The latter is not identical with the former, as many people have supposed. It is the former that is counted among the properties peculiar to the intrinsically Necessary Existent, rather than the latter, for the luminous separate beings also possess the latter property. If a separate being had a state of perfection awaiting realization that could come to be in it, this would entail the realization of dispositional possibility in it and its being affected by the world of motion and bodily configurations. That would necessitate its becoming corporeal and turbid, despite its being immaterial and luminous. This is a contradiction.” End of quotation.

The proof is as follows. If what is intrinsically necessary in existence, and free of quiddity, had an aspect of possibility in relation to some perfection among existential perfections, its essence in itself would lack that perfection, and the absence of that perfection would be established in it. It would therefore be composed of existence and nonexistence. This entails composition in the essence; composition entails need; and need entails possibility. Yet its necessity has been assumed. This is a contradiction.

Another proof: if the essence of the intrinsically Necessary Existent were not sufficient for the necessity of some attribute of perfection that it could possess, it would need another with respect to that attribute. Let us then consider the intrinsically necessary essence in itself, setting aside the existence and nonexistence of that other. If it is necessary together with the existence of that attribute, the causality of the other becomes redundant, though the other has been assumed to be a cause. This is a contradiction. If it is necessary together with the absence of that attribute, a contradiction likewise follows.

It has been objected that the intellect’s not taking the cause into consideration does not conflict with its being realized in reality. Likewise, considering a quiddity insofar as it is itself, and its being free under this consideration of existence, nonexistence, and the cause necessitating either, does not conflict with its possessing one of the two externally and with the occurrence of its cause.

The reply is that this is an analogy between different cases. The aspect of quiddity insofar as it is itself differs from the aspect of actuality. The intellect may therefore consider a quiddity and restrict its attention to it insofar as it is itself, without attending to anything else—existence, nonexistence, or their cause.

External existence is different. The aspect of its essence is identical with the aspect of actuality and the very substance of realization. It therefore cannot be considered without taking into account everything connected with it, whether cause or condition.

The proof can be formulated in another way. The essence’s being insufficient for the necessity of one of its attributes of perfection calls for its needing another for that attribute’s necessity. That other is thus the necessitating cause. This entails that what is intrinsically necessary in existence possesses necessity through another, whose impossibility has already been established.

An objection has been raised against the thesis itself: it is contradicted by the relations and connections that accrue to the necessary essence from its acts pertaining to its possible, originated effects. Relations and connections subsist through their terms and follow those terms in possibility—for example, creating, providing sustenance, giving life, causing death, and the rest.

This is answered by noting that these relations and connections, and the attributes derived from them, are meanings abstracted from the level of action rather than the level of essence, as will be explained.

Certainly, the existence of these relations and connections has a real connection with Him, exalted is He; and the attributes derived from them for the essence are necessary through the necessity of the essence. His being such as to create, His being such as to provide sustenance, and so forth are therefore necessary attributes. They ultimately refer to the illuminative relation. A detailed account of this will be given later, God willing.

From the foregoing, the following have become clear.

First, necessary existence is pure existence, having no quiddity and accompanied by no nonexistence. It therefore possesses every perfection in existence.

Second, it is one with the unity of purity, which is called “true unity.” This means that anything you suppose to be a second to it must necessarily differ from it by some perfection absent from it. Its essence would thus be composed of existence and nonexistence and would cease to be sheer and pure existence, although it has been assumed to be pure. This is a contradiction. In its sheer essence, then, it is such that whenever you suppose a second to it, that second returns to being the first.

This is what is meant by their saying: “It is one, but not numerically.”

Third, it is simple and has no part, either intellectually or externally. Otherwise, it would cease to be pure existence, although it has been assumed to be pure. This is a contradiction.

Fourth, that from which its necessity is abstracted is precisely that from which its existence is abstracted. It follows that each of its attributes—all of which are necessary—is identical with every other attribute, and all are identical with the exalted essence.

Fifth, necessity, like unity, is among the aspects of necessary existence and does not fall outside its essence. It is the firmness of existence, which ultimately consists in its explicit contradiction of absolute nonexistence and its exclusion of it. Nonexistence therefore cannot befall it.

Possible existence, too, contradicts and excludes nonexistence. But it is connective in relation to its cause, which is the intrinsically Necessary Existent, either immediately or through an intermediary; it subsists through that cause and is in no way independent of it. Consequently, no judgment applies to it in itself except with its cause conjoined to it. It is therefore necessary through the necessitation of its cause, the intrinsically Necessary Existent, and resists and excludes nonexistence with that cause conjoined to it.

[translation] “A state awaiting realization” renders ḥāla muntaẓara: an unrealized state or perfection still capable of being acquired. “The illuminative relation” renders al-iḍāfa al-ishrāqiyya.

[Original quotation source · restored locator] Al-Asfār, vol. 1, p. 122.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: حيثيّه → حيثيّة. Tāʾ marbūṭa in the single external-existence paragraph; early p.56/later p.95. Grade: orthographic-transcription-repair; consonantal-reading-or-standard-spelling. Source: 1404 pp. 56; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 95

Adopted: للذاّت الواجبيّة → للذّات الواجبيّة. Move shadda from alif to dhāl; consonantal text unchanged. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 57; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 95,96

N-IV-05 · Chapter Five: A Thing Does Not Exist Unless It Becomes Necessary, and the Refutation of the Doctrine of Priority
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 74–78
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=74&type=c_fbook

It has already been explained that quiddity, at the level of its essence, is nothing but itself: neither existent nor nonexistent, nor anything else. Both the necessity of existence and the necessity of nonexistence are denied of it by simple negation; this is possibility. In the intellect, therefore, it stands in an equal relation to existence and nonexistence. The intellect has no doubt that its being qualified by either existence or nonexistence cannot derive from the quiddity itself, since its relation to the two is equal, and that neither can this occur without a cause. Rather, it depends on something beyond the quiddity that takes it out of this state of equality and gives preponderance to existence or nonexistence for it. This is the cause.

The cause's giving preponderance to the side of existence can only be its making existence necessary. Without this necessitation, existence would not be determined for the quiddity: either alternative would remain admissible, and the question would remain, “Why did it become existent when nonexistence was admissible for it?” A cause's bringing something into existence is therefore not complete unless it first makes existence necessary for the effect.

The account of the cause of nonexistence and its imparting impossibility to its effect is analogous to the account of the cause of existence and its imparting necessity.

Thus a cause of existence is not a complete cause unless it necessitates, and a cause of nonexistence is not a complete cause unless it is such as to impart impossibility to its effect. A thing does not exist unless it becomes necessary, and it does not become nonexistent unless it becomes impossible.

Some have said: “The necessity of the effect's existence entails that the cause, without qualification, is compelled—mūjab, with the jīm vocalized with a fatḥa—and is not free in its choice. It therefore entails that the Necessary Existent, exalted is He, is compelled in His act and is not free in His choice, which is impossible.”

This is answered as follows. The necessity with which the effect is qualified is necessity through another. The effect's necessity is abstracted from its existence and does not extend beyond it. It is impossible for an effect to influence the existence of its cause, when the effect follows upon the cause, is posterior to it, and subsists through it.

What has preceded makes clear the invalidity of the doctrine of priority in all its forms.

To explain: some theologians supposed that to say that a possible thing is qualified by necessity when either existence or nonexistence acquires preponderance for it entails that the Necessary Existent, in being the source of bringing things into existence, is an agent who is compelled—mūjab, with the jīm vocalized with a fatḥa; exalted and holy is He above that. They therefore held that either alternative acquires preponderance for the possible thing when the quiddity leaves its state of equality and tends toward one of the two sides, so that existence has priority for it, or nonexistence has priority for it, without either side reaching the point at which it takes the quiddity beyond the limit of possibility. Thus, among quiddities, those that exist have acquired preponderance through existence having priority for them without necessity, and those that do not exist through nonexistence having priority for them without necessity.

They divided priority into intrinsic priority, which the quiddity demands through its essence or from which it is inseparable, and nonintrinsic priority, which an external cause imparts. Each of these two kinds is either sufficient for the effect to occur or insufficient.

It is reported of some of the ancients that they recognized a priority of existence in some existents, the consequence of which is that they exist more frequently, that their existence is more intense and stronger, or that fewer conditions are required for their occurrence. They recognized a priority of nonexistence in some others, the consequence of which is that they exist less frequently, that their existence is weaker, or that more conditions are required for their occurrence.

It is reported of some that they recognized priority only on the side of nonexistence, and only in relation to one group of existents.

It is reported of some that they recognized a priority of nonexistence in relation to all possible existents, on the ground that nonexistence occurs more easily.

These are their various positions.

What has preceded makes clear that the doctrine of priority is unsound at its root. The attainment of priority on either the side of existence or that of nonexistence does not eliminate the admissibility of the other alternative's occurrence.

The question of why the alternative possessing priority becomes determined, while the other alternative remains admissible, is still in place—even if priorities proceed without end—until one reaches something through which the alternative possessing priority becomes determined and the admissibility of the other alternative is eliminated. That is necessity.

Moreover, the doctrine of priority invalidates the necessary truth that possible quiddities depend on a cause for their existence and nonexistence. On this doctrine, the less preponderant alternative may occur even though priority has been attained by the other alternative and its complete cause is present.

It has already been explained that, in that case, the cause of the less preponderant alternative that occurs cannot be realized. Its occurrence would thus not depend on a cause. This is contrary to the supposition.

There are other arguments with which they have refuted these positions and clarified their unsoundness. We have refrained from presenting them, since the matter is already clear from what has preceded.

As for the claim that necessity through another—that is, the effect's being made necessary by its cause—entails that the cause is compelled, mūjab with the jīm vocalized with a fatḥa, its unsoundness is clear, as explained earlier. This necessity is an intellectual abstraction from the effect's existence and is not something added to that existence. In its entire reality, the effect is something derivative from its cause, subsisting in its very essence through the cause, and posterior to it. Something whose condition is this cannot intelligibly influence the cause and act upon it.

One consequence of this issue is that propositions whose modality is priority are not demonstrative propositions, since there is no modality other than necessity and possibility—unless, indeed, the meaning comes down to a kind of gradation.

A clarification

The necessity of existence for quiddity discussed above is necessity through another. It is prior to the quiddity's existence and is abstracted from that existence. There is also another, posterior necessity that attaches to an existent quiddity, called necessity conditional upon the predicate. For if it were possible for a quiddity qualified by existence, while it remains so qualified, to be overtaken by the nonexistence that opposes and excludes that existence, this would amount to the possibility of two contradictories being conjoined, which is impossible. It follows that existence cannot be separated from the quiddity so long as it is qualified by existence, and insofar as it is so qualified. This is the necessity of existence in this respect.

An analogous account applies to the posterior impossibility that attaches to a nonexistent quiddity.

An existent quiddity is thus encompassed by two necessities, and a nonexistent quiddity by two impossibilities.

It should be understood that this posterior necessity is necessity through another, just as the prior necessity is necessity through another. This is because the posterior necessity is abstracted from the quiddity's existence insofar as the quiddity is qualified by it, whereas the prior necessity is abstracted from that existence insofar as it is related to the cause that bestows it.

[translation] “Priority” translates awlawiyya: a preponderance of one alternative that, in the doctrine under criticism, falls short of making that alternative necessary. It does not mean temporal precedence or desirability.

[translation] The argument distinguishes mūjib, “necessitating,” from mūjab, “compelled.” At the statement of when a cause of existence becomes complete, the active vocalization موجِبَة is editorially supplied to express its necessitating the effect. The 1404 print (p.59) and 1428 edition (vol.1 p.99) leave the decisive vowel unmarked there; the subsequent objection expressly identifies the passive reading by “with fatḥa on the jīm.”

[Unadopted later editorial reading] The 1404 print reads «أن يؤثر المعلول»; the 1428 edition reads «أن يؤثّر وجوب المعلول». The later editor expressly identifies his preferred reading in a variant note. The reader retains «أن يؤثّر المعلولُ»; «أن يؤثّر وجوبُ المعلولِ» is recorded as an editorial alternative. Retain the early statement about the effect, which already excludes the necessity abstracted from it from compelling the cause. The later editor’s addition وجوب is a plausible specification, but it narrows an intelligible early sentence; preserve it as an attributed alternative. This is printed/digital edition evidence, not manuscript certification.

N-IV-06 · Chapter Six: The Possible Existent’s Need for a Cause, and Why Possibility, Rather Than Origination, Is the Ground of That Need
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 78–83
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=78&type=c_fbook

The possible existent’s need for something beyond its quiddity—that is, its dependence upon such a thing in acquiring existence or nonexistence—is among the primary self-evident truths. Assent to it requires no more than conceiving its subject and predicate. For when we conceive a quiddity as possible, equally related to existence and nonexistence, and conceive the preponderance of either side for it and its acquisition of that side as dependent upon something beyond the quiddity, we assent to this without delay. Thus, the possible existent’s being characterized by either of the two attributes, existence and nonexistence, depends upon something beyond itself, which we call the “cause.” No sound intellect doubts this.

To allow that it may be characterized by either side, while it is possible and equally related to both, neither through itself nor through something beyond itself, is to depart from the natural disposition of human understanding.

Is the ground of its need for a cause its possibility or its origination?

A group of theologians have maintained the second position.

The truth is the first, which is the position maintained by the philosophers. They argue for it as follows. A quiddity, considered with its existence, necessarily exists, and, considered with its nonexistence, necessarily does not exist. These are two necessities conditional upon the predicate, and necessity is the ground of independence from a cause or reason. Origination is a thing’s existence after its nonexistence. If you wish, say that it is the succession of one of these two necessities after the other. Necessity, as you have learned, is the ground of independence from a cause. Unless, therefore, the quiddity is considered in respect of its possibility, independence is not removed and need is not established. Need is established only through its ground, and it has no ground other than possibility.

Another argument: origination, namely existence’s being preceded by nonexistence, is an attribute of a particular existence. It is therefore preceded by the effect’s existence, since that which is qualified precedes its attribute. Existence is preceded by the cause’s bestowal of existence; the bestowal of existence is preceded by the effect’s necessity; and its necessity is preceded by the cause’s necessitation, as explained earlier. The cause’s necessitation is preceded by the effect’s need, and the effect’s need is preceded by its possibility. For if it were not possible, it would be either necessary or impossible, and necessity and impossibility are grounds of independence from a cause. Consequently, if origination were the ground of need—and a cause necessarily precedes its effect—it would precede itself by several degrees, which is impossible.

The ground is therefore possibility, since nothing else capable of being its ground precedes need, and need is present or absent together with possibility.

This argument rules out origination’s being something on which need depends under every possible version of the claim: that origination alone is the ground; that possibility and origination together are the ground; that origination is the ground and possibility a condition; or that possibility is the ground and origination a condition, or the absence of origination an impediment.

They have argued against possibility alone being the ground of need as follows. If the ground of need for a cause were possibility without origination, it would be permissible for something beginningless in time to exist—that is, something not preceded by temporal nonexistence. But this is impossible. Because its existence is perpetual, nonexistence has no access to it such that removing that nonexistence would require a cause to bestow existence upon it. The perpetuity of its existence therefore makes it independent of a cause.

The reply is that the subject of need is the quiddity insofar as it is possible, rather than the quiddity insofar as it exists. The quiddity with the attribute of possibility is retained with perpetual existence just as it is retained with any other existence. A quiddity whose existence is beginningless therefore needs a cause insofar as it is possible, just as does a quiddity whose existence originates; and perpetual existence is bestowed upon it just as originated existence is. As for the existent quiddity insofar as it exists, it has necessity conditional upon the predicate. Necessity is the ground of independence from a cause in the sense that what exists, insofar as it exists, does not need another existingness to supervene upon it.

Moreover, by the origination they require as a condition of need, they mean temporal origination: existence’s being preceded by temporal nonexistence. Their claim is therefore contradicted by time itself, since time’s being preceded by temporal nonexistence has no meaning.

Furthermore, affirming time before every possible quiddity affirms the motion that traces out time; this affirms something moving in which the motion subsists; and this affirms a moving body, matter, and form. Thus, whenever existence is supposed for a possible quiddity, there is an interval of time before it, and whenever an interval of time is supposed, a possible quiddity accompanies it. Time, then, is not preceded by temporal nonexistence.

Some have replied to the counterexample that time is an intellectually considered, imagined affair, to which there is no harm in ascribing beginninglessness, since it has no reality beyond imagination.

The difficulty is that this destroys the position they have built by grounding the possible existent’s need in its temporal origination, since on this account what is originated and what is beginningless become one and the same.

Others have replied that time is abstracted from the existence of the Necessary Existent, exalted is He, and so belongs to the domain of the First Principle, exalted is He; there is therefore no harm in its being beginningless.

It has been objected that time is intrinsically changing, and abstracting it from the essence of the Intrinsically Necessary Existent would entail change entering His essence, exalted and sanctified is He.

This has been answered by saying that it is permissible for an abstracted meaning not to correspond in every respect to the instance from which it is abstracted, and thus to differ from it.

The difficulty is that permitting the abstracted concept to differ from that from which it is abstracted is sophistry. For if a concept could differ from its instance, the edifice of cognitive assent would be destroyed at its foundation.

A clarification

It was explained in the discussions of nonexistence that nonexistence is sheer nullity: it has no thinghood, and there is no differentiation within it. Nevertheless, the intellect sometimes relates it to existence, so that it gains a certain mental establishment and a certain share of existence. One nonexistence is thereby distinguished from another: the nonexistence of sight from the nonexistence of hearing, for example, and the nonexistence of a human being from the nonexistence of a horse. The intellect then assigns to it the necessary judgments it apprehends, which in truth amount to establishing the corresponding judgments concerning existence.

Of this kind is the intellect’s judgment that the possible quiddity, in acquiring nonexistence, needs a cause, namely the nonexistence of the cause of existence.

When the intellect conceives the quiddity as such, devoid of both realization and nonrealization, and then compares existence and nonexistence to it, it necessarily finds that the quiddity’s realization through existence depends upon an existent cause. It follows that if the cause of its existence does not exist, the caused quiddity does not exist. The judgment is thus completed: because of its possibility, the possible quiddity needs a determining factor for its being characterized by either existence or nonexistence, to make that side preponderate. The determining factor for existence is the existence of the cause, and the determining factor for nonexistence is its nonexistence. That is, if the cause bestowing existence is absent, the caused quiddity does not exist. The reality of this is that the possible quiddity’s existence depends upon the existence of its cause.

[translation] Ḥudūth is rendered “origination”; where the text specifies ḥudūth zamānī, it means temporal origination. Qidam zamānī is rendered “beginninglessness in time,” rather than the broader and potentially ambiguous “eternity.”

N-IV-07 · Chapter Seven: The Possible Existent Needs a Cause in Its Continuance Just as It Needs One in Its Origination
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 83–83
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=83&type=c_fbook

This is because the ground of its need for a cause is its possibility, which is inseparable from its quiddity, as explained earlier. The quiddity remains with it in its continuance just as it is present with it in its origination. It therefore needs the cause that bestows its existence both in origination and in continuance, which is what was to be shown.

Another argument: the concrete identity of each thing is its own particular existence, while quiddity is derivative and abstracted from it, as explained earlier. The existence of the possible effect is a connective existence, dependent in its very essence upon its cause and constituted through it. It has no independence apart from the cause and never sheds this condition, as will be explained, God willing. Its condition with respect to needing a cause is therefore the same in origination and continuance, and need is inseparable from it.

The difference between the two arguments is that the first establishes the conclusion through quidditative possibility, meaning the equal relation of the quiddity to existence and nonexistence; the second establishes it through existential possibility, meaning the existential poverty constituted through the cause’s independence.

[Textual correction · source comparison] The stored transcription reads «عن هذه الشأن»; the 1404 print and 1428 edited digital text support «عن هذا الشأن». The reader adopts «عن هذا الشأن». Both independent witnesses use masculine هذا with الشأن. English already correctly translates this condition. This is printed/digital edition evidence, not manuscript certification.

N-IV-08 · Chapter Eight: Some Judgments Concerning What Is Intrinsically Impossible
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 84–88
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=84&type=c_fbook

Since intrinsic impossibility is the necessity of nonexistence when the supposed essence of a thing is considered, it stands opposite to intrinsic necessity, which is the necessity of existence when the actual essence of a thing is considered. Judgments corresponding to the opposites of those concerning intrinsic necessity therefore apply to it.

In the Asfār, after explaining that just as the intellect cannot comprehend the reality of the intrinsically Necessary Existent because of His supreme glory and the infinitude of His greatness and majesty, so too it cannot conceive the intrinsically impossible, precisely as intrinsically impossible, because of its extreme deficiency, its sheer nullity, and its being no thing, he says:

“Just as it has been established that what is intrinsically necessary cannot be necessary through another, so too what is intrinsically impossible cannot be impossible through another, by a corresponding argument. And just as a single thing cannot have two necessities, one intrinsic and one through another, or both intrinsic, or both through another, so a single thing cannot have two impossibilities in these ways.

“It has therefore become clear that whatever is characterized by necessity or impossibility through another is intrinsically possible.

“Whatever entails the intrinsically impossible is inevitably impossible in the respect in which it entails the impossible, even if it has another respect in which it is possible. Its entailment of the impossible, however, is only in its respect of impossibility. For example, a body’s being unlimited in its dimensions entails something intrinsically impossible: that what is bounded should be unbounded. This ultimately amounts to a thing’s being other than itself while being identical with itself. One of these is intrinsically impossible, and the other is impossible through another. The latter is therefore necessarily possible under a consideration other than that of its connection with the intrinsically impossible. This corresponds to what you have learned about a thing’s entailing the intrinsically Necessary Existent: it does so, not in respect of its possible quiddity, but in respect of the necessity of its existence as a possible existent.

“In general, just as entailment in existence between two things requires a relation of cause and effect between the things connected by entailment, so too entailment in nonexistence and impossibility between two things cannot be separated from a connection between them.

“Just as two necessary existents, were we to suppose them, would not be connected by entailment but would merely accompany one another by chance and coincidence, so entailment in the technical sense cannot obtain between two intrinsically impossible things. It obtains instead between something intrinsically impossible and something impossible through another; and the latter is inevitably intrinsically possible, as has already been stated.

“This distinguishes an implicative conditional from a coincidental conditional. In the first, the consequent, whether affirmative or negative, is judged true on the supposition that the antecedent, whether affirmative or negative, is true, because of an intrinsic connection between them.

“In the second, the same judgment is made without a connection of entailment, merely because the antecedent and consequent happen to coincide.

“During debate, when an impossible supposition is made in order to establish the impossibility of some matter by a reductio or a direct argument, it has become common among dialecticians to say: ‘Your supposition is impossible. It may therefore entail the contradictory of what you claimed it entails, since one impossibility may follow from another.’ The error of this is evident. An impossibility does not entail just any impossibility whatsoever. It entails an impossibility such that, if the two were supposed to exist, a relation of cause and effect would obtain between them.” End of quotation.

If it is said: “The intrinsically impossible is simply what the intellect supposes and reports to be intrinsically impossible. What, then, does its inability to comprehend it mean?”

The answer is: What is meant is that it has no actual reality to which knowledge could attach. Indeed, that which we suppose to be intrinsically impossible and judge accordingly is intrinsically impossible by primary predication, and is judged to be impossible; by common predication, however, it is a possible, existent cognitive form.

This resembles the response given to the apparent contradiction in our statement, “Nothing can be asserted of the absolutely nonexistent.” This indicates the denial of assertion concerning the absolutely nonexistent, while itself being an assertion concerning it. The response is that assertion is denied of the absolutely nonexistent by common predication, since it has no thinghood such that something could be asserted of it. This very statement, however, is an assertion concerning what is absolutely nonexistent by primary predication, which is a possible mental existent.

If it is said: “The claim that two intrinsically impossible things can only accompany one another coincidentally is inadmissible. For the meanings that the intellect establishes to be impossible for the intrinsically Necessary Existent—such as a partner, a quiddity, composition, and so on—must be attributes intrinsically impossible for Him. Were they impossible through another, they would be intrinsically possible for Him, as stated above.

“Yet no possible attribute belongs to Him, exalted is He, since it has been demonstrated that what is intrinsically necessary in existence is necessary in existence in every respect.

“Moreover, as was indicated at the beginning of the book, the arguments establishing the denial of these impossible attributes are demonstrations from concomitants that proceed by way of general relations of entailment.

“The conclusions, namely the impossibility of these attributes, thus stand in a relation of entailment to the premises. They are consequently all effects of something beyond themselves and impossible through another. But it has been demonstrated that they are intrinsically impossible. This is a contradiction.”

The answer is: The impossible attributes that demonstrations from concomitants deny of the intrinsically Necessary Existent all ultimately amount to the denial of intrinsic necessity, which is identical with the intrinsically Necessary Existent. They are therefore one with respect to their supposed instance, although multiple in concept, just as the affirmative attributes of the intrinsically Necessary Existent are identical in instance with pure necessary existence, although multiple in concept.

The inseparability of these attributes and the demonstrative passage from some of them to others are due to their unity with respect to their supposed instance, even though, at the level of concepts, this takes the form of a relation of entailment between them. The same is true of the affirmative attributes. This is expressed by saying that essential attributes, such as intrinsic necessity, belong intrinsically and through a necessitation by the essence. Yet no necessitation or causality obtains between a thing and itself.

This is the meaning of the statement: “The proof of the existence of the True Originator can only take the form of an exposition resembling a demonstration from the cause.”

For example, the impossibility of a quiddity, which we establish by way of the impossibility of possibility for Him, exalted is He, and the impossibility of possibility itself both ultimately amount to denying the nullification of intrinsic necessity, a nullification that is impossible for Him. The intellect has brought this denial before itself in conjunction with the intrinsic necessity abstracted from the very essence.

Know also that, just as entailment between two intrinsically impossible things is impossible, so too it is impossible for the possible to entail something intrinsically impossible. For if the possible antecedent can be realized while the consequent is intrinsically impossible, and entailment between them has been supposed, this requires the realization of the antecedent without the consequent. That denies the entailment, and is a contradiction.

An objection has been raised: The nonexistence of the first effect, which is possible, entails the nonexistence of the intrinsically Necessary Existent, which is intrinsically impossible.

“It is therefore permissible for the possible to entail something intrinsically impossible, just as the reverse is permissible: for example, the nonexistence of the Necessary Existent entails the nonexistence of the first effect.”

The objection is answered as follows. What is meant by “the possible” is a quiddity equally related to the two sides of existence and nonexistence.

It is known that, in its essence, it has no connection with anything beyond its own essence, which is affirmed of itself by primary predication. Thus no connection obtains between the quiddity of the first effect and the intrinsically Necessary Existent.

Its existence, however, is connected with His existence and necessary through His necessity; its nonexistence is intellectually connected with His nonexistence and impossible through the impossibility of His nonexistence. Neither of these is possible in the sense of being equally related to existence and nonexistence.

As for their counting the existence of the possible existent as possible, possibility here means the poverty and intrinsic dependence of the quiddity’s existence upon the cause’s existence. It does not mean possibility as an equal relation to existence and nonexistence. The objection thus contains a fallacy in which existential possibility is substituted for quidditative possibility.

Conclusion

The preceding discussions have made clear that necessity, possibility, and impossibility are modes of the relations in propositions, and that no proposition is without one of them. They have also made clear that necessity and possibility are existential matters, because propositions qualified by these modalities correspond completely to external reality precisely insofar as they are qualified by them.

The two therefore exist externally, but through the existence of their subjects, rather than through a distinct, independent existence. They are among the existential aspects belonging to the existent without qualification, like unity, multiplicity, origination, beginninglessness, and the other philosophical meanings examined in philosophy. This means that things are characterized by them externally, while their presentation as predicates occurs in the mind. These are called “philosophical secondary intelligibles.”

Impossibility, however, is a privative matter.

All this is from the standpoint of the intellect’s taking quiddities and concepts as the subjects of judgments.

From the standpoint that actual existence is their subject in truth because existence is primary, necessity is existence’s utmost intensity, inseparable from its subsisting through itself and being independent through itself. Possibility is existence’s poverty in itself and dependence upon another, such that it is not intrinsically independent of that other, as is the case with the existence of possible quiddities. The two are therefore aspects subsisting in existence and lying nowhere outside it.

[translation] “Demonstration from concomitants” renders burhān innī here, where the author explicitly speaks of proceeding through general entailments; “demonstration from the cause” renders burhān limmī. The discussion preserves the text’s causal conception of entailment.

Quiddity and Its Properties

N-V-01 · Chapter One: Quiddity in Its Own Essence Is Neither Existent Nor Nonexistent
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 91–92
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=91&type=c_fbook

Quiddity (māhiyya), which is “what is said in answer to the question ‘What is it?’,” does not, considered as such and with regard to its own essence, preclude being characterized as existent or nonexistent. It is therefore, in its own essence, neither existent nor nonexistent. This means that neither existence nor nonexistence is included in its own essence, whether as identical with it or as a part of it, although in reality it is invariably characterized by one of them, in the manner of being characterized by an attribute extrinsic to the essence. In other words, quiddity is neither existent nor nonexistent by primary predication, although by common predication it is either existent or nonexistent. This is what they mean when they say: “The exclusion of existence and nonexistence from quiddity considered as such is the exclusion of two contradictories from a particular level. This is not impossible; what is impossible is their exclusion from reality absolutely and at all its levels.” Their meaning is that the contradictory of existence included in the essence is not nonexistence included in the essence. Rather, it is the negation of existence included in the essence: “included in the essence,” which specifies the level, qualifies the existence, not its negation. That is, it is the negation of what is qualified, not a qualified negation.

For this reason they say: “If a question is asked about quiddity considered as such in terms of the two sides of a contradiction, both sides must be denied in the answer, with the negation placed before the qualification ‘considered as such,’ so that the answer expresses the negation of what is qualified, not a qualified negation.

“Thus, if one asks, ‘Is quiddity considered as such existent, or is it not existent?,’ the answer is: ‘It is not the case that quiddity considered as such is existent, nor that it is nonexistent.’ This expresses the fact that neither existence nor nonexistence is included in quiddity’s own essence.”

All other opposed meanings that have the force of contradictories resemble existence and nonexistence in being extrinsic to quiddity considered as such, even those counted among the inseparable concomitants of quiddities. Thus quiddity considered as such is neither one nor many, neither universal nor particular, nor either member of any other such pair of opposites. Four considered as such is neither even nor odd.

[translation] “In its own essence” renders fī ḥadd dhātihā. The passage distinguishes denying that a predicate belongs to that essential level from denying the predicate absolutely; the placement of negation in the translated answer makes this scope explicit.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: وإنمّا المستحيل → وإنّما المستحيل. Misplaced shadda; early p.72/later p.120. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 72; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 120

Adopted: حدّالذات → حدّ الذات. Word separation. Grade: spacing-or-punctuation-normalization; no-substantive-reading-change. Source: 1404 pp. 72; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 119,120

Adopted: حتّى يفيد سلبُ المقيَّدِ دون السلب المقيَّدُ → حتّى يفيد سلبَ المقيَّدِ دون السلب المقيَّدِ. The answer expresses the negation: سلب is the object of يفيد, and the phrase after دون is genitive. External witnesses early p.72/later pp.120–121 leave these decisive case vowels unmarked. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 72; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 120,121

N-V-02 · Chapter Two: The Ways in Which Quiddity Is Considered
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 92–94
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=92&type=c_fbook

Quiddity admits of three ways of being considered in relation to the particular features that accompany it: it may be taken subject to the condition of something, subject to the condition of exclusion, or without condition. This division is exhaustive.

The first consists in taking quiddity as accompanied by the particular features that attach to it, so that it is true of the whole. An example is taking the quiddity of human being subject to its being together with Zayd’s particular features, so that it is true of him.

The second consists in taking it alone. This is done in two ways. In one, consideration is confined to its essence, disregarding everything else. This is what is meant by “subject to the condition of exclusion” in discussions of quiddity. In the other, it is taken alone in such a way that whatever accompaniment is supposed to accompany it will be additional to it and will not enter into it. It will thus be a subject for the supposed accompaniment and will not be predicated of it.

The third consists in stipulating neither accompaniment nor nonaccompaniment. Rather, quiddity is taken without qualification, unrestricted by either negation or affirmation.

Quiddity subject to the condition of something is called “mixed”; quiddity subject to the condition of exclusion is called “abstracted”; and quiddity without condition is called “unrestricted.”

What is divided into these three divisions is quiddity itself. It is the natural universal and is called “the unconditioned as the subject of division.” It exists externally, since some of its divisions, such as the mixed, exist externally.

What exists of the universal in each individual is numerically different from what exists of it in another individual.

If what existed of it in the external individuals were numerically one, the very same one would be many, which is impossible; and the one would possess opposed attributes, which is impossible.

This is the meaning of their statement: “The relation of quiddity to its individuals is like the relation of many fathers to their children, rather than the relation of one father to his many children.”

Quiddity is therefore multiple externally through the multiplicity of its individuals. In the mind, however, it is one under the description of universality and commonality, as will be explained.

[translation] The three considerations are bi-sharṭ shayʾ, bi-sharṭ lā, and lā bi-sharṭ. The last must be distinguished from lā bi-sharṭ maqsamī, the unconditioned quiddity that is itself the subject divided into all three considerations.

[Later editor’s critical observation · not authorial] At vol. 1, printed p. 123 n. 1 of al-Zāriʿī’s revised 1428 AH edition, the editor challenges the inference that because some conditioned instances of quiddity exist outside the mind, quiddity as the unconditioned dividendum must likewise exist there. The editor’s argument distinguishes the dividendum’s preservation through the divisions from the existence of the dividendum as such in all divisions. This is a later philosophical criticism, not a textual variant or an opinion to attribute to Ṭabāṭabāʾī. The English translation of the authorial proposition remains unchanged.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: بما هي مقارنةً → بما هي مقارنةٌ. Predicate of the pronoun هي is nominative; both witnesses print comparison phrase without this final case vowel. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 73,74; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 122,123

N-V-03 · Chapter Three: The Universal and the Particular
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 94–98
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=94&type=c_fbook

There is no doubt that a quiddity with many individuals is true of each of its individuals and is predicated of it. This means that whenever an individual of the quiddity occurs in the mind and is presented to the quiddity there, the quiddity is united with it and is that individual. This property is called universality, and it is what is meant by the individuals’ sharing the quiddity. Considering quiddity in itself, the intellect does not refuse to allow it to be true of many, whether or not it has many individuals externally.

Universality is thus a mental property that accrues to quiddity in the mind. External, concrete existence is coextensive with individuality and precludes being shared. Universality is therefore among the concomitants of the mental existence of quiddity, just as particularity and individuality are among the concomitants of its external existence.

It has been said: “Universality and particularity concern the manner of apprehension. Through the strength of its apprehension, sense grasps a thing completely, so that it is distinguished absolutely from everything else and is individuated. Through the weakness of its apprehension, the intellect grasps it faintly, so that what it grasps is indeterminate among several things and can apply to many. It is like a figure seen from afar that is not distinguished completely, so that it is uncertain, for example, whether it is Zayd, ʿAmr, a piece of wood set upright, or something else, although it is only one of these possibilities. It is also like a worn dirham that is indeterminate among different dirhams, although it is only one of them.”

This is unsound. If things were so, the instance of quiddity would in reality be only one of its individuals, and universal propositions would be false, such as “Every possible existent has a cause,” “Every four is even,” and “Every multiplicity is composed of units.” Self-evident judgment rejects this. The truth, then, is that universality and particularity are concomitants of the existence of quiddities: universality belongs to their mental existence, and particularity to their external existence.

It has likewise been said: “The quiddity existing in the mind is particular and individual, just like the quiddity existing externally, since it exists in a particular mind and subsists in a particular soul. Thus the human quiddity existing in Zayd’s mind, for example, differs from the human quiddity existing in ʿAmr’s mind; and what exists of it in Zayd’s mind today differs from what existed in his mind yesterday; and so on.”

This too is unsound. Under the aspect mentioned—that is, its subsisting in and qualifying a particular soul, and likewise its being one of the soul’s qualities and a perfection of it—the intelligible quiddity is one of the external existents that fall outside our discussion. We are discussing quiddity with its mental existence, in which its external effects do not follow from it. Under this aspect, it does not resist being true of many.

Things that share a universal meaning are distinguished from one another in one of three ways. If they share only an accidental feature that lies outside their essence, they are distinguished by their entire essence, as are two species belonging to two different accidental categories that share the property of being accidents. If they share an essential feature, then, if they share part of the essence—which must be the genus—they are distinguished by another part, namely the differentia. An example is human being and horse, which share animality and are distinguished by rationality and neighing. If they share the entire essence, they are distinguished by a separable accidental feature. For if it were inseparable, no individual would lack it, since a concomitant of the species is a concomitant of all its individuals.

Some have added to these three divisions a fourth: distinction through completeness and deficiency, intensity and weakness, within the very nature that is shared. This is gradation. The truth is that quiddity as such does not admit of gradation; gradation pertains only to existence.

All of this concerns universality and its being a mental property of quiddity. As for particularity, which is the impossibility of a thing’s being shared and is also called individuality, the truth is that it is through existence, as al-Fārābī—may God have mercy on him—held, and as Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may God have mercy on him—held after him.

He says in the Asfār: “The truth is that a thing’s individuation—in the sense that, by the very conception of it, it cannot be shared—is only through something additional to quiddity that, by its own essence, precludes the conception of its being shared.

“What individuates a thing—in the sense of that through which it becomes incapable of being shared—is in reality nothing other than that thing’s existence itself, as the Second Teacher held. Every existence is individuated through its own essence. If the particular mode of a thing’s existence is disregarded, the intellect does not refuse to allow it to be shared, even if a thousand specifying features are added to it. For distinction in reality differs from individuation: the former belongs to a thing in comparison with those things that share something general with it, whereas the latter belongs to it considered in itself. Indeed, if it had nothing sharing something with it, it would not need an additional distinguishing feature, even though it would possess individuation in itself. It is not implausible that distinction should bring about a thing’s preparedness for individuation. For, in the case of a material species dispersed among individuals, unless matter has acquired a disposition specifically for one of them, that individual’s existence does not emanate from the highest principle.” End of quotation.

From this it becomes clear, first, that the individuating accidents to which they have attributed individuation are signs and concomitants of individuation. These accidents are all accidents, as some people’s words apparently indicate; specifically position, when, and where, as others have expressly stated; or specifically time, as still others have maintained. The same applies to the view that matter individuates.

Second, there is some correctness in the statement of some that “What individuates a thing is its proximate agent, which bestows its existence”; in the statement of others that “What individuates is the agent of all things, namely the Necessary Existent—exalted is He—who bestows every existence”; and in the statement of others that “An accident is individuated through its subject.”

Nevertheless, this is an attribution to a remote cause. The proximate cause on which individuation depends is the existence of the thing itself. For a thing’s concrete existence, insofar as it is concrete existence, cannot be shared. It is thus individuated through its own essence, and quiddity is individuated through it. The agent or subject has a role in individuation insofar as each is among the causes of existence, but the nearest cause is the existence of the thing itself, as you have learned.

Third, the particularity of an object known through sensation does not arise from the object itself insofar as it is a mental concept. It arises, rather, from sensory contact with what is external and from the person’s knowledge that this is a kind of being affected by the external concrete thing. Likewise, the particularity of an image in imagination arises from its connection with sense: for example, when someone brings to mind an image stored from sensation, or combines the stored sensory images that he possesses into an image of an imaginary individual. Understand this.

[translation] “When” and “where” render the technical category names matā and ayna; “position” renders waḍʿ. The quoted “Second Teacher” is al-Fārābī, whose position the preceding sentence identifies.

[Original quotation source · restored locator] Al-Asfār, vol. 2, p. 10.

N-V-04 · Chapter Four: Essential and Accidental Predicates
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 98–99
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=98&type=c_fbook

The concepts taken into account in quiddities—those included in their definitions, with whose removal the quiddities themselves are removed—are called “essential predicates.”

Other concepts predicated of them, which fall outside their definitions, such as “writer” in relation to the human being and “walking” in relation to animal, are called “accidental predicates.”

An accidental predicate is of two kinds. If its abstraction and predication depend upon the conjunction of something with the subject, as the abstraction of “hot” and its predication of a body depend upon the conjunction of heat with that body, it is called “a predicate through an added attribute” (maḥmūl bi-l-ḍamīma). If they do not depend upon the conjunction of anything with the subject, it is called “an extrinsic predicate” (al-khārij al-maḥmūl), such as “higher” and “lower.”

This is the well-known account. It has already been established that an accident is one of the degrees of the existence of substance.

An essential predicate is distinguished from what is not essential by its properties, which are consequences of its being essential. These are that it necessarily belongs to that of which it is an essential predicate, because a thing necessarily belongs to itself; that it has no need of a cause, since the cause that brings into existence that of which it is an essential predicate is itself the cause that brings the essential predicate into existence, owing to their identity; and that it precedes that of which it is an essential predicate by priority in substantial constitution, as will be explained, God willing.

It is clear from what has preceded that the predication between an essence and its essential parts is primary predication.

This removes the objection to the priority of the parts of a quiddity over the quiddity itself: the sum of the parts is identical with the whole, so the priority of the sum over the whole would be the priority of a thing over itself, which is impossible.

The reason is that an essential predicate, whether more general, namely the genus, or more specific, namely the differentia, is identical with the essence, and the predication between them is primary predication. It is called a “part” only because it occurs as a part of the definition.

Moreover, the objection to the priority of the parts over the whole is answered by saying that the priority belongs to all the parts, considered severally, over the whole; the two considerations are different.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: والعَرّضيّ → والعَرَضيّ. Remove spurious shadda on rāʾ; the term is accidental predicate. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 77,78; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 130,131

Adopted: كوُنُه → كونُه. Repair displaced vowel within verbal noun كون; consonants unchanged. Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 78; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 131,132

Grade: contextual-editorial-vocalization; decisive-vowels-not-claimed-as-external-attestation. Source: 1404 pp. 78; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 131,132

N-V-05 · Chapter Five: Genus, Differentia, Species, and Some Related Matters
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 99–103
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=99&type=c_fbook

A complete quiddity having its own real effects is called a “species” insofar as it is such, for example, human being, horse, and sheep.

It has been explained in logic that some of the essential meanings of species included in their definitions are shared by more than one species, such as animal, which is shared by human being, horse, and others. Other such meanings belong exclusively to one species, such as rational, which belongs exclusively to human being. The shared part is called a “genus,” and the exclusive part is called a “differentia.”

Genus and differentia are divided into proximate and remote. Genus and species are also divided into highest, intermediate, and lowest. All of this is explained in its proper place.

Now, if we take, for example, the meaning of animal found in more than one species and conceive it as a substance that is a body, grows, senses, and moves voluntarily, we may conceive it on its own, so that every meaning accompanying it, such as rational, is additional to it and outside its essence. The meaning we have conceived will then be different from the combination of it and its accompaniment, and will not be predicated of that combination, just as it will not be predicated of the accompaniment.

Thus the conceived concept of animal is other than the concept of rational animal and other than the concept of rational. The meaning conceived in this way is matter in relation to the additional meaning accompanying it, and a material cause in relation to the combination of it and its accompaniment.

We may also conceive it in relation to a number of species that share it. For example, we may conceive the meaning of animal just mentioned as the animal that is either a human being, a horse, a sheep, or some other species of animal. The meaning conceived in this way will be an incomplete quiddity, lacking determination until the differentia exclusive to one of those species is conjoined with it and determines it as a complete quiddity, so that it becomes that very species. Thus, for example, the human differentia, rational, may be conjoined with animal, so that it is that very rational animal, namely the human species. The shared essential meaning taken under this consideration is called a “genus,” and that which determines it is called a “differentia.”

The two considerations just mentioned that apply to the shared part—taking it with the condition of exclusion and taking it without a condition—also apply to the exclusive part.

Under the first consideration, it is a form for the other, accompanying part, and a formal cause of the combination; it is not predicated of any of these. Under the second consideration, it is a differentia that determines the genus and completes the species, and is predicated of the species by primary predication.

It follows that the more general part in quiddities, namely the genus, is constituted by the more specific part, namely the differentia, according to intellectual analysis.

On the manner in which a genus is constituted by a differentia, the Asfār states: “This constitution is not in external existence, since the two are united in existence, and when two things are united within a given domain, one cannot constitute the other in existence there. Rather, it is according to the intellect’s analysis of the specific quiddity into two intellectual parts and its judgment that one is a cause of the other. For the parts of a single quiddity necessarily need one another, and the part needed, which is the cause, can only be the differentia. It is impossible for the generic part to be a cause of the existence of the differential part; otherwise opposed differentiae would be its concomitants, so that one and the same thing would be diverse and opposed to itself. This is impossible.

“It therefore remains that the differential part is a cause of the existence of the generic part. It divides the unrestricted generic nature, is a cause of the portion that constitutes the species’ share of that nature, and is a part of the combination resulting from that portion and from what distinguishes it from other things.” Here the quotation ends.

If it is said: “If the differentia is a cause of the unrestricted genus, it does not divide it. But if it is a cause of the share within its own species, which belongs exclusively to that species, the specialization must first be supposed, so that the differentia can be a cause of that specialized share. Yet once it has become specialized, it has entered existence and thereby has no further need of a cause.”

The reply is that the specificity by which the indeterminate genus becomes a share exclusive to the species is one of the features of its existential determination, which comes to it from its cause, namely the differentia. A cause precedes its effect in existence. The specialization is therefore obtained through the differentia, and through it the genus, which lacks such specialization in itself, is divided.

There is no difficulty in several differentiae being causes of a single generic quiddity, owing to the weakness of its unity.

If it is said: “The determination by which a genus enters existence is its determination through individual existence. Until it is invested with external existence, it is not complete and has none of the features of existence. What, then, is meant by counting the differentia as a cause of it?”

The reply is that its determination through the differentia means its establishment in intellectual conception and its becoming a complete specific quiddity. What it acquires through individual existence is the realization of that complete quiddity in such a way that external effects follow from it.

What the differentia confers, therefore, is the determination of the indeterminate generic quiddity and its becoming a complete specific quiddity. What individual existence confers is the determination of the complete quiddity and its becoming an external reality from which effects follow.

The foregoing makes the following points clear.

First, the genus is the species in an indeterminate manner, and the differentia is the species in a determinate manner. The species is the complete quiddity, without regard to indeterminacy or determination.

Second, both the genus and the differentia are predicated of the species by primary predication. As for the relation between the genus and the differentia themselves, the genus is a general accident of the differentia, and the differentia is a specific property of the genus. Predication between them is common predication.

Third, it is impossible for more than one genus to be realized at the same level in a single specific quiddity, or for more than one differentia to be realized at the same level in a single specific quiddity. This would entail that one and the same thing is many, which is impossible.

Fourth, genus and matter are identical in essence and differ in consideration. When matter is taken without a condition, it is a “genus”; when a genus is taken with the condition of exclusion, it is “matter.” Likewise, differentia and form are identical in essence and differ in consideration. A differentia taken with the condition of exclusion is a form, just as a form taken without a condition is a differentia.

This is evident in composite material substances. Matter and form exist externally in them, and from these the meanings of matter and form are taken. These meanings are then taken without a condition and become genus and differentia.

Accidents, however, are externally simple and are not composed of matter and form. That by which they share something in common is identical with that by which they are distinguished.

But the intellect finds common and exclusive features in them and considers these as their genera and differentiae. It then considers them with the condition of exclusion, whereupon they become their intellectual matters and forms.

The same account also applies to immaterial substances.

[translation] “With the condition of exclusion” renders bi-sharṭ lā, and “without a condition” renders lā bi-sharṭ. Here “specific” means pertaining to a species; the “specific property” (khāṣṣa) is the logical proprium.

[textual] The 1417 digital base transposes the words in the sentence identifying the determined genus with the human species. The 1404 print (p.79, canvas83) and the later edited digital text (I.134) both read فيكون هو الحيوان الناطق بعينه, “so that it is that very rational animal.” Arabic and English are corrected together. This is an attested transcription repair, not a manuscript claim.

[Authorial source citation] The author cites al-Asfār, volume II, pp.29–30. This reference appears after the quotation in the 1404 print (p.80, canvas84); the later edited presentation places the reference in I.135, note1. It is restored here as a source note.
