N-V-06 · Chapter Six: Some Matters Concerning the Differentia
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 103–107
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=103&type=c_fbook

The philosophers use the term “differentia” in two senses.

The first is the most specific and best known of the concomitants that accrue to a species. Such a concomitant is counted as a differentia and placed in definitions in the position of real differentiae only because it is difficult to obtain the real differentiae that constitute species, or because the language contains no name that denotes them by complete correspondence. An example is “rational” (nāṭiq), taken as the differentia of the human being. What is meant by nuṭq is either speaking, which in one respect belongs to audible qualities, or apprehending universals, which they class among qualities of the soul. Quality of whatever kind is an accident, and accidents do not constitute substances. This is called a “logical differentia.”

The second is that which really constitutes the species and determines the genus. It is the principle of the logical differentia, as when the human being’s possessing a rational soul is taken as the differentia of the human species. This is called a “derivational differentia.”

Now, the ultimate differentia is the entire reality of the species, for it determines the genus, making it determinate and completing it as a species. Whatever is included indeterminately in its genera and its other differentiae is included determinately in it.

It follows that the species remains the same species through its differentia, even if some of its genera change. For this reason, in material composites, if its form—which is the differentia taken with the condition of exclusion—becomes separate from matter—which is the genus taken with the condition of exclusion—the reality of the species is preserved through the form. An example is the human being, whose soul becomes immaterial and separates from the body.

Furthermore, the differentia does not fall under the genus that it determines: that is, the genus is not included in its definition as a genus is included in a species. Thus the differentiae of substances are not substances.

For if a differentia fell under its genus, it would need a differentia to constitute it. We would then transfer the argument to that differentia, and a regress would follow through a series of infinitely many differentiae. Infinitely many species would be realized in each differentia, and the genus would be repeated as many times as there are differentiae. Clear reason rejects this.

Moreover, the relation between genus and differentia would become identity, and predication between them would be primary predication. It would cease to be the case that the genus is a general accident of the differentia and that the differentia is a specific property of the genus.

This does not conflict with the occurrence of predication between a genus and the differentia that divides it, as when we say, “Every rational being is an animal,” and, “Some animal is rational.” This is common predication between a specific property and a general accident, as was indicated earlier. What we have denied is primary predication.

For example, substance is true of the differentiae that divide it, without their falling under it in such a way that it becomes part of their quiddity.

If you say: “The foregoing denial that the differentia of a species falls under its genus conflicts with the philosophers’ statement, when dividing substance into intellect, soul, prime matter, corporeal form, and body, that corporeal form and soul are two species of substance. A thing’s being a species of a category entails its being subsumed and included under that category.

“It is well known that corporeal form is the differentia of body taken with the condition of exclusion. Its being a species of substance therefore involves a substantial differentia’s falling under the genus substance, and substance’s being included in its definition.

“A similar account applies to their counting the soul as a species of substance. Moreover, they have demonstrated that the human soul is an immaterial substance that remains after separation from the body. The rational soul is the form of the human being; and this very same soul, taken without a condition, is the differentia of human quiddity.”

I reply: The judgment concerning concepts differs with the intellectual consideration applied to them. In the discussion of existence for itself and existence for another, it was explained that existence in itself is that from which a thing’s quiddity is abstracted.

But from considering its existence for something, no quiddity is abstracted, even though its existence for another is identical with its existence in itself.

The differentia is a concept relative to the genus. Its aspect is that it is an essential distinguishing principle of the species, whose existence is for the genus. It therefore has no quiddity insofar as it is a differentia.

This is the meaning of their statement: “That the genus is a general accident of the differentia, and the differentia a specific property of it, entails that the differentiae of substances are not substances in the sense of falling under the meaning of substance as species fall under their genus. Rather, they fall under it as things having a concomitant fall under that concomitant, which does not enter into their quiddity.”

As for form insofar as it is a form constituting matter, since it is taken with the condition of exclusion in relation to matter, there is no primary predication between them. It therefore does not fall under the genus. Otherwise, it would be a species between which and the genus there is identity and primary predication. This contradicts the supposition. Nevertheless, there is common predication between it and matter, on the view that the composition of matter and form is a composition by union.

Certainly, since form is the entire quiddity of the species—as they define it, that through which a thing is actually what it is—the differentiae of substances are substances, because they are identical with the reality and actuality of the species. But this does not require their falling under the genus substance in such a way that substance is included in their definition and there is primary predication between it and them.

It is thus clear from the foregoing that differentiae insofar as they are differentiae are simple, not composed of genus and differentia. They are purely essential distinguishing principles. Likewise, material forms, which are material in their essence and exist for matter, are externally simple, not composed of matter and form, and intellectually simple, not composed of genus and differentia. Otherwise, each one would be an infinite series of species, as was indicated earlier.

As for the immaterial soul, insofar as it is a differentia of the species, its aspect is that of attributive existence. You have learned that attributive existence has no quiddity. But insofar as the soul is immaterial in its essence, its immateriality makes possible its existence for itself, just as it exists in itself. It is the entire reality of the species, and substance is therefore true of it. It is thus the substantial species of which it was a formal part, and it is not a form. This does not conflict with its existence also being for matter: that dependence belongs only to the level of action, not to the essence. It is material in its action, not in its essence.

This is according to the view that the immaterial soul is spiritual in its origination and its subsistence, as the Peripatetics maintain. According to the view that it is corporeal in its origination and spiritual in its subsistence, it first becomes immaterial in its essence while still being connected with matter in its action. It then becomes separate from matter in its action as well, through separation from the body.

[translation] Nāṭiq is conventionally translated as “rational,” but the argument here explicitly considers both senses of nuṭq: articulate speech and the apprehension of universals. “Derivational differentia” translates faṣl ishtiqāqī, the author’s term here for the real constitutive principle distinguished from the logical differentia.

[translation] “Complete correspondence” renders muṭābaqa, a term for a word’s signifying the whole of the meaning for which it is assigned. “With the condition of exclusion” and “without a condition” retain the renderings of bi-sharṭ lā and lā bi-sharṭ used in the preceding chapter.

[translation] In the sentence about substance’s being true of its differentiae, the negative scope includes the consequence expressed by fa-yakūna: the differentiae are not subsumed under substance in the manner that would make substance a constituent of their quiddity. The later objection and reply explicitly distinguish this from substance’s being truly predicated of them.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: وهو إنمّا يعدّ → وهو إنّما يعدّ. Correct misplaced shadda in إنما. Grade: 1404 printed facsimile visually inspected; corresponding 1428 edited digital presentation read. No manuscript attestation claimed.. Source: 1404 p.82; 1428 digital p.137

N-V-07 · Chapter Seven: Some Properties of Species
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 107–108
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=107&type=c_fbook

A species is a complete quiddity that has its own effects in existence.

It is divided into that whose effects follow from it upon nothing further than the external existence that individuates it as an individual, such as the human being. This is called a “real species.” The other division is that whose effects follow from it only upon the addition of a differentia or differentiae. It is therefore a genus in relation to species below it, although it is a species when considered with regard to its complete quiddity. Such are the highest and intermediate species: body, for example, which is a higher species of substance and then a genus of vegetable and mineral species; and animal, which is an intermediate species of substance and a genus of the human being and the other animal species. This is called a “relative species.”

Now, the parts of a specific quiddity exist externally through one existence, namely the existence of the species. For the predication between each of them and the species is primary predication, and the species exists through one existence. In the mind, however, they differ through indeterminacy and determination. For this reason, each of genus and differentia is accidental to the other, as has been explained.

Hence their statement that, in real composites—that is, material species—there must be poverty and need among the parts, some needing others, so that they become connected and united as one reality. They have counted this as self-evident.

A real composite is distinguished from other composites by real unity. Through the composition of its parts, something else arises beyond them, having a new, distinctive effect beyond the effects of the parts. It is unlike composites existing only by consideration, which have no effect beyond the effects of their parts, such as an army composed of individuals or a house composed of bricks, plaster, and other things.

Hence the preferable view is that the composition of matter and form is a composition by union, not by conjunction, as will be explained, God willing.

Furthermore, some specific quiddities have many individuals, such as species that have some connection with matter: an element, for example, or the human being. Others are confined to a single individual, such as a species immaterial in both essence and action, namely an intellect.

For multiplicity must be either the whole essence of the specific quiddity, a part of that essence, or outside it as either an inseparable or a separable attribute.

On the first three suppositions, it would be impossible for an individual of that quiddity to be realized. Whatever is supposed to be an individual of it would necessarily be multiple. Every multiplicity is composed of units, yet every supposed unit would necessarily be multiple, and every multiplicity is composed of units, and so on. The matter would proceed without end and never reach a unit. No unit would therefore be realized, and so no individual of the quiddity would be realized. Yet it has been supposed to have many individuals. This contradicts the supposition.

On the fourth supposition, multiplicity would be through a separable accident that accrues to the species. Multiplicity is realized through the contrast between its being conjoined with the species and its not being so conjoined. The occurrence of every separable accident depends upon a prior possibility whose bearer is matter. The species would therefore necessarily be material.

Thus every species having many individuals is material. By contraposition, this becomes: every immaterial species is confined to a single individual. This was what was required.

[translation] “Specific” means pertaining to a species. “Composition by union” renders tarkīb ittiḥādī, contrasted with tarkīb inḍimāmī, “composition by conjunction.” The distinction concerns real unity, not merely the spatial arrangement of parts.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Recorded; authorial text retained: بانضمامه إليه وعدم انضمامه الكثرةُ → بانضمامه إليه عدم انضمامه الكثرة. The earlier printed witness supports the conjunction; the later digital omission is not adopted. Current English correctly renders the contrast. Grade: 1404 printed facsimile visually inspected; corresponding 1428 edited digital presentation read. No manuscript attestation claimed.. Source: 1404 p.86; 1428 digital p.145


The Ten Categories

N-VI-PRE · Stage VI: The Ten Categories — Introduction
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 109–109
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=109&type=c_fbook

These are the highest genera to which quiddities ultimately resolve through analysis.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

N-VI-01 · The categories and their number
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 111–114
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=111&type=c_fbook

There is no doubt that a contingent existent has a quiddity: its essence, whose relation to existence and nonexistence is equal, and which is stated in answer to “What is it?” Nor is there doubt that these quiddities have common and distinctive constituents—genera and differentiae—and that some genera are more general and others more specific. They may, that is, form an ascending order from the more specific to the more general. This series must therefore terminate in a genus above which there is no further genus. Its proceeding to infinity is impossible, since this would entail that a contingent thing's essence consisted of infinitely many parts. None of these quiddities could then be understood through all its essential constituents. Moreover, considered on condition of exclusion, these genera are external or intellectual matters. Matter is among the causes of constitution, and these are finite, as will be explained, God willing.

It follows that there are highest genera above which no genus stands. These are called “categories.”

From this the following become clear. First, the categories are simple, not composed of genus and differentia. Otherwise there would be a genus higher than them, contrary to the hypothesis.

Second, they differ from one another in the entirety of their simple essences. Otherwise they would share an essential constituent, namely a genus; a genus would then stand above them, contrary to the hypothesis.

Third, a single quiddity cannot fall under more than one category. One and the same thing cannot be both substance and quantity, or both quantity and quality, and similarly in other cases. It follows that any meaning found in more than one category does not itself fall under a category. If it fell under what it is predicated of, it would belong to two mutually disparate genera, or to several such genera, which is impossible. The same holds of concepts predicated of both the Necessary and the contingent, as was indicated earlier.

Fourth, simple quiddities, such as substantial differentiae and an isolated species—if there is such a thing—fall outside the categories. This was explained in the stage on quiddity.

Fifth, the Necessary and the impossible fall outside the categories, since they have no quiddities, whereas categories are generic quiddities.

Most Peripatetics maintain that there are ten categories: substance, quantity, quality, position, where, when, possession, relation, acting, and being acted upon. Their account relies on induction. No demonstration has been established that there is no category above these which is more general than all of them, or more general than some of them.

As for the concepts of quiddity, thing, existent, and the like, which apply to all ten; the concepts of accident, configuration, and state, which apply to the nine other than substance; and relational configuration, which applies to the last seven, called relational accidents: these are general concepts abstracted from their mode of existence and do not belong to the order of quiddity.

A thing's quiddity is its essence, predicated of it in answer to “What is it?” There is no identity except that of an existing thing; a thing's thinghood is its being existent, and what does not exist has no thinghood. A thing's accidentality consists in its existence subsisting in another; its being a configuration or a state is close to this. A thing's relational character consists in its existence being in another, without standing outside that other's existence.

These, then, are concepts abstracted from the mode of existence and predicated of more than one category. They are therefore not categories, as explained above.

Some maintain that there are four categories, reducing the relational categories to one: substance, quantity, quality, and relation. This is refuted by what has already been explained: relation is a nonquiddative concept abstracted from the mode of existence. If the mere generality of a concept sufficed to make it a category, the categories should be reduced to two, substance and accident, because the concept of accident applies to every category other than substance—or indeed to one category, quiddity or thing.

The Master of Illumination maintains that there are five categories: substance, quantity, quality, relation, and motion. The objection to the preceding view also applies to his. In addition, motion too is a concept abstracted from a mode of existence, namely flowing existence which does not remain fixed and stable. There is therefore no basis for including it among the categories.

The reader adopts «وهو الوجود السيّال». Both independent external edition witnesses give the phrase once; the stored Arabic duplicates وهو الوجود. English already renders one occurrence. This is printed/digital edition evidence, not manuscript certification.

N-VI-02 · The definition of substance and its being a genus of the quiddities beneath it
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 114–117
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=114&type=c_fbook

Quiddity divides primarily into a quiddity which, when it exists externally, exists not in a subject independent of it—this is the quiddity of substance—and a quiddity which, when it exists externally, exists in a subject independent of it—these are the nine accidental categories.

Substance, then, is a quiddity which, when it exists externally, exists not in a subject independent of it. This defines substance by an attribute inseparable from its existence; it is not an essential definition composed of genus and differentia, for such a definition has no meaning in the case of a highest genus. Likewise, defining accident as a quiddity which, when it exists externally, exists in a subject independent of it defines the nine accidental categories by an inseparable attribute of their existence; it is not an essential definition at all.

Although not an essential definition, this definition includes everything it should and excludes everything else. “Quiddity” includes quiddities generally and excludes what is necessary in itself, since it is pure existence with no quiddity. The qualification “when it exists externally” indicates that we are defining the quiddity of substance that is substance by common predication. Unless a concept is realized in external existence, it is not a real quiddity with its real effects. This excludes mental substances, which are substances by primary predication. A concept's being predicated of itself is primary predication and does not entail that the concept falls under itself. Qualifying the subject as “independent of it” indicates the definition of a subject through its inseparable attribute: it subsists through itself, that is, exists for itself. Substance therefore exists not in a subject: its existence is not for another, as that of accidents is, but for itself.

It has been said that the qualification of independence is intended to include substantial forms inhering in matter. Although they exist in a subject, their subject is not independent of them but needs them. The objection is that substantial forms are in fact simple quiddities which do not fall under the category of substance and have no genus, as was indicated in the stage on quiddity.

That both divisions—substance and accident—exist externally is, in general, self-evident. Whoever denies the existence of substance unwittingly asserts the substantiality of accidents. Some accidents, such as relational accidents, are unquestionably accidental.

Substance is a genus of the specific quiddities to which it applies, constitutes them, and enters into their definitions. For the dependence of accidental quiddities, in their external existence, upon a subject independent of them entails the existence of a quiddity which is in itself their subject and independent of them. Otherwise the series of dependence would proceed to infinity, and no quiddity would become established. This is evident.

The following argument has also been offered for substance's being a genus of what falls beneath it. That the existence of substance is not in a subject is one attribute common to substantial quiddities, belonging to them inseparably without regard to anything extrinsic. If substance were not their genus, but merely an inseparable attribute of their existence while they were quiddities disparate in their entire essences, a single concept would have to be abstracted from many disparate instances precisely as disparate. This is impossible. These many disparate quiddities must therefore possess one common quiddative constituent whose existential concomitant is their existence not being in a subject.

The objection is that the attribute in question is a meaning abstracted from the kind of existence these substantial quiddities have, not from the quiddities themselves. Likewise, existence in a subject—one attribute inseparable from the nine accidental categories—is one meaning abstracted from the kind of existence all accidents have. If the unity of the attribute abstracted from substances entailed one common quiddative constituent in substantial quiddities, the unity of the attribute abstracted from the accidental categories would likewise entail one common quiddative constituent in them, serving as their genus. Quiddities would then terminate in two categories, substance and accident.

What establishes substance's being a genus of the quiddities beneath it is therefore the earlier argument: an accident's need for a subject in which to subsist entails a quiddity subsisting through itself.

It follows from the foregoing that a single thing cannot be both substance and accident. It suffices to observe that substance exists not in a subject, while accident exists in a subject. These two attributes plainly cannot combine in one thing.

[Author’s footnote · restored from printed editions] The existence of an accident for another (qualifying another) is a negative meaning that the accidental quiddity does not require. But the existence of substance is existence for itself, subsisting through its own self, which is its quiddity. It is one positive meaning required by the quiddity as a quiddity requires its concomitants. A single meaning is not abstracted from mutually disparate quiddities. We indicated this earlier by saying: “Otherwise the series of dependence would proceed without end …”. Witness differences: The 1404 note says ناعتا للغير and معنى واحد إيجابي; 1428 says ناعتا لغيره and omits واحد. The earlier note is transcribed without silently conflating these readings. 1428 also adds ل to the closing quotation وإلا لذهبت.

N-VI-03 · The primary divisions of substance
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 117–118
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=117&type=c_fbook

They say: substance either is in a substrate or is not. What is in a substrate is material form. What is not in a substrate either is a substrate for something subsisting in it or is not. The former is prime matter. The latter either is composed of prime matter and form or is not. The former is body. The latter either has, in some respect, a receptive connection with body or has none. The former is soul, the latter intellect. The primary divisions of substance are therefore five: material form, prime matter, body, soul, and intellect.

This is not an intellectual division turning upon affirmation and negation. A substance composed of an inhering substance and a substance serving as substrate is not, as a matter of intellectual possibility, confined to body. It is possible that existence includes a material substance composed of matter and a form other than corporeal form. Their restriction of the primary material species to body rests instead upon their induction. Moreover, you have already learned that substantial form does not fall under the category of substance, although “substance” applies to it as an extrinsic concomitant.

After referring to this division, the author of the Asfār says: “The better way to state the division is this: if substance admits the three dimensions, it is body. Otherwise, if it is a part of body through which body is actual, whether in its genus or in its species, it is form, either dimensional or natural. If it is a part through which body is potential, it is matter. If it is not a part of body, then, if it directly governs body, it is soul; otherwise it is intellect.”

He then indicates why this division is better: “This is because it will emerge throughout our investigations that human psychic substance is matter for the cognitive form through which it becomes another, actually perfected substance, belonging to determinate species with another mode of existence than the natural existence belonging to these determinate natural species.” End of quotation.

The objection to the former division applies equally to this one. Moreover, coordinating natural form—which is subsequent to body's specific constitution—with dimensional form does not fit the division's being primary.

In any event, what concerns us here is to investigate the reality of body and of its two parts, matter and corporeal form. A complete investigation of soul belongs to psychology. Its reality will become partially clear in the two stages on potentiality and actuality, and on intellect and intelligible. The reality of intellect is discussed in theology in the narrower sense. It too will become partially clear in the stages on potentiality and actuality, and on intellect and intelligible, God willing.

[Textual emendation · 1428 AH editor] The 1417 AH base reads «عطف الصور الطبيعيّة» (plural). The revised 1428 AH edition, vol. 1 p. 159 n. 1, explicitly records that copies read the plural, but prints «عطف الصورة الطبيعيّة» (singular), matching the singular predicate «وهي متأخرة». The singular has been adopted in Arabic and English; the plural is preserved here as the earlier transmitted reading. This is editorial emendation, not a newly attested manuscript variant.

[Original quotation source · restored locator] Asfār, vol. 4, p. 234.

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

N-VI-04 · The quiddity of body
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 119–126
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=119&type=c_fbook

There is no doubt that body exists, in the sense of a substance in which three lines intersecting at right angles can be posited, even if they are not actually present in it, as in a sphere or a cylinder.

Although our senses, to which our knowledge ultimately leads, include none that apprehends a substantial existent, and apprehend only the states and accidental attributes of bodies, the various kinds of experience guide us conclusively to the fact that what lies between the surfaces and limits of bodies is, in general, filled: it is not empty of a substance extended in its three dimensions.

What sense finds of this substance extended in three dimensions, it finds as a single continuum capable of division into actual parts, not as a collection of actual parts separated by gaps. This is how matters stand for sense. As to how they stand in reality, different views have been maintained.

The first holds that body is composed of parts that have position but are altogether indivisible: they cannot be divided externally, in imagination, or intellectually. These parts are finite in number. This is the position of the majority of the theologians.

The second holds, like the first, that body is composite, but that its parts are infinite in number. It is attributed to al-Naẓẓām.

The third holds that body is composed of actual, finite, small, solid parts. Their smallness and solidity prevent external division, although they admit division in imagination and intellect. This is attributed to Democritus.

The fourth holds that body is a single continuum, as it appears to sense, and admits division into a finite number of parts. This is attributed to al-Shahrastānī.

The fifth holds that body is a simple substance consisting in substantial continuity and extension, which admits division externally, in imagination, and intellectually. This is attributed to the divine Plato.

The sixth holds that body is composed of a substance and an accident: substance and mathematical corporeality, which is quantitative extension in three dimensions. This is attributed to the Master of Illumination.

The seventh holds that body is a substance composed of two substances. One is matter, which is the potentiality for every actuality; the other is substantial continuity, which is its form. The form is a substantial continuity and extension admitting division into infinitely many parts in the sense that division never comes to a halt. A difference between two accidents divides it, and a cutting instrument likewise divides it by cutting. When the instrument becomes ineffective owing to the smallness or solidity of the part, imagination takes up the division; when imagination becomes unable to continue because of the extreme smallness of the part, intellect divides it in a universal manner: whenever it is divided into parts, the new part has a volume, with one side distinct from another, and admits division without this process coming to a halt. Thus the occurrence of division does not annihilate body. This is the position of Aristotle and the great masters among the philosophers of Islam.

These are the views that have reached us concerning the quiddity of the substance called body. Each has one or more weaknesses, to which we shall point as far as possible.

As for the first view, attributed to the theologians—that body is composed of altogether indivisible parts, finite in number and capable of being indicated by sense, with a cutting instrument passing through the gaps between them—the objection is this. If the supposed part has volume, it necessarily has one side distinct from another, and intellectual division applies to it, even if its extreme smallness makes division externally or in imagination impossible. If it has no volume, its combination with other such parts cannot produce a body possessing volume.

Again, let us suppose an indivisible part between two other such parts. If it prevents the two outer parts from touching, it is divisible, since each outer part meets something of it other than what the other meets. If it does not prevent them from touching, the middle part's existence and nonexistence are equivalent. The same applies to any middle part that may be supposed. Nothing would then obstruct anything else, which is self-evidently false.

Again, let us suppose an indivisible part above two such parts and over their meeting point. If, with all or some of itself, it meets the whole of both, it is divided. If its whole meets only the whole of one of them, it is not over their meeting point, though it was supposed to be there. If, with all or some of itself, it meets something of each, it is divided and both of them are divided as well. Many forms of demonstration against the indivisible part have been presented and are recorded in the philosophers' books.

The second view, attributed to al-Naẓẓām, holds that body is composed of infinitely many indivisible parts. It is subject to the objections directed against the first view, with the additional objection that, if the parts have volume, their infinite number necessarily makes the body formed by their combination infinite in volume. Self-evidence rules this out.

The third view, attributed to Democritus, holds that body is composed of small, solid parts that cannot be divided externally, although division in imagination and intellect is possible. But these parts must be substances with volume, and hence bodies possessing substantial continuity, from which sensible bodies are composed. What this view establishes is therefore that there are primary bodies which are the principles of these sensible bodies. Moreover, it does not establish the denial of prime matter or invalidate body's composition from prime matter and corporeal form. Prime matter will be established in the next chapter. This view thus amounts to establishing corporeal form for the primary bodies which are the principles of sensible bodies and into which the latter ultimately resolve by division.

The fourth view, attributed to al-Shahrastānī, holds that body is a single continuum, as it appears to sense, and admits division into a finite number of parts. It entails that intellectual division comes to a halt, which is self-evidently false.

The fifth view, attributed to Plato, holds that body is a simple substance consisting in substantial continuity and admitting division without end. We deny that body is simple, in view of the forthcoming demonstration of prime matter in body. Furthermore, that body's substantial continuity is precisely what sense apprehends in sensible bodies is itself a question, to be discussed, God willing.

The sixth view, attributed to the Master of Illumination, holds that body is composed of a substance and an accident: matter and mathematical body, which is a species of continuous quantity. First, it is meaningless for an accident to constitute a substance. It also entails the composition of a real quiddity from two categories, substance and quantity, although the categories differ in their entire essences.

Second, quantity is an accident that requires a subject wherever it occurs. The measured extension by which a body's length, breadth, and depth are determined is a quantity requiring a subject in which to inhere. Unless its subject possessed some continuity capable of being qualified by determination, quantity would neither accrue to it nor inhere in it. If we take a quantity of wax and shape it into a sphere, then a cylinder, then a cone, then a cube, and so forth, we find that the shapes change and replace one another, while a continuity of the wax remains preserved through these different, successive shapes.

There are, then, two continuities: an indeterminate continuity not determined in itself, without which there would be no wax; and a determinate continuity and extension whose disappearance does not entail the disappearance of the body of wax. The first is the body's form; the second is an accident that accrues to body. Division accrues to body through this accident. In respect of its intrinsic indeterminate continuity, however, body admits the positing of three dimensions in it.

The Shaykh says in the Healing: “Corporeality in truth is the form of continuity which admits what we have mentioned—the positing of three dimensions. This meaning is other than magnitude and other than mathematical corporeality. Inasmuch as this body has this form, it does not differ from another body by being larger or smaller, nor is it related to it by being equal to it, measured by it, a measure of it, commensurable with it, or incommensurable with it. These belong to body only insofar as it has magnitude and insofar as a part of it measures it. This consideration is other than the consideration of corporeality we have mentioned.” End of quotation.

In general, incorporating accidental quantitative extension into the quiddity of substance—apart from its own invalidity—confuses substantial continuity with the accidental extension that is mathematical body.

The seventh view, attributed to Aristotle, holds that body is composed of prime matter and corporeal form. This form is substantial continuity as apprehended by sense: the character of a thing in which three extensions intersecting at right angles can be posited, admitting division into infinitely many parts. Prime matter will be established below, while corporeal form, which is continuity, has already been explained.

There is no objection to body's being composed of matter and a substantial continuity that admits division without end. There is, however, no demonstration that body's continuity is as sense presents it, for sense is not immune from error.

After precise technical experiments, natural scientists have recently discovered that bodies are composed of atomic parts which possess bulk, with intervals between them many times greater than the extension of their own bulk. Let this view, therefore, be applied to the atomic bodies they have discovered, which are the principles from which sensible bodies are formed; and let the existence of body in this sense serve as a postulate for us.

If, however, we accept the assertion that “matter”—by which they mean the primary atomic bodies—“can be transformed into energy and is a collection of accumulated quanta of energy,” philosophical investigation would have to take energy as a higher species ordered beneath substance and before body, and then arrange the various discussions in a manner appropriate to this arrangement. Let this be pondered.

[Textual correction · source comparison] The stored transcription reads «الأبحاث المتفرّفة»; the 1404 print and 1428 edited digital text support «الأبحاث المتفرّقة». The reader adopts «الأبحاث المتفرّقة». Stored ف replaces the ق printed in both external witnesses. This is printed/digital edition evidence, not manuscript certification.

[Original quotation source · restored locator] al-Shifāʾ, p. 64 (the pagination cited in the original).

[External comparison and editorial decisions] Complete external comparison reconciled in Stage X. The early 1404 witness is a printed facsimile and the later 1428 witness is an edited digital presentation. No manuscript collation or independent human certification is claimed.

Adopted: and then arrange the derivative discussions in a manner appropriate to this arrangement → and then arrange the various discussions in a manner appropriate to this arrangement. Both external witnesses read المتفرقة (various/separate), not المتفرعة (derivative). This follows the recommended Arabic transcription repair. Grade: lexical-English-correction-against-jointly-attested-Arabic. Source: 1404 pp. 94,95,96,97,98,99; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 160,161,162,163,164,165,166

Adopted: الجزءالمفروض → الجزء المفروض. Restore word boundary, A17. Grade: spacing-or-punctuation-normalization; no-substantive-reading-change. Source: 1404 pp. 96,97; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 163,164,165

N-VI-05 · The quiddity of matter and the demonstration of its existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 126–130
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=126&type=c_fbook

There is no doubt that body, insofar as it is a substance in which the three dimensions can be posited, is something actual. Insofar as other primary perfections called specific forms, which perfect its substance, and secondary perfections consisting of accidents outside its substance can exist in it, it is something potential.

The aspect of actuality differs from the aspect of potentiality, since actuality is realized only through possession, whereas potentiality accompanies lack.

That which, by its own nature, receives these primary and secondary perfections possible for it and becomes united with them is therefore something other than the continuous form through which it is actual. Substantial continuity, considered precisely as such, is nothing but substantial continuity. The aspect of the potentiality and possibility of subsequent perfections is something outside this continuity and different from it. Besides substantial continuity, then, body has another part whose very nature is the reception of subsequent forms and accidents. This is the part called “hyle” or “matter.”

It has thus become clear that body is a substance composed of two substantial parts: matter, whose being consists in receiving forms having some kind of attachment to body and the accidents attached to them, and corporeal form. Matter is a substance receptive to corporeal forms and accidents, and substantial extension is its form.

It should not be objected: There is no doubt that the forms and accidents that arise subsequently in bodies are preceded by a possibility in their substrate and by its preparedness and readiness for them. The closer the possible thing comes to occurring, the more specific and intense the preparedness becomes, until, when its preparedness is complete, the possible thing exists through an effusion from the agent. What, then, prevents us from attributing reception to body—that is, substantial continuity—by means of a preparedness inhering in it as an accident, without needing a substantial preparedness and reception that we establish as a part of body? Furthermore, reception and preparedness are accidental concepts subsisting in another and cannot therefore constitute a substantial reality. Moreover, it is self-evident that preparedness ceases when that for which something is prepared becomes actual. If there were a prime matter that was substantial preparedness and reception and a part of body, it would cease with the realization of the possible thing for which it was prepared. Body would then cease with the cessation of its part and be annihilated through its annihilation, contrary to what is self-evident.

The reply is that the difference between body, insofar as it is nothing but substantial continuity, and each specific form prevents it from being the subject of reception and preparedness for that form. Another thing is needed that does not resist being united with each subsequent form. It must be receptive in its own nature to each of them, while the specific preparednesses mediating between it and possible forms are various determinations of the receptivity that belongs to it in its own nature. The relation of these diverse, determinate preparednesses to matter’s intrinsically indeterminate preparedness is like that of mathematical bodies and quantitative extensions, which are determinations of substantial extension and continuity, to substantial continuity itself.

If body, insofar as it is substantial continuity, were the subject of preparedness, then, since body is among the things that originate and are preceded by possibility, it would carry the possibility of itself. It would consequently precede itself in time.

As for the claim that the concept of reception is an accidental meaning subsisting in another, and that it therefore makes no sense to call matter reception in its very essence, thereby making reception a substance: the answer is that this is an inquiry into reality. In inquiries into reality we follow demonstration, not words with their lexical concepts and customary meanings.

Nor is there any difficulty in preparedness ceasing when that for which something is prepared and has potentiality actually comes to be. Matter is, in its own nature, the potentiality for everything without any one of those things being determined. The determination of this potentiality, which entails a determination of its object, is an accident whose subject is matter. When the possible object of potentiality becomes actual, the determinate potentiality and specific preparedness cease, while matter remains as it was: the potentiality for possible forms.

Thus, if by saying that preparedness ceases when the possible thing for which something is prepared becomes actual, the objector means the unrestricted preparedness belonging to matter, the claim is denied. If he means the specific preparedness that is an accident subsisting in matter, it is conceded; but its cessation does not entail the cessation of matter.

It should not be objected: The argument—that is, establishing matter by the difference between potentiality and actuality—is undermined by the human soul. The soul is simple and immaterial, yet has potential effects, such as the occurrence of volitions and conceptions, and so on. It is actual in its immaterial essence and potential with respect to its secondary perfections. If, despite its simplicity, it can be both actual and potential, then body too should be able to possess actuality and potentiality without being composed of matter and form.

The reply is that the soul is not wholly immaterial in both essence and action. Rather, it is attached to matter in its action. It has actuality insofar as it is immaterial and potentiality insofar as it is attached to matter.

It should not be objected: The argument is undermined by the human soul in another respect. They have stated—and rightly so—that the human intellectual soul is matter for immaterial intelligibles. Although it is immaterial, whenever it intellects an intelligible it becomes that intelligible.

We reply that the immaterial soul’s emergence from potentiality into actuality through union with one intellect after another is not a case of motion in the familiar sense of the first perfection of what is potential insofar as it is potential. Otherwise it would entail potentiality, preparedness, change, and time, all of which contradict immateriality, which is complete actuality devoid of potentiality. Rather, calling the soul matter for intelligible forms means that its immaterial existence intensifies, without change or time, through union with an intellectual degree above its own degree of existence, through the bestowal of the higher degree. This is the condition for the bestowal of the degree above that higher degree.

In general, the soul’s being matter for immaterial intelligible forms differs in kind from materiality as it is found in the world of bodies. Sufficient evidence is the absence there of the properties of corporeal matter.

It should not be objected: The argument is undermined by matter itself. In itself it is an actually existing substance, and it has the potentiality to receive things. It must therefore be composed of a form through which it is actual and a matter through which it is potential. We transfer the argument to the matter of matter, and so on, producing an infinite regress. This shows that containing potentiality and actuality does not entail composition in body.

The reply, as the Master answered, is that matter contains potentiality and actuality, but its potentiality is identical with its actuality and its actuality identical with its potentiality. In its own nature it is the pure potentiality for things. It has no actuality except the actuality of being the potentiality for things.

It should not be objected: The argument is undermined by intellect. It acts upon what is beneath it and is affected by what is above it. It therefore has two aspects, action and being acted upon. On your account it must consequently be composed of matter and form, acting through form and being acted upon through matter.

The reply is that being acted upon and reception there differ from the being acted upon and reception under discussion in bodies. An intellect’s being acted upon and receiving existence from what is above it amount to nothing but its existence being bestowed upon it, without a preceding potentiality and preparedness bringing its subject closer to actuality. It is only the intellect that posits for an intellect a quiddity and considers that quiddity receptive to existence and nonexistence, thereby considering its investiture with existence to be reception and being acted upon. “Reception,” like “being acted upon,” is thus shared by the two meanings. What entails composition is reception in the sense of preceding preparedness and potentiality, not reception in the sense of the bestowal of existence. An intellect acts through precisely that through which it receives and is acted upon.

[Textual emendation · 1428 AH editor] The 1404 Qum facsimile, printed p. 102 (NYU canvas 106), and the 1417 transcription, p. 129, read «بإضافة المرتبة العالية» (through the addition of the higher degree). The 1428 editor, vol. 1 p. 171 n. 1, explicitly records that reading and corrects to «بإفاضة المرتبة العالية» (through the bestowal of the higher degree). N-VI-06 · Matter does not exist apart from corporeal form, nor corporeal form apart from matter
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 130–134
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=130&type=c_fbook

Matter cannot be stripped of form because, in its very essence and substance, it is the potentiality for things. Its only share of actuality is the actuality of having no actuality. Existence necessarily accompanies actuality as opposed to potentiality. In its existence, therefore, matter needs an actual existent with determinate existence, with which it unites and through whose determinacy it becomes determinate. This is what is called “form.”

Furthermore, were matter to exist apart from form, it would possess actuality in its existence, although it is sheer potentiality for things. Two incompatible features would thus be combined in a single essence, which is impossible.

Since matter's existence is constituted by the existence of form, form has an aspect of agency in relation to it. Its agency is not complete, however, because forms succeed one another in matter, whereas a single effect can have only a single cause. Matter consequently has an agent higher in existence than matter and material things, which produces matter and preserves its existence through the union with it of one form after another. Form is thus a partner in the cause of matter.

One should not object: “According to them, matter is numerically one, whereas some form or other is one universally. What is numerically one is stronger in existence than what is universally one. If some form or other causes matter, what is stronger in existence must therefore be an effect of what is weaker, which is impossible.”

The reply is that, although matter is numerically one, its unity is indeterminate and weak because its existence is indeterminate and it is sheer potentiality. The unity of form, which is a partner in the cause that is the separate existent, is supported by the unity of that separate existent.

The separate existent's sustaining and preserving matter by means of some form or other is like preserving a roof from collapse by erecting one support after another.

In the discussions of substantial motion, God willing, what reveals the true situation concerning the multiplicity of these forms succeeding one another in matter will be presented.

What has preceded shows that every actuality and determinacy that occurs to matter comes through the actuality of form, since matter's determinacy comes through the determinacy of form. It also shows that form is a partner in the cause of matter, and that form is prior to matter in existence, although matter is prior to form in time.

As for corporeal form's not being stripped of matter: any body whatsoever has separable accidents that successively occur to it—various motions, quantities, qualities, places, positions, and so forth—and specific forms that succeed one another in it. All these depend on a prior possibility and preparedness whose bearer can only be matter. No body, therefore, exists except in matter.

Furthermore, body, insofar as it is a substance admitting the three dimensions, is a single, complete specific nature, even though there are species beneath it. It is unlike the concept of substance, which has only the status of a generic quiddity and has no determination other than the determinations of the species subsumed beneath it.

Since body is a specific nature, it must by its nature and in itself either be independent of matter and have no need of it, or need it. If it were independent in itself, it could not inhere in matter, because inherence is identical with dependence. Yet we find some bodies inhering in matter; hence body is not independent of it. If it depends on matter in itself, the dependence—which is inherence—is established for every body.

One should not object: “Why may it not be independent of matter in itself, while association with matter occurs to some of its individuals through a cause external to its essence, as separable accidents occur to specific natures?”

The reply is that, as indicated, body's association with matter consists in its inherence in matter. In other words, its existence comes to be for matter and to qualify it. Dependence occurring to it through an external cause after it was in itself independent of matter would therefore mean that its existence came to be for another after being for itself. This is necessarily impossible.

Know that although the question is framed in terms of corporeal form's separation from matter, the proof applies to every form to which further perfections can possibly accrue.

The discussion of substantial motion will explain that material substance moves through its forms until it attains sheer actuality with no potentiality. This occurs by putting on one form after another, rather than by stripping off one and putting on another. Accordingly, the impossibility of material form's separation from matter is restricted to the condition of motion; it does not apply once motion has been completed and its goal attained.

This is supported by what the Shaykh and Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn have stated: matter does not enter the definition of body as genera enter the definitions of their species.

The quiddity of body—substance admitting the three dimensions—contains no mention of matter, which is substance in which the potentiality for things resides. By contrast, body, for example, is included in the definition of growing body, growing body in the definition of animal, and animal in the definition of human being.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn explains this by saying that, if matter entered the quiddity of body, its belonging to body would be self-evident, as is characteristic of an essential constituent. Yet initially we doubt whether matter belongs to body, and then establish it by demonstration; there is no demonstration of an essential constituent.

There is no conflict between saying that matter lies outside the quiddity of body and saying that it is united with corporeal form, as necessarily follows from the conjunction of what is potential with what is actual. The unity asserted is a unity in existence, not in quiddity.

It follows that, were some material species to become separate from matter, this would not entail a transformation through a change in definition. Matter is a necessary accompaniment of its existence, not a part of its quiddity.

[Textual correction · source comparison] The 1404 print reads «والمعلول الواحد لا تكون لها إلا علة واحدة»; the 1428 edition reads «والمعلول الواحد لا يكون له إلّا علّة واحدة». No dedicated later variant note is claimed for this difference. The reader adopts «والمعلول الواحد لا تكون له إلاّ علّةٌ واحدةٌ». The masculine pronoun refers grammatically to المعلول; later p.174 attests له without a dedicated variant note. Fresh inspection shows that early p.103 actually reads لها, contradicting report C. This is a mixed later-reading and contextual-vocalization decision, not mere orthography. This is printed/digital edition evidence, not manuscript certification.

N-VI-07 · Establishing specific forms: the substantial forms that differentiate the substance of body in general into species
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 134–137
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=134&type=c_fbook

We find differences among bodies in respect of concepts that apply to them, whose belonging to them is self-evident and which cannot be separated from them. We cannot conceive a body without conceiving it, for example, as an element, a mineral compound, a tree, an animal, and so on. Body's qualification by these concepts in this manner indicates that they are among its constituents. Since each is more particular than body, they constitute the substance of its essence, and a species of body results through the addition of each to it. Only a substance can constitute a substance. These, therefore, are substantial forms that differentiate body into species.

One should not object: “We do not grant that only a substance can constitute a substance. Frequently something exists and ‘substance’ is predicated of it in answer to ‘What is it?’; then some accident is added, and the answer to the question ‘What is it?’ changes. Iron is a substance, for example, but when a sword is fashioned from it through the addition of accidental configurations, the answer to ‘What is it?’ differs from the answer given when it is iron. Likewise, clay and stone are substances, but when a building is constructed from them, the answer to ‘What is it?’ is ‘a house,’ although only accidental configurations have been added.”

The reply is that this confuses real species, which are real composites whose composition produces a single identity beyond their parts, with effects beyond those of their parts—as with elements and generated natural beings—with composites constituted by consideration, whose combination produces neither anything beyond their parts nor any effect beyond the effects of their parts, as with artificial objects such as swords and houses, and with other composites of this kind.

In general, nothing beyond the parts themselves results from a composite constituted by consideration. A composite of substance and accident is neither a substance nor an accident, and so has no quiddity that could answer “What is it?” This is because the categories differ through the entirety of their simple essences; a quiddity cannot be constituted from more than one of them.

Nor should one object: “The claim that specific forms are substances conflicts with their statement that ‘the differentiae of substances are not subsumed under the genus substance.’”

The reply is that this was discussed in the stage on quiddity. It became clear there that the substantiality of the differentiae of substances—which are specific forms taken with the condition of exclusion—means that the genus substance applies to them as a general accident applies to a property. They constitute the species and are accidental to the genus.

Another argument: We find that bodies differ in the effects present in them, including both inseparable and separable accidents. Each body's exclusive possession of its particular effects necessarily requires something that determines that specificity.

This determining factor cannot be their shared corporeality, since it is common to all bodies. Nor can it be their shared matter, whose function is reception and preparedness rather than action and entailment. Nor can it be a separate existent, whose relation to all bodies is equal.

The determining factor also cannot be one of the subsequent accidents, such that one effect is made specific by a preceding effect. For we would transfer the discussion to the preceding effect, producing an infinite regress, a circle, or an endpoint in something not external to the substance of the body possessing the effect. The first two are impossible, leaving the third: the effects depend on something not external to the substance of the body. This must be a constituent of it, and a constituent of substance is a substance.

Since this substantial constituent is more particular than body in general, it is a substantial form that differentiates body into species.

Bodies in their diversity thus possess substantial specific forms that are the principles of effects differing with the differences among species.

One should not object: “The individuals of each corporeal species possess distinctive effects and individuating accidents, such that what belongs to one individual is not found in another. Your argument applies to these as well. Why, then, do you not establish, beyond the forms you have called specific forms, individual forms constituting the species' quiddity?”

The reply is that the accidents called ‘individuating accidents’ are necessary accompaniments of individuation, not what individuates. Individuation comes through existence, as explained in the stage on quiddity. Accidents are individuated through the individuation of their subjects, for there is no sense in an accident being universal while it inheres in an individuated subject. The principle of the actual accidents subsequently attaching to an individual is the specific nature in that individual, which entails a broad range of quantity, quality, position, and other accidents.

External, coincident causes and conditions then specify what the specific nature entails. As these causes and conditions change, the individual passes from one accident with which it is qualified to another of the same species or genus.

Conclusion of the chapter: Since specific form constitutes its secondary matter, namely the body composed of matter and corporeal form, it is an efficient cause of body and prior to it, just as corporeal form is a partner in the cause of prime matter.

Two consequences follow. First, existence belongs primarily to specific form; through its existence corporeal form exists, and then prime matter exists with its actual existence.

Second, specific forms do not preserve a single corporeality until its replacement. Rather, corporeality exists through their existence. When a specific form is replaced by another that differs from it in species, the body ceases with the cessation of that form, and another corporeality then comes to be with the coming-to-be of the subsequent form.

[Textual emendation · 1428 editor] The early 1404 print, p.106, and stored base read «ولم ينضم إليها», while the revised 1428 edition, vol.1 p.180 n.6, explicitly prints «ولم ينضمّ إليهما» for the clay and stone. The dual pronoun is adopted here, with the earlier feminine singular preserved in this note. N-VI-08 · Quantity, one of the accidental categories
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 137–139
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=137&type=c_fbook

It has already been explained that an accident is a quiddity which, when it exists in concrete things, exists in a subject independent of it. Accidentality is like a general accident belonging to nine categories, which are highest genera with no genus above them. Accordingly, the definition given for each is a description through a property, rather than a real definition possessing a genus and differentia.

The two masters, al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā, defined quantity as “the accident in which, through itself, there can be found one thing that measures it by repetition.” This is the best of the definitions given for it.

The definition “the accident that admits division in itself” has been criticized as defining something through what is more particular than it: susceptibility to division belongs specifically to continuous quantity, whereas discrete quantity already possesses actual parts.

Likewise, the definition “the accident that admits equality” has been criticized as circular, since equality is unity in quantity.

In any case, these definitions contain three properties of quantity: being measured by repetition, divisibility, and equality.

Adopted: شيءواحد → شيء واحد. Restore the word boundary present in both external witnesses. Grade: jointly-attested-word-separation. Source: 1404 pp. 109; 1428 vol. 1 pp. 184

N-VI-09 · The divisions of quantity
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 139–141
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=139&type=c_fbook

Quantity is divided in its primary division into continuous and discrete.

Continuous quantity is quantity in which parts can be supposed that meet at common limits. A common limit is that which can be made the beginning of one part just as it can be made the end of another. Thus, if a line is supposed to be divided into three parts, the middle section can be made the beginning or end of either of the two sides. The two sections then constitute a single section, and the line has two sections. Continuous quantity has also been defined as that which admits division without end.

Discrete quantity is the opposite of continuous quantity. It is number, which results from the repetition of the unit. It is actually divided into parts, with no common limit between them. If five, for example, is divided into two and three, then, were there a common limit belonging to their parts, the result would be four; were the limit additional to them, the result would be six.

Continuous quantity is divided into two: stable and nonstable. Stable quantity is that which is fixed and whose parts are actually present together, as in a surface. Nonstable quantity is that whose supposed parts are not actually present together, as in time. Every actual part of time is a potentiality for the following part, and the two are therefore not actual together, since a thing's actuality does not coexist with its potentiality.

Stable quantity is divided into mathematical body, which admits division in its three directions—breadth, length, and depth; surface, which admits division in two directions—breadth and length; and line, which admits division in one direction.

Discrete quantity, namely number, self-evidently exists externally. The existence of nonstable continuous quantity, namely time, will be established in the discussions of potentiality and actuality. As for stable continuous quantity, mathematical body and surface exist externally: there are determinate, finite natural bodies separate from one another. Their determinateness entails mathematical body, and their finitude entails surface. Line exists externally if the existence of bodies with intersecting surfaces, such as the cube, cone, pyramid, and the like, is established.

Every rank in the endless series of numbers is a particular species of number distinct from the others, since each has numerical properties that do not extend beyond it to another. Time is one species, even though the subjects in which it occurs are the several species of substantial and accidental motions, because there is a common measure among their instances.

Mathematical bodies with no common measure, such as the sphere, cone, cube, and the like, are distinct species. The same holds for surfaces with no common measure, such as the plane surface and the kinds of convex and concave surfaces; and likewise for lines with no common measure, if they exist, such as the straight line and the kinds of arcs. Irregular bodies, surfaces, and lines are not species, but composites of various species.

[Unadopted later pronoun reading] The 1404 print p.111 and the stored base read «بين أفرادها», with the feminine pronoun referring to the preceding motions. The 1428 edition vol.1 p.188 reads «بين أفراده», referring to time instead. The reader retains the earlier Arabic and translates “among their instances” to preserve that reference. This is an unfootnoted printed-edition variation, not an authorial or manuscript correction.

[Later editor’s objection · common boundary] The 1428 editor at vol.1 p.186 n.2 objects to the author’s middle-segment example. He prefers Sabzawārī’s examples of a point between parts of a line, a line between parts of a surface, a surface between parts of a body, and an instant between parts of time. He argues that a middle piece does not distinguish continuous from discrete quantity, since a middle unit could similarly be selected in seven. Both editions retain the author’s example in the body; this is an attributed philosophical objection, not an attested replacement.

[Later philosophical objection · irregular figures] At 1428 vol.1 p.188 n.2, the editor quotes a pupil of the author who disputes the final claim about irregular figures. The objection is that an irregular figure may be one actual shape; imagining its partition for calculation does not establish actual composition, any more than imagining two triangles makes a square actually composite. Both editions retain the author’s conclusion. This note reports the objection without silently substituting it for the source text.

N-VI-10 · Various judgments concerning quantity
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 141–144
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=141&type=c_fbook

It was indicated earlier that among the properties of quantity are equality and inequality; division, externally as in number or in the imagination as in other quantities; and the presence within it of a measure that measures it. They have also set out other judgments.

First: discrete quantity, namely number, exists in both material and immaterial things. Nonstable continuous quantity, namely time, exists only in material things. Stable continuous quantity—mathematical body, surface, and line—does not exist in immaterial things except according to those who establish an immaterial realm of magnitude that possesses the effects of matter without matter itself.

Second: there is no contrariety in number. A condition of contrariety is the utmost difference between the contraries, and no two numbers have the utmost difference. For any two supposed ranks of number, adding one to the greater increases its distance from the smaller.

As for the argument that every rank of number is constituted by what lies below it, whereas a contrary is not constituted by its contrary: its difficulty is that, if a rank of number were composed of the lower ranks, those lower ranks would have equal eligibility to constitute it. Ten, for example, can be supposed composed of nine and one, eight and two, seven and three, six and four, or five and five. The determination of some rather than others as its parts would be a preponderance without a determining ground, which is impossible. When mathematicians say that ten is the sum of eight and two, they mean that one rank of number equals two ranks. They do not mean that the one rank, which is a single species, is identical with the two ranks, which are two species. An analogous argument applies to continuous quantity in general.

Nor is mathematical body contrary to a surface or a line, or a surface contrary to a line. There is no single subject here upon which the two succeed one another, and no utmost difference can be conceived between them.

Third: quantity admits no gradation in intensity and weakness. This is self-evident, or nearly so. It does, however, admit gradation in increase and decrease: one line may be longer than another when compared with it in existence, though not in its possessing the quiddity of line. A surface likewise exceeds or falls short of another surface of its species, as does a mathematical body.

Fourth: they say that dimensions are finite, and offer several arguments. One of the clearest is this. Suppose an infinite line and a sphere from whose center a line extends parallel to that infinite line. If the sphere rotates, the two lines will meet, according to Euclid's postulate. The line extending from the center will thus intersect the supposed infinite line after having been parallel to it. There must, therefore, be a point on the infinite line that is the first point of intersection. But this is impossible, since no point of intersection can be supposed on the line without there being a point farther along it that the rotating line intersects first. Other demonstrations of the impossibility of an infinite dimension have been offered, including the proof by correspondence, the ladder proof, and others.

Fifth: a void is impossible, for it would entail dimension subsisting through itself, without a subject in which it subsists. Discussion of this will come in the inquiry into place.

Sixth: number is not finite. This means that no rank of number exists without a greater one being conceivable, and a still greater one beyond that; the series does not stop until the act of consideration ceases. This infinite is called the indefinitely continuable. At any time, only a finite quantity of the series actually exists, while what exceeds it remains potential.

As for the series actually proceeding to infinity, with infinity understood as an affirmative qualification rather than as a simple negation of a limit, this is unintelligible. There is consequently no whole or totality of the infinite in this sense, and no fractional relation such as a half, a third, or a quarter is realized in it; otherwise it would become finite.

N-VI-11 · Quality and its primary division
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 144–146
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=144&type=c_fbook

They define quality as an accident that, through itself, admits neither division nor relation. “Accident” excludes that which is necessary through itself and excludes substance. The qualification “does not admit division” excludes quantity, while “does not admit relation” excludes the seven relational categories. “Through itself” includes that to which division or relation belongs incidentally.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn says: “Since the categories are highest genera with no genus above them, it is impossible to give them an essential definition. Their proposed definitions are therefore incomplete descriptions, in which mention of properties suffices to distinguish them. No necessary and comprehensive property of quality has been found apart from the combination of its being an accident and its differing from quantity and the relational accidents. It was consequently defined in terms amounting to: ‘An accident different from quantity and the relational accidents.’

“But this defines something through things equal to it in being known or unknown, since none of the highest genera is clearer than another. If this were permissible, its like would be permissible for the other categories—and indeed would be more appropriate there, since relational things are known only after their subjects, namely qualities. They therefore replaced mention of quantity and the relational accidents with mention of the property that is clearer.” This ends the passage in abridgment.

Quality is divided in its primary division into four general classes: sensible qualities, psychic qualities, qualities peculiar to quantities, and dispositional qualities. Their reliance in restricting them to these four is on induction.

N-VI-12 · Sensible qualities
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 146–149
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=146&type=c_fbook

One of the properties of sensible qualities is that they act by assimilation—that is, by making another thing resemble themselves. Heat makes what adjoins it hot, and blackness, for example, casts its image, that is, its likeness, upon the eye.

Sensible qualities are divided into visible, audible, gustatory, olfactory, and tangible qualities.

Among visible qualities are colors. The prevalent view is that they are objective qualities existing outside sensation; that their simple kinds are white and black; and that the other colors result from various combinations of these two. It is also said: “The simple colors that are the fundamental ones are five: black, white, red, yellow, and green. The remaining colors are compounded from them.” Another view is: “Color is an imaginative quality with no existence beyond sensation, like the halo, rainbow, and the like. It results from kinds of mixture of air with transparent bodies, or from reflection from them.”

Light is another visible quality. It needs no definition. It is sometimes defined as “that which is manifest through itself and makes other things manifest.” What should be meant here is its making bodies manifest to sight. If making manifest were taken without qualification, that would be a property of existence.

In any case, their familiar doctrine is that light is a visible quality found in bodies luminous through themselves or in a body facing a luminous thing, without its being transferred from the luminous thing to the illuminated one. Darkness is opposed to it as privation is opposed to possession. It has also been said that light is a corporeal substance, and that it is the manifestation of color.

Audible qualities are sounds. Sound is a quality resulting from a forceful striking or a forceful pulling apart, followed by undulation of the air that carries sounds. When the undulation reaches the air adjoining the auditory canal, sound is perceived. Sound is neither the undulation nor the pulling apart or striking itself. Nor is perceived sound an image in sensation with no existence outside sensation.

Gustatory qualities are the tastes apprehended by taste. They have counted nine simple kinds: pungency, saltiness, bitterness, fattiness, sweetness, blandness, acridity, astringency, and sourness. Other tastes are compounded from these.

Olfactory qualities are the kinds of odors perceived by smell.
