We have no names for the kinds of odors we apprehend by which to identify them, except by relating them to their subjects, as when we say ‘the odor of musk’ or ‘the odor of roses’; by their agreeableness or disagreeableness to our constitution, as when we say ‘a pleasant odor’ or ‘a foul odor’; or by relating them to taste, as when we say ‘a sweet odor’ or ‘a sour odor.’ All this indicates the weakness of human beings in the sense of smell, as the Shaykh mentions.

Tangible qualities are the kinds of qualities perceived by touch. They have counted twelve simple kinds: heat, cold, moisture, dryness, subtlety, density, viscosity, friability, desiccation, wetness, heaviness, and lightness. Some have added roughness, smoothness, hardness, and softness, though the familiar view is that these are composite.

N-VI-13 · Qualities peculiar to quantities
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 150–153
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=150&type=c_fbook

These are qualities that occur in a body through its quantity: quantity is characterized by them first, and the body is then characterized by them on account of its quantity. Examples are circularity in a line and evenness in a number.

Induction yields three divisions: first, shape and angle; second, qualities other than shape and angle, such as circularity and straightness, which occur in lines, surfaces, and mathematical bodies; and third, qualities that occur in number, such as evenness, oddness, being square, being a root, and so forth.

Some have added bodily configuration to these three, intending by it the combination of color and shape. This is refuted by the fact that it does not possess a real unity with a real quiddity; it is instead a composite constituted by consideration. Were it possessed of a quiddity, it would have to fall under both visible qualities and qualities peculiar to quantities. These are two mutually exclusive genera, so that would be impossible.

As for the first division, shape is a configuration that quantity acquires through being completely enclosed by one boundary or several boundaries. Examples are the shape of a circle enclosed by a single line; the shapes of a triangle, square, and polygon enclosed by several boundaries; a sphere enclosed by a single surface; and a cone, cylinder, and cube enclosed by more than one surface.

Shape belongs to qualities because the definition of quality applies to it. It is neither the surface or body, nor the boundaries enclosing it, nor their totality. It is, rather, the configuration arising from a surface or body enclosed by particular boundaries.

An angle is the configuration that arises from incomplete enclosure by two boundaries, or by several boundaries meeting at a boundary. Examples are a plane angle, formed by the enclosure of two lines meeting at a point; a solid angle, formed by the enclosure of the surface of a cone terminating at its vertex; and an angle of a cube, enclosed by three surfaces.

The argument that an angle is a quality rather than a quantity is analogous to the foregoing argument that shape belongs to the category of quality.

The Shaykh allowed that the configuration formed by the enclosure of two surfaces—for example, two surfaces of a cube—meeting at a line is an angle, because the properties of an angle apply to it.

As for the second division, straightness in a line, and circularity which is opposed to it, belong to the category of quality rather than quantity. They differ specifically from one another.

They belong to the category of quality because we apprehend the concepts of straightness and circularity, both of which are self-evident concepts, without finding in them the meaning of divisibility. Divisibility is indeed inseparable from them in existence, since they occur in quantity. Yet if divisibility were part of their definitions, or one of their best-known properties, our apprehension of them would not be devoid of it.

They are two differing and mutually distinct species because, if they were one species, the difference found between them would be accidental, neither part of the essence nor a necessary concomitant of it. It would then be intellectually possible for the characteristic of straightness to cease in a straight line while the line itself remained, and for it subsequently to become characterized by circularity. But that is impossible. A line is the boundary of a surface, just as a surface is the boundary of a body; the condition of a boundary cannot change until the condition of that which it bounds changes. If the condition of the surface with respect to its unfolding and extension did not change, the condition of the line with respect to its straightness would not change. And if the condition of the body with respect to its unfolding and extension did not change, the condition of its surface in this respect would not change. Through such a change the mathematical body perishes and another comes into existence, and likewise the surface that bounds it and the line that bounds that surface. When the subject of an attribute perishes and a numerically different subject comes into existence, the attribute is likewise numerically different.

Since a straight line cannot remain when its straightness ceases, we know that straightness is either its differentia or a necessary concomitant of its differentia. A straight line therefore differs specifically from a circular one. The same is true of a plane surface in relation to other surfaces, and of each of those other surfaces in relation to a surface that differs from it; it is also true of mathematical bodies in relation to those that differ from them.

Two consequences follow. First, there is no contrariety between straightness and circularity: they do not succeed one another in a single subject, nor is there between them the utmost opposition. The same applies between a line and a surface, between a surface and a mathematical body, among surfaces themselves, and among mathematical bodies themselves.

Second, there is no intensification or weakening between straightness and circularity. Gradation requires the more intense to contain the less intense together with something additional, whereas it has become clear that the straight does not contain the circular, or conversely.

As for the third division, it includes the evenness and oddness that occur in number, and likewise being square, being a root, being a cube, and the like.

These are qualities rather than quantities, since the definition of quality applies to them. This is evident when we consider that every rank of number is a species whose specific character is independent and distinct from the others, while sharing divisibility with all the other ranks. Being divisible into two equal parts, or not being so divisible, is an attribute of division that is not itself divisible and is not itself relational. It is therefore neither quantity nor any of the relational accidents. Evenness and oddness can consequently be nothing but qualities occurring in quantity.

A similar explanation applies to the remaining attributes of numbers, such as being square, being a root, and so forth.

Reflection on what has preceded makes three points clear. First, there is no contrariety between these numerical attributes. Evenness and oddness have no shared subject in which they succeed one another, as is required for contrariety.

Second, these numerical attributes admit no gradation by intensity and weakness or by increase and decrease. Just as curvature or circularity does not change into another curvature or circularity unless its subject perishes and a numerically different subject comes into existence, so evenness, for example, does not change into the evenness of an evenly even number unless its subject—the thing numbered—perishes and a numerically different subject comes into existence. In this event the evenness, which is an accident, perishes and a numerically different evenness comes into existence. This has nothing to do with gradation.

Third, recalling what has preceded, we know that qualities peculiar to quantities exist both in material things and in immaterial imaginal beings, on the assumption that the imaginal is immaterial.

N-VI-14 · Dispositional qualities
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 153–155
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=153&type=c_fbook

These are also called capacity and incapacity.

The meaning common to them—which has the standing of a species in relation to quality in general and of a genus in relation to their particular species—is an intense bodily preparedness directed toward something external: that is, a preparedness by which the occurrence of something from outside is favored.

They have two species. The first is an intense preparedness to be affected, such as susceptibility to illness and softness. The second is an intense preparedness not to be affected, such as resistance to illness and hardness.

Some have added to these a third species: an intense preparedness for acting, such as aptitude for wrestling.

The Shaykh rejected this, and Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn followed him. He says in the Asfār:

“There is no disagreement that the capacity to be affected and the capacity to resist fall under this kind. As for whether the capacity to act falls under it, the well-known view is that it does. The Shaykh excluded it, and his is the correct position, as the reason for it will become clear to you.

“If one wishes to formulate a meaning common to the two divisions while excluding the third, one should say: it is a quality through which one of the two sides, receptivity and nonreceptivity, is favored in that which receives the quality.

“To show that the capacity to act cannot properly be included under this kind, as the Shaykh maintained, we must first recognize a universal principle: the grounds of action are always necessary concomitants of the essence. Every essence possessing a reality has a demand for an effect: when left to its own nature, with no impediment, it produces that effect. It therefore needs no capacity additional to itself in order to act. If another capacity is supposed to be added to it, then in relation to that capacity the essence is its recipient, rather than its agent. If the essence and the capacity are considered together, their totality is another thing; if it has an action, its action necessarily follows, without a delayed preparedness in it for the occurrence of that action. If such preparedness for agency is posited for it, this would first entail a passive capacity to receive that through which its being an agent becomes complete. The supposed preparedness would thus in reality be for its being affected, rather than for its agency. There is consequently no preparedness for agency: preparedness belongs primarily and intrinsically to being affected, and incidentally to agency.

“From what we have demonstrated it is therefore established that there is intrinsically no capacity or preparedness for a thing's being an agent. Capacity and preparedness are only for being affected, and for a thing to become receptive to something after it had not been so.” End of quotation.

N-VI-15 · Psychic qualities
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 155–162
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=155&type=c_fbook

As for preparedness itself, it has been said: “It belongs to relation, since it is intelligible only between two things, something prepared and that for which it is prepared. Thus it is not a species of quality.” From some writers it appears that it is a quality entailing a relation, like knowledge, which is one of the psychic qualities and entails a relation between its subject …

A psychic quality is, as the Shaykh says, “that which is not connected with bodies in general.” If it is not firmly established, it is called a “state”; if it is firmly established, it is called a “habit.” Since the relation between a state and a habit is that of weakness and intensity, and since they count the two degrees of weakness and intensity as two different species, their position entails counting a state as different from a habit both in species and in existence.

Psychic qualities are numerous. In this discussion they have mentioned only some of those whose investigation is especially relevant.

Among them is will. It is said in the Asfār: “It would appear that its meaning is clear to the intellect and is not confused with anything else, although it is difficult to express it in a way that conveys a conception of its true nature. It differs from appetite, just as its opposite, aversion, differs from repugnance. Hence a person may will something for which he has no appetite, such as drinking an unpleasant medicine that benefits him; and he may have an appetite for something that he does not will, such as eating a delicious food that harms him.” End of quotation. A similar explanation makes it clear that will also differs from the intensified desire by which some have defined it.

A summary that clarifies the will upon which the act of an agent who chooses depends is as follows. Rational principles require that every species of substance be an efficient principle of the acts whose production is attributed to it. These acts are secondary perfections of the species. Thus the human soul, which is an immaterial substantial form whose activity is connected with matter, is the efficient cause of the acts proceeding from a human being. But it is a cognitive principle: nothing proceeds from it unless it has distinguished it, as one of its secondary perfections, from other things. Before acting, therefore, it needs a conception of the act and assent to its being a perfection for it. If this assent is necessary or a firmly established habit, it judges that the act is a perfection and does not undertake deliberation. An example is a speaker who utters one letter after another without deliberating. If he deliberated over some of them, he would become bewildered and hesitant and would stop speaking. If, however, the assent is not necessary and already settled, the soul resorts to deliberation and to investigating the considerations that favor one course. If it finds something that establishes the act as a perfection, it judges accordingly.

Then, as has been said, this cognitive form is followed by desire for the act, since the act is a secondary perfection caused by the soul. Desire is then followed by will. Although no expression conveys a conception of will’s true nature, evidence for its existence after desire is supplied by what we find in someone who wills an act but is unable to perform it and is unaware of his inability: he cannot perform the act, although he has willed it. Will is then followed by the operative power that moves the muscles; it moves the muscles, and this is the act.

The principles of voluntary action in us are therefore knowledge, desire, will, and the operative motive power. This is what we find in ourselves in our voluntary acts. Close consideration of the condition of other animals shows that, in their voluntary acts, they are like human beings.

The following points have thus become clear. First, the efficient principle of a human being’s voluntary acts, insofar as they are his secondary perfections, is the human being insofar as he is a cognitive agent. Knowledge completes his agency: through it, the human agent distinguishes the perfection to be pursued from other things. Desire follows this knowledge without depending upon another desire or a will; and will necessarily follows desire without depending upon another will, for otherwise wills would regress infinitely. Counting will as the efficient cause of the act is therefore misplaced. Will, and the desire preceding it, are instead concomitants of the knowledge that completes the agent’s agency.

Second, those human acts in whose production knowledge has a role are never devoid of the agent’s will, even an act performed under coercion. The discussion of the divisions of agent will provide further material useful here.

Third, the criterion of an act’s being chosen is that the human being stands in an equal relation to performing it and refraining from it, even though, when considered as complete in agency, he necessarily acts.

Another psychic quality is power. It is a condition in an animal through which an act can proceed from it if it wills, and does not proceed from it if it does not will. Its opposite is inability.

As for the power attributed to the Necessary Existent, exalted is He: since what is necessarily existent through itself is necessarily existent in every respect, His power is His actual being, through His essence, the principle of everything. Since it is identical with the essence, it has no quiddity; rather, it is sheer existence.

Knowledge too is counted, as has been said, among psychic qualities. What is meant is acquired mental knowledge insofar as it inheres in the soul as an accident inheres in its subject, for the definition of quality applies to it. Knowledge by presence, however, is the presence of the known, in its external existence, to the knower; and existence is neither substance nor accident.

The knowledge that belongs to quality is restricted to beings endowed with souls. As for separate immaterial beings, it has already been established that their knowledge is by presence, not acquired. Nevertheless, the acquired cognitions that exist in their effects are present to them, although these cognitions too, insofar as they are their products, are present to them.

Character also belongs here. It is the psychic habit from which acts proceed easily, without deliberation. It is called character only when it constitutes a practical intellect that is a principle of voluntary acts. It is not the power to act, for power bears an equal relation to acting and refraining, whereas character is related only to the act. Nor does “character” mean the act itself, even though the term is sometimes applied to it: character is the firmly established condition upon which the act is based.

Character has many branches, and the subdivisions resulting from them are almost innumerable. But the roots of human character, considered in relation to the powers that impel a human being to act, are three. These are the appetitive powers, which impel him to attract the good and beneficial that suit him; the irascible powers, which impel him to repel the evil and harmful; and the intellect, which guides him toward goodness and happiness and restrains him from evil and misery.

If the habit operative in matters of appetite maintains moderation, by doing what ought to be done in the way it ought to be done, it is called “temperance.” If it deviates toward excess, it is called “voracity”; and if it falls into deficiency, it is called “apathy.” Likewise, the habit associated with anger has a moderate condition called “courage,” an excessive extreme called “recklessness,” and a deficient extreme called “cowardice.” Likewise, if the habit that judges good and evil, benefit and harm, maintains the moderate mean and occupies itself with what ought to be done in the way it ought to be done, it is called “wisdom.” If it moves to the extreme of excess, it is called “cunning,” and if to the extreme of deficiency, “stupidity.”

The disposition resulting from the conjunction of these three habits stands to them as a temperament stands to the things blended to produce it. Its effect is to give each power its due. When this disposition is balanced, it is called “justice”; when it passes to the extreme of excess, it is called “injustice”; and when it passes to the extreme of deficiency, “submission to injustice.”

The moderate mean in these fundamental habits, and in the branches that derive from them, is a praiseworthy virtue. The two extremes, those of excess and deficiency, are blameworthy vices. Investigation of these virtues and vices is entrusted to a discipline other than the present one.

From the foregoing it is clear, first, that character exists only in the human world and among other beings endowed with souls that attain perfection through voluntary acts in a way appropriate to the perfection of their existence. There is no character in separate immaterial beings, since they have neither practical intellect nor voluntary acquisition of perfection.

Second, each of these traits of character, which are psychic qualities, has, insofar as it is a firmly established habit, a corresponding state of the same quality. Examples are appetite, anger, fear, alarm, sorrow, anxiety, shame, joy, happiness, distress, and others. Their natural causes are investigated in medicine; their correction and management in a way consonant with human happiness are investigated in the discipline of ethics.

Among psychic qualities are pleasure and pain. Pleasure, according to their definition, is apprehension of what is agreeable insofar as it is agreeable. Pain is apprehension of what is contrary insofar as it is contrary. They therefore belong to quality insofar as they are forms of apprehension.

They divide as apprehension divides: some are sensory, some imaginal, and some intellectual. Sensory pleasure is exemplified by the soul’s apprehension of sweetness through taste and of a pleasant fragrance through smell. Imaginal pleasure is its apprehension of an imaginal form of some sensory pleasure. Intellectual pleasure is its apprehension of some of the true intellectual perfections it has attained. Intellectual pleasure is the most intense and powerful of pleasures because of its immateriality and stability. Sensory, imaginal, and intellectual pain are the opposites of pleasure in each of these domains.

Pleasure is in every case existential, whereas pain is privative; it stands opposed to pleasure as privation is opposed to possession.

It should not be objected: “There is no doubt that pain is intrinsically evil. Since it is apprehension of what is contrary insofar as it is contrary, it is something existential, for apprehension is existential. This overturns their assertion that evil is nothing but privation.” The reply is that the existence of each thing, whether mental or external, is that thing itself. The presence of any privative thing to the apprehender is therefore that privative thing itself, because existence and quiddity, and knowledge and the known, are united. Thus the pain existing within the sphere of apprehension is an instance of pain, and is precisely that privative pain which is intrinsically evil.

Notice. The foregoing account of quality, its judgments, and its properties is what has been transmitted from the earlier philosophers. Modern natural scientists have investigated deeply what the earlier philosophers classified as quality, and have discovered many judgments and effects concerning it. A reflective investigator ought to consult these findings and give them due consideration in the inquiry.

[translation] The supplied transcription opens with the end of the preceding discussion of preparedness, then runs into this chapter’s heading. Its comparison with knowledge breaks off after “between its subject”; the ellipsis marks that textual break, and no missing counterpart has been supplied.

[Textual emendation · 1428 AH editor] At N-VI-15 the 1417 AH base reads «بما أنّها كمالاتها الثانية» (in this second occurrence of the phrase) and «يتمّيز به الكمال من غيره». The 1428 AH editor, vol. 1 p. 209 nn. 3–4, explicitly prints «كمالاته الثانية» (the human's perfections) and «يميّز به» (the human agent distinguishes through knowledge), recording the earlier readings as copy readings. The reader adopts these two editorial emendations, while leaving intact an earlier occurrence «من كمالاتها الثانية» that correctly refers to the feminine soul. The previous readings remain here; no manuscript-attested variant is claimed.

N-VI-16 · Relation
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 162–167
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=162&type=c_fbook

First discussion: the meaning of the relational character of the categories

You have learned that seven of the categories are relational accidents: relation, where, when, position, possession, acting, and being acted upon. Their being relational means that they are configurations subsisting in their subjects on account of relations present in them. It does not mean that these categories are identical with those existential relations. For you learned in the discussion of dependent and independent existence that a relation is a bond existing in another, with no independence whatever: it is neither predicated of anything nor has anything predicated of it. It therefore has no quiddity, since a quiddity is what is said of a thing in answer to “What is it?” Categories are generic quiddities. Thus a relation is neither a category nor something falling under a category.

Moreover, in some of these categories the relation is reciprocal and multiple, whereas the repetition of a quiddity has no meaning. This is so in the category of relation, in which reciprocity is necessary, and in the category of position, which involves the relation of some of a thing’s parts to others and the relation of the whole to what is outside it; the latter may rest upon a very large number of relations.

It has thus become clear that a relational category is a configuration acquired by a thing on account of a certain relation or relations subsisting in it.

Second discussion: the definition of relation

Relation is a configuration acquired through a thing’s relation to another thing which is itself related to the first thing, to which it is referred. An example is the relational configuration in a brother: it involves this brother’s relation of brotherhood to his brother, who is in turn related by brotherhood to this brother to whom he is referred.

The difference between it and relation in the unrestricted sense

The relation involved in the category of relation is thus reciprocal. This is the difference between the relation within this category and relation in the unrestricted sense. The existence of an unrestricted relation is one existence subsisting in its two terms, whatever they may be. The case is different in the category of relation: the relation is reciprocal, and each of the two relatives has a relation distinct from that in the other, although the two necessarily accompany one another and cannot be separated either in the mind or externally.

The definition of relation that we have supplied is not a logical definition by genus and differentia, as was previously indicated in analogous cases. It is a description, if it is better known than what it describes.

Perhaps what the intellect understands by the word “relation,” together with some examples, is better known to it than the description we have provided. There is therefore little benefit in prolonging the discussion of its qualifications, whether in criticism or in defense. The same applies to the other definitions they have supplied for it.

The difference between the relative in the strict and in the ordinary senses

Sometimes “relative” is used to mean the category itself, which they call “the relative in the strict sense.” Sometimes it means the subject of the category. Sometimes it means the subject and the accident together, which they call “the relative in the ordinary sense.” For ordinary people think that what is related to a father, for example, is the human being characterized by sonship, whereas the connection on both sides belongs in truth to the relation itself.

Third discussion: relation exists externally

Relation exists externally, and sense perception supports this. The senses apprehend various external relations whose objective effects cannot be doubted, such as the relation of father and son, above and below, near and far, and others.

As for its manner of existence, the intellect abstracts, for each of the two subjects possessing the mutually concomitant reciprocal relation, a qualification characterizing that subject, without adding anything to them. Relation therefore exists through the existence of its subject, without there being a separate, independent existence corresponding to it.

After discussing this point, the author of the Asfār says: “In general, the relative, insofar as it is relative, is simple. It has no independent, separate existence externally. Its existence consists in its attaching to things whose condition is such that they admit comparison with something else. The heaven’s existence in itself is the existence of substances, while its existence in such a manner that, when it is compared with the earth, being above is understood, is the existence of relations.” End of quotation.

Fourth discussion: some judgments concerning relation

Among the judgments concerning relation is that the two relatives are equivalent with respect to existence and nonexistence, potentiality and actuality. If one exists, the other exists, and likewise on the side of nonexistence. If one is potential, the other is potential, and likewise on the side of actuality.

An objection has been raised: this is contradicted by priority and posteriority among the parts of time. Its earlier and later parts are relatives, yet the existence of one necessarily accompanies the nonexistence of the other.

It is also contradicted by our knowledge of certain future things. Knowledge exists at present, while the known thing is nonexistent and has not yet come to exist, although knowledge and the known are relatives.

The reply to the first counterexample is that the togetherness of the parts of time is not instantaneous, such that the two parts exist in a single instant. Rather, their togetherness is their continuity within the single gradual existence in which their togetherness is identical with priority and posteriority, just as the unity of number is identical with its multiplicity.

The reply to the second counterexample is that the relation is between knowledge and the form of the known that is present to the knower. This is what is known intrinsically, as opposed to what is known incidentally, which is an external entity. Future things are present to the knower through their form, which is known intrinsically, even though they are absent in their external reality, which is known incidentally. Furthermore, the truth is that knowledge is identical with the known, as will be explained in the stage concerning the intellective subject and the intelligible.

Just as the two relatives are equivalent with respect to existence and nonexistence, potentiality and actuality, they are also equivalent with respect to universality and particularity. Universal fatherhood is correlated with universal sonship, and individual fatherhood with individual sonship.

Among the properties of relation is that it occurs in all the categories, including itself. In substance there are father and son; in continuous quantity, greater and smaller; in discrete quantity, more and less; in quality, hotter and colder; in relation, nearer and farther; in where, higher and lower; in when, earlier and later; in position, more upright and more bent; in possession, more clothed and more bare; in acting, cutting and severing more forcefully; and in being acted upon, being heated more intensely and more weakly.

Fifth discussion

Relation is divided into that whose terms are alike, in which there is no difference between its terms, such as one neighbor and another, one brother and another, and one nearby thing and another; and that whose terms differ, such as father and son, higher and lower.

It is also divided into external relation, such as father and son, and mental relation, such as universal and individual, and more general and more specific.

[Textual emendation · 1428 AH editor] At N-VI-16 the 1417 AH base reads «ينتزع من الموضوعين ... وصفاً ناعتاً لهما». The 1428 AH revised edition, vol. 1 p. 218 n. 4, explicitly corrects to «ينتزع لكلّ من الموضوعين ... وصفاً ناعتاً له», referring a separate relational predicate to each term rather than one quality belonging identically to both. Both panels follow the editor's reading, and the earlier wording is preserved here. The editor also notes that «لوقوعها» is preferable to «لوقوعه» in a preceding phrase; the main 1417 text already has «لوقوعها», so no correction was necessary there. In the same chapter, the editor also reports that the copies read «المضاف إلى الابن» but corrects to «المضاف إلى الأب» (vol. 1 p. 218 n. 1). In this passage a son is related to his father; ‘to a son’ contradicts the accompanying example of sonship. The online Arabic and English now adopt this explicitly recorded correction, retaining the older wording in the apparatus.

N-VI-17 · Where
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 167–170
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=167&type=c_fbook

First discussion: the definition of where

Where is a configuration acquired by a body through its relation to place.

The existence of place, with the characteristics familiar to us, is self-evident. It is that from which and to which a body can move, and in which it can remain at rest; that which has position, meaning that it can be indicated as here or there; that which is measurable and has a half, a third, and a quarter; and that which is such that two bodies cannot occupy one and the same instance of it.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified—says: “These are four marks upon which the disputants have agreed, so that their disagreement will not be merely verbal.”

They have disagreed about its reality, offering five views. First, it is the prime matter of body. Second, it is form. Third, it is a surface of a body that meets the body occupying the place, whether the former contains the latter or is contained by it. Fourth, it is the inner surface of the containing body that touches the outer surface of the contained body. This is the view of the First Teacher, followed by the two masters al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā. Fifth, it is an extension equal to the dimensions of the body occupying the place, and is therefore a substantial extension separate from matter. This is the view of Plato and the Stoics, and was adopted by the investigator al-Ṭūsī—may his secret be sanctified—and Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn. These are five views. A sixth is the denial of place by some thinkers.

Since the four marks just mentioned are self-evident and beyond doubt, those who deny place must reduce it to the category of position: substance and the remaining accidents certainly do not fit it.

The objection to this is that a body may move from place to place without any change in its substance or in its other accidents apart from where; conversely, a change in position may occur without locomotion. Place is therefore distinct from all of them, including position.

The view that place is prime matter or form does not fit the preceding marks. Place is sought through motion and left through motion, whereas neither prime matter nor form is sought or left through motion. Furthermore, a composite is attributed to its prime matter, as when one says “a wooden door” or “a door made of iron,” but it is not attributed in this way to its place.

The views upon which one can rely are therefore that place is a surface, or that it is a substantial extension separate from matter.

The two parties have lengthy arguments and disputes, set out in the more extensive works.

Among the strongest objections to the view that place is a surface is that it entails a thing’s being at rest and in motion at the same time. A bird remaining stationary in the air and a fish remaining stationary in water must, when air and water flow past them, be in motion, because the surface of air or water surrounding them changes; yet they are necessarily at rest.

Furthermore, place is characterized as empty or full, and this characterizes an extension, not a surface.

Among the strongest objections to the view that place is a substantial extension separate from matter is that it entails the interpenetration of two magnitudes, which is impossible. For it involves a body, with its individual magnitude extending in three dimensions, occupying a place that is an individual magnitude equal to it, and the two becoming one individual magnitude. There is no doubt about the impossibility of this—unless it is denied on the ground that what prevents interpenetration may be prime matter together with magnitude, or form together with magnitude, or both together with it.

Second discussion: the point at issue

You have learned that where is a configuration acquired by a thing through its relation to place. The discussion concerns its being a configuration acquired through a relation, rather than the existence of the relation itself, just as in the preceding discussion of relation.

Third discussion: the divisions of where

Where is sometimes divided into primary and real, and secondary and nonreal. The first is a thing’s being in the place proper to it, which cannot accommodate anything else together with it, as when water is in a jug. The second is exemplified by saying, “So-and-so is in the room.” The room is not occupied by him alone, but accommodates him and others. More remote still are his being in the house, then in the town, and so on. This is not a real division; what is divided is where as understood in the extended usage of ordinary speech.

Closely related is its division into generic where, which is being in place; specific where, such as being in the air; and individual where, such as this individual’s being at this time in his real place.

N-VI-18 · When
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 170–172
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=170&type=c_fbook

When is the configuration acquired by a thing through its relation to time.

It will be explained, God willing, that every motion, by virtue of its flowing, gradual existence, has a nonstable measure proper to it and different from the nonstable extension belonging to any other motion. Every particular motion that is numerically one therefore has a particular time that is numerically one. Some of these times, however, admit coincidence with others. The continuous general time by which we measure motions is the time of the daily motion, adopted as a standard by which we measure times and motions. Through it, their relations to one another in priority and posteriority, length and shortness, are determined. Events acquire, in accordance with their relation to time, a configuration that is when.

The discussion of when closely resembles that of where. There is a when proper to a motion which does not accommodate another together with it: this is primary and real when. There is also a when that encompasses this motion and others, such as this motion’s occurring in such-and-such an hour, day, month, year, century, and so on.

The difference between where and when in this respect is that many things share one particular time by coinciding with it, whereas one particular where accommodates only one body.

When admits a division corresponding to the division of temporal events. Some have gradual existence and coincide with time itself. Others have instantaneous existence and are related to a limit of time, such as arrivals, contacts, and separations.

As has also been said, when is divided into intrinsic and incidental. Intrinsic when belongs to motions, which coincide with time through themselves. Incidental when belongs to moving things, which coincide with it through their motions; in respect of the substance of their own essence, they have no when. This is based on the denial of substantial motion. If substantial motion is affirmed, however—as will be explained, God willing—there is no difference between motion and the moving thing in this respect.

When is also divided in accordance with the division of the categories in which motions occur.

N-VI-19 · Position
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 172–173
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=172&type=c_fbook

Position is the configuration that a thing acquires from the relation of some of its parts to others and of the whole to what is external, as in the configurations of standing, sitting, lying on one’s back, and lying face down.

Position is divided into what is natural and what is not natural. An example of natural position is a tree’s resting upon its root and trunk; an example of what is not natural is the situation of the inhabitant of a house in relation to the house.

It is also divided into actual and potential position.

It has been said that position admits contrariety, intensity, and weakness. An example of contrariety is the position of a human being with the head toward the sky and the feet toward the ground, as contrary to that person’s position when inverted. These two positions are positive attributes that succeed one another in a single subject, cannot coexist in it, and stand at the utmost opposition to one another. The same applies to lying on one’s back and lying face down. Examples of intensity are being more upright or more bent.

There is some obscurity in conceiving utmost opposition in position. Let this be considered.

Notice

“Position” has two other meanings besides its categorical meaning.

The first is a thing’s being susceptible to sensible indication. Indication, as reported from the Healing, is the determination of the direction belonging to a thing among the directions of this world. Accordingly, every body and bodily thing admits position in this sense. Thus a point has position, whereas a unit does not.

The second is a meaning more restricted than the first: a quantity’s being susceptible to sensible indication, such that one may ask, “Where is it among the directions, and where are some of its continuous parts in relation to others?”

This has been disputed on the ground that a line and a surface, and indeed a mathematical body, have no “where” apart from their attachment to corporeal matter. Mere quantitative continuity, therefore, does not suffice to make something susceptible to sensible indication unless it is joined to matter.

An imaginal form divested of quantity nevertheless admits an imaginal indication; an intellectual form likewise admits an indication appropriate to it.

[Critical English collation · 1428 printed digital witness] In «نعم للصورة الخياليّة المجرّدة من الكمّ إشارة خياليّة» the prepositional phrase «من الكمّ» depends on «المجرّدة»: the imaginal form is divested of quantity, not an 'imaginal form of quantity'. The former translation misattached the phrase and inverted the point. Corrected against Qum 1417 text and independently compared with the 1428 AH revised vol. 1 p. 228 transcription; no manuscript attestation is claimed.

N-VI-20 · Possession
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 173–174
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=173&type=c_fbook

This is also called “ownership.” It is the configuration arising from one thing’s encompassing another in such a way that the encompassing thing moves when the encompassed thing moves.

The subject is the encompassed thing. Complete encompassing is exemplified by an animal’s skin encompassing it; incomplete encompassing is exemplified by wearing a shirt, shoes, or a ring, and similar cases.

Possession is divided into natural possession, as in the first example, and nonnatural possession, as in the other examples.

In the Asfār it is said: “Ownership is sometimes expressed as the category of ‘having.’ Some of it is natural, as the faculties’ belonging to the soul, and some is an external consideration, as a horse’s belonging to Zayd. Ownership in fact differs from the present technical usage, for it belongs solely to the category of relation.” End of quotation.

The truth is that real ownership, as in the faculties’ belonging to the soul, is an existential aspect: the existence of one thing subsists through another in such a way that it belongs exclusively to the latter, which disposes of it however it wishes. It is therefore not a categorical meaning. Conventional ownership, as in a horse’s belonging to Zayd, is an intellectually instituted counterpart of real ownership, rather than the category of relation. God willing, we shall refer to this discussion in the stage concerning the intellective subject and the intelligible.

N-VI-21 · The two categories of acting and being acted upon
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 174–176
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=174&type=c_fbook

The first is a nonstable configuration arising in an agent from its producing an effect for as long as it produces that effect, as in the heating performed by what heats for as long as it heats, or the cooling performed by what cools for as long as it cools.

The second is a nonstable configuration arising in the recipient of an effect for as long as it is affected, as in the becoming hot of what is heated for as long as it becomes hot, or the becoming cold of what is cooled for as long as it becomes cold.

Among the distinctive features of these two categories is, first, that, as the examples show, they occur in other categories, such as quality, quantity, position, and others.

Second, their subject, insofar as it is their subject, is never devoid of motion. For this reason they are expressed by “acting” and “being acted upon,” which suggest motion and gradual occurrence, rather than by “action” and “affection,” which are sometimes applied to instantaneous, nongradual influence and reception of influence.

In general, the two categories are configurations that occur in their subject as aspects of the motion it possesses.

In the Asfār it is said: “Know that the external existence of either of them does not consist in the very progression toward a degree, for that is precisely what motion means. Nor is the existence of either of them the existence of the categories through which causing motion and undergoing motion occur: quality, such as blackness; quantity, such as the magnitude of a growing body; position, such as sitting and standing upright; or anything else of this sort.

“Rather, their existence consists in the existence of something belonging to these categories for as long as it produces or receives an effect. The existence of blackness or heat, for example, insofar as it is blackness, belongs to the category of quality. The existence of either of them insofar as it occurs gradually and produces something else that occurs gradually, or arises from something else that occurs gradually, belongs to the category of acting or being acted upon.

“As for its gradual progression itself—that is, its passage from potentiality to actuality—whether on the side of the agent or on the side of the recipient, it is precisely motion and nothing else. Thus the manner of their external existence, and their being accidents, have been established.” End of quotation.

An objection to the existence of these two categories runs as follows. The influence exerted by an agent cannot be a positive attribute additional to the agent itself. Otherwise it would require a further influence upon that influence; transferring the argument to this further influence yields a regress extending without end, although it is enclosed between two limits: the agent and the recipient.

A parallel objection applies if the recipient’s reception of an effect is additional to the recipient itself. If receiving the effect were additional to the recipient itself, it would require another reception. Transferring the argument to this further reception produces a regress, again enclosed between two limits. Producing and receiving an effect, whether instantaneous or gradual, must therefore be privative attributes that do not exist externally.

The answer is that this argument succeeds only if the positive effect hypothesized has a separate existence requiring a new, separate influence peculiar to it.

If, however, it is established through the establishment of another thing, it is produced by the very production that concerns the thing it follows. Gradual influence and the gradual reception of influence exist through the very bringing into existence of the quality—for example, blackness in what blackens and what becomes black. No proof of a thing’s existence is stronger than the truth of its concept of an external entity in an external proposition.


Unity and Multiplicity

N-VII-01 · The concepts of unity and multiplicity are self-evident and need no definition
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 179–181
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=179&type=c_fbook

An existent is divided into the one and the many: every existent is either one or many. The truth is that unity and multiplicity are among the general concepts whose conception is self-evident and which need no definition, like necessity and possibility. This is why the definitions offered for them involve either circularity or defining a thing through itself. Thus the one is defined as “that which is not divided in the respect in which it is said to be one.” This includes division, which is multiplicity, in the definition of the one, besides defining the one through the one. The many is then defined as “that which is assembled from units.” This includes unity in the definition of the many, although multiplicity had already been included in the definition of the one, and so is circular. It also defines the many through an assemblage, which is precisely the many.

The truth, then, is that the definitions given for them are verbal definitions, intended to draw attention to their meanings and distinguish them from among the meanings already stored in the soul.

The one is “that which is not divided, insofar as it is not divided.” The qualification “insofar as” allows this to include the non-genuine one, which is divisible in some respects. The many is “that which is divided, insofar as it is divided.”

It has thus become clear that an existent is divided into the one and the many, and that these are two distinct meanings, distinct as one member of a division is from the other.

Notice

They say, “Unity is coextensive with existence.” Every existent is therefore one insofar as it is existent. Even an existent multiplicity, insofar as it exists, is one multiplicity. The fact that number is predicated of it bears witness to this, since number is composed of units. We say one multiplicity, two multiplicities, and three multiplicities; one ten, two tens, and three tens; and so on.

It might be imagined that dividing an existent into the one and the many conflicts with the one’s being coextensive with an existent. For the many, insofar as it is many, is existent, given the division just mentioned; and the many, insofar as it is many, is not one. This yields the conclusion that some existent is not one, contradicting their statement that every existent is one.

The answer is that the one can be considered in two ways. It can be considered in itself, without comparing some of its instances with others; in this consideration it is coextensive with an existent and embraces its instances, both one and many. Or it can be considered by comparing some of its instances with others. There are then instances that do not possess the meaning of indivisibility found in other instances. Ten, for example, does not possess the indivisibility found in one, although it does possess it when compared with tens.

The many that is not one is therefore that which is compared, insofar as it is compared, and what stands opposite it is the one in the second consideration. The one in the first consideration, however, embraces both the one and the many that are the two members of the division.

A parallel is the division of an existent without qualification into the potential and the actual, although the actual is coextensive with an existent without qualification. Another is the division of existence into mental existence and external existence to which effects attach, although external existence to which effects attach is coextensive with existence without qualification.

All of these arise from the differences of gradation belonging to the graded reality of existence.

Similar to the preceding misconception is the thought that unity is one of the meanings abstracted by the intellect: if it were an external reality, it would have a unity, and its unity would have another unity, and so on without end.

The answer is that its unity is identical with its essence. It is one through itself, just as was explained earlier concerning existence: it exists through itself, without needing an existence additional to its essence.

N-VII-02 · The divisions of the one
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 182–183
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=182&type=c_fbook

The one is either genuine or non-genuine. The genuine one is that which is characterized by unity through itself, without an intermediary in the attribution, such as one human being. The non-genuine one is the opposite, such as a human being and a horse, which are united in animality. It necessarily terminates in a genuine one.

The genuine one is either an essence identical with unity or an essence characterized by unity.

The former is a thing in its sheer purity, which admits neither a second nor repetition. Its unity is called “true unity.” Here the one and unity are a single thing. The latter is exemplified by “one human being.”

That whose unity is other than true unity is either one in particular or one in general.

The former is the numerically one, whose repetition produces number. The latter is exemplified by one species and one genus.

The one in particular either is indivisible also with respect to the nature to which unity is attributed, just as it is indivisible with respect to its attribute of unity, or is divisible with respect to that nature.

The former is either the very concept of unity and indivisibility or something other than it. Something other than it is either positional, such as one point, or non-positional, such as an immaterial being. An immaterial being either has some connection with matter, such as the soul, which is connected with matter in its activity, or has no connection with matter at all, such as an intellect.

The latter—what admits division with respect to the nature to which unity is attributed—either admits it intrinsically, as does one magnitude, or incidentally, as does one natural body on account of its magnitude.

The one in general is either one through conceptual generality or one through generality in the sense of existential comprehensiveness. The first is either specifically one, such as human being, generically one, such as animal, or accidentally one, such as walking and laughing. The one through generality in the sense of existential comprehensiveness is exemplified by all-pervading existence.

The non-genuine one is that which is characterized by unity incidentally through something else, because it has some manner of union with it. Examples are Zayd and ʿAmr, united in humanity, and a human being and a horse, united in animality.

The names of the non-genuine one differ according to the respect of unity. Union in the meaning of a species is called “likeness”; in the meaning of a genus, “generic sameness”; in quality, “similarity”; in quantity, “equality”; and in position, “parallelism” and “coincidence.”

The existence of each of these divisions is evident. So too is the fact that unity applies to its divisions as a graded meaning applies to its different instances. This is how they have set out the matter.

N-VII-03 · Identity is a consequence of unity, and otherness a consequence of multiplicity
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 183–185
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=183&type=c_fbook

Identity is among the concomitants of unity, just as otherness is among the concomitants of multiplicity.

By identity is meant unity in some respect together with difference in some respect. It follows that predication is valid between any two different things that possess some unity, although customary usage restricts predication to certain kinds of unity.

An objection has been raised: if predication is valid wherever two different things possess some unity, it should be valid in the case of a single continuous magnitude, which has many potential parts existing through one actual existence. Some of its parts could then be predicated of others, and some of its parts of the whole, and conversely. We could say, “This half of the cubit is the other half,” “This half is the whole,” or “Its whole is its half.” The invalidity of these statements is self-evident.

The answer, as Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified—explained, is that so long as a single continuum has not been divided in any manner, externally or mentally, no multiplicity whatsoever has been realized in it. The condition of predication, namely some unity together with some multiplicity, is therefore absent, and there is no predication. Once it has been divided in some manner, its single identity is abolished, and the continuity constituting its unity ceases. The condition of predication, namely some multiplicity together with some unity, is again absent, and there is no predication.

It has thus become clear that predication obtains between any two things that differ in one respect and are united in another, provided that the unity coexists with the difference. Ordinary usage, however, as we have indicated, confines predication to two cases of unity together with difference.

The first is where subject and predicate are united in concept while differing through some consideration. An example is the difference between what is undifferentiated and what is detailed in our statement, “The human being is a rational animal.” A definition is conceptually identical with what it defines; they differ only in being undifferentiated and detailed. Another is the difference between indeterminacy and its absence in our statement, “The human being is an animal,” for the genus is the species in an indeterminate condition. Another is the difference between determination and its absence in our statement, “The human being is rational,” for the differentia is the species as determined, as was explained in the discussions of quiddity. There is also the difference introduced by supposing a thing to be denied of itself, so that its self differs from its self, and then predicating it of itself to dispel the imagined difference. Thus one says, for example, “The human being is a human being.”

Since this predication is sometimes considered in external existence, it is more appropriate to define it as the unity of subject and predicate in essence. This predication is called “primary essential predication.”

The second is where subject and predicate differ in concept but are united in existence, as in our statements, “Zayd is a human being,” “Cotton is white,” and “One who laughs is one who wonders.”

This predication is called “common predication.”

There is a point here to which attention must be drawn. It was explained in earlier discussions that existence is divided into existence in itself and existence in another. It is also divided into existence for itself and existence for another, the latter being attributive existence. It was also explained that two quiddities cannot exist through one existence in itself: one existence cannot exclude nonexistence from the very selves of two distinct quiddities. That would be the unity of a multiplicity, which reason judges impossible.

It follows that predication, which is the unity in some respect of two different things, is not realized in their respective existences in themselves. It is realized only in attributive existence, when one of the two, through its existence, qualifies the other, and the other is qualified by it.

In other words, one of the two different things is the subject in its existence in itself, and the other is the attribute in its existence in itself. Their unity lies in the attributive existence that the attribute bestows upon the subject.

This is what the logicians mean when they say, “A proposition resolves into two nexuses: the subject nexus, in which only the subject itself is considered, while any attribute contained in it is merely a designation pointing to that subject; and the predicate nexus, in which only the attribute is considered.”

There is a third kind of predication employed by the philosopher, called “the predication of reality and its attenuated manifestation.” It rests on the unity of subject and predicate in the root of existence and their difference in perfection and deficiency. It signifies that the deficient exists in the perfect in a higher and nobler manner, and that a higher degree of existence contains the perfection of the degrees beneath it.

N-VII-04 · Predication as “is it” and as “has it”
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 185–187
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=185&type=c_fbook

Predication is divided into predication as “is it” and predication as “has it.”

The first is that in which the predicate is established of the subject without any need to consider an additional factor, as when we say, “The human being is one who laughs.” It is also called “predication by agreement.”

The second is that in which establishing the predicate of the subject requires considering an additional factor, such as supplying the word “possessor” or using a derivative expression. An example is our saying, “Zayd is justice,” meaning “possesses justice” or “is just.”

Predication is also divided into definitive and non-definitive.

The first is that whose subject has actual individuals to which its designation applies, as in our statements, “The human being is a writer,” and “The writer is one who moves his fingers.”

The second is that whose subject has supposed rather than actual individuals, as in our statements, “The absolutely nonexistent cannot be spoken of,” and “The conjunction of contradictories is impossible.”

Predication is also divided into simple and compound, called respectively the “simple existential proposition” and the “compound existential proposition.”

A simple existential proposition is one whose predicate is the existence of the subject, as when we say, “The human being exists.”

A compound existential proposition is one whose predicate is one of the subject’s effects or accidental attributes, as when we say, “The human being is one who laughs.” It signifies the establishment of something for something, whereas a simple existential proposition signifies the establishment of the thing itself.

This removes an objection some have raised against the universality of the principle of presupposition, which states that “the establishment of something for something presupposes the establishment of that for which it is established.” They maintain that the principle is contradicted by a statement such as “Quiddity exists.” According to the principle of presupposition, the establishment of existence for quiddity presupposes the establishment of quiddity. We then transfer the discussion to that establishment: it in turn presupposes the prior establishment of quiddity, and so on without end, yielding an infinite regress.

The answer, on the basis of what has been established, is that the proposition is a simple existential proposition. A simple existential proposition signifies only the establishment of the thing, not the establishment of something for something, such that it would require quiddity to exist before its existence.

Some have answered the objection by replacing presupposition with entailment. They say that the correct principle is that the establishment of something for something entails the establishment of that for which it is established, even if through this very establishment; thus the establishment of existence for quiddity entails the establishment of quiddity through this very establishment. This, however, concedes the objection.

Still less satisfactory is the statement of some that the principle admits an exception in the case of the establishment of existence for quiddity.

N-VII-05 · Otherness and its divisions
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 187–189
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=187&type=c_fbook

It was explained that otherness is among the concomitants of multiplicity. Otherness is divided into essential and nonessential.

Essential otherness occurs when one of two things excludes the other through its very essence, so that the two cannot coexist because of their essences. An example is the otherness of existence and nonexistence. It is called “opposition.”

Opposition has been defined as “the impossibility of two things’ coexistence in one substrate, in one respect, at one time.” The impossibility of coexistence is attributed to the two things to indicate that it is due to their very essences. By “one substrate” is meant a subject in the broadest sense, even if merely supposed by reason, so that the definition includes the opposition of affirmation and negation: the content of a proposition serves as a subject for them. The qualification “in one respect” excludes cases in which the two coexist in one thing in two different respects, as when Zayd is a father to ʿAmr and a son to Bakr. The qualification concerning one time accommodates opposition involving time. The occurrence of two contraries in one subject at two different times therefore does not invalidate the definition.

The definition is not contradicted by two like things, whose coexistence is rationally impossible. One of two like things does not exclude the other through its essence, namely the specific quiddity common to them. Their coexistence is impossible only because one existence cannot be repeated, as was explained in the discussions of existence.

Nor is the definition contradicted by the contradictory of a consequent together with the antecedent itself. The contradictory of the consequent opposes the antecedent itself only because it opposes the consequent, which is its contradictory. Its coexistence with the antecedent is therefore impossible incidentally, through its contradictory, and not through itself.

Nonessential otherness occurs when two things do not coexist for reasons other than their very essences, as in the separation of sweetness and blackness in sugar and charcoal. This is called “difference.”

“Other” is also said with regard to individuation and number.

Opposition is divided into four kinds: the opposition of contradiction, of privation and possession, of correlation, and of contrariety.

The more accurate way to establish these divisions is to say: either one of the two opposites is the absence of the other, or it is not. In the first case, either there is a receptive subject, as with sight and blindness, in which case the opposition is that of privation and possession; or there is none, as with affirmation and negation, in which case the opposition is that of contradiction. In the second case, where both are positive, either one can be conceived only together with the other and in relation to it, as with higher and lower, in which case the opposition is that of correlation; or this is not so, in which case the opposition is that of contrariety.

N-VII-06 · Contradictory opposition
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 189–192
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=189&type=c_fbook

This is the opposition of affirmation and negation, as in our statements “Zayd is white” and “Zayd is not white,” or of simple expressions that have the meaning of affirmation and negation, such as “human being” and “non-human-being,” “blindness” and “non-blindness,” and “nonexistent” and “non-nonexistent.”

Two contradictories cannot both be true and cannot both be false. Or, if you prefer: they cannot both obtain and cannot both fail to obtain. Contradictory opposition thus comes down to a genuine disjunctive proposition: “Either the affirmation is true or the negation is true.” In reality, then, contradiction is between affirmation and negation.

This does not conflict with contradiction occurring between simple expressions. Whenever we take a concept in itself and then add to it the meaning of negation, as with human being and non-human-being, or horse and non-horse, contradiction arises between the two concepts. For when we take two contradictory concepts, such as human being and non-human-being, we have no doubt that the opposition belongs equally to both concepts: human being intrinsically excludes non-human-being, just as non-human-being intrinsically excludes human being. It is self-evident that if establishment and existence were not considered on the side of human being, it would neither exclude nor contradict non-human-being. Human being and non-human-being contradict one another only because they have the meaning of the existence of the human being and the nonexistence of the human being. This is possible only by considering existence as belonging to the human being, and likewise nonexistence. Human being and non-human-being therefore contradict one another because they resolve into two simple existential propositions: “The human being exists” and “The human being does not exist.”

A parallel account applies to the contradictories “Zayd’s standing” and “Zayd’s not standing.” They have the meaning of the existence of standing for Zayd and the nonexistence of standing for Zayd, and resolve into two compound existential propositions: “Zayd is standing” and “Zayd is not standing.” Contradictory opposition is therefore in reality between affirmation and negation, or, if you prefer, between existence and nonexistence. It will nevertheless be explained in the discussions of the intellective subject and the intelligible object, God willing, that the intellect first apprehends the concept of existence as a dependent meaning within propositions. It then forms an independent nominal meaning from this by transforming it and taking independently what was previously a connective. It conceives of nonexistence on the model of what it has done with existence. Contradictory opposition thus belongs primarily and intrinsically to affirmation and negation, and to other things incidentally through them.

Accordingly, statements that attribute contradiction to propositions—for example, the statement in the Tajrīd, “The opposition of negation and affirmation refers to utterance and judgment”—mean negation and affirmation insofar as they bear upon one and the same propositional content.

It has also become clear that in their statement, “The contradictory of each thing is its removal,” removal means intrinsic exclusion: affirmation and negation each intrinsically exclude what opposes it. Some, however, interpret removal as negation and denial, explicitly maintaining that the contradictory of human being is non-human-being, and that the contradictory of non-human-being is non-non-human-being, while human being is a necessary concomitant of the contradictory rather than the contradictory itself. Their interpretation would entail that contradictory opposition is invariably one-sided, which is self-evidently false.

One judgment concerning contradictory opposition is that the opposition between two contradictories occurs in the mind, or, by a kind of figurative usage, in language. Opposition is a relation subsisting in two terms, whereas one term of a contradictory pair is nonexistence, and nonexistence is an intellectual consideration with no external instance. This differs from the opposition of privation and possession. As will be explained, God willing, the nonexistence in the latter is nonexistence referred to something existent, and therefore has a share in existence. The opposition there genuinely subsists in two existent terms.

Another judgment concerning this opposition is that there can be no intermediate between things opposed in this way. Nothing is without one or the other of the two contradictories being true of it. Anything posited is either Zayd, for example, or is not Zayd; it is either white or is not white; and so forth. Any two contradictories taken together thus encompass all things.

Another judgment is that the two contradictories cannot both be true or both be false. As already indicated, this takes the form of the genuine disjunctive proposition, “Either the affirmation is true or the negation is true.” This is a primary, self-evident proposition upon which the truth of every proposition depends, whether that proposition is immediately evident or requires investigation. For example, “Four is even” is true only if “Four is not even” is false. “The world has come to be” is true only if “The world has not come to be” is false. For this reason the proposition asserting the impossibility of two contradictories both obtaining or both failing to obtain is called “the first of first principles.”

For this same reason, doubt about the truth of this genuine disjunctive proposition destroys knowledge of any proposition whatsoever. Knowledge that a proposition is true occurs only when its contradictory is known to be false. Doubt about this genuine disjunction entails doubt about the falsity of the contradictory, and hence doubt about the truth of the other contradictory. Doubt about it therefore annihilates all knowledge and undermines it at its root. Human nature itself rejects this. The sophist’s professed doubt is a claim that never goes beyond words. A detailed discussion of this will follow.

N-VII-07 · The opposition of privation and possession
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 192–193
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=192&type=c_fbook

This is also called the opposition of privation and having. Its terms are an existent feature that occurs in a subject naturally capable of possessing it, and the absence of that existent feature in that subject. An example is sight and blindness, the latter being the lack of sight in a subject naturally capable of seeing.

Whether the subject of the possession is taken as an individual nature, a specific nature, or a generic nature makes no difference to the occurrence of this opposition. A generic nature, and likewise a specific nature, is a subject of an individual’s attribute just as the individual itself is its subject. Thus the lack of sight in a scorpion—as has been said—is blindness and a privation, since its genus, animal, is naturally capable of seeing, even if the specific nature of scorpion is not characterized by sight. Likewise, a human being’s beardlessness and lack of facial hair before puberty is a privation, since the specific nature of the human being has that capacity, even though the class of those who have not reached puberty does not possess a beard. The opposition of privation and possession in this unrestricted sense is called “genuine.”

It is sometimes restricted by time, with the requirement that the privation occur at the time appropriate to the possession. In that case the opposition is called “commonly recognized.” On this usage, a human being’s beardlessness before puberty is not a privation at all, and likewise the scorpion’s lack of sight is not blindness.

This is closer to a terminological convention. There is therefore no difficulty if cases whose subject is the genus or species fall outside privation and possession on this usage without falling under any of the other three oppositions, although the kinds of opposition are confined to four.

[Later editor’s factual caution · not authorial] The author gives the inherited example of the scorpion’s supposed lack of sight («فعدم البصر في العقرب ـ كما قيل ـ») to illustrate privation relative to an animal genus. The 1428 AH editor, vol. 1 printed p. 258 n. 4, notes that some modern descriptions report scorpion eyes. The original statement is an expressly qualified historical logical illustration, not a reliable zoological observation. The translated argument and the author/editor distinction are preserved.

N-VII-08 · Correlative opposition
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 193–194
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=193&type=c_fbook

As the division has shown, two correlatives are two existent features, one of which cannot be conceived without conceiving the other. They stand in a reciprocal relation: one cannot be conceived without conceiving the other, which is itself conceived through the first. For this reason they cannot occur together in one thing in the same respect, since a relation cannot run between a thing and itself.

An objection has been raised against treating correlation as one of the four kinds of opposition: opposition in general is itself a kind of correlation, since two opposites, insofar as they are opposites, are correlatives. Counting correlation as a kind of opposition would therefore amount to making something a coordinate division alongside one of its own subdivisions.

The reply is that the concept of opposition is an instance of correlation, whereas an instance of correlation is one of the kinds and instances of opposition. What falls under correlation is the concept of opposition; what stands alongside correlation as a coordinate division is an instance of opposition. A mental concept is often an individual instance of that to which it is opposed. The concept of the particular, for example, is in one respect an instance of the universal and in another respect opposed to it. There is therefore no difficulty.

One rule concerning correlation is that the two correlatives correspond necessarily in respect of existence and nonexistence, and likewise of potentiality and actuality. If one exists, the other necessarily exists; if one does not exist, the other necessarily does not exist; and if one is potential or actual, the other necessarily is so as well. It follows that they are simultaneous: neither precedes the other, whether in the mind or externally.

[English precision · corrected against authorial Arabic] For «المتضايفان متكافئان وجوداً وعدماً وقوّةً وفعلا», ‘correspond necessarily in respect of existence, nonexistence, potentiality, and actuality’ replaces the misleading ‘coextensive’ (which suggests identity of extension). The author argues existential and modal simultaneity of opposed correlatives, not identity of their sets of instances. The corrected 1428 AH transcription (vol. 1 p. 259) agrees at this clause. This is a translation correction, not an Arabic emendation or manuscript attestation.

N-VII-09 · Contrary opposition
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 194–198
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=194&type=c_fbook

You have learned that the result of the preceding division is that contraries are two existent things that are not correlatives and that do not coexist in one locus, at one time, and in one respect.

It is reported that the ancients were content with this much in defining contrariety. They therefore allowed contrariety to occur between substances and allowed its terms to number more than two.

The Peripatetics, however, added further qualifications to what follows from the division. They described contraries as “two existent things that are not correlatives, that succeed one another in a single subject, that fall under a proximate genus, and between which there is the utmost difference.”

For them, therefore, contrariety is confined to two ultimate species of accidents falling under one proximate genus and differing to the utmost. Contrariety between more than two terms is impossible.
