To explain: every quiddity—indeed, every concept—is in itself separate from any other concept whatsoever. This has nothing to do with contrariety, although the negation of the other concept is true of it. Likewise, every complete species, with its external existence and external effects, is distinct from other complete species, with their own external existence and that of their effects. They do not coincide in such a way that the existence peculiar to one species, in excluding its own nonexistence, excludes the nonexistence of some particular other species. This, too, has nothing to do with opposition or contrariety.

Contrariety, which is opposition between two existent things, requires instead that each thing exclude the other through its quiddity, be directed toward it, and resist coexistence with it.

This entails, first, a third thing for which both exist and with which both are united. That for which an existent thing exists and with which it is united is a subject in the unrestricted sense, encompassing both the substrate of a substance and the subject of an accident. Yet contrariety does not occur among substances, as will be explained. The two things must therefore be two accidents possessing a single subject.

Second, because each of the two species is directed toward the other, they must mutually exclude one another, each excluding the other through its differentia, which constitutes the completeness of its specific nature. A differentia excludes another differentia only if both divide one genus; that is, the two species must fall under one proximate genus. Understand this point.

The objection cannot be raised that, since the differentia is part of the quiddity, it has no independent judgment and that the judgment belongs to the species. The differentia is the very species in its determination, and its judgment is precisely the judgment of the species. Moreover, contrariety does not occur between the highest genera constituted by the ten categories, since more than one of them can coexist in a single locus. Quantity, quality, and the other accidents, for example, coexist in one corporeal substance. The same applies to some intermediate genera falling under one category in relation to others falling under another, and likewise to ultimate species under one category in relation to certain ultimate species under another.

Induction thus shows that contrariety occurs only between two species falling under a proximate genus of the accidental categories: blackness and whiteness, for example, which they count among visible qualities, and rashness and cowardice among psychic qualities.

As for the requirement of the utmost difference between contraries, they initially judged certain things to be contraries and subsequently encountered things intermediate between them whose character was relative. Between the contraries blackness and whiteness, for example, lie yellow, red, and green. In relation to blackness these belong to whiteness, and in relation to whiteness they belong to blackness. Similarly, courage lies between rashness and cowardice. They therefore required a contrary to differ to the utmost and to stand at the greatest distance from its contrary.

This is why they denied contrariety among substances. Among substantial species there is no relative species measured against two extremes, nor are there two extreme species differing to the utmost.

One judgment concerning contrariety is that it cannot occur between more than two terms. It is an opposition, and opposition is a relation; a single relation cannot obtain between more than two terms. This judgment applies generally to all kinds of opposition.

It is said in the Asfār: “Among the judgments concerning contrariety, given our requirement of the utmost distance, is that one thing has only one contrary. On this account, a contrary is that whose existence entails the nonexistence of the other contrary. If a thing is unitary and has several contraries, their opposition to it must be either in one respect or in several respects. If they oppose it in one respect, what is contrary to that thing is in reality a single thing and a single contrary, although several contraries were posited. If they oppose it in several respects, the thing does not possess a simple reality. It is instead like a human being, who is contrary to the hot insofar as he is cold, contrary to the cold insofar as he is hot, and contrary to many things because he contains their contraries.

“Real contrariety is therefore only between heat and coldness, or blackness and whiteness, and each term has one contrary. As for a hot thing and a cold thing, the contrariety between them is incidental.” End of quotation.

Another judgment is that contraries succeed one another in the subject, owing to the requirement of the utmost difference between them, whether or not there is an intermediate or several intermediates that, in relation to either side, belong to the other side. Its consequence is that the subject is not devoid of both together, whether they alternate in it one after the other or one contrary necessarily accompanies its existence, as whiteness accompanies snow and blackness accompanies pitch.

Another judgment is that the subject in which they succeed one another must be one in a particular sense, rather than one in a general sense. There is no impossibility in two contraries existing in two subjects, even when those subjects are one in species or genus.

Conclusion

They have disagreed over the mutual incompatibility of the one and the many, which cannot coexist in one thing in one respect. Is it an intrinsic opposition? If so, is it one of the four kinds of opposition, or a fifth kind beyond the four mentioned? If it belongs to the four, is it correlative opposition or contrary opposition? Each of these possibilities has its adherents, as detailed in the longer works.

The truth is that it does not belong to opposition in the technical sense at all. The basis of such opposition is essential otherness: the two opposing things exclude and repel one another through their very essences. Essential difference and incompatibility cannot possibly resolve into unity and concord. The one and the many are not like this. They are two divisions of the existent insofar as it is existent, and it has already been established that unity is coextensive with existence. Every existent, insofar as it is existent, is one, just as every one, insofar as it is one, is existent.

The one and the many are thus each an instance of the one. In other words, that by which the one and the many differ resolves into that by which they are united. This is characteristic of gradation, rather than opposition.

Unity and multiplicity accordingly belong to the gradation of existence. Through them existence divides into one and many, while the one remains coextensive with the existent without qualification. In the same way, existence divides into external and mental existence, while external existence is coextensive with existence without qualification; and it divides into what is actual and what is potential, while what is actual is coextensive with existence without qualification.

Furthermore, none of the four kinds of opposition, with their respective properties, applies to the one and the many. In contradiction and in privation and possession, one opposing term is the nonexistence of the other, whereas both the one and the many are existent. Correlatives are coextensive in existence, nonexistence, potentiality, and actuality, but the one and the many do not have this character. Contraries differ to the utmost, but the one and the many do not. Whatever numerical multiplicity is set against numerical unity, there is a greater multiplicity, more distant from unity, since number is infinite. There is therefore none of the four oppositions between the one and the many. Since the division is exhaustive, there is no opposition between them at all.


Cause and Effect

N-VIII-01 · Establishing causality and being an effect, and that they concern existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 201–203
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=201&type=c_fbook

It was established earlier that quiddity, considered in itself, is neither existent nor nonexistent. Its relation to existence and nonexistence is therefore equal. For either side to prevail for it, it needs something other than itself, external to its essence. Sound reason declares it impossible that either side should prevail without a determinant, whether from its own essence or from something else. You also learned earlier that saying that it needs something else for its nonexistence involves a certain figurative usage. What this really means is that the absence of the other thing needed for its existence is inseparable from the absence of its existence, because its existence depends on the existence of that other thing. Plainly, this dependence is upon the existence of the other thing, since what is nonexistent has no reality as a thing. We call this existence upon which something depends a “cause,” and the thing that depends upon the cause its “effect.”

Now what the cause produces, the influence it places in the effect, must be either the effect’s existence, its quiddity, or its quiddity’s becoming existent. But what is produced cannot be the quiddity, because, as established earlier, quiddity is intellectually considered, whereas what the effect receives from its cause is something fundamental. Moreover, causality and being an effect constitute a particular objective connection between the effect and its cause. Otherwise, everything would be the cause of everything, and everything the effect of everything. Quiddity, in its own essence, has no connection with anything else.

Nor can what is produced be the becoming, for in that case the fundamental objective influence would be the becoming, which is a relational affair subsisting in two terms, while the quiddity and its existence would, on the hypothesis, both be intellectually considered. It is impossible for something fundamental and objective to subsist in two intellectually considered terms.

Since what is produced cannot be either the quiddity or the becoming, it must be existence. This is what was to be demonstrated.

The foregoing establishes, first, that there are cause and effect. Second, every contingent thing is an effect. Third, causality and being an effect are an existential connection between an effect and its cause, and this connection obtains between the effect’s existence and the cause’s existence. Although dependence, need, and poverty are sometimes attributed to quiddity, the primary locus of need and poverty is the effect’s existence; its quiddity is needy in consequence of that existence.

Fourth, since need and poverty belong primarily to the existence that is an effect, and this existence is needy in its very essence—otherwise need would be accidental to it and it would be independent in its essence, whereas being an effect is incompatible with independence—the very essence of the effect’s existence is need itself. That is, it is not independent in itself but subsists through its cause, which bestows it.

It follows that the effect’s existence, when measured against its cause, is relational existence, existing in another. When considered in relation to its quiddity, from which it excludes nonexistence, it is existence in itself, substantial or accidental according to what the character of its quiddity requires.

N-VIII-02 · The divisions of cause
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 204–205
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=204&type=c_fbook

Cause is divided into complete and incomplete. A complete cause comprises everything upon which the effect depends, so that, with it, nothing remains for the effect but to be realized. An incomplete cause comprises some, but not all, of what the effect depends upon for its realization.

They differ in that the existence of a complete cause entails the existence of the effect, as will be explained, and its nonexistence entails the effect’s nonexistence. The existence of an incomplete cause does not entail the effect’s existence, but its nonexistence does entail the effect’s nonexistence, because the effect depends upon it as well as upon something else. It should be understood that the nonexistence of the cause, whether complete or incomplete, is a complete cause of the effect’s nonexistence.

Cause is also divided into one and many, because the effect is among the concomitants of the cause’s existence, and a concomitant may be more general.

It is also divided into simple and composite. A simple cause has no parts, and a composite cause is the opposite. A cause may be simple in external reality, as are an intellect and accidents, or simple in intellectual analysis, having neither an external composition of matter and form nor an intellectual composition of genus and differentia. The simplest of simple beings is that which has no composition of existence and quiddity: the Necessary Existent, exalted is He.

Cause is also divided into proximate and remote. A proximate cause has no intermediary between itself and its effect; a remote cause has an intermediary between itself and its effect, as does the cause of a cause.

It is also divided into internal and external. The internal causes are matter in relation to what is composed of it and form—matter being that through which the thing is potential—and form in relation to the composite—form being that through which the thing is actual. They are called the “two causes of constitution.” The external causes are the agent, from which the effect proceeds, and the end, for the sake of which the effect proceeds. They are called the “two causes of existence.” Their explanation will follow.

Cause is also divided into genuine causes and preparatory causes. Preparatory causes bring matter closer to the agent’s bestowal by preparing it to receive that bestowal, as the passing of temporal intervals brings matter closer to the occurrence of the events that occur in it.

N-VIII-03 · The necessity of the effect’s existence when its complete cause exists, and of the cause’s existence when its effect exists
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 205–214
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=205&type=c_fbook

This is necessity relative to another, distinct from the necessity through another discussed earlier in the proposition, “A thing does not exist unless it becomes necessary.”

As for the necessity of the effect’s existence when its complete cause exists: if its existence were not necessary when its complete cause exists, its nonexistence would be admissible. If its nonexistence were supposed together with the existence of the complete cause, either the cause of its nonexistence—namely, the nonexistence of its cause—would be realized while the cause of its existence existed, or it would not. In the first case, two contradictories would coexist: the cause of existence and its nonexistence. In the second, the effect’s nonexistence would be realized without a cause. This is impossible.

Likewise, if the effect’s nonexistence were not necessary when its cause did not exist, its existence would be admissible. Suppose it existed while the cause of its nonexistence—namely, the nonexistence of the cause of existence—was realized. If the cause of existence existed, two contradictories would coexist: the cause of existence and its nonexistence, which is the cause of nonexistence. If the cause of existence did not exist, the effect would exist while its cause did not.

Another demonstration: the effect’s existence depending upon the cause’s existence entails the impossibility of the effect’s existing without the cause. In other words, the nonexistence of the cause is a cause necessitating the nonexistence of the effect. This effect, namely the effect’s nonexistence, depends upon its cause, namely the cause’s nonexistence. It must therefore be impossible when that cause is absent. This means that the effect’s existence is necessary when its cause exists. Understand this.

You may object: what the contingent thing’s need for a determinant and its existence’s dependence upon a complete cause require is that the existence of the complete cause, in whatever domain it may be, entails the existence of the effect, in whatever domain it may be. They do not require that effect and cause be together in existence without separation in their domain.

Why, then, could the cause not exist, entailing the effect’s existence although the effect did not yet exist, and then cease to exist, with the effect existing some time afterward when no cause existed? Or why could the complete cause not exist while the effect did not yet exist, and then happen to bring the effect into existence and do so? This is especially clear where the complete cause is an agent acting by choice.

I reply: there is no meaning to the intervention of nonexistence between the complete cause’s existence and its effect’s existence, however this is supposed. It was established above that the dependence of the effect’s existence upon the cause’s existence is accomplished only through an objective existential connection. Through this connection, the effect’s existence is relational existence, subsisting in its very essence through the independent existence of the complete cause. Supposing the effect to exist in a domain in which its complete cause is nonexistent therefore supposes relational existence to be realized without an independent existent accompanying and sustaining it. This is an evident contradiction.

Supposing the complete cause to exist while its effect does not yet exist supposes an independent existent that actually sustains another, but has as yet no relational existent to sustain. This is an evident contradiction.

As for choice, some have supposed that an agent acting by choice, such as a human being in relation to voluntary acts, is a cause whose relation to acting and refraining is equal. It can therefore give preponderance to whichever of the two it wishes without necessitating it, because its relation to them is equal.

This is mistaken. The human being acting by choice is not a complete cause of the act, but an incomplete cause. The act has other incomplete causes, such as matter, its presence, the coincidence of the time of its presence with the time of the act, the soundness and responsiveness of the active organs, the motive for the act, the will, and many other things. When these come together, they constitute a complete cause with which the act is necessary. The human being himself is one part of the complete cause. The act’s relation to him is one of possibility, not necessity. The discussion concerns the necessitation exercised by the complete cause, not by cause without qualification.

Moreover, to admit that an agent acting by choice stands in an equal relation to the act and its nonexistence is to deny the causal connection. This entails admitting that everything could be the cause of everything, and everything the effect of everything.

You may object: grant that the human being acting by choice is not a complete cause. The Necessary—exalted is His name—is nevertheless an agent acting by choice, and He is the complete cause of everything other than Himself. The world’s being necessary in relation to Him is incompatible with its temporal origination. For this reason, some have maintained that the act of an agent acting by choice needs no determinant. Others have maintained that will is intrinsically a determinant, with no need for any further determinant. Another group has maintained that the Necessary—exalted is He—knows everything knowable: what He knows to be possible and about to occur, He does; what He knows to be impossible and not to occur, He does not do. Others have maintained that His acts—exalted is He—follow benefits, even though we do not know them: whatever act has a benefit at a particular time, which would be lost if He did not perform it at that time, He performs at that time and no other.

I reply: His being an agent acting by choice—exalted is He—means that there is nothing beyond Him that compels Him to act or refrain and thereby makes this necessary for Him. The thing supposed to compel Him would either be His effect or not be His effect. The latter is impossible, because it would be another necessary existent, or the act of another necessary existent, and the proofs of oneness refute this. The former is also impossible, because it entails that an effect, whose existence subsists through the cause and is posterior to it, influences the existence of its cause, from which it receives existence.

The Necessary’s acting by choice therefore does not conflict with His necessitating the act that proceeds from Himself. Nor does His necessitating the act conflict with His acting by choice in it.

As for the temporal origination of the world, meaning everything other than the Necessary: for the world to be temporally originated would mean that it is preceded by a stretch of time devoid of the world, with nothing accompanying it but the Necessary—exalted is He—and as yet no trace of the world. But the nature of time is a quantitative, contingent nature, existent and caused by the Necessary—exalted is He—and one of His acts. It is therefore part of the world.

There is no meaning to the world, which includes time, being temporally originated and preceded by temporal nonexistence. There is no temporal “before” outside time.

Some have sensed this difficulty and sought to remove it by claiming that time is an intellectually considered, imaginary affair that does not exist.

This is rejected because claiming that time is intellectually considered and imaginary amounts to admitting that there is no real temporal origination.

Others have sought to remove the difficulty by saying that time is a reality abstracted from the essence of the Necessary—exalted is He—in respect of His enduring.

The objection is that this entails change in the essence of the Necessary—exalted and sanctified is He—because what is abstracted is identical with that from which it is abstracted, and time’s being intrinsically changing is self-evident.

It has been answered that what is abstracted could differ from that from which it is abstracted by failing to correspond to it.

This is rejected because allowing a disparity between what is abstracted and that from which it is abstracted is sophistry, and knowledge would thereby be abolished altogether. It also admits the falsity of the original claim.

As for the statement that an agent acting by choice may choose one of two equal alternatives rather than the other without a determinant giving it preponderance: they have illustrated this by someone fleeing a beast of prey who encounters two equal paths and chooses one without a determinant.

This is a claim without proof. The argument has already been given that, when a contingent thing has two equal alternatives, a determinant is needed for either alternative to prevail.

If it is said that the determinant is the agent, for example through his will, as in the preceding example of someone fleeing a beast of prey, the answer is that this returns to the following position, whose falsity will be explained.

As for the example of someone fleeing a beast of prey, it is not conceded. On the supposition that the alternatives are equal in every respect, the person in question would remain where he was and would not move at all.

Moreover, allowing a contingent thing to acquire preponderance without a determinant closes the path to establishing the Maker—exalted is He.

As for the statement that “will is intrinsically a determinant, through which one of several equal acts is determined without any further determinant”: if will gives preponderance to an act, it does so only by being directed toward it. But its being directed in the first place toward one of several things equal in every respect is impossible.

The claim that giving preponderance to one of several equal acts is a distinctive property of will has no substance. Will is a psychic, cognitive quality that is realized only in relation to its object, to which the knowledge preceding it has given preponderance.

Unless the prior knowledge gives preponderance to the object of will, will is not realized, so that an act could acquire preponderance through it.

As for the statement that “He—exalted is He—knows everything knowable: what He knows will occur, He does; what He knows will not occur, He does not do”—or, in other words, “He does what He knows to be possible, but not what is impossible”—possibility is a necessary concomitant of quiddity. The abstraction of quiddity depends upon the realization of existence, and the thing’s existence depends upon a determinant’s giving it preponderance. Knowledge of possibility is therefore posterior to the determinant by several degrees and cannot be the determinant.

As for the statement that “His acts—exalted is He—are not devoid of benefits, even though we do not know them; whatever act has a benefit at a particular time, which would be lost if He did not perform it then, He delays until that time”: besides being subject to the objection raised against the preceding position, the benefit supposed to be connected with the particular time of any of His acts, however it is conceived, has a contingent quiddity, neither necessary nor impossible. Like the acts possessing those benefits, it is therefore His act—exalted is He.

Everything other than Him—exalted is He—comprising both benefits and the things possessing benefits, is His act. It does not transcend the level of possibility and cannot dispense with a determining cause that is a complete cause. Beyond the contingent there is only the Necessary—exalted is He. He is therefore the complete cause necessitating the totality of His act, which has no determinant other than Him.

Since the world is composite and has parts standing in existential relations to one another, the existence of some of its parts may indeed serve to give preponderance to the existence of others. Yet all end in the one cause besides which there is no cause and other than which there is no determinant: the Necessary—exalted is His name.

The conclusion from everything preceding is that the effect’s existence is necessary when the complete cause exists.

Some who found no escape from the complete cause’s necessitating its effect have said that “the cause of the world is the will of the Necessary, not His essence—exalted is He.”

This is the most untenable statement made on this question. If what is meant by His will is essential will, it is identical with the essence. Saying that will is the cause would then be the same as saying that the essence is the cause, whereas the proponent distinguishes the two, accepting one and rejecting the other.

If what is meant is will as an attribute of act, which is among the attributes of act outside the essence, it would be one of the contingent things beyond the world, and from it we would infer the existence of one of the contingent things. So much for this.

As for the necessity of the cause’s existence when the effect exists: if the cause’s existence were not necessary when the effect existed, it would be possible, since the supposition of its impossibility is dispelled by the slightest attention. If it were possible, its nonexistence would be admissible while the effect existed with its existence subsisting through it. This would entail the effect’s existing without a cause.

You may object: the effect needs its cause for origination, not for persistence. The cause could therefore cease to exist after the effect originated, while the effect remained as it was.

I reply: this rests upon the view of some that the effect needs its cause in origination but not in persistence. Once the effect originates through the cause’s bringing it into existence, its need for the cause ends. They illustrate this by a building and its builder: the builder is a cause of the building, but when he has built it and the building stands, its need for the builder ceases, and the builder’s nonexistence does it no harm.

This is rejected because need for a cause is an inseparable property of quiddity, owing to its possibility in becoming qualified by existence or nonexistence. Quiddity together with its possibility is preserved in persistence just as it is preserved in origination. The cause must therefore exist in the state of persistence just as it must exist in the state of origination.

Moreover, it was established above that the effect’s existence in relation to the cause is relational existence, subsisting through it and not independent of it. If the effect became independent of the cause in its persistence, it would be independent of it and would not subsist through it. This is a contradiction.

Another demonstration: it is said in the Asfār:

“This too—meaning that origination is the reason for needing a cause—is false. If we analyze origination into prior nonexistence, subsequent existence, and that existence’s being after nonexistence, and then inquire into the reason for dependence upon an agent—whether it is one of these three things or a fourth thing distinct from them—none of the alternatives remains except the fourth. Prior nonexistence cannot be the reason, because it is sheer negation and is unfit for causality. Existence cannot be the reason, because it needs to be brought into existence, and this is preceded by the need for existence, which depends upon the reason for needing it. If we made existence the reason, a thing would depend upon itself through several degrees.

“As for origination, it depends upon existence, because it is a manner and an attribute of existence. You have learned that existence depends upon the reason for dependence through several degrees.

“If origination were the reason for need, it would precede itself through several degrees. The reason for dependence is therefore something additional to what has been mentioned.”

The foregoing also disposes of another mistaken notion held by some: their statement that “prior nonexistence is a condition of the possibility of an act.” The priority intended is temporal priority. The statement thus amounts to saying that an effect, insofar as it is the act of its cause, must be temporally originated. They justify this by saying that the perpetual existence of a thing is incompatible with its need. This position also entails the effect’s not existing when the cause exists.

The reason it is refuted is that the reason for needing a cause is possibility, which is a necessary concomitant of quiddity. Quiddity accompanies the effect however its existence is supposed, with no distinction between perpetual existence and any other.

Moreover, the effect’s existence is relational in relation to the cause, subsisting through it and not independent of it. It is impossible for it to turn into something independent of the independent existent through which it subsists, whether it is perpetual or finite in duration.

Moreover, this position entails that time falls outside the domain of contingent things, and the ways in which this is false have already been explained.

[translation] “Necessity relative to another” (al-wujūb bi-l-qiyās) is kept distinct from “necessity through another” (al-wujūb al-ghayrī), following Stage IV, Chapter 2. The first concerns the necessary relation between the terms; the second concerns necessity conferred by a cause.

[translation] “Domain” renders wiʿāʾ in the objection about the cause and effect. It denotes their context or mode of existence and should not be restricted to a spatial container.

[textual] The final reply concerning will as an attribute of act retains the compressed wording, “one of the contingent things beyond the world, and from it we would infer the existence of one of the contingent things.” This reading was visually checked against the NYU/Princeton 1404 AH witness, printed pp. 163–164 (PDF pp. 167–168), and agrees with the later transcription. No explanatory premise has been inserted into the authorial text.

N-VIII-04 · From the one, only one proceeds
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 214–216
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=214&type=c_fbook

By “one” is meant a simple thing whose essence contains no compositional aspect that produces multiplicity.

The one cause is therefore a simple cause that is a cause through its simple essence. The one effect is a simple effect that is an effect through its simple essence. “One” thus means what is opposed to a many possessing parts or distinct units that cannot be referred back to one aspect.

To explain: the principle from which the effect’s existence proceeds is the cause’s existence, which is the very essence of the cause. The cause is the very existence from which the effect’s existence proceeds, even when everything else is disregarded. There must be an intrinsic affinity between an effect and its cause that specifically determines its proceeding from that cause. Otherwise, everything would be the cause of everything, and everything the effect of everything.

If many effects, insofar as they are many, distinct, and not reducible to one aspect, proceeded from one cause whose essence possessed only one aspect, many distinct and mutually opposed aspects would be established in the cause’s essence. Yet it was supposed simple and possessed of one aspect. This is a contradiction.

From the one, therefore, only one proceeds. This is what was sought.

An objection has been raised against this by appeal to a contrary consideration: it entails that the Necessary—exalted is He—cannot bring more than one thing into existence. This restricts His power, whereas it has been demonstrated that His power is unrestricted and identical with His transcendent essence.

The answer is that the supposition has been demonstrated impossible. Power does not relate to the impossible, because it is sheer nullity with no reality as a thing.

Unrestricted power therefore retains its unrestrictedness. Every existent is either His effect—exalted is He—without an intermediary, or an effect of His effect. The effect of an effect is truly an effect.

The following consequences follow from this.

First, one does not proceed from many.

If one should proceed from many, either it is one in species, possessing many individuals, each of which depends upon a particular cause, as with heat proceeding from fire, light, motion, and other things; or its unity is a weak numerical unity, like specific unity, so that its existence depends upon many. An example is numerically one prime matter whose existence depends upon an immaterial being that sustains it through forms coming successively to it, one after another, as the philosophers have said. The discussion of this has already been given.

Alternatively, the many possess an aspect of unity upon which the effect depends; or the many constitute a composite possessing parts that produces the one through one of those parts, so that the production is attributed to the composite itself.

Second, many causes do not act upon one effect, whether they act jointly at the same level or successively, with one cause taking over from another. The first entails a contradiction in the essence of the one by making it many. The second entails the same impossibility just mentioned.

Third, if many proceed from one, it must contain an aspect of multiplicity and composition upon which the many depend, besides the supposed aspect of unity. An example is a single human being who performs many acts belonging to many categories distinct in their entire essences.

[translation] The “one” in this principle is explicitly defined as what is simple in the respect from which it acts. The chapter separately treats specific unity, weak numerical unity, and composite agents; these qualifications belong to the author’s formulation.

N-VIII-05 · The impossibility of circularity and infinite regress in causes
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 216–221
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=216&type=c_fbook

Circularity is a thing’s existence depending upon something whose existence depends upon it. It may occur without an intermediary, as when A depends upon B and B depends upon A; this is called “explicit circularity.” Or it may occur through an intermediary, as when A depends upon B, B upon C, and C upon A; this is called “implicit circularity.”

Its impossibility is close to self-evident, because it entails a thing’s preceding itself in existence, which is necessarily impossible.

Infinite regress is an existent thing’s being ordered after another thing actually existing together with it, the second being similarly ordered after a third, the third after a fourth, and so on without end.

The series may extend in this way in both directions, with something prior before every prior and something posterior after every posterior, or in one direction only.

In every case, however, the conditions are that the members of the series actually exist, that they coexist in existence, and that there be an ordering among them.

Infinite regress in causes is an effect’s being ordered after a cause, its cause after another cause, and its cause’s cause after another cause, and so on without end.

Infinite regress in causes is impossible.

The demonstration is that the effect’s existence is relational in relation to its cause and subsists only through its cause. The cause is the independent existent that sustains it, as established above.

If its cause is the effect of a third, and so on, that cause is not independent in relation to what is above it. If the series went on without end and did not terminate in an uncaused cause that was independent and not relational, none of the series’ members would be realized. Relational existence is impossible except together with an independent existent.

Another demonstration is known as the demonstration of the intermediate and the extremity. The Shaykh set it out in the Shifāʾ, where he says:

“If we suppose an effect, a cause of that effect, and a cause of its cause, it is impossible for every cause to have a cause without end. For if the effect, its cause, and its cause’s cause are considered as a whole with respect to their relations to one another, the cause of the cause is an unqualified first cause of the other two. Both of the others stand to it as effects, though they differ in that one is an effect through an intermediary and the other an effect without an intermediary. Neither the final term nor the intermediate is such a cause: the intermediate, which is the cause immediately adjoining the effect, is a cause of only one thing, and the effect is not a cause of anything.

“Each of the three has a distinctive property. The property of the extremity that is an effect is that it is not a cause of anything. The property of the other extremity is that it is a cause of everything other than itself. The property of the intermediate is that it is a cause of one extremity and an effect of the other, whether the intermediate is one or more than one.

“If it is more than one, it makes no difference whether its ordering is finite or infinite. If it is ordered in a finite multiplicity, the total number of terms between the two extremities is like a single intermediary, sharing the property of a single intermediary in relation to the two extremities. Each extremity will then have its own distinctive property. Likewise, if it is ordered in an infinite multiplicity so that no extremity is present, the whole of the infinite has the property of an intermediary. For whatever aggregate you take is a cause of the final effect’s existence and is itself an effect: each of its members is an effect, and the aggregate’s existence depends upon them. Whatever depends for its existence upon an effect is an effect. Yet that aggregate is a condition of the final effect’s existence and a cause of it. However much you increase what you delimit and take together, this judgment remains, without end.

“It is therefore impossible for an aggregate of causes to exist without there being among them an uncaused, first cause. For the whole of the infinite would be like an intermediary without an extremity, and this is impossible.”

Another demonstration, known as “the firmest and shortest,” is due to al-Fārābī. Each member of a series actually extending in order without end is like every other in that it does not exist unless another beyond it exists beforehand. It is therefore true of all the infinite members taken together that they do not enter existence unless something beyond them already exists beforehand.

The self-evident judgment of reason is then: from where could anything in that series come to exist, so that anything after it could exist?

Other arguments have been offered for the impossibility of infinite regress, most of which are open to objections.

A clarification

Some have said:

“The criterion for judging impossibility in every demonstration offered against infinite regress is the joint fulfillment, in the direction of infinity, of the two conditions of ordering and actual coexistence in existence. These entail the impossibility of infinite regress among causes in the ascending direction, where causes are ordered without end, but not in the descending direction, where an effect is ordered after its cause, then the effect’s effect after the effect, and so on without end.

“The difference is that causes are together at the degree of the effect’s existence and encompass it. Their priority over it is discerned only through a kind of analysis. Effects, by contrast, are not at the degree of their causes. The series’ ascending therefore entails the ordered causes’ coming together with their actual existences at the degree of the effect from which the series begins, for example. In a descending series, by contrast, the ordered descending effects do not come together upon the first cause from which the series begins, for example.”

This concludes the summary of that statement.

You will recognize that the two preceding demonstrations, transmitted from the Shaykh and al-Fārābī, apply to both ascending and descending series when the series consists of complete causes.

As for incomplete causes, the two demonstrations apply when the series ascends, because the incomplete cause must exist when the effect exists and together with it. This differs from a descending series, because the effect need not exist when the incomplete cause exists.

The claim that the criterion of impossibility is the infinite’s coming together in one member of the series, and that this occurs in ascent but not in descent, is therefore not conceded.

Another clarification

It was established above that infinite regress is impossible only when the members of the series actually exist, coexist in existence, and are ordered one after another.

If some members exist potentially, as with some degrees of number, it is not impossible, because what actually exists of it is always finite. Likewise, if the members actually exist but do not coexist in existence, as with temporal events, some of which are nonexistent when others exist, it is not impossible, because what exists of them is always finite. Likewise, it is not impossible if they actually exist together but lack an ordering among them, meaning that some do not depend upon others in existence: for example, an infinite number of existents among which there is neither causality nor being caused.

The reason is that, when any of these three conditions is absent, there is no single existent infinite series to which the demonstrations of impossibility could apply.

Another clarification

The preceding demonstration entails the impossibility of infinite regress in all the kinds of causes: efficient, final, material, and formal. It also entails its impossibility among complete causes, because the ground of impossibility is existential dependence extending without end, which occurs in every kind of cause.

This also establishes the impossibility of infinite regress among the parts of a quiddity: for example, a genus having a genus without end, or a differentia having a differentia without end. For genus and differentia are matter and form taken without condition. Moreover, if a single quiddity were composed of infinitely many parts, its intellection would be impossible, which is false.

[translation] The chapter requires actual existence, coexistence, and existential ordering for the regress it rules out. It expressly distinguishes this from potential infinity, succession of temporal events, and an infinity lacking causal ordering. “Beforehand” in the al-Fārābī argument expresses the priority at issue in the causal series; it need not mean an earlier time.

[translation] “Taken without condition” renders lā bi-sharṭ, the logical consideration under which matter and form are genus and differentia. The phrase does not mean that their existence is unconditioned.

N-VIII-06 · The efficient cause
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 221–222
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=221&type=c_fbook

It was established above that a contingent quiddity, in becoming qualified by existence, needs a determinant of its existence. Reason has no doubt that the determinant of existence has a role in relation to existence different from the role that quiddity has in relation to it.

The determinant, or some of its parts, has a role in relation to existence resembling giving, which we call “action” or something conveying that meaning. Quiddity has a role resembling taking, which we call “reception” or something conveying that meaning.

It is impossible for quiddity to possess the role of the determinant, for then it would not need a determinant. Nor can the determinant possess the role of quiddity, for this would contradict the supposition.

It is also impossible for the two roles to be identical. The role that is reception necessarily accompanies lack, while the role that is action necessarily accompanies possession.

This meaning is clear in the actual events we observe in the material realm. They involve causes moving matter toward forms that it lacks, so that it receives those forms and takes shape through them. Had it possessed them, it would not receive them while possessing them. Reception therefore necessarily accompanies lack. What belongs to the causes is action appropriate to their essences, necessarily accompanying possession.

A material event therefore depends for its existence upon a cause that produces it, which we call an “efficient cause,” and upon a cause that receives it, which we call a “material cause.” It will be established that there exist contingent quiddities immaterial and free of matter. Because of their possibility, they need a determining cause; because of their immateriality, they have no need for a material cause. They therefore also have an efficient cause.

No contingent existence, whether material or immaterial, can dispense with an efficient cause. Whoever seeks to restrict causes to the material cause and deny the efficient cause seeks to establish an act without an agent: he has mistaken swelling for fatness.

[translation] The closing Arabic proverb, fa-stasmana dhā waram, literally describes taking a swollen creature for a fat one. It conveys mistaking an unsound appearance for a substantial result.

N-VIII-07 · The divisions of the efficient cause
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 222–226
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=222&type=c_fbook

They have distinguished several kinds of agent, which some have counted as eight. Their classification proceeds as follows. An agent either possesses knowledge of its act that contributes to the act, or it does not. In the second case, either its act agrees with its nature, and it is an “agent by nature,” or its act does not agree with its nature, and it is an “agent by constraint.” In the first case, namely where it possesses knowledge of its act that contributes to it, either the act is not by its will, and it is an “agent by coercion,” or the act is by its will. In the latter case, either its knowledge of its act is at the degree of the act—indeed, identical with the act—and it is an “agent by consent,” or its knowledge of its act precedes the act. In this latter case, either its knowledge of the act is accompanied by a motive additional to its essence, and it is an “agent by intention,” or it is not accompanied by any additional motive and the knowledge itself is the principle from which the effect proceeds. In this last case, either its knowledge is additional to its essence, and it is an “agent by providence,” or it is not additional, and it is an “agent by manifestation.” An agent, however conceived, is an “agent by subordination” if both it and the act attributed to it are the act of another agent.

The efficient cause thus has eight divisions.

First: the agent by nature. This is an agent with no knowledge of its act, whose act nevertheless agrees with its nature. An example is the soul at the degree of the natural bodily faculties: it performs their acts by nature.

Second: the agent by constraint. This is an agent with no knowledge of its act, whose act does not agree with its nature. An example is the soul at the degree of the natural bodily faculties when they deviate through illness. Their acts then deviate from the course of health because of constraining factors.

Third: the agent by coercion. This is an agent that knows its act, which is not performed by its will, as when a human being is coerced into doing something he does not will.

Fourth: the agent by consent. This is an agent that wills its act through knowledge, whose detailed knowledge of the act is identical with the act itself. Before the act, it has only comprehensive knowledge of it through its knowledge of its own essence, which entails comprehensive knowledge of its effect. An example is a human being who produces imaginal forms: his detailed knowledge of them is identical with those forms. Before they exist, he has comprehensive knowledge of them through knowing his own essence as their producer. Another example is the Necessary’s agency in relation to things—exalted is He—according to the Illuminationists.

Fifth: the agent by intention. This is an agent possessing knowledge and will, whose detailed knowledge of its act precedes the act, with an additional motive. An example is a human being in voluntary acts; another is the Necessary according to the majority of the theologians.

Sixth: the agent by providence. This is an agent possessing knowledge prior to its act and additional to its essence, where the cognitive form itself is the principle from which the act proceeds, without an additional motive. An example is a human being on a high tree trunk who falls to the ground merely by imagining that he will fall. Another example is the Necessary—exalted is He—in bringing things into existence, according to the Peripatetics.

Seventh: the agent by manifestation. This is an agent that performs an act and possesses detailed knowledge of it identical with its comprehensive knowledge of its own essence. An example is the immaterial human soul. Since it is the ultimate form of its species, it is, despite its simplicity, the principle of all its secondary perfections. Because it is their cause, it possesses them in its own essence. Its knowledge of its essence by presence is knowledge of the details of its perfections, even though these are not distinguished from one another. Another example is the Necessary—exalted is He—on the basis of what will be explained, God willing: He has comprehensive knowledge of things in the very act of detailed disclosure.

Eighth: the agent by subordination. This is an agent that, together with its act, belongs to another agent; it is therefore an agent subordinated in its act. Examples are the natural, vegetative, and animal faculties subordinated to the human soul in their acts, and the cosmic causes subordinated to the Necessary—exalted is He.

There is a difficulty in counting the agent by coercion and the agent by providence as two distinct kinds, each separate from the agent by intention. To explain: we attribute the operations accompanying every observed species—that is, its secondary perfections—to that species itself. Every species is thus an efficient cause of its secondary perfections. Species are of two kinds in this respect. Some produce their acts through their nature without the mediation of knowledge, as with the elements. Others produce acts in which knowledge plays a part, as with human beings. The second kind is equipped with knowledge, and there is no doubt that it is equipped with it only to distinguish acts that are its perfection from those that are not, so that it may perform what contains its perfection and refrain from what does not. For example, a child puts what he picks up into his mouth. If he finds it suitable for nourishment, such as fruit, he eats it; if not, he leaves it and throws it away. Knowledge thus mediates to identify the act containing perfection and distinguish it from others. If the knowledge and assent that mediate are present to the agent and require no deliberative thought for assent—as with knowledge arising from established dispositions and the like—he immediately wills and performs the act. If the matter is doubtful and requires assent, he begins applying descriptions and attributes of perfection to the act. If this ends in assent that it is a perfection, he performs it; if it ends in the contrary, he refrains. This inclination and turning toward one of the alternatives is what we call “choice,” and we count the act proceeding from it as a voluntary act.

It is therefore clear that the act of this kind of knowing agent depends upon the presence of assent to the necessity of the act: that is, to its being a perfection and to the opposite alternative, refraining, being otherwise. If assent to this is present in the soul without any need for intellectual effort, the agent immediately performs the act. If it is not present, deliberation and thought are needed until necessity and preponderance are applied to the act to be performed, and impossibility and lack of preponderance to refraining from it. It makes no difference whether the act’s preponderance and the lack of preponderance in refraining arise from the nature of the situation or from a coercer’s coercion. An example of the first is someone seated beneath a wall on the point of collapsing upon him, who stands up for fear that it will fall on him. An example of the second is someone seated in the shade of a wall, whom a tyrant threatens that, unless he stands up, he will bring the wall down upon him. He too stands up for fear of its falling upon him. In both cases, the act is willed, and the assent is of the same kind.

It follows that a coerced act is not distinct from a voluntary act, nor does it differ from it in external existence in a way that makes the agent by coercion a separate division alongside the agent by intention.

The most the coercer does is to make the act have only one alternative. The coerced agent then confronts an act with only one alternative, which he can only perform, just as if the act were such by its own nature.

Rational people have, indeed, distinguished the two acts in their social practices, to preserve the good of society and uphold their prevailing laws, which entail praise and blame, reward and punishment. The division of acts into voluntary and coerced is therefore an intellectually considered division, not a real division.

It also follows that the agent by providence is a kind of agent by intention. The conception of falling entertained by someone standing on a high tree trunk, for example, is the same knowledge present both in the frightened person whom the conception of falling overwhelms, so that he falls, and in someone accustomed to standing there through repeated practice, who neither fears nor falls. An example of the latter is a builder on very high buildings and walls.

Someone who climbs onto a high wall and stands there knows that he may remain in place and be safe, or fall from it and perish. If fear and severe alarm overwhelm him and draw his soul into attending exclusively to the conception of falling, he falls. This differs from the person accustomed to it, in whom both forms are present without fear or alarm. He chooses to remain in place and therefore does not fall.

This providential act’s lack of a proper rational end does not entail its being devoid of any motive whatever. Motive is more general than such an end, as will be explained in the discussion of play and futile activity.

[translation] The eight labels render bi-l-ṭabʿ, bi-l-qasr, bi-l-jabr, bi-l-riḍā, bi-l-qaṣd, bi-l-ʿināya, bi-l-tajallī, and bi-l-taskhīr. “Constraint” is reserved here for qasr and “coercion” for jabr, maintaining the distinction drawn in the initial classification.

[translation] “Providence” renders ʿināya in a technical classification of agency. Here the immediate principle of action is a cognitive form without an additional motive; the term is also applied to the human example of imagining a fall. The author subsequently challenges the separation of this class from intentional agency.

[translation] “Comprehensive” knowledge renders ijmālī in contrast to tafṣīlī, “detailed.” In agency by manifestation the author explicitly identifies comprehensive knowledge with detailed disclosure; “comprehensive” should therefore not be read as merely vague or incomplete knowledge.

N-VIII-08 · None exercises efficacy in existence, in the true sense of the word, except God—glorified is He
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 226–227
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=226&type=c_fbook

It was established above that causality and being caused pervade existents. Every existent is either a cause that is not an effect, an effect that is not a cause, or a cause of something and an effect of something. It was established that the series of causes terminates in an uncaused cause, the Necessary—exalted is He. It was established that He—exalted is He—is one with true oneness, admitting neither a second nor repetition, and that every supposed existent other than Him is necessary through Him and possible in itself. It was established that the effect cannot dispense with its efficient cause, just as it cannot dispense with its complete cause. He—exalted is He—is therefore the complete cause of all precisely while being an efficient cause. It was established that causality concerns existence, which is the effect of the maker, and that the effect’s existence is relational in relation to its cause and subsists through it. Likewise, the cause’s existence is independent in relation to the effect and sustains it. No determination belongs to the effect except one that belongs to the cause’s existence and exists through it.

He—exalted is He—is therefore the agent absolutely independent in being a principle, subsisting through Himself in His bestowal of existence and His causality. He exercises efficacy in the true sense of the word. None exercises efficacy in existence except Him. Nothing other than Him possesses more than relative independence, whereas independence is the ground of causality and the bestowal of existence. Efficient causes within existence are therefore preparatory causes bringing effects nearer to the bestowal of the first principle and the agent of all—exalted is He.

This is with regard to the fundamental reality of existence realized through its degrees in concrete things. With regard to the substantial and accidental quiddities considered by the intellect, qualified by existence and independent in this respect, He—exalted is He—is the cause in which all causes terminate. Whatever things terminate in Him without an intermediary have Him as their cause; whatever terminate in Him through an intermediary have Him as the cause of their cause. The cause of a thing’s cause is a cause of that thing. He—exalted is He—is therefore the agent of everything, and all causes are subordinated to Him.

[translation] The chapter distinguishes two standpoints: existence as fundamental, and the intellect’s consideration of substantial and accidental quiddities. Its description of intermediate efficient causes as preparatory belongs to the first standpoint; the final paragraph retains their causal ordering under the second.

N-VIII-09 · An agent complete in its agency is stronger than its act and prior to it
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 227–228
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=227&type=c_fbook

That the agent is stronger and more intense in existence follows from the fact that the act, which is its effect, is relational in relation to it and subsists through it in its very identity. The agent is the independent being that sustains and encompasses it. This is all that we mean by greater intensity of existence. This applies to the complete cause just as it applies to the efficient agent.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may God have mercy on him—counted this question as self-evident, saying: “Self-evidence judges that the efficient cause is intrinsically stronger than its effect in that through which it exercises causality. In anything else, this cannot be affirmed with certainty at the outset.” End of quotation.

As for its being prior in existence to its act, this belongs to the innately evident propositions, because the existence of the act depends upon the existence of its agent. This too applies not only to the agent but also to the complete cause and the other causes.

It has been said: “The complete cause is together with the effect, because its parts include matter and form, which are together with the effect—indeed, identical with it. It therefore does not precede the effect, since this would entail a thing’s preceding itself.”

The answer is that matter, as established above, is a material cause of the aggregate of matter and form, namely the composite thing. Likewise, form is a formal cause of the aggregate of the two. The aggregate produced from them, however, is not a cause of anything. Each of the two is a prior cause, while the aggregate is a posterior effect. There is therefore no difficulty.

This is the meaning of the statement: “What is prior is all the individual components; what is posterior is the aggregate on condition of their being combined.”

N-VIII-10 · A simple thing cannot be both agent and recipient
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 228–230
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=228&type=c_fbook

The prevalent position among the philosophers is that one thing, insofar as it is one, cannot be both agent and recipient under any circumstances. The qualification “in one respect” excludes material species, which act through their forms and receive through their matters, as fire produces heat through its form and receives it through its matter. Later thinkers have maintained that this is permissible without qualification.

The truth is to distinguish two cases. Where reception means affection and external acquisition of perfection, reception and action cannot coexist in one thing insofar as it is one. Where reception means being characterized by something and its being abstracted from the thing’s essence, without external affection or being affected, as with the necessary concomitants of quiddities, the two can coexist.

The argument is that reception in the sense of affection and being affected necessarily accompanies lack, whereas action necessarily accompanies possession. These are two distinct, mutually opposed aspects that cannot coexist in one thing insofar as it is one. As for the necessary concomitants of quiddities, such as the evenness of four, the complete essence cannot be conceived as devoid of its necessary concomitant, so that lack could be conceived in it. Reception here means only being characterized by something, and there is no difficulty in this.

The proponents of the prevalent view have offered two arguments for impossibility without qualification.

The first is that action and reception are two distinct effects and therefore cannot proceed from one thing insofar as it is one.

The second is that the relation of the recipient to what it receives is one of possibility, whereas the relation of the agent complete in its agency to its act is one of necessity. If one thing were both agent and recipient of something, its relation to that thing would be both possibility and necessity. These are incompatible, and incompatibility between necessary consequences entails incompatibility between that of which they are consequences.

Even if these two arguments were complete, they would establish no more than the impossibility of action and reception—in the sense of affection and being affected—coexisting in one thing insofar as it is one. Reception in the sense of being characterized by something, as quiddities are characterized by their necessary concomitants, is not an effect proceeding from the essence and preceded by possibility.

Moreover, neither argument is free of difficulty.

As for the first, making reception an effect proceeding from the recipient entails that the recipient is an efficient cause of reception. The difficulty would then arise in a simple recipient’s receiving form, since it produces reception and becomes a part of the composite. These are two effects that do not proceed from one.

As for the second, the relation of the efficient cause, insofar as it is one of the four causes, to the act is not a relation of necessity. The mere existence of the efficient cause does not require the effect’s existence unless the other causes join it—unless, of course, the agent alone is a complete cause. Merely supposing the agent complete in its agency, meaning that it is an actual agent through the remaining causes joining it, does not change its own relation to the act from possibility to necessity.

Later thinkers have argued for the permissibility of one thing’s being both agent and recipient, insofar as it is one, by appealing to the necessary concomitants of quiddities, especially simple quiddities. Every such quiddity has a concomitant or concomitants, such as contingency and its being a quiddity and a concept. The same applies to concepts abstracted from the essence of the Necessary—exalted is He—such as necessity of existence and oneness. The essence is both their agent and their recipient.

As you have learned, this argument is complete only where reception means being characterized by something. Reception and action are then one.

Where reception is affection, being affected, and acquiring perfection, however, reception necessarily accompanies lack and action necessarily accompanies possession. These are incompatible and do not coexist in one thing.

[Arabic transcription normalisation] The imported digital Arabic began «االمشهور», containing a duplicated initial alif. Corrected to «المشهور». This is an evident orthographic/transcription repair only, not an emendation of the author's logical argument or a manuscript variant.

N-VIII-11 · The final cause and the proof of its existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 230–237
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=230&type=c_fbook

It will be explained, God willing, that motion is the first perfection of what is potential insofar as it is potential. There is therefore a second perfection toward which the moving thing directs itself through the motion that terminates in it. This is the final perfection that the moving thing attains through its motion. It is sought for its own sake, whereas motion is sought for its sake. Hence the saying: “Motion is not sought for its own sake, nor is it something that a thing’s essence requires.”

This second perfection is what is called “the end of motion.” Through it the moving thing acquires perfection. Its relation to motion is that of completeness to deficiency. No motion is without it; otherwise, motion would turn into rest.

Because of the connection and fixed relation between the end and motion, there is a kind of unity between them. Through this, the end is related to the mover just as motion is, and is related to the moving thing just as motion is.

When the mover is nature and it moves a body through one of the accidental motions—in position, quality, quantity, or place—through which the body acquires perfection, the end is the completeness toward which the moving thing directs itself through its motion and which the moving nature seeks through its causing motion. Without the end, there would be neither the mover’s causing motion nor the moving thing’s motion.

A body moving, for example, from one position to another seeks the second position. It directs itself toward it by leaving the first position for a flowing position, in which it exchanges one instantaneous instance for another like it, until it settles into a fixed, unchanging position and remains there. This is the completeness sought for its own sake, and the mover also seeks it.

If the mover is a knowing agent whose knowledge contributes to its act, as with animal and human souls, the motion together with its end, which is completeness, is willed by it. But the end is willed for its own sake, and motion follows it, since motion is for the sake of the end, as established above. A knowing agent may, however, imagine something that necessarily accompanies the end or happens alongside it to be the end of the motion. It then takes this as that in which the motion terminates, sets up an imagined association between the two, and directs the motion toward it. Examples are someone who moves to a place to meet a friend, walks to a watering place to drink water, or goes to the market to buy and sell.

All this concerns an act that is an accidental motion, whether natural or voluntary.

When the act is substantial, as with substantial species, the case is as follows. If it belongs to the substances that have some connection with matter, it will be shown, God willing, that all of these undergo substantial motion. Their existences are flowing, terminating in fixed, nonflowing existences in which they come to rest. They therefore have a completeness that is the direction toward which they turn. Their efficient, moving causes will this completeness for its own sake, and their substantial motions are willed for its sake. If the act belongs to the substances immaterial in both essence and action, then, because its existence is actual and free of potentiality, it is not divided into completeness and deficiency as other things are. Rather, it is complete in itself, willed for its own sake and intended for its own sake. Act and end are one here: the act, in its reality at the level of the agent’s existence, is an end for that same act in its attenuated reality. This does not mean that the act is its own final cause, prior to itself, for a thing’s being its own cause is impossible.

It has thus become clear that every agent has an end in its act, and this is the act’s final cause. This was what was sought.

Several points follow from the foregoing.

First: the end of an act—that to which the agent’s exigency is directed primarily and for its own sake—may be one with the act. This means that the end is the reality of the act established at the level of the agent’s existence, and amounts to the unity of the agent and the end. This is the case when the agent’s act is an existent immaterial in essence and action, fully actual in itself and willed for its own sake. The end may also not be one with the act, the two being distinct. This is the case when the act is an accidental motion, or belongs to the substances that have some connection with matter, such as souls and forms impressed in matters. The agent attains ends of this kind through causing motion, and motion is not sought for its own sake. Motion is therefore realized, and the end follows it. The end may return to the agent, as when someone is saddened by an afflicted person’s suffering and removes it in quest of joy; or to the matter, as when someone moves into a position that improves his condition; or to something other than either, as when someone honours an orphan to make the orphan happy.

Second: knowing agents know the end before the act, even if the end is realized after the act and follows upon it. Agents of this kind will their acts, and will, whatever its character, is preceded by knowledge. If there is a causing of motion, the motion is willed for the sake of the end. The end is thus willed by the agent before the act. If there is no causing of motion and the act itself is the end, willing and knowing the act are willing and knowing the end.

The saying that “the end is before the act in conception and after it in existence” holds only for ends other than those of natures, since natures lack knowledge.

The saying that “the final cause is an efficient cause of the agent’s agency” is not without looseness of expression. Natural agents have no knowledge, so their ends cannot be cognitively present to them in a manner that gives the agent its agency. As for external existence, the end’s existence follows that of the act, and the act is posterior in existence to the agent insofar as it is an agent. It is therefore impossible for the end to be a cause of the agent’s agency.

As for knowing agents that are not natural agents, either their end is identical with their act, in which case the act is an effect of its agent and an effect cannot be a cause of its cause; or their end’s existence follows that of their act and is posterior to it, in which case it cannot be a cause of that act’s prior agent. The end’s cognitive presence to the agent before the act is a mental existence, too weak to be a cause of an external thing, namely the agent insofar as it is an agent.

The truth, as will be explained in detail, is that knowing agents, through their specific existences, are efficient causes of the acts connected with them and belonging to them as consequences of their specific natures. Similarly, every natural species is an efficient principle of the acts occurring about it and proceeding from it. Since these are knowing agents, however, the presence in them of the cognitive form of the act is a condition completing their agency, upon which their actual exercise of causality depends.

This is what is meant by the final cause’s being a cause of the efficient cause’s agency. Otherwise, the agent, through its specific nature, is an efficient cause of the acts proceeding from it and subsisting in it. These are its secondary perfections, through which it acquires perfection.

Third: although at first sight the end sometimes returns to the agent, sometimes to the matter, and sometimes to something other than either, closer consideration shows that it always returns to the agent. Someone who does good to a poor person to make that person happy suffers at the sight of the wretched condition he sees him in; through his beneficence, he wishes to remove that suffering from himself. Likewise, someone who travels to a place to rest there really wishes to relieve himself of the awareness of the fatigue he experiences in his body.

In general, an act is always of a kind with its agent, suited to it and pleasing to it; the same holds for the end that follows the act. It is therefore a good and a perfection for the agent.

It has been said: “The higher does not acquire perfection through the lower and does not will it, because it is a cause, and a cause is stronger in existence and higher in rank than its effect.”

The answer, as has been said, is that the agent wills the lower only insofar as it is one of its own effects. In reality, will is directed primarily to the agent itself, and derivatively to the end that follows the act.

When an agent conceives the perfective end, it sees itself in respect of its exigency and causality for that end. A hungry person who wishes to eat in order to become satiated, for example, sees himself in respect of his exigency for this act upon which the end follows. That is, he sees himself as possessing satiety in respect of that exigency, and wishes to become such in actual external existence.

If the agent has some connection with matter, it acquires perfection through the actuality of the end, which is its own essence insofar as it is an agent. As for the end outside its essence whose existence follows the act, it acquires perfection through this derivatively.

If the agent is immaterial in both essence and action, it is complete in itself and does not acquire perfection through its end, which is its fully actual essence and, in reality, its complete essence.

The foregoing therefore shows, first, that the agent’s end in its act is its own acting essence insofar as it acts. The end of the act that follows upon it is an end willed only derivatively.

Second, the end is always a perfection for the agent. If the agent has some connection with matter, it acquires perfection through the end that is its own acting essence insofar as it acts. If it is entirely immaterial, the end is identical with its essence, which is the perfection of its essence, without being perfection after deficiency or actuality after potentiality.

It is thus clear that the saying, “Every agent that has an end in its act acquires perfection through its end and benefits from it,” is not without looseness of expression. It holds universally only for agents that have some connection with matter.

Notice. Some theologians have maintained that the Necessary—exalted is He—has no end in His acts, because He is intrinsically independent of everything else. This is what they mean when they say: “God’s acts are not explained by purposes.” Other theologians have maintained that He—exalted is He—has ends and benefits in His acts that return to others and from which His creation benefits.

The first view is refuted by what has already been established: an agent’s act must either be a good sought by it for its own sake or terminate in a good sought for its own sake. The existence of an end does not entail the agent’s need for it, since the end may be identical with the agent, as established above.

The second view does not entail His—exalted is He—need for another, His acquiring perfection through the ends that follow His acts, or His benefiting from them. Nevertheless, it still entails the higher’s willing the lower and the nobler’s seeking the less noble. If the end that called Him to act, and upon which His act and indeed His agency depended, were the good and benefit following upon the act, something other than Him would exercise some influence upon Him. Yet He is a first agent, complete in agency, whose agency does not depend upon anything.

Rather, the truth, as established above, is that an agent insofar as it is an agent has, in reality, no end for its act other than its own acting essence insofar as it acts. Nothing impels it toward the act except itself, and the end that follows the act is an end only derivatively. He—exalted is He—is an agent complete in agency and a first cause in which every cause terminates. His essence—exalted is He—insofar as it is identical with knowledge of the order of good, is the end of His essence that acts to produce every good other than Him and is the principle of every perfection other than Himself.
