Our statement that “the agent’s agency depends upon the final cause,” which appears to distinguish what depends from that upon which it depends, does not contradict our statement that “the end of the necessary essence is identical with the transcendent essence.” What is meant here by dependence and exigency is the more general sense of inseparability.

As Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn indicated, this belongs to those instances of verbal looseness in which reliance is placed upon the understanding of someone trained in the sciences. An example is their explanation of the intrinsically Necessary as “that which, through its essence, requires existence,” or as “an existent necessary through its essence.” The wording appears to make the essence a cause of its existence, although its existence is identical with it.

In general, His—exalted is He—knowledge in His essence of the order of good is the end of His agency, which is identical with His essence. Indeed, deeper investigation shows that He—exalted is He—is the end of ends.

You have learned that the existence of every effect, insofar as it is an effect, is relational in relation to its cause and has no independence apart from it. It is clear that dependence cannot have its complete meaning without being related to something depended upon in its own right; otherwise, there would be an infinite regress. Likewise, seeking, intending, willing, directing oneself, and the like cannot be realized in their proper meanings without terminating in something sought for its own sake, intended for its own sake, willed for its own sake, and approached for its own sake.

Since He—exalted is He—is the first cause in which the existence of everything other than Him terminates, He is the independence of every independent thing and the support of everything that relies upon another. No seeker seeks and no willing being wills anything but Him; no being turns toward anything but Him, whether without an intermediary or through one. He—exalted is He—is therefore the end of everything that has an end.

[translation] “Exigency” renders iqtiḍāʾ: what the agent’s nature calls for or requires. In the example of hunger, the text distinguishes satiety as required by the agent’s nature from satiety actually realized externally; it does not say the hungry person is already actually satiated.

[translation] The identity of act and end in an immaterial act is stated through the distinction between its higher reality (ḥaqīqa) in the agent and its attenuated reality (raqīqa) as effect. The translation retains this qualification, which prevents the claim from becoming an assertion that a thing causes itself.

[textual] The phrase rendered “sets up an imagined association between the two” is the terse wa-yūjidu baynahumā takhayyulan (ويوجد بينهما تخيلا). It is retained without emendation: the wording also appears in the 1404 AH NYU/Princeton scan, printed p.181 (PDF p.185). The translation takes this to describe an imagined association by which something accompanying the proper end is adopted as the end of the motion.

N-VIII-12 · Unpremeditated action, necessary intention, habit, and similar acts are not without an end
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 237–240
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=237&type=c_fbook

It may be imagined that some voluntary acts have no end: children’s games, breathing, a sick person’s turning from side to side while asleep, playing with one’s beard, and the like. Such cases would invalidate the universality of the proposition that “every act has an end.”

This is answered by reflecting on the principles of our voluntary acts and the way their ends follow upon them.

We say: the philosophers have stated that our voluntary acts and movements by choice have a proximate principle that directly produces the movements called acts. This is the motive power distributed through the muscles, which moves them. Before the motive power there is another principle, the appetitive faculty, which culminates in will and resolve. Before the appetitive faculty there is yet another principle, the cognitive form arising from thinking or imagining that calls for the act for the sake of its end. These are three principles apart from the volitional principle.

The motive power is a natural principle with no awareness of the act. Its end is therefore that in which the motion terminates, as is the case with natural agents.

As for the other two principles—the appetitive faculty and the cognitive form—their end may be the same as that of the motive power, namely that in which the motion terminates. The three principles then share one end. An example is someone who imagines settling in a place other than his present one, longs for it, moves toward it, and settles there. Their end may also differ from that of the motive power, as when someone conceives a place other than his present one and moves there to meet a friend.

The remote principle, namely the cognitive form, may be merely imaginative, the form of the act being present in imagination without thinking. It may also be a form of thought, necessarily accompanied by a particular imagining of the act.

Furthermore, this form may alone be a principle of the appetitive faculty; or it may be its principle with assistance from nature, as in breathing; from bodily temperament, as in a sick person’s turning from side to side while asleep; or from disposition and habit, as in playing with one’s beard.

If the three principles agree in their end—as when someone imagines the form of a place, longs for it, and moves toward it, this being called “unpremeditated action”—the act, together with its principles, has its end.

If the cognitive principle gives rise to appetite with assistance from nature, as in breathing, or from bodily temperament, as when a sick person turns from a side on which remaining has become wearisome to another side, this is called “necessary intention.” If it does so with assistance from disposition, as in playing with one’s beard, the act is called “habit.” Each of the act’s principles has its end in these cases.

There is no difficulty in the agent’s being inattentive and failing to attend to the imaginative form of the end present to it in some or all of these cases. Imagining the end is different from knowing that one imagines the end, and knowledge is different from knowledge of knowledge.

In all these cases that are called “idle play,” the end is not an end of thought. There is no difficulty in this, since the cognitive principle in them is an imaginative form rather than a form of thought. There is no principle of thought in them such that they would have an end of thought.

Alternatively, you may say that they contain a conjectural principle of thought considered in a general way, toward which the appetite arising from imagining the form of the act points. A child, for example, conceives settling in a place other than his present one. From this arises an appetite that points toward the act’s being preferable and something that should be done. He then judges its preferability in a general way; his appetite intensifies, he wills, and he acts. The act is not preceded by a detailed cognition completed by a judgement of preferability. This is like someone speaking from an acquired disposition, who utters one letter after another without detailed conception and assent. The act nevertheless proceeds from knowledge and choice.

Likewise, there is no difficulty in the absence of the end in some natural or voluntary motions that are interrupted before reaching it. In that case the act is called “abortive.” An act’s having no end is one thing; the failure of its end to occur because the motion is interrupted and comes to nothing is another. What is claimed to be impossible is the first, not the second. This is clear.

It should be known that the principles of our voluntary action are ordered as explained above. There is a motive power upon which the act follows. This power follows upon will, and will follows upon the appetitive faculty without a further will intervening between them. The appetitive faculty follows upon the cognitive form, whether a form of thought or of imagination, without a will being directed to it. Rather, knowledge itself produces appetite. This is what they have said.

Their attributing appetite to certain psychic qualities is not incompatible with this, since psychic qualities necessarily accompany knowledge.

The Shaykh says in the Shifāʾ:

“The arousal of this appetite must have some cause: either habit, or weariness with one condition and a wish to pass into another, or eagerness on the part of the motive and sensory powers to renew an act of causing motion or of sensation.

“Habit is pleasant; passing from what has become wearisome is pleasant; and eagerness for a new act is pleasant—I mean with respect to the animal and imaginative power.

“Pleasure is, in truth, the sensory, animal, and imaginative good. It is what is supposed to be good when considered in relation to the human good.

“If the principle is imaginative and animal, its good must also be imaginative and animal. This act, then, is not devoid of a good relative to it, even if this is not a true good—that is, a good with respect to intellect.” End of quotation.

Since appetite is directed only to a perfection that is lacking and does not yet exist, it belongs exclusively to a knowing agent that has some connection with matter. An immaterial agent therefore has, among the principles of voluntary action, only knowledge and will. This differs from the knowing agent that has some connection with matter: it possesses knowledge, appetite, will, and the material power that directly performs the act, as explained above. This is what they have said.

[translation] Juzāf is rendered “unpremeditated action,” not action without any end. The chapter’s argument is precisely that such action has an imaginative end even without a detailed deliberative judgement. “Necessary intention” (qaṣd ḍarūrī), “idle play” (ʿabath), and “abortive” (bāṭil) retain the distinct categories used here.

[textual] The phrase ghayr al-irādiyya (غير الإرادية) is rendered “apart from the volitional principle.” The concluding paragraph expressly distinguishes knowledge, appetite, will, and the material motive power. The three principles first listed are thus counted apart from will. The same wording is visible in the 1404 AH NYU/Princeton scan, printed p.186 (PDF p.190); no Arabic emendation is made.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 186–188 (canvases 190–192); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 61–64. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «فهذه مباد ثلاثة غير الإراديّة.» → «فهذه مباد ثلاثة غير الإرادة.» [L1]. Evidence: 1404p186/c190; 1428v2p62n3. Earlier printed reading: «فهذه مباد ثلاثة غير الإرادية». Adopt the explicit later-editor reading الإرادة, preserving the early/base الإراديّة. The argument enumerates three principles apart from volition; the existing English already conveys this.

1428v2p62nn1–2: The editor says universal cognition does not motivate until particular imagination intervenes, and that cognition of an act implies cognition of its end and benefit. These are later interpretive comments.

N-VIII-13 · The rejection of chance as the absence of a connection between agent and end
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 240–243
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=240&type=c_fbook

It may be imagined that some ends following upon acts are not intended by their agents, and therefore that not every agent has an end in its act.

Examples given are someone who digs a well to reach water and comes upon a treasure: finding the treasure is an end following upon the act, yet it is neither connected with the digger nor intended by him; and someone who enters a house to shelter in its shade, whereupon it collapses upon him and he dies: death is not an end intended by the person entering.

The first kind of chance is called “good fortune,” and the second “ill fortune.”

The truth is that there is no chance in existence. The demonstration is that possible events, with respect to their occurrence, fall into four divisions: what always occurs; what occurs for the most part; what occurs and fails to occur equally often; and what occurs only in a minority of cases.

The intellect necessarily judges that what always occurs and what occurs for the most part each have a cause. The difference between them is that what occurs for the most part is sometimes opposed by an obstacle that prevents it from occurring. What always occurs, by contrast, has no such obstacle. Since the occasional failure of what occurs for the most part is due to an assumed obstacle, it necessarily always occurs on condition that the obstacle is absent. For example, a human child is usually born with five fingers; occasionally this fails, and a child is born with an additional finger, because an obstacle opposes the formative power in the act that it requires. On condition that the obstacle is absent, the formative power always produces five fingers.

A similar account applies to what occurs in a minority of cases. Given the particular obstacle opposing the cause that operates for the most part, it necessarily always occurs. In the example of the additional finger, whenever the formative power encounters in the receptacle surplus matter suitable for the form of a finger under its particular conditions, it always forms a finger.

An account similar to that concerning what occurs for the most part and what occurs in a minority of cases applies to what occurs and fails to occur equally often, such as Zayd’s standing and sitting.

Real causes therefore always exercise their efficacy, without failing either in their act or in their end. The assertion of chance arises from ignorance of the real causes and from attributing an end to something other than that to which it belongs.

A well-digger’s finding a treasure, when attributed to its essential cause—digging the well on condition that it is aligned with the treasure buried beneath it—is an essential end that invariably follows, and has nothing to do with chance. When attributed to digging a well without qualification and without any further condition, however, it is a chance occurrence and an accidental end attributed to something other than its invariably operative essential cause.

Similarly, the death of someone upon whom a house collapses after he has entered it for shade, when attributed to its essential cause—entering a house on the point of collapse and remaining in it until it collapses—is an essential end that invariably follows. When attributed to entering a house for shade without qualification, however, it is a chance occurrence and an accidental end attributed to something other than its essential cause.

Other particular examples of chance are to be understood on the model of these two examples. Proponents of chance have appealed to particular examples of this sort, whose character you now know.

Democritus is reported to have held that the world came to be by chance. Bodies, on this account, are composed of small, solid corpuscles dispersed in an infinite void. Their natures are alike, their shapes differ, and they are perpetually in motion. A number of them happened to collide and come together in a particular arrangement, and this world came to be. He nevertheless maintained that animals and plants do not come to be by chance.

Empedocles is reported to have held that the coming-to-be of elemental bodies occurs by chance: those that happened to combine in a manner suited to survival and reproduction survived, whereas those that happened not to do so did not survive but disintegrated.

Several arguments have been offered for this position.

The first argument: nature does not deliberate, so how can it perform its act for the sake of an end?

The answer is that deliberation does not give the act an end. It merely distinguishes the act from other acts and determines it. The end then follows upon the act in its own right, not through being made so by a maker. It is the difference between motives and deterrents that necessitates the exercise of determining deliberation. Without this, there would be no need for it. This is how acts proceeding from acquired dispositions occur. A speaker produces one letter after another in their different configurations without deliberating; if he deliberated, he would become hesitant and cease speaking. Likewise, if a craftsperson deliberated in the course of practising the craft, he would become hesitant and stop.

The second argument: in the order of nature there are kinds of corruption and death, and varieties of evil and harm, occurring in an unchanging order through causes that do not fail. These are not intended by nature but arise from the necessity of matter. Let us therefore judge that the kinds of good and benefit following nature’s act likewise arise in this manner, without nature’s intending them or having a motive that calls it to them.

The answer is as follows. Some of these evils consist in natural agents’ failure to reach their ends because their motions are interrupted. Nature’s being directed toward an end does not require that it reach it; abortive action has already been discussed. Other evils are themselves ends occurring in a constant order, in things whose good predominates over their evil. These are ends intended secondarily. The ends intended primarily are the predominant goods inseparably connected with these evils. This will be explained in detail in the discussion of divine decree. Nature, in its acts terminating in these evils, is like a carpenter who wishes to make a door from timber. He begins to carve and saw, then assembles and constructs it. A necessary consequence is the waste of some of the timber through sawing and carving. This is intended by him secondarily, as a consequence of his intention to make the door.

The third argument: one nature performs different acts. Heat, for example, melts wax, hardens salt, darkens the fuller’s face, and whitens the surface of the cloth.

The answer is that one nature performs only one act having one end. The different effects following upon its act are necessary concomitants of its conjunction with diverse and different factors and impediments.

The foregoing establishes that the ends following upon agents’ acts are essential ends that invariably follow their real causes and explanatory principles. Rare effects called chance occurrences are accidental ends attributed to something other than their real causes. Those very same effects invariably follow when related to their real causes.

There is therefore no avoiding the affirmation of an existential connection between them and their real efficient cause. If we could doubt the connection of these ends with their agents despite the stated invariability with which they follow, we could also doubt the connection of an act with its agent. For this reason, many proponents of chance have denied the efficient cause just as they have denied the final cause, and have confined causation to the material cause. The efficient cause has already been discussed.

[translation] “Always” and “invariably” render dāʾimī in the argument about events under their complete causal conditions. The point is not that a treasure is found whenever anyone digs any well; the text expressly adds the conditions under which the outcome follows.

[translation] The accounts of Democritus and Empedocles are translated as historical attributions made in the Arabic text, including “elemental bodies” (al-ajrām al-usṭuqusiyya). They have not been replaced with a modern reconstruction of either philosopher’s doctrines.

N-VIII-14 · The material and formal causes
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 244–246
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=244&type=c_fbook

You have learned that species possessing perfection potentially cannot lack, within their essential substance, a substance that receives the actuality of their primary and secondary perfections, namely forms and accidents. If its aspect is one of potentiality in one respect and actuality in another—as with a body, which is actual with respect to its corporeality and potential with respect to the forms and accidents that supervene upon its corporeality—it is called “secondary matter.” If its aspect is purely that of potentiality, it is what the analysis of secondary matter ultimately reaches: that which is potential in every respect except the respect of its being potential in every respect. It is called “hyle” and “prime matter.”

Matter has causality in relation to the material species composed of it and form, because that species’ existence necessarily depends upon it. Insofar as matter is a part of the composite, it is a cause of it. In relation to the other part that it receives, namely form, however, it is its matter and its effect, since, as established above, form is a co-cause of matter.

A group of naturalists has confined causality to matter alone, denying the other three causes.

This is refuted, first, by the fact that matter’s essential aspect is potentiality and reception, necessarily accompanying lack. It is evident that this does not suffice to bestow and produce actuality, which necessarily accompanies possession. The only alternative would be for actuality to come to exist without a cause, which is impossible.

Second, it has been established that a thing does not exist until it becomes necessary. Since matter’s character is possibility and reception, it cannot be the ground of this necessity abstracted from the effect’s existence, whose reality is necessity, entailment, and inseparability. There must therefore be something beyond matter that grounds the effect’s necessity and existence. This is the efficient cause that bestows the effect’s existence.

Third, matter has one nature and, if it produces an effect, produces only one uniform effect. They have conceded this. It follows that all the differences among things would have to be reduced to matter’s unity in essence and attribute. This amounts to every thing’s being identical with every other thing, which the intellect necessarily rejects.

As for the formal cause, it is form—in the sense of that through which a thing is actually what it is—in relation to the thing composed of it and matter. The composite necessarily depends upon it.

Form in relation to matter, however, is not its formal cause. Matter is not composed of form and something else, with an intrinsic need for form. Rather, it needs form for its determinate realization, which lies outside its essence. For this reason, form is a co-cause in relation to matter and gives it determinate realization, as explained above.

Know that the form giving matter determinate realization may become part of the matter in relation to a subsequent form. Consequently, the acts and effects that belonged to it when considered as the form giving matter determinate realization are attributed to the form in relation to which it has become part of the matter. Consider a plant, for example. The vegetal form gives determinate realization to the secondary matter that is the body. It has actual effects, namely the effects of corporeality and vegetative life.

When an animal form subsequently supervenes upon it, the vegetal form becomes part of its matter, and the animal form possesses the distinctive acts and effects that previously belonged to the vegetal form. Thus, whenever a new form supervenes upon a composite, the preceding forms become parts of the secondary matter, and the new form possesses the acts and effects that belonged to those preceding forms. It has already been established that the final form is the entire reality of the species.

Know also that the composition of matter and form is not a composition by juxtaposition, as is attributed to the majority. Rather, it is a unitive composition, as is required by the coming together of the indeterminate and that which gives it determinate realization, and of potentiality and actuality. Otherwise, the composition would not be real, and no new species with its own distinctive effects would result.

[translation] “Co-cause” renders sharīkat al-ʿilla. Form helps cause matter’s determinate external realization; the text distinguishes this from being a formal cause of matter itself. A formal cause is here the form in relation to the composite of matter and form.

[translation] “The final form is the entire reality of the species” retains the claim that the latest constitutive form encompasses the powers of the preceding forms, rather than treating the composite as a mere aggregate of independent forms.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 192–194 (canvases 196–198); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 70–72. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-VIII-15 · The corporeal cause
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 246–246
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=246&type=c_fbook

Corporeal causes are finite in their effects with respect to number, duration, and intensity, because corporeal species undergo motion in their substances and accidents. The natures and active powers they possess are therefore resolved and divided into parts, each surrounded by a preceding and a succeeding nonexistence, and limited in both essence and effect.

Moreover, corporeal causes act only when there is a particular spatial disposition between them and the matter that is affected.

The philosophers say: “Because corporeal causes need matter for their existence, they also need it for their production of existence, which follows from their own existence. Their need for matter in producing existence consists in their acquiring, through matter, a particular spatial disposition in relation to their effect.

“For this reason, proximity, distance, and particular spatial dispositions contribute to the manner in which corporeal causes exercise their efficacy.”

[translation] Waḍʿ here is the spatial disposition of a corporeal cause relative to the matter on which it acts, as the following examples of proximity and distance indicate. It is not a claim about merely conventional or posited relations.

Potentiality and Actuality

N-IX-PRE · Introduction: Potentiality and Actuality
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 249–250
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=249&type=c_fbook

The existence of a thing among concrete entities, such that the effects sought from it follow upon it, is called “actuality,” and one says that its existence is actual. Its possibility before it is realized is called “potentiality,” and one says that its existence is potential. A sperm-drop, for example, is potentially a human being so long as it remains a sperm-drop. When it becomes a human being, it becomes a human being actually, possessing the effects of humanity sought from a human being.

It is most likely that “power” originally signified the principle of arduous, forceful actions: that is, a thing's being such that forceful actions proceed from it. Its meaning was then extended to the principle of resistance to being affected—that is, a thing's being such that it is difficult to affect—on the assumption that being affected is an effect present in its principle, just as acting and exerting an influence are effects present in the agent. They then extended “power” to the principle of being affected even where this was not difficult, because they supposed that difficulty and ease in being affected constituted a single graduated kind. Thus they said, “Such-and-such a thing has the potential to become such-and-such,” and “Such-and-such is in it potentially.” This is the usage of ordinary people.

When the philosophers observed that temporally originated forms and accidents possess, before their existence, a possibility corresponding to the receptivity that ordinary people call potentiality, they called the existence that a thing has in possibility “potentiality.” They also called the principle of action “power,” applying this term to efficient causes and speaking of natural powers and powers of the soul.

They called the existence opposed to it—the existence upon which the effects sought from the thing follow—“actual existence.” Accordingly, they divided the existent without qualification into that whose existence is actual and that whose existence is potential.

These two divisions are the subject of investigation in this stage, which contains fourteen chapters.

[translation] The Arabic quwwa covers both “power” and “potentiality.” The introductory discussion traces the extension of the former sense into the latter.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 196–197 (canvases 200–201); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 77–78. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-IX-01 · Every Temporally Originated Thing Is Preceded by the Potentiality for Its Existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 250–251
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=250&type=c_fbook

This is because, before its existence is realized, it must be possible for it to exist: it must be admissible both that it be characterized by existence and that it not be so characterized. If it were not possible before its origination, it would be either impossible or necessary. If impossible, its realization would be impossible, although it has been assumed to originate in time; this is a contradiction. If necessary, it would exist and its nonexistence would be impossible; yet it may fail to occur and not exist.

This possibility is something existent externally, and is not an intellectual consideration attaching to the quiddity of a possible thing, because it is characterized by intensity and weakness, proximity and remoteness. A sperm-drop containing the possibility of becoming a human being, for example, is nearer to the possible human being than is food that can become a sperm-drop and then a human being. Likewise, the possibility in the sperm-drop is more intense than that in the food.

Moreover, this possibility existent externally is not a substance subsisting by itself, as is evident. Rather, it is an accident subsisting in a subject that bears it. Let us call it “potentiality,” and call the subject that bears it “matter.” Every temporally originated thing, therefore, has matter preceding it and bearing the potentiality for its existence.

Matter must not be incapable of uniting with the actuality whose possibility it bears; otherwise it would not bear that possibility. In itself, therefore, it is the potentiality for the actuality whose possibility it bears. If it possessed an actuality of its own, it would be incapable of receiving another actuality. Rather, it is a substance whose actuality of existence consists in being the potentiality for things. Yet, because it is a potential substance, it subsists through another actuality. When the possible thing arises—namely, the actuality whose possibility matter bore—the previous actuality ceases, and the subsequent actuality takes its place. The matter of water, for example, bears the potentiality for air while still subsisting through the form of water. When it becomes air, the form of water ceases, the form of air takes its place, and matter subsists through it.

The matter of the newly originated actuality and of the previous, vanishing actuality is one and the same. Otherwise, matter would originate with the origination of the new actuality, and would require another possibility and another matter. We would transfer the discussion to those two, with the result that a single originated thing would have infinitely many possibilities and matters; this is impossible.

A similar difficulty follows if matter is supposed to have a temporal origination.

What has preceded makes three points clear.

First, the relation between matter and the potentiality it bears is like the relation between natural body and mathematical body: the potentiality for a particular thing is a determination of indeterminate matter.

Second, the origination of things that originate in time is inseparable from a change in forms, if they are substances, or in states, if they are accidents.

Third, potentiality always subsists through an actuality, and matter always subsists through a form that preserves it. When one form originates after another, the new form takes the place of the old and sustains matter.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 197–198 (canvases 201–202); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 79–82. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «فقوّة الشيء الخاصّ تَعَيَّن قوّة المادّة المبهمة.» → «فقوّة الشيء الخاصّ تَعَيُّنُ المادّة المبهمة.» [L1]. Evidence: 1404p198/c202; 1428v2p82n1. Earlier printed reading: «فقوة الشيء الخاص تعين قوة المادة المبهمة». Adopt as an explicitly attributed later-editor choice, including the nominal vocalization تعيّن. The editor deletes the second قوّة and identifies determinate potentiality as a determination of indeterminate matter, citing a separate Asfār gloss that has not been independently collated here. Preserve the complete early/base clause and revise the English with it.

N-IX-02 · Further Discussion of Potential and Actual Existence, and the Division of Existence into Them
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 251–253
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=251&type=c_fbook

The substantial species before us admit of change, becoming other than they were at first. A substance that does not grow can change into a growing substance, and a growing substance can change and become an animal, with both the recipient and what is received being determinate. This entails that an existent, established relation obtains between them.

Moreover, we find that this relation varies in proximity and remoteness, intensity and weakness. A sperm-drop is nearer to an animal than food is, although both share the possibility of becoming an animal.

Proximity, remoteness, intensity, and weakness are positive attributes that can characterize only something existent. The relation in question therefore necessarily exists.

Every existent relation requires both of its terms to exist within the domain in which it exists. It necessarily subsists through them; its existence does not fall outside theirs; and one term of the relation exists for the other. All this has already been explained in the stage concerning the division of existence into existence in itself and existence in another.

Since what is received does not exist in the recipient with the external existence that is the source of its effects, it exists in the recipient with a weak existence upon which not all its effects follow.

Since each of its two existences, weak and intense, is the very same thing, they are one. What is received thus has one existence with two degrees: a weak degree upon which not all its effects follow, and an intense degree that is otherwise. Let us call the weak degree “potential existence,” and the strong degree “actual existence.”

Furthermore, what is received in its potential existence and in its actual existence is one continuous existent. Otherwise, the relation would cease, although it has been assumed to be established and existent; this is a contradiction.

Likewise, what is received in its potential existence and the recipient exist through one existence. Otherwise, one term would not exist for the other, the relation would cease, and the same contradiction would follow. Thus the existence of the recipient, the potential existence of what is received, and its actual existence are all one existence possessing different degrees. In it, that by which the degrees differ returns to that in which they agree. This is gradation.

This applies when we assume a single recipient and a single thing received. Suppose instead a chain of recipients and received things extending in both directions, finite or infinite. Each link contains the possibility of the actuality following it and the actuality of the possibility preceding it, as is the case with the chain of occurrences in external reality. All the limits would then have one existence, continuous with the continuity of the chain and possessing different degrees. If this single existence were divided into two portions, the earlier portion would contain the potentiality for the later, and the later would contain the actuality of the earlier.

If, for example, the earlier portion were itself divided into two, the first would contain the potentiality for the second, and the second the actuality of the first. However far one pursued division and subdivision of this single continuous existence, the situation would remain the same. Potentiality and actuality are therefore mingled and interwoven in it.

Every limit of this single continuous existence is a perfection in relation to the preceding limit, and an imperfection and potentiality in relation to the following limit, until it ends in a perfection without imperfection—that is, an actuality without potentiality—just as it began from a potentiality without actuality.

The definition of motion therefore applies to it: motion is the first perfection of what is potential insofar as it is potential.

This single continuous existence is a gradual, flowing existence that passes over matter bearing potentiality. The differing determinations are the limits of motion and the forms of matter.

All this concerns substantial species. The account of accidents is analogous to that of substances, and a detailed discussion of them will follow.

It is now clear that a thing's potentiality is a certain establishment of it upon which not all the effects of its actual existence follow; that existence divides into the actual and the potential; and that it divides into the stable and the flowing.

It is also clear that anything whose existence has a potentiality possesses a flowing, gradual existence, and that motion is present there. Whatever does not have a flowing, gradual existence—that is, whatever is stable—has no potentiality for its existence, and thus has no matter. Whatever has motion has matter; whatever has no matter has no motion. Accidents, insofar as their existences are for their subjects, have motion consequent upon the motion of their subjects, as will be explained in detail.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 198–201 (canvases 202–205); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 83–86. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

1428v2p83n4 continuingp84; p84nn1–2: The editor disputes the inference from transformation to a real two-term relation, proposes a disposition of the changing thing alone, and cites objections that relations may be mental or connect existent/nonexistent terms. These are not authorial retractions.

1428v2p84n4: External existence here contrasts with potential existence, not with mental existence; English remains consistent.

N-IX-03 · Further Clarification of the Definition of Motion and What It Depends Upon
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 254–255
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=254&type=c_fbook

It has been established that motion is a mode of existence through which a thing passes gradually from potentiality to actuality, such that the parts supposed for its existence do not coexist. In other words, every limit of its existence is an actuality for the supposed preceding part and a potentiality for the supposed following part. Motion is therefore a thing's gradual passage from potentiality to actuality.

The First Teacher defined it as “the first perfection of what is potential insofar as it is potential.” To explain: when a body occupying one place, for example, aims to occupy another, it leaves the first place by beginning to traverse the distance to the second, until it occupies it. While in the first place, the body has two perfections in relation to which it is potential: the traversal, which is a first perfection, and occupying the second place, which is a second perfection. Motion—the traversal—is a first perfection of the body that is potential in relation to both perfections. This is not without qualification, however, but insofar as the body is potential in relation to the second perfection, because the existence of the traversal depends upon it.

This makes clear that the existence of motion depends upon six things. First is the starting point: that from which the motion proceeds. Second is the endpoint: that toward which it proceeds. On one side, motion terminates in potentiality without actuality, either in reality or by consideration; on the other, it terminates in actuality without potentiality, either in reality or by consideration, as will become clear, God willing. Third is the course within which motion occurs: the category. Fourth is the subject to which motion belongs: the moving thing. Fifth is the agent through which motion occurs: the mover. Sixth is the magnitude by which motion is measured: time.

[translation] “The First Teacher” is Aristotle. “Course” translates masāfa: here it includes the category in which change occurs and is not restricted to spatial distance.

[Authorial footnote · restored from printed witnesses] Gradualness is a self-evident meaning, with sense perception assisting its apprehension. This definition is not a real definition, for real definition applies to quiddity; motion is a mode of existence, and existence has no quiddity.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 201–202 (canvases 205–206); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 87–88. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

1428v2p87n3 after منه رحمه الله: Editor explains that self-evidence of gradualness answers an alleged circularity in defining motion by gradual passage; cites the author following al-Mutarahat. This addition is distinct from the signed authorial note.

1428v2pp87–88n4: Reports sources attributing definition to Aristotle and other formulations; not an independently verified Aristotelian quotation.

N-IX-04 · The Division of Change
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 255–256
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=255&type=c_fbook

You have learned that a thing's passage from potentiality to actuality necessarily involves change, either in its essence or in the states of its essence. Alternatively, one may say: either in what is essential to it, as when one substantial species changes into another, or in what is accidental to it, as when a thing changes in its accidental states.

Change is either gradual or, in contrast, instantaneous. Gradual change—which entails divisibility into parts that neither endure nor coexist—is motion.

Instantaneous change, insofar as it requires a subject that receives the change and a potentiality preceding the occurrence of the change, is realized only through motion. You have learned that passage from potentiality to actuality, however it is supposed, takes place only through motion. Yet, because it is an instantaneous change, it belongs among the meanings applicable to the instantaneous parts of motion, such as arriving, leaving, coming into contact, and separating. Change, however it is supposed, therefore takes place only through motion.

Motion is sometimes considered as the moving thing's being between its starting point and endpoint in such a way that, at whatever limit of the course is supposed, it is not at that limit before or afterward. This is a simple, stable, undivided state, called “intermediate motion.” At other times, motion is considered as the thing's being between the starting point and endpoint in such a way that it has a relation to the supposed limits of the course, each of which is an actuality for the preceding potentiality and a potentiality for the following actuality: a limit that it leaves and a limit that it approaches. This entails division into parts, gradual lapse and passage, and the non-coexistence of the parts. It is called “traversal motion.”

Both considerations exist externally, because both apply to external reality. That is to say, motion has a relation to its starting point and endpoint that entails neither division nor flow; and it has a relation to its starting point, endpoint, and the limits of the course that entails the flow of existence and division.

As for the image of motion that imagination obtains by taking one limit after another and gathering them into a continuous image whose parts coexist, this is a mental thing that does not exist externally. The parts of motion, if parts are supposed for it, cannot coexist. Otherwise, motion would be stable rather than flowing, which is a contradiction.

[translation] Intermediate motion (al-ḥaraka al-tawassuṭiyya) considers the moving thing’s state of being between endpoints; traversal motion (al-ḥaraka al-qaṭʿiyya) considers its successive passage through the course. The distinction is between two considerations of motion, not two kinds of moving object.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 202–203 (canvases 206–207); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 89–91. N-IX-05 · The Starting Point and Endpoint of Motion
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 256–257
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=256&type=c_fbook

It was established that motion is divisible in itself. It should be understood that its division is potential, not actual, as is the case with stable continuous quantity: line, surface, and mathematical body. If motion were actually divided, with its parts separated from one another, division would end in parts occurring instantaneously, and motion would cease to be motion.

Nor does its divisibility stop at a limit beyond which it cannot proceed. If it stopped at a limit that division could not pass, motion would be composed of indivisible parts, whose impossibility has already been established.

From this it is clear that motion has neither a starting point nor an endpoint in the sense of a first part indivisible in the direction of motion and a last part likewise indivisible. A part in this sense, as has been shown, occurs instantaneously, and the definition of motion as the flow and gradual unfolding of existence does not apply to it.

The earlier statement that motion terminates on its two sides in a starting point and an endpoint defines its limits through what lies outside motion itself.

At its beginning, substantial motion terminates in potentiality without actuality, except for the actuality of being potentiality without actuality. This is prime matter. At its conclusion, it terminates in actuality without potentiality, which is immateriality. We shall clarify this further, God willing.

Accidental motions terminate, on the side of their beginning, in the matter of their subject, which receives the motion. On the side of their conclusion, natural motion terminates in the rest required by nature; forced motion terminates in a configuration at which the effect of the compulsion is exhausted; and voluntary motion terminates in what the moving thing regards as a perfection for itself in which it ought to settle.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 203–204 (canvases 207–208); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 92–93. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «إلى فعل لا قوّةَ معها» → «إلى فعل لا قوّةَ معه» [L1]. Evidence: 1404p204/c208; 1428v2p93n1. Earlier printed reading: «إلى فعل لا قوة معها». Adopt the explicit later-editor masculine pronoun referring to فعل. The earlier reading remains in the apparatus.

N-IX-06 · The Course of Motion
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 257–258
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=257&type=c_fbook

This is the category in which motion occurs, as when a body moves in its quantity through growth or in its quality through alteration.

It is necessary that what is essential does not change. The categories, being the highest genera of the quiddities beneath them, are essential to those quiddities. Motion changes the moving thing in the respect in which it moves. If motion occurring in quality, for example, were a change of the moving thing in the quiddity of quality, this would be a change in what is essential, and that is impossible.

There is therefore no motion in a category in the sense of change in its existence in itself—the existence that excludes nonexistence from it—for a quiddity's existence in itself is the quiddity itself.

If there is motion and change in a category, it occurs in its qualifying existence insofar as that existence qualifies. A thing has a quiddity in consideration of its existence in itself. As regards its existence as qualifying another, as in accidents, or as qualifying itself, as in substance, it has no quiddity. There is therefore no impossibility in motion occurring in a category.

A body moving in its quantity or quality, for example, undergoes no change in its quiddity, nor is there any change in the quiddity of the quantity or quality in which it moves. The change occurs only in the quantified or qualified character applying to the body.

This is the meaning of their statement that gradation pertains to accidental predicates, not to the accidents themselves.

Although qualifying existence has no quiddity, its unity with existence in itself allows the quiddity belonging to existence in itself to be attributed to it. This entails that motion in a category means that, at every instant of the motion, a species of that category comes upon the moving thing, without any species remaining upon it for more than one instant. Otherwise, there would be change in a quiddity, which is impossible.

[translation] “Qualifying existence” renders al-wujūd al-nāʿit. The argument distinguishes an accident considered in itself from its character as qualifying a subject; “accidental predicates” renders ʿaraḍiyyāt, distinguished here from aʿrāḍ, the accidents themselves.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 204–205 (canvases 208–209); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 94–96. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «لأنّ وجود الماهيّة في نفسها هي نفسها.» → «لأنّ وجود الماهيّة في نفسها هو نفسها.» [L2]. Evidence: 1404p204/c208; 1428v2p96. Earlier printed reading: «لأن وجود الماهية في نفسها هي نفسها». Adopt the later unfootnoted masculine pronoun referring to وجود; no conceptual change in the English follows.

N-IX-07 · The Categories in Which Motion Occurs
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 258–260
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=258&type=c_fbook

The well-known position among the ancient philosophers is that motion occurs in four categories: quality, quantity, place, and position.

As for quality, the occurrence of motion in it in general is evident, particularly in qualities specific to quantities, such as straightness, flatness, and curvature. A body moving in its quantity necessarily moves in the qualities subsisting in that quantity.

As for quantity, motion in it is a body's continuous, ordered, gradual change in its quantity. Growth, for example, is a continuous increase of the body's volume, gradually and in an ordered proportion.

An objection has been raised: growth occurs only through the addition of external parts to the body's parts. The later, larger volume is a quantity belonging to the combination of the original and added parts, whereas the earlier, smaller volume is the quantity belonging to the original parts alone. These two quantities are distinct and discontinuous because their subjects are distinct. Growth is therefore the disappearance of one quantity and the origination of another, not motion.

The reply is that the addition of further parts is beyond doubt, but nature transforms the added parts into the form of the original parts and thereby increases the quantity of the original parts continuously, in an ordered and gradual manner. This is motion, as is evident.

As for place, motion in it is evident, as in bodies' passage from one place to another. Whether place constitutes an independent category in itself, however, is not free from doubt.

As for position, motion in it is also evident, as in a sphere's rotation about its axis. Its position changes through a continuous, gradual change in the relations of the points supposed on its surface to what is outside it.

They said that motion does not occur in the remaining categories: acting, being affected, when, relation, possession, and substance.

As for acting and being affected, gradualness is included in their concepts. They therefore have no instance existing in an instant. Motion in them would require division into parts existing in an instant, which they do not possess. It would also entail motion within motion.

The same applies to “when.” Since it is a configuration resulting from a thing's relation to time, and is gradual with time's gradual passage, it has no instance existing in an instant in which motion divisible into instantaneous determinations could occur.

As for relation, it is abstracted from and dependent upon its two terms, and has no independent attribute such as motion.

Likewise, change in possession follows change in its subject: for example, a change in the shoe or foot from its previous condition in the case of being shod.

As for substance, motion in it would entail motion occurring without a stable subject persisting as long as the motion persists. This would entail motion without anything moving.

The arguments they have advanced can be challenged.

Their account of acting, being affected, and “when” can be challenged because motion within motion is possible, as we shall explain, God willing.

Relation and possession are relational categories, like position. Their dependence upon their terms in respect of motion does not conflict with motion genuinely occurring in them. Possessing an attribute consequently is different from possessing it incidentally.

As for their claim concerning substance, the absence of a subject in substantial motion is denied. Its subject is matter, as has already been explained and will be clarified further, God willing.

[translation] The categorical terms “place,” “when,” and “possession” render ayn, matā, and jida. “Consequently” renders bi-l-tabaʿ: a dependent but real attribution, distinguished here from an incidental attribution, bi-l-ʿaraḍ.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 205–207 (canvases 209–211); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 97–100. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

1428v2p99n2,p100n3: Editor rejects division into instantaneous parts and cites a pupil denying that every motion needs a subject; these remain separate from author’s replies.

N-IX-08 · Clarifying Motion in the Category of Substance and the Fundamental Questions That Follow from It
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 261–264
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=261&type=c_fbook

Although the restriction of motion to the four accidental categories is the well-known position transmitted from the ancients, their reported statements contain indications of motion occurring in the category of substance, even though they did not state it explicitly.

The first to adopt this position and discuss its proof fully was Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may God have mercy on him. It is the truth, as we demonstrated in the second chapter. He advanced various arguments for his position. Among the clearest is that accidental motions are flowing and changing in their existence, and are effects of the natures and specific forms belonging to their subjects. The cause of what changes must itself change. Otherwise, the effect, through its change, would diverge from its cause, which is impossible.

The natures and substantial forms that are the proximate causes of the attached accidents in which motion occurs therefore change in their existence and renew themselves in their substance, although they are stable in their quiddity and fixed in their essence, because what is essential does not change.

They explained the change and renewal affecting these accidents, despite the stability of their cause—nature or something else—by circumstances externally added to them. These include the occurrence of degrees of remoteness from and proximity to the end in natural motions; encounters with stronger and weaker obstacles and preparatory factors in forced motions; and renewed particular volitions arising at every limit of the course in voluntary motions.

The difficulty is that we transfer the discussion to the renewal of these circumstances that bring about the changing motion: whence does it arise? It must ultimately terminate in what is renewed intrinsically.

If it is said: “We explain the production of renewed motion by a stable cause in precisely the way you do, without needing to make nature intrinsically renewed. Motion is intrinsically renewed, and there is no difficulty in something renewed proceeding from something stable if renewal is essential to it. Bringing its essence into existence is identical with bringing its renewal into existence, as you yourself have acknowledged.”

The reply is that the renewal present in accidental motion is not a renewal of motion itself. An accidental category does not have an existence in itself that is for itself, such that it could be qualified by itself and be something renewed just as it is renewal. Its existence is for another: the substantial subject. A body's motion in its colour, for example, is the body's change and renewal in the colour belonging to it, not the renewal of its colour.

Substance is otherwise. Its existence in itself is for itself; thus it is both renewal and something intrinsically renewed. Bringing this substance into existence is the very bringing into existence of something renewed, and bringing something renewed into existence is the bringing into existence of this substance. It is not bringing a substance into existence so that it may then become renewed. Understand this.

Another argument: accidents are degrees of the existence of substances, since, as previously established, their existence in themselves is identical with their existence for their subjects. Their change and renewal therefore occur only together with the change and renewal of their substantial subjects. Accidental motions are thus evidence of substantial motion.

What has preceded makes several matters clear.

First, the changing substantial forms that come upon matter one after another are, in reality, a single flowing substantial form passing over matter. Its subject is matter preserved through some form, as established in the stage on substances and accidents. From each limit of this form we abstract a concept different from that abstracted from another limit. We call it a “specific quiddity,” differing from other quiddities in its effects.

Motion in general is never without an element of gradation, because it is passage from potentiality to actuality and a journey from imperfection to perfection. Nevertheless, in substance there is also another intensifying motion: the motion of prime matter toward nature, then plant, then animal, then human being. Each of these motions has particular effects following upon it, until motion terminates in actuality without potentiality.

Second, whatever accidents attach to substances possess motion consequent upon the substances that are their subjects. Stable attributes in changing, renewed subjects would make no sense. Moreover, accidents inseparable from existence, like the concomitants of quiddity, are produced through the simple production of their subjects, without any act of production intervening between them and their subjects. This concerns the inseparable accidents that we regard as stable and unchanging.

As for separable accidents that come upon their subjects through motion—as in motions occurring in the four categories of place, quantity, quality, and position—the appropriate account is to regard their motion as motion within motion. These should be called “second motions,” and the first kind “first motions.”

An objection to the possibility of motion within motion is that motion must be potentially divisible into parts existing in an instant. Motion within motion, however, is assumed to consist of gradual, divisible parts, from which motion therefore cannot be composed. Furthermore, motion would require the moving thing's arrival at each of its limits to be a further penetration into that limit, rather than a departure from it; motion would consequently fail to occur.

The response is that what we accept is that motion can be divided into parts through which its continuity and extension are cut, and that this ends in instantaneous parts. We do not accept that it must reach such parts without an intermediary. Motion may be divided into instantaneous parts that are not gradual in the manner characteristic of that motion, and those parts may then be divided into ultimate instantaneous parts.

The division of motion and the termination of its division in instantaneous parts resemble an accident's subsistence in a substance. Sometimes this subsistence is without an intermediary; sometimes it occurs through an intermediary and terminates in substance through one or more intermediaries. Thus a line subsists in a surface, a surface in a mathematical body, and a mathematical body in a natural body.

As for penetrating further into the limits, this requires only the occurrence of slowness in motion. The cause of slowness may be the composition of motion, as we shall indicate later, God willing.

Third, prime matter, insofar as it is pure potentiality, has absolutely no actuality except the actuality of being pure potentiality. In whatever actuality comes upon it, it therefore follows the form that sustains it. It is distinguished through the distinction of the form with which it unites, individuated through that form's individuation, and dependent upon it in unity and multiplicity. It does, however, possess an indeterminate unity resembling the unity of a generic quiddity.

Since prime matter is the subject of universal substantial motion, the material world in its entirety is a single flowing reality directed from the stage of pure potentiality toward actuality without potentiality.

[translation] Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn is Mullā Ṣadrā. “Simple production” renders jaʿl basīṭ: bringing something into existence, distinguished from making an already posited thing possess an additional determination.

[textual] The difficult wording about instantaneous parts followed by further division is attested in the 1404 print, p. 210 (canvas 214), the 1417 base, and 1428 vol. 2 p. 106. The English takes “not gradual” as relative to the motion under consideration. This is an explicit interpretation, not a witness-supported emendation. The following line/surface/body analogy is the author’s explanation.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 207–210 (canvases 211–214); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 101–106. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-IX-09 · The Subject of Motion
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 265–266
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=265&type=c_fbook

It has become clear that the subject of these motions is matter. This is the account in outline.

In detail, you have learned that wherever motion occurs there are matter and form, potentiality and actuality. You have also learned, in the discussions of quiddity, that genus and differentia are matter and form considered without a condition of exclusion. Specific quiddities may be ordered in descending levels: a higher species, an intermediate species, and a lowest species. Alternatively, many species may fall under one proximate genus in a parallel order rather than a hierarchical one.

This entails that, in the first kind of substantial species, prime matter is determined through a first form; the two together then constitute a second matter for a second form; and the two together in turn constitute matter—also called second matter—for a subsequent form. In the second kind, there is matter with multiple forms succeeding one another in it: whenever one of them comes to reside in it, it is incapable of receiving another form.

When these hierarchical and parallel substantial differentiations into species are referred to motion, then, in the second kind, the matter that is the subject of motion at its beginning remains the very same subject as the forms succeed one another until motion ends. This holds whether it is prime matter or second matter. The same judgment applies to accidental motions.

In the first kind, hierarchical motion, prime matter is the subject of the first form, and the two together are the subject of the second form. This occurs not by stripping off and putting on, as in the second kind, but by putting on after putting on.

This entails that the motion is intensifying rather than uniform. It also entails that the matter of the first form is no longer, by itself, the subject of the second form. Rather, the subject of the latter is prime matter together with the first form, while prime matter alone is an accompanying factor.

At this degree, the second form is the actuality of the species, and the resulting effects belong to it. Only actuality exercises determination, and there is only one actuality: that of the second form.

This is the meaning of their statement that the final differentia encompasses all the perfections of the preceding differentiae and is the basis for abstracting them; and that, if it were to become detached from matter and established on its own, the reality of the species would not thereby cease. The same applies to every form following another form.

From this, three points become clear.

First, motion in the second kind is simple. In the first kind it is composite, because the subject changes at every limit. This change, however, does not occur through the destruction of the preceding subject and the origination of a subsequent one, but through perfecting. At every limit, the actuality of that limit and the potentiality for the following limit together become a potentiality for the actuality of the following limit.

Second, descending motion, in which the subject proceeds from intensity to weakness and from perfection to imperfection, is unintelligible. It would entail some actuality's becoming a potentiality for its own potentiality—as if a human being were to move from humanity to animality, from animality to vegetative being, and so forth.

What appears to be weakening motion is therefore incidental motion following another, intensifying motion that conflicts with the supposed descending motion. Withering is an example.

Third, every motion, whatever it may be, is bounded by a beginning and an end. Each of its limits terminates on its two sides in potentiality without actuality and in actuality without potentiality; the whole has the same judgment as its parts. This does not conflict with the earlier statement that motion has neither a first nor a last part. What was denied there was its beginning with an actually indivisible part and its ending with such a part.

A part in this sense never passes from potentiality to actuality, nor does the specific quiddity abstracted from such a limit ever pass from potentiality to actuality.

[translation] The Arabic distinguishes parallel species (ʿarḍī, with a quiescent rāʾ) from accidental motions (ʿaraḍī, with a vowel on the rāʾ). “Putting on after putting on” preserves the author’s metaphor for cumulative substantial perfection.

[Textual emendation · 1428 AH editor; early reading preserved] The complete IX.9 authorial chapter was compared with the 1404 Qum facsimile, printed pp.210–212 (NYU canvases 214–216), and the later 1428 edition, vol.2 pp.107–110. The early print at p.211 and the 1417 base at p.265 both read «كما في القسم الأول» (as in the first kind). The 1428 editor explicitly records this copy reading and corrects it to «كما في القسم الثاني» at vol.2 p.108 n.4. The reader adopts the attributed later correction: stripping off and putting on belongs to the second, parallel kind, whereas the first, hierarchical kind is putting on after putting on. The English now says “as in the second kind.” The early facsimile does NOT attest الثاني. This resolves the previously unadopted internal conjecture; the original note identifier is preserved for continuity. No manuscript attestation or specialist certification is claimed.

[Grammatical recast · 1428 AH editor] The early 1404 facsimile p.211 and the 1417 base p.265 read «هي الموضوع بعينه ما تعاقبت». The 1428 editor, vol.2 p.108 n.3, recasts this as «هي بعينها موضوع ما تعاقبت». The reader adopts that disclosed grammatical recast; the claim that the same matter persists through the succession of forms is unchanged. The later note quotes its copies with «تعاقب» without a final feminine ت, whereas the directly inspected early facsimile and the current 1417 transcription have «تعاقبت». The collective copy report must not be substituted for the exact reading of either inspected witness. The English already conveys the same subject claim.

[Pronoun correction · 1428 AH editor] The 1404 early print p.212 and the 1417 base p.266 have the masculine possessive in «قوة لقوته» (base with vocalization: «قوّةً لقوّته»). The 1428 editor, vol.2 p.109 n.2, corrects the pronoun to the feminine «لقوّتها», referring to «فعليّة ما». The reading text adopts «قوّةً لقوّتها». The English already refers to an actuality’s own potentiality and is unchanged. This pronoun correction is separate from the editor’s philosophical objection to the argument against descending motion.

[Later editor’s philosophical objection · not authorial text] The editor disputes the argument against descending motion. A subject's coexisting potentiality, not its present actuality, can be actualized at a less perfect level. Such decline can therefore still be potentiality becoming actuality. He also questions treating withering merely as incidental motion. Preserve this disagreement alongside the author's argument. This is separately attributed later commentary; the authorial argument remains in the Arabic and English. The works invoked by the editor have not been independently collated here.

[Later editor’s philosophical objection · not authorial text] The editor invokes Bidāya and the author's Asfar gloss: motion as motion need not have a distinct enduring substrate; substantial motion can itself be a self-subsisting fluid substantial actuality. Keep this outside authorial text. This is separately attributed later commentary; the authorial argument remains in the Arabic and English. The works invoked by the editor have not been independently collated here.

N-IX-10 · The Agent of Motion: The Mover
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 266–268
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=266&type=c_fbook

It should be understood that, however motion is supposed, its mover is other than the moving thing.

If the motion is substantial, occurring in the essence of the thing that is truly moving, as previously explained, to suppose that the moving thing is itself the mover would be to suppose that a thing is the agent bringing itself into existence. The impossibility of this is necessary. The agent bringing motion into existence is therefore the agent bringing the moving thing into existence. It is a substance separate from matter, which brings the substantial form into existence and sustains matter through it. The form is a co-principle with the agent, as previously established.

If the motion is accidental and the accident inseparable from existence, the agent bringing the motion into existence is the agent of the moving subject, through the very production of that subject. No further production intervenes between the subject and the motion. If a production intervened, and the moving thing—which is material—were itself the agent of motion within itself, it would be an agent acting without the mediation of matter. But it was established in the discussion of cause and effect that material causes act only through the mediation of matter and the intervention of a positional relation between themselves and their effects. They act only upon what is outside themselves.

The agent of an inseparable concomitant of existence is thus the agent of that which entails it: a substance separate from matter that produces the form and the concomitant of its existence through a single production, and sustains matter through the form.

If the motion is accidental and the accident separable, the proximate agent of motion is nature, on the basis that the actions arising in each substantial species are ascribed to that species' nature.

To explain in detail: the subject either performs its actions uniformly or does not. The first is nature, defined as the principle of motion and rest in that in which it resides. The second is soul, which commands several natures and powers and employs them to obtain the action it wishes. In either case, its action may be congenial to it, such that, left to itself, it would perform it; this is natural motion. Alternatively, its action may be otherwise, as required by an opposing obstacle; this is forced motion.

On all these assumptions, the agent of motion is nature. In natural motion, nature initiates motion only when a congenial form has disappeared or an uncongenial configuration has arisen, so that it lacks a perfection it requires. It seeks that perfection and proceeds toward it through motion. Form is the agent of this motion, and matter its recipient.

In forced motion, the external coercer may cease while the forced motion persists. Since nature's ordinary agency has in fact been suspended, the agent can only be nature under compulsion.

As for motion belonging to the soul, the soul's commanding the different natures and powers in order to perfect itself through their actions is excellent evidence that the proximate agents in such motions are the natures and powers implanted in the organs.

[Witness confirmation] The phrase وقد بطلت فاعلية الطبيعة بالفعل is confirmed in 1404 p.213 (canvas217) and 1428 vol.2 p.113. The English addition ordinary remains an interpretation, not an independently attested replacement reading.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 212–214 (canvases 216–218); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 111–113. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.
