N-IX-11 · Time
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 268–271
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=268&type=c_fbook

Among the events before us, we find some realized after other events that precede them. Those that come afterward have a certain dependence upon those that come before: a dependence in which the earlier and the later do not coexist. This differs from other kinds of priority and posteriority, such as the priority of a cause or part of a cause over an effect. This is a necessary premise about which we have no doubt.

What we have supposed to be earlier is itself divisible into an earlier and a later in this same sense—that is, such that they do not coexist. Likewise, everything obtained through division and possessing the character of being earlier is divisible into an earlier and a later, without division coming to a halt.

There is therefore a continuous, non-stable quantity here. If it were not a quantity, there would be no division. If it were not continuous, the later would not be realized within what is earlier, or conversely; the two would instead be separate. In other words, the two parts of this quantity would have no common limit. If it were not non-stable, the earlier and the later would actually coexist.

Since quantity is an accident, it has a subject in which it resides. Yet whenever we remove motion from the case in question, this magnitude disappears, and whenever we posit motion, it is established. This is what we call “time.” Time therefore exists, and its quiddity is that of a continuous, non-stable magnitude occurring as an accident of motion.

Several matters become clear from what has preceded.

First, whenever we posit a motion or substitute one motion for another, this quantity called time is established. Every motion, whatever it may be, therefore has its own time, individuated through the individuation of that motion and measuring it. Nevertheless, we take the time of certain motions as a measure by which we measure other motions. Thus we take the time of daily motion as a measure for the other motions contained in universal and particular cosmic events, correlating them with the divisions we assign to this time: centuries, years, months, weeks, days, hours, minutes, seconds, and so forth.

Second, the relation of time to motion is like the relation of mathematical body to natural body: the relation of the determinate to the indeterminate.

Third, just as motion is divided into portions possessing common limits, with boundaries between them that exist only potentially—instantaneous determinations—so time is divided into portions possessing common limits, with boundaries between them that exist only potentially: instants. An instant is the limit of time, like a point, which is the limit of a line. It is something privative whose share in existence consists in its relation to that of which it is the limit.

From this it is clear that instants cannot follow immediately upon one another. An instant is only a privative boundary between two portions of time. Two things of this kind cannot occur unless a portion of time lies between them.

Fourth, things differ in their correspondence to time. Traversal motion corresponds to time immediately, and the parts of this motion are characterized by priority, posteriority, and the like consequently upon the parts of time being so characterized.

Every event existing in an instant, such as arrival, departure, contact, and separation, corresponds to an instant. Intermediate motion corresponds to the instant through traversal motion.

It is also clear that imagining an intermediate counterpart in time—the so-called flowing instant that traces temporal extension—is an imaginative, figurative conception. How could it be otherwise, when time is a quantity divisible in itself?

The comparison with a flowing unit that traces number through its repetition, or with a flowing point that traces a line, is misplaced. A unit is not a number: it traces number through its repetition, not through itself. A point is a privative limit, and a line's being composed of points is imaginary.

Fifth, time has no actually existent extremity in the sense of a part constituting its beginning or end that is indivisible along temporal extension. Otherwise, a magnitude would be composed of parts with no magnitude—the indivisible part—which is impossible. Time comes to an end on either side only through the ending of the motion in which it resides.

Sixth, nothing precedes time except through a non-temporal priority, such as the priority of the cause of existence, the cause of motion, and motion's subject over time.

Seventh, temporal earlierness and laterness do not obtain between two things unless there is a common time to which both correspond. Thus, if something temporally earlier is established in relation to a motion or a moving thing, this requires a common time between them. That entails a common motion, which in turn entails common matter between them.

Observation

Considering time together with motions—time being a changing magnitude—provides their measurement and the priority and posteriority that follow from it. Stable existents may also be considered together with changing things. This gives the coexistence of the stable with the changing, called “dahr.” The stable existent may be considered together with stable things, giving the coexistence of the stable with stable things and the coexistence of the universal stable existent with the stable things beneath it. This is called “sarmad.” Neither dahr nor sarmad contains priority or posteriority, because neither contains change or division.

It is said in the Asfār: “Existents that are neither motion nor in motion are not in time. Rather, their stability is considered together with changing things, and this coexistence is called dahr. Likewise, the coexistence of changing things with changing things, considered not with respect to their change but with respect to their stability—for nothing lacks some mode of stability, even if it is the stability of change—is also a coexistence in dahr. If stable things are considered together with stable things, this coexistence is sarmad. Neither this coexistence nor the preceding one has a corresponding priority and posteriority. There is nothing impossible in this, for neither priority nor posteriority is a correlative of coexistence such that coexistence would entail it.” End of quotation.

[translation] Dahr and sarmad are retained as technical terms for atemporal modes of coexistence; translating both simply as “eternity” would obscure the distinction made here.

[translation] In «والحركة التوسّطيّة منطبقةٌ عليه بواسطة القطعيّة», the pronoun «عليه» most directly refers to the immediately preceding «الآن» (instant), not the more distant «الزمان» (time). The English therefore says 'corresponds to the instant through traversal motion'. This is an internal grammatical/semantic translation correction against the 1417 AH Arabic digital text, not a printed-witness variant. The following discussion rejects an independently real 'flowing instant' as a constituent of time.

[English collation · Stage V] Corrected ‘time’ → ‘instant’ at «منطبقة عليه» after «الآن», based on direct local rereading of 1417 printed-digital Arabic pp.269–270. No claim of an independent 1404/1428 print collation or an expert attestation.

N-IX-12 · The Meaning of Speed and Slowness
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 271–273
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=271&type=c_fbook

We know speed and slowness by comparing motions with one another. Suppose two motions, one swift and one slow, along a course. If the course is the same, they differ in time: the time of the swift motion is shorter and that of the slow motion longer. If the time is the same, the course traversed by the swift motion is greater and that traversed by the slow motion less.

They are relational meanings realized through comparison. A slow motion becomes swift when compared with one slower than itself, and a swift motion becomes slow when compared with one swifter than itself.

If we suppose a sequence of motions successively increasing in speed, each intermediate motion, excluding the two extremes, is characterized by both speed and slowness: swift in comparison with one side and slow in comparison with the other. The two are thus relational attributes that do not stand in opposition, like length and shortness, or largeness and smallness.

The claim that slowness in motion results from intervening periods of rest is refuted by what has already been established: motion is continuous and admits division only potentially.

It has sometimes been said that speed and slowness are contraries. It is said in the Asfār: “The opposition between speed and slowness is not that of correlatives, since correlatives are mutually inseparable in both modes of existence, whereas these two are not mutually inseparable in either. Nor is their opposition one of affirmation and negation. If the two motions are equal in time, the swift traverses a distance that the slow does not; if equal in distance, the time of the slow is longer. One therefore has a deficiency in distance and the other a deficiency in time. There is no better reason to make one privative than to make the other so. Consequently, the only remaining opposition between them is contrariety.” End of quotation.

The difficulty is that they stipulate, for contrariety, the greatest possible difference between its two terms. This is not realized between speed and slowness: no motion is so swift that a swifter one cannot be supposed, and none so slow that a slower one cannot be supposed.

This concerns relational speed and slowness. Speed in the sense of running and flowing, however, is a property of motion without qualification, opposed to no slowness.

[translation] The “two modes of existence” in the quotation are mental and external existence. The final sentence uses “speed” in an extended sense of flow, distinct from comparative rate of motion.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 217–218 (canvases 221–222); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 119–121. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-IX-13 · Rest
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 273–274
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=273&type=c_fbook

Motion and rest cannot coexist in a body in the same respect at the same time. There is therefore an opposition between them. Motion is positive, for it has been established that it is the mode of flowing existence. Rest, however, is not a positive thing. If it were, it would be stable existence, which is actual in every respect. Yet stable existences—immaterial beings—are neither rest nor possessors of rest.

Motion is therefore positive and rest privative. Their opposition is consequently neither that of correlatives nor that of contraries. Nor are they contradictories, for then rest would apply to everything that is not motion, such as separate intellects, which are actual in every respect, and their acts. Rest is thus the absence of motion in what is capable of motion. Their opposition is that of privation and possession.

It should nevertheless be understood that rest has no instance in any material substance, because, as established, their existence flows. Nor does it have an instance in any of their accidents, which follow their substantial subjects in motion because they subsist through them.

There is, however, a relative rest with which material subjects may be characterized in relation to the second motions occurring in the four accidental categories: quantity, quality, place, and position.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 218–219 (canvases 222–223); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 122–123. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «فليس تقابُلُها تقابُلَ التضايف والتضادّ.» → «فليس تقابُلُهما تقابُلَ التضايف والتضادّ.» [E2]. Evidence: 1404 p.218/c222; 1428 p.122. Earlier printed reading: «فليس تقابلهما تقابل التضايف والتضاد». Restore dual pronoun attested in both 1404 and 1428; retain old base reading in apparatus.

N-IX-14 · The Divisions of Motion
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 274–276
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=274&type=c_fbook

Motion is divided according to the division of the six things upon which its essence depends.

It is divided according to the starting point and endpoint: motion from one place to another, from sitting to standing, from one colour to another, or a body's motion from one magnitude to another.

It is divided according to the category: motion in quality, quantity, place, or position.

It is divided according to the subject: the motion of a plant, an animal, or a human being.

It is divided according to time: motion by night or by day, in summer or in winter.

It is divided according to the agent: natural motion, forced motion, and motion belonging to the soul.

They said that the proximate agent in all these cases is nature, and that the soul's production of motion takes place by commanding natural powers, as previously indicated.

They also said that what intervenes between nature and motion is the principle of inclination that nature produces in the moving thing. The detailed account belongs to natural philosophy.

Conclusion

Just as “power” is applied to the principle of receptivity, so it is applied to the principle of action, especially when that principle is strong and intense. Thus “natural powers” denotes the principles of natural effects, and “powers of the soul” the principles of the soul's effects, such as sight, hearing, imagination, and others. When this active power is accompanied by knowledge and volition, it is called an animal's capacity to act. It is an efficient cause whose causality becomes complete—such that the act necessarily follows—only through external conditions: the presence of receptive matter, the establishment of a position appropriate to the act, the fitness of the instruments of action, and so forth. When these come together, the causality is complete and the act becomes necessary.

This shows the error in some people's defining capacity without qualification as “that together with which both acting and refraining are admissible.” The relation of acting and refraining to an agent is one of admissibility and possibility only when the agent is part of a complete cause. Taken alone, as an incomplete cause, the act ascribed to it does not become necessary through it. As for an agent complete in its agency, which is alone a complete cause—such as the Necessary Existent, exalted is He—there is no meaning to its relation to acting and refraining being one of possibility, that is, to the two relations being equal.

An objection is that the necessity of His act would entail His being necessitated and compelled to act, which conflicts with capacity.

The response is that this necessity attaches to the act from Him, exalted is He, and is His effect. It is unintelligible that a thing's effect, dependent upon it in existence, should influence the essence of the thing acting. Nor is there another agent influencing Him and making Him compelled to act.

Likewise, the claim that “an act's admissibility depends upon its being preceded by temporal nonexistence, so that an act not preceded by temporal nonexistence is impossible” is false. Its error is that it rests upon making origination, rather than contingency, the reason for needing a cause. This was refuted in the discussions of cause and effect. Moreover, it is contradicted by time itself: for the production of time to be preceded by its temporal nonexistence would establish time before itself, which is necessarily impossible.

Likewise, the claim that capacity arises only together with the act, and that there is no capacity for an act before it, is false. Its error is that its proponents regard capacity as the admissibility of acting and refraining. If someone refrains from an act for a time and then acts, it was true before the act that both acting and refraining were admissible for him; this is capacity. Moreover, their claim contradicts their admission that the act depends upon capacity, because the accompaniment of capacity and act conflicts with one depending upon the other.

[translation] Quwwa is rendered “power” here; qudra is rendered “capacity to act” or “capacity.” The chapter extends the account of motion to the relation between capacity, a complete cause, and the necessity of its effect.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 219–221 (canvases 223–225); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 124–126. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Recorded variant; body retained: early «وخاصة إذ كانت قوية شديدة»; base «وخاصّة إذا كانت قويّةً شديدةً»; later «وخاصّة إذا كانت قويّة شديدة». Evidence: 1404 p.220/c224; 1428 p.125 n.1. Editor explicitly substitutes إذا for إذ; the current base already has إذا.

Adopted reading: «يتوقّف تمام علّيّتها بحيث يجب معها الفعل إلى اُمور خارجة» → «يتوقّف تمام علّيّتها بحيث يجب معها الفعل على اُمور خارجة» [L2]. Evidence: 1404 p.220/c224; 1428 p.125. Priority, Posteriority, Beginninglessness, and Origination

N-X-01 · Priority and Posteriority
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 279–282
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=279&type=c_fbook

It appears that the first meaning of priority and posteriority to become known was their sensible meaning. A starting point is supposed to which two things share a relation: one possesses whatever relation the other possesses, but not conversely. The attribute belonging to the former is called priority, and that belonging to the latter posteriority. For example, take the prayer niche of a mosque as the starting point. The prayer leader and the follower share a relation to it: whatever proximity to the niche the follower possesses, the leader possesses, but not conversely. The leader is therefore prior and the follower posterior.

It is evident that the attributes of priority and posteriority differ with the supposed starting point. In this example, the leader is prior and the follower posterior if the niche is the starting point. If the doorway is taken as the starting point, the reverse obtains: the follower is prior and the leader posterior. Nor does it matter whether the ordering is conventional and instituted, as in the example above, or natural. Suppose, for example, body, then plant, then animal, then human being. If body is the starting point, plant is prior and animal posterior; if human being is the starting point, animal is prior and plant posterior. This is called priority and posteriority according to order.

They then generalized this meaning, applying it to nobility, excellence, baseness, and similar cases of superiority in non-sensible respects, as in the priority of the knowledgeable over the ignorant or the brave over the cowardly. If, for example, the species with the effects of its perfection is taken as the starting point, the knowledgeable and the ignorant, or the brave and the cowardly, differ in their relation to it. These are called priority and posteriority in nobility.

They also moved to temporal priority and posteriority. Two portions of time, such as today and yesterday, share in bearing the potentiality for later portions. But whatever potentiality one of them—today—bears, the other—yesterday—also bears, and not conversely. Yesterday bears the potentiality for today, whereas today bears its own actuality; actuality does not coexist with potentiality. Hence the two portions of time do not coexist in actual existence.

There is therefore priority and posteriority among the parts of time such that the prior does not coexist with the posterior, unlike other kinds of priority and posteriority.

Likewise, events that are motions corresponding to time possess temporal priority and posteriority through the mediation of time, which is their determination, just as a natural body possesses the three dimensions through the mediation of mathematical body, which is its determination.

This alerted them to further kinds of real priority and posteriority in existence. They investigated them and enumerated nine kinds, including both those constituted by consideration and those that are real.

The first, second, and third are priority and posteriority according to order, nobility, and time, already discussed.

The fourth is priority and posteriority by nature: the priority of an incomplete cause over its effect, where removing the cause removes the effect but the cause's existence does not necessitate the effect; and the posteriority of the effect to it.

The fifth is priority and posteriority by causality: the priority of a complete cause, through whose existence the effect becomes necessary, over its effect; and the effect's posteriority to it.

The sixth is priority and posteriority in quiddative constitution: the priority of the parts of a quiddity—genus and differentia—over the quiddity, and its posteriority to them, on the assumption of the primacy of quiddity.

These last three—priority and posteriority by nature, by causality, and in quiddative constitution—are called intrinsic priority and posteriority.

The seventh is priority and posteriority in dahr: the priority of a complete cause over its effect and the posteriority of its effect to it, considered not with respect to the cause's necessitating and bestowing the effect's existence, as in causal priority and posteriority, but with respect to its existence being distinct and separate from the effect's existence, and the effect's nonexistence being established at the level of the cause's existence. An example is the priority of the realm of intellectual immateriality over the realm of matter. This division was added by the verifying master al-Dāmād.

The eighth is priority and posteriority in literal and figurative attribution: two things share in being characterized by an attribute, but one possesses it intrinsically and the other incidentally. What possesses it intrinsically is prior, in this sense, to what possesses it incidentally, and the latter is posterior. An example is the priority of existence over the quiddity existent through it, on the view that existence is primary in being existent and realized, while quiddity exists through it incidentally. This division was added by Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified.

The ninth is priority and posteriority by existential reality: the priority of the existence of a complete cause over the existence of its effect [and the posteriority of the effect's existence to it]. This differs from priority and posteriority by causality. It too was added by Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified.

It is said in the Asfār: “In general, the existence of every necessitating cause precedes the existence of its intrinsic effect with this kind of priority. The philosophers defined the efficient cause as that which acts upon something other than the agent. Thus the priority of the agent's essence over the effect's essence is priority by causality. But the priority of existence over existence is another priority, distinct from causal priority, for between them there is neither influencing and being influenced nor agency and being acted upon. Their condition is instead that of a single thing possessing modes and phases, undergoing development from one phase to another.” End of quotation.

[translation] “Quiddative constitution” renders tajawhur in this classification; its example is the relation of genus and differentia to the constituted quiddity. “By existential reality” renders bi-l-ḥaqq, distinguished from bi-l-ḥaqīqa wa-l-majāz, literal and figurative attribution.

[translation] Al-Dāmād is Mīr Dāmād; Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn is Mullā Ṣadrā. Dahr is the technical atemporal relation discussed at IX.11.

[Editorial supplement] In the ninth division, 1404 p.226 and the base Arabic lack an explicit reciprocal clause. The 1428 editor, vol.2 p.133 n.1, adds [وتأخر وجود معلولها عنه] and states that it is not in the original. The English reciprocal clause is therefore shown in brackets as an explanatory supplement; it is not an authorial restoration.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 223–226 (canvases 227–230); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 127–133. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-X-02 · The Criterion of Priority and Posteriority in Each Division
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 282–285
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=282&type=c_fbook

As indicated in the preceding chapter, the criterion is that which the prior and the posterior share, of which the prior possesses something that the posterior does not, while the posterior possesses nothing that the prior does not also possess.

The criterion of priority and posteriority according to order is the relation to a specified starting point. In sensible order, the prayer leader and follower share a relation to the supposed starting point, whether the niche or the doorway. The leader is prior if the niche is the starting point, and the follower prior if it is the doorway. Likewise, in an ordering of genera and species, each genus is prior to its species if the highest genus is the supposed starting point, and each species prior to its genus if the reverse is assumed.

The criterion of priority and posteriority in nobility is that two things share a meaning capable of being characterized by excellence and distinction, or by vice. The brave and the cowardly, for example, share humanity, which is capable of possessing the virtue of courage. The brave possesses what the cowardly possesses, but not conversely. The priority of the baser over another in baseness is analogous.

The criterion of temporal priority and posteriority is that two supposed parts of time share a lapsing, passing existence in which potentiality and actuality are interwoven, such that the actuality of one depends upon its potentiality being present together with the other. The part accompanied by the potentiality for the other part is prior; the part for which this is otherwise is posterior. Today and tomorrow, for example, share a non-stable quantitative existence in which tomorrow's actuality depends upon its potentiality being realized together with today. When tomorrow actually exists, its potentiality has ceased and today has passed. Today is therefore temporally prior and tomorrow temporally posterior.

Through this same criterion, priority and posteriority obtain among temporal events through the mediation of time, since those events are motions possessing times.

The criterion of priority and posteriority by nature is existence. The prior is distinguished by the later's existence depending upon it, such that, if the prior were not realized, the posterior would not be realized, but not conversely. This is the case with the priority of an incomplete cause: removing it removes the effect, although its existence does not entail the effect's existence.

The Master of Illumination is reported to have said that temporal priority and posteriority belong to priority and posteriority by nature, since they ultimately amount to the later part's existence depending upon the earlier part's existence, such that it ceases if the latter is removed.

It was objected that the two are different kinds. In natural priority the prior and posterior may coexist, whereas in temporal priority they cannot. Moreover, priority and posteriority belong intrinsically to the parts of time.

The truth is that there is no way to deny that temporal priority and posteriority rest upon existential dependence between the two parts. Yet because this existence is non-stable, with every part accompanied by the potentiality for the following part, the two cannot coexist: a thing's potentiality cannot coexist with its actuality. What can be accepted of the assertion that priority and posteriority are intrinsic to time is that they are inseparable from its lapsing, non-stable existence, in which potentiality and actuality differ.

Whoever wishes to reduce temporal priority to natural priority must therefore explain natural priority as that in which existential dependence is found, and then divide it into cases allowing the prior and posterior to coexist—as with an incomplete cause and its effect—and cases not allowing this—as with some parts of time preceding others.

The criterion of priority and posteriority by causality is the complete cause's and its effect's sharing necessity of existence, with the necessity of the cause—the prior—being intrinsic, and the necessity of the effect—the posterior—being through another.

The criterion of priority and posteriority in quiddative constitution is their sharing in the establishment of quiddity. The posterior depends upon the prior for its establishment, as a complete quiddity depends upon its parts.

The criterion of priority and posteriority in dahr is that a universal level of existence shares, with what is above or below it, occurrence in concrete reality, while concretely depending upon what is above it, or having what is below it depend upon it, in such a way that the two do not coexist. The nonexistence of what depends is included at the level of that upon which it depends. Examples are the priority of the world of separate intellects over the imaginal world, and the priority of the imaginal world over the material world.

The criterion of priority and posteriority in literal attribution is their sharing establishment in a sense broad enough to include both literal and figurative establishment. Literal establishment belongs to the prior, figurative to the posterior—as in existence's priority over quiddity through its primacy.

The criterion of priority and posteriority by existential reality is their sharing existence in a sense broad enough to include both independent and linking existence. The cause's existence is prior through its independence, and the effect's existence is posterior through its being linking existence.

[translation] The Master of Illumination is Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī. The imaginal world (ʿālam al-mithāl) is an ontological realm of immaterial forms, not simply private imagination.

[Textual variant · 1428 AH revised edition] The 1404 Qum facsimile, printed p. 228 (NYU canvas 232), and the 1417 transcription, p. 284, read «عدم التوقّف» (non-dependence). The 1428 revised edition, vol. 2 pp. 136–137, prints «عدم المتوقّف» (the nonexistence of what depends), without a footnote identifying this particular change. The Arabic and English follow that attested later-edition reading; the earlier reading is preserved here. This is a later printed variant, not an independently verified manuscript reading.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 226–229 (canvases 230–233); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 134–137. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Recorded variant; body retained: early «لوجوده المقتضي غير القار»; base «لوجوده المتقضّي غير القارّ»; later «لوجوده ، المتقضّي غير القارّ». Evidence: 1404 p.228/c232; 1428 p.136 n.2. Editor reports المقتضي and corrects to المتقضي. Base already follows the corrected form; no new patch.

N-X-03 · Coexistence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 285–286
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=285&type=c_fbook

Coexistence is two things' sharing a meaning without differing in the perfection and deficiency constituted by priority and posteriority. Yet not every pair lacking a certain kind of priority and posteriority coexists in that kind. Separate substances have neither temporal priority and posteriority nor temporal coexistence. Things coexisting in time must be temporal things capable of temporal priority and posteriority. If they share a temporal meaning without priority and posteriority within it, they coexist in it.

This makes clear that the opposition of coexistence to priority and posteriority is that of privation and possession. Coexistence is two things' sharing a meaning without differing in priority and posteriority, while being capable of priority and posteriority in that meaning. Priority and posteriority are positive determinations, and coexistence is privative.

Coexistence in order is exemplified, in sensible order, by two followers standing behind the prayer leader in relation to the supposed starting point in the mosque. In an ordering of species and genera, it is exemplified by two species or two genera under a genus, in relation to the species or genus in question.

Coexistence in nobility is exemplified by two brave people equal in their disposition.

Coexistence in time is exemplified by two motions occurring in the very same time. There is no coexistence among the parts of time itself, since any two of its parts necessarily have priority and posteriority.

Coexistence by nature is exemplified by two equal parts in relation to the whole.

Coexistence by causality is exemplified by two effects of one complete cause. There is no coexistence between two complete causes in this respect, because they cannot combine upon a single effect. Coexistence in literal and figurative attribution, and coexistence by existential reality, follow the same pattern as coexistence by causality.

Coexistence in dahr is exemplified by two parts of a level of concrete reality, if multiplicity is supposed within that level.

[translation] Maʿiyya is rendered “coexistence,” but the classification extends beyond simultaneity: it includes parity in order, nobility, causality, and other respects.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 229–230 (canvases 233–234); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 138–139. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

1428 p.138 n.3: Sabzawari’s argument against treating coexistence as simply positive is an additional quoted discussion, not early authorial prose.

1428 p.139 n.1: The editor argues that the author’s positive wording sharing a meaning conflicts with privation and recommends a negative definition. This must remain commentary, not a replacement of the authorial definition.

1428 p.139 nn.2–4: Adds examples of accidents and matter/form and says only God is a complete cause; separate from authorial text.

No new Arabic or English body change is adopted from this completed review. Existing body readings and earlier adopted decisions are preserved.

N-X-04 · The Meaning and Divisions of Beginninglessness and Origination
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 286–287
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=286&type=c_fbook

When the elapsed duration of one thing's existence is greater than the elapsed duration of another's—as when fifty years of Zayd's life have passed and forty years of ʿAmr's—ordinary people call the one with the longer duration “old” and the one with the shorter duration “recent.” The meaning obtained from this is that the old thing existed during a time when the recent thing did not yet exist. In other words, the recent thing's existence is preceded by nonexistence during a time when the old thing existed, whereas the old thing is otherwise.

When these two meanings are generalized and taken in a real sense, they become essential attributes of the existent insofar as it is existent. The existent without qualification is divided into them, and their investigation becomes philosophical.

The existent is therefore divided into the beginningless and the originated. The beginningless is that whose existence is not preceded by nonexistence; the originated is that whose existence is preceded by nonexistence.

Of the previously mentioned meanings and kinds of precedence, four can properly be included in the definitions of origination and beginninglessness: temporal precedence, causal precedence, precedence in dahr, and precedence by existential reality.

There are consequently four divisions of beginninglessness and origination: temporal beginninglessness and origination; causal beginninglessness and origination, commonly called intrinsic; beginninglessness and origination by existential reality; and beginninglessness and origination in dahr. We shall discuss each in detail.

[translation] Qidam and ḥudūth begin here with ordinary “oldness” and “recentness,” then acquire technical senses. “Beginninglessness” must therefore be read according to the specified kind of precedence, not invariably as infinite elapsed time.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 230–231 (canvases 234–235); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 140–141. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

1428 pp.140–141 notes: Adds equivalent formulations of the dichotomy and references to Sadr for existential-reality and Dammad for dahr origination. No missing authorial material.

No new Arabic or English body change is adopted from this completed review. Existing body readings and earlier adopted decisions are preserved.

N-X-05 · Temporal Beginninglessness and Origination
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 287–288
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=287&type=c_fbook

Temporal origination is a thing's existence being preceded by temporal nonexistence: its occurring after it was not, with a posteriority that does not coexist with priority. Nonexistence is temporal only if the existence opposed to it is temporal. This means that the thing's existence is gradual, corresponding to a portion of time preceded by another portion to which its nonexistence corresponds.

Opposed to origination in this sense is temporal beginninglessness: a thing's existence not being preceded by temporal nonexistence. This entails that the thing exists in, and corresponds to, every portion of time supposed before a given portion.

These meanings apply only to temporal things contained in and corresponding to time: motions and moving things. Time itself is not characterized by temporal origination or beginninglessness, because time has no other time to which its existence could correspond and in which it could be either preceded or not preceded by nonexistence.

Nevertheless, since time is intrinsically characterized by mutually non-coexistent earlierness and laterness, every part of it has its existence preceded by its nonexistence, whose instance is every preceding part. Every part of time is therefore temporally originated in this sense, and so is the whole. Time is the non-stable magnitude of motion, which is a thing's gradual passage from potentiality to actuality. The actuality of time's existence is accordingly preceded by the potentiality for its existence; this is temporal origination.

Origination in the sense of time's existence being preceded by a nonexistence outside its existence, with a precedence in which the before does not coexist with the after, would suppose temporal earlierness being realized without time being realized.

This is what is indicated by the saying attributed to the First Teacher: “Whoever asserts the origination of time has asserted its beginninglessness without being aware of it.”

Observation

It was established in the discussions of potentiality and actuality that every individual motion has its own individual time, specific to it and measuring it. Among these is universal time, which belongs to the universal substantial motion through which the matter of the material world moves in its forms. There are also the distinct times belonging to and measuring distinct accidental motions.

The time by which ordinary people measure the world's events is the time of daily motion. Applying events to it is intended to establish their relations to one another in priority and posteriority, length and shortness, and the like.

Remembering this, know that the account we have given of temporal origination and beginninglessness applies to every time, of whatever kind. Do not overlook this.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 231–232 (canvases 235–236); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 142–144. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «متّصفاً بالذات بالقبليّة والبعديّة بالذات غير المجامعتين» → «متّصفاً بالذات بالقبليّة والبعديّة غير المجامعتين» [L1]. Evidence: 1404 p.231/c235; 1428 p.143 n.2. Earlier printed reading: «متصفا بالذات بالقبلية والبعدية بالذات». Adopt explicit 1428 deletion of repeated بالذات, preserving the full early reading in apparatus.

N-X-06 · Intrinsic Origination and Beginninglessness
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 288–289
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=288&type=c_fbook

Intrinsic origination is a thing's existence being preceded by the nonexistence established at the level of its essence. Intrinsic beginninglessness is its contrary.

They said that everything possessing a quiddity is intrinsically originated. Their argument is that every contingent thing deserves nonexistence through itself and existence through another. What belongs through itself is prior to what belongs through another; its existence is therefore preceded by nonexistence through itself.

It was objected that, if a contingent thing intrinsically required nonexistence, it would be impossible. Rather, because it is contingent, it is true of it in itself neither that it exists nor that it does not exist. Just as it deserves existence through an external cause, so it deserves nonexistence through an external cause. Neither existence nor nonexistence is therefore prior to the other in relation to it, and its existence through another is not preceded by its nonexistence through itself.

The reply is that what is intended is its not deserving existence through itself, as a straightforward negation, not as the affirmation of a negative predicate. This meaning belongs to it in itself before the existence coming from another.

Another argument: every contingent thing has a quiddity different from its existence; otherwise it would be necessary, not contingent. Whatever has a quiddity different from its existence cannot receive its existence from its quiddity. Otherwise, the quiddity would exist before obtaining its existence, which is impossible. Its existence is therefore received from another, and is intrinsically preceded by another. Everything of this kind is intrinsically originated.

It follows that what is intrinsically beginningless is intrinsically necessary in existence, and also that what is intrinsically beginningless has no quiddity.

[translation] The reply distinguishes denying that a quiddity deserves existence by itself from affirming that it positively deserves nonexistence. This is the logical distinction between a negative proposition and an affirmative proposition with a negative predicate.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 232–233 (canvases 236–237); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 145–146. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

1428 pp.145–146 notes: Attributes arguments, objection and reply to Razi and Sadr; quotes Sabzawari and the author’s Asfar gloss distinguishing the two definitions of intrinsic origination. This material is supplementary, not an omitted Nihaya paragraph.

No new Arabic or English body change is adopted from this completed review. Existing body readings and earlier adopted decisions are preserved.

N-X-07 · Origination and Beginninglessness by Existential Reality
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 289–290
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=289&type=c_fbook

Origination by existential reality is the existence of an effect being preceded by the existence of its complete cause, considering the relation of priority and posteriority between the two existences, rather than between the existent quiddity of the effect and the cause, as in intrinsic origination.

This is because genuine establishment and realization in concrete reality belong to existence, not to quiddity. To the cause—and especially to the unrestricted Necessary Cause in which the matter ultimately terminates—belong only independence and self-sufficiency. To the effect's existence belong only intrinsic dependence upon the cause's existence, intrinsic poverty in relation to it, and subsistence through it.

It is necessary that what is independent, self-sufficient, and subsistent through itself precedes what is dependent, poor, and subsistent through another. The effect's existence is therefore originated in this sense, preceded by the existence of its cause; the cause's existence is beginningless relative to it and prior to it.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 233–234 (canvases 237–238); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 147. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-X-08 · Origination and Beginninglessness in Dahr
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 290–290
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=290&type=c_fbook

Origination in dahr is an existent, caused quiddity being preceded by its nonexistence established at the level of its cause. Its nonexistence, with its delimitation, is abstracted in relation to its cause, even though the cause possesses the perfection of its existence in a higher and nobler manner. Its cause is therefore prior to it with a priority that does not coexist with the effect's posteriority, insofar as a thing's nonexistence does not coexist with its existence.

Beginninglessness in dahr is the cause's not being preceded by the effect with this kind of precedence.

Our explanation makes clear that the establishment of the effect's nonexistence at the level of the cause does not conflict with the established principle that the cause is the fullness and perfection of its effect's existence. What is denied at the level of the cause's existence is the effect with its delimitation, not its existence insofar as it is existence received from the cause. The effect with its delimitation must differ from its cause. This entails that it is true to deny of the cause that it is the effect with its delimitation; otherwise, the two would be identical.

There nevertheless remains the question of the claim that the priority and posteriority in this origination and beginninglessness cannot coexist.

[Explicit later-editor correction] The 1404 Qum print p. 234 (canvas 238; high-resolution detail re-inspected during Stage X) reads فعليّتها, as does the base. The 1428 editor, vol. 2 p. 148 n. 3, explicitly corrects this to فعلّتها, “then its cause.” The adopted Arabic and existing English causal sense follow that disclosed later correction. The inherited claim that فعلّتها was visible in the early print is withdrawn.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 234 (canvases 238); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 148–150. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «فعلّيّتها قبلَها» → «فعلّتُها قبلَها» [L1]. Evidence: 1404 p.234/c238, high-resolution detail; 1428 p.148 n.3. Earlier printed reading: «فعليّتها قبلها». Adopt the explicit later-editor reading its cause, with early فعليتها retained and misattributed existing note corrected.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

1428 p.149 nn.1–2: Explains the identity counterfactual and attributes the non-coexistence claim to Dammad.

Although the heading uses “intellect” (al-ʿaql)—a term conventionally applied to universal rather than particular apprehension—the investigation extends to both.

N-XI-01 · The definition of knowledge, its primary division, and some of its properties
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 293–297
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=293&type=c_fbook

The existence of knowledge is immediately certain to us through our own awareness, and its concept is likewise self-evident to us. What we seek in the discussion of this chapter is only to identify its most distinctive properties.

We say: it was established in the discussion of mental existence that we have some knowledge of things outside us, in the sense that they are present to us through their very quiddities, rather than through their external existences upon which their external effects follow. This is one division of knowledge, called “acquired knowledge.”

Another instance of knowledge is the knowledge each of us has of his own self, to which he points and which he expresses by “I.” He is never distracted from himself or unaware of witnessing his own self, even if we suppose that he is unaware of his body, its parts, and its organs.

His knowledge of himself does not consist in the quiddity of his self being present to him conceptually, as acquired knowledge. For a concept present in the mind, however it is supposed, does not in itself exclude being true of many things; it becomes individuated only through external existence. But what he witnesses of himself and expresses by “I” is intrinsically an individual thing, incapable of being shared by many. It has already been established that individuation is through existence. Our knowledge of ourselves therefore consists in our selves being present to us through their external existence, which is identical with our individual existence upon which effects follow.

Furthermore, if what was present to our selves when we knew them was the quiddity of our selves rather than their existence, while our existence already has a quiddity subsisting through it, one existence would have two quiddities existing through it. This would be the coincidence of two identicals, which is impossible. Thus our knowledge of ourselves consists in their presence to us and their not being absent from us through their external existence, rather than through their quiddity alone. This is another division of knowledge, called “knowledge by presence.”

The division of knowledge into these two divisions is exhaustive. The known is present to the knower either through its quiddity, which is acquired knowledge, or through its existence, which is knowledge by presence.

This is the division into acquired knowledge and knowledge by presence to which an initial consideration leads. Deeper consideration, however, shows that acquired knowledge too ultimately rests upon knowledge by presence.

To explain: a cognitive form, however it is supposed, is immaterial and devoid of potentiality. It is evident that, insofar as it is known, it is actual and contains absolutely no potentiality for anything. If any change in it were supposed, the new form would be distinct from the form previously known. Were the cognitive form material, it would not exclude change.

Moreover, were it material, it would not lack matter’s inseparable properties: divisibility, time, and place.

Knowledge, insofar as it is knowledge, does not admit of being halved or divided into thirds, for example. If it were impressed in corporeal matter, it would divide with the division of that matter. Nor is it restricted to a time. If it were material—and every material thing is in motion—it would change as time changed. Nor can it be pointed out in a place. If it were material, it would occupy a place.

You may object: the cognitive form’s indivisibility insofar as it is knowledge does not conflict with its being impressed in a body, such as a part of the brain, and being divisible incidentally, through its substrate. A quality, such as a color occurring on the surface of a body, likewise excludes division insofar as it is a quality, yet is divisible incidentally through its substrate. Why, then, may the cognitive form not be material, impressed in a substrate, and divisible incidentally through the division of that substrate—especially given the familiar view that knowledge is a psychic quality?

Moreover, the correspondence of knowledge to time, particularly in sensory and imaginative knowledge, should not be doubted: consider the sensation of material activities at the time of their existence.

Moreover, there is no doubt that certain parts of the brain are specially associated with knowledge, which functions properly when they do and is impaired when they are impaired, as is accepted in medicine. The cognitive form therefore has a place, just as it has a time.

We reply: there is no doubt, as stated, that the cognitive form excludes and does not admit division insofar as it is knowledge.

But there is likewise no doubt that its being divided incidentally through the division of a substrate, such as a neural part, is false. We sense and imagine forms vastly greater than the neural part supposed to be their substrate: the sky in all its reaches, the earth in all its regions, lofty mountains, broad deserts, and swelling seas. It is impossible for the large to be impressed in the small.

The claim that “the apprehension of largeness and smallness in a cognitive form occurs only by comparing the parts of the cognitive form with one another” is of no help. What is witnessed is the large thing in its largeness, rather than the universal quantitative ratio between the large and the small—for example, that the ratio between them is one hundred to one.

The sensed or imagined cognitive form, with its magnitude, therefore subsists in its own right in the realm of the soul, without being impressed in a neural part or in some other material thing, and without being divided incidentally through the division of such a thing. Mentally pointing to some parts of what is known and separating them from the other parts is like pointing to some parts of the sensed or imagined Zayd and then to some other parts. This is not division at all. It is turning away from the original cognitive form and bringing about two other forms.

Since the cognitive form is not impressed in a neural part and is not divided incidentally through its division, the connection of the cognitive form to the neural part and to the activity it performs during apprehension is a preparatory connection. That is, the activity performed by the neural part prepares the soul for the presence and manifestation, within its own realm, of the particular cognitive form with the particular characteristics of the known.

Likewise, the temporal concurrence that appears between our apprehensions and time is really between time and the preparatory material activity performed by the soul in the organ of apprehension, rather than between time and the cognitive form insofar as it is knowledge. Evidence for this is that we often apprehend something known, retain it, and then recall that very same thing after many years have passed, without any alteration. Had it been restricted to time, it would have changed as time changed.

What has been established is that the cognitive form, whatever it may be, is immaterial and devoid of potentiality. Being such, its existence is stronger than that of the material object upon which sense falls and at which imagination and intellection terminate, and it possesses the effects of its immaterial existence. As for the effects of its external material existence, which we suppose to be the object of apprehension, they are not effects of what is truly known and present to the apprehender, such that they should or should not follow upon it. Rather, a misapprehension leads the apprehender to suppose that what is present to him during apprehension is the form externally attached to matter. He therefore seeks its external effects, fails to find them accompanying it, and judges that what is known is the quiddity without the external effects following upon it.

Accordingly, what is known in acquired knowledge of something with a certain attachment to matter is an immaterial existent that is an efficient principle of that thing and possesses its perfection. It is present to the apprehender through its external existence, and this is knowledge by presence. This is followed by the apprehender’s transition to the quiddity of that thing and to the effects that follow upon it externally. In other words, acquired knowledge is an intellectual consideration to which the intellect is compelled. It is taken from an object known by presence: an immaterial existent, imaginal or intellectual, present to the apprehender through its external existence, even though apprehended from afar.

Let us return to our discussion of the definition of knowledge. We say: there is no doubt that knowledge occurs and exists for the knower.

This is not just any sort of occurrence. It is the occurrence of something actual with pure actuality, containing absolutely no potentiality for anything. We find through immediate awareness that the cognitive form, considered as such, has no potentiality for another form and does not admit change from the actuality it possesses. It is therefore the occurrence of something immaterial, free of the deficiencies of potentiality. We call this “presence.”

The presence of one thing to another is its occurrence for that other in such a way that it is fully actual, without an attachment to matter that would leave it deficient with respect to some of its perfections that remain potential.

The presence of knowledge to the knower requires that the knower too be complete and actual in itself, without deficiency in respect of some of the perfections possible for it. This means that it is immaterial and devoid of potentiality. Knowledge is thus the occurrence of something immaterial for something immaterial. Or, if you wish, you may say: it is the presence of one thing to another.

[translation] In the paragraph beginning “What has been established,” wahm is rendered “misapprehension” in the context of mistakenly identifying the form present to cognition with the externally material form. It is distinguished here from takhayyul, rendered “imagination” earlier in the same paragraph.

N-XI-02 · The union of the knower with the known, termed the union of the intellect with the intelligible
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 298–301
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=298&type=c_fbook

One thing’s knowledge of another is the occurrence of the known—that is, the cognitive form—for the knower, as explained above. A thing’s occurrence is its existence, and its existence is the thing itself. Knowledge is therefore identical with what is known per se. A necessary consequence of the known’s occurrence for the knower and presence to it is that the knower is united with it, whether it is known by presence or through acquired knowledge. If the object of acquired knowledge subsists in its own right, its existence is for itself. Yet it is also for the knower. The knower must therefore be united with the known, since it is necessarily impossible for a thing to exist both for itself and for another. If it is something whose existence is for another—namely, its subject—and is also for the knower, then the knower is united with its subject. What exists for another is united with that other, and is therefore united with whatever that other is united with.

The same reasoning applies to what is known by presence and the one who knows it.

You may object: it was established in the discussions of mental existence that to say knowledge belongs to the category of the known means that the concept of that category is included in the knowledge. That is, the category is predicated of it by primary predication, rather than by common predication, which is the criterion for a quiddity’s falling under a category and for effects to follow upon it, including the existence’s being for itself or for another. A mental substance, insofar as it is mental, is therefore not a substance by common predication existing for itself; nor is a mental accident, insofar as it is mental, an accident by common predication existing for another.

In brief, there is no sense in the union of the intellect, which is an external existent upon which effects follow, with the mental intelligible, which is a mental concept upon which those effects do not follow.

As for knowledge by presence, either what is known is the knower itself, as in our knowledge of ourselves, or it is not. In the latter case, what is known is either a cause of the knower, an effect of the knower, or, together with the knower, an effect of a third thing. In a thing’s knowledge of itself, what is known is identical with the knower; there is no multiplicity there for union to apply. This is evident. In a cause’s knowledge of its effect, or an effect’s knowledge of its cause, however, there is no doubt that cause and effect must be distinct. Otherwise a thing would precede itself in existence and be posterior to itself in existence, which is self-evidently impossible.

As for one of the two effects of a third cause knowing the other, their necessary distinction in individuality excludes union. Moreover, union would entail that all immaterial beings—each of them knowing all and known by all—were a single individual.

We reply: the objection concerning acquired knowledge is answered by what was established above, that all acquired knowledge ultimately rests upon knowledge by presence. What is then present to the knower as its object is an immaterial existent through its external existence, which is either for itself or for another.

As for the objection concerning knowledge by presence, recall that an existent which is an effect can be considered in two ways. First, it may be considered in itself, disregarding its cause. It then has a contingent quiddity and exists in itself, excluding nonexistence from its quiddity; predicates may be predicated of it, and it may be predicated of something. Second, it may be considered by comparing its existence with the existence of its cause. It was established in the discussions of cause and effect that the existence of an effect, insofar as it is intrinsically dependent, is a relational existence in relation to its cause, with no independent selfhood. It has nothing but its subsistence through the existence of its cause, without anything being predicated of it or it being predicated of anything.

With this established, when the knower is the cause and the known is the effect, the relation between them is that of the relational to the independently existent in itself. It is evident that a relational existent excludes existence for something, since this presupposes existence in itself, whereas the relational existent exists in another. A condition of something’s being known is that it exist for the knower. The effect, however, is relational and subsists through the existence of the cause, in the sense that it is neither outside nor absent from it. Its existence for the cause is therefore fulfilled only through what sustains it, namely, the cause’s existence. What the cause knows is thus itself insofar as it sustains the effect’s existence. The cause intellects its own essence, and the effect is not outside it—not in the sense of being a part or of composition. The predication between them is the predication of the effect, as sustained by the cause, of the cause. This is a kind of predication of a reality and its attenuated reality.

Similar reasoning applies to knowledge of the relational: everything relational that is known is known through knowledge of the independent being through which that relation subsists.

When the knower is the effect and the known is the cause, the known must exist for the knower, while it is impossible for anything to exist for a relational existence. The cause’s existence for the effect is therefore fulfilled only through the effect’s subsistence through the cause. The cause itself exists for itself, while the effect is not outside it. The cause knows itself, and this knowledge is ascribed to the effect insofar as the effect is not outside it. In knowing the cause, the effect attains only as much of the cause’s existence as it can encompass. In this predication, the cause is predicated of the effect insofar as the effect is sustained by the cause. This too is a kind of predication of a reality and its attenuated reality.

Thus an effect’s knowledge of its cause ultimately amounts to the cause’s knowledge—considered together with its effect—of itself considered alone. And a cause’s knowledge of its effect ultimately amounts to the cause’s knowledge—considered in itself—of itself considered together with its effect.

When the knower and the known are two effects of a third cause, union of the knower and the known does not mean that the two individuals turn into a single individual. It means, rather, that the quiddities of both the knower and the known are abstracted from the knower.

As for counting a thing’s knowledge of itself as a union of the knower and the known, this is in consideration of the abstraction from it of the two concepts of knower and known, which are distinct concepts. It is therefore called “union,” although in itself it is one.

What has been said also shows the invalidity of the objection that all immaterial beings would have to be a single individual. It has become clear that the individuality of the knower or the known is not abolished by the union in question.

[Explicit later-editor correction] At the limit of the effect’s knowledge of its cause, the 1404 print p. 242 (canvas 246) and the base read من وجوده. The 1428 editor, vol. 2 p. 163 n. 1, explicitly prints من وجودها, referring to the cause. The Arabic and English adopt that attributed reading. The original masculine pronoun and previous English are preserved in the integration decision.

N-XI-03 · The division of acquired knowledge into universal and particular, and related matters
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 301–304
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=301&type=c_fbook

Acquired knowledge divides into universal and particular.

The universal is that which the intellect does not refuse to suppose true of many, such as the intelligible human, which the intellect allows to be true of many things externally.

The particular is that which the intellect refuses to allow to be true of many. One example is knowledge of this human obtained through a kind of connection with his present matter; this is called “sensory knowledge” or “sensation.” Another is knowledge of an individual human without his matter being present; this is called “imaginative knowledge.”

These two divisions of knowledge are counted as particular, incapable of being true of many, only because the organs of sense are connected to the matter of the external known in sensory knowledge, and because imaginative knowledge depends upon prior sensory knowledge. Otherwise the cognitive form, whether sensory, imaginative, or of another kind, does not in itself exclude being true of many.

Corollaries

First: it is clear from the foregoing that the connection of the organs of sense with external matter, and the material action and affection involved in this, serve to prepare the soul to apprehend the form of the known, whether particular or universal.

It follows that the following account is offered as an illustrative comparison to facilitate understanding: “Intellection consists in stripping the known of matter and of the other individuating accidents surrounding it until only the quiddity remains, stripped of these coverings. This differs from sensation, which requires matter to be present and the individuating accidents to surround it; and from imagination, which requires the accidents and individuating configurations to remain, but does not require matter to be present.”

The truth is that what is sensed per se is an immaterial cognitive form. The presence of matter and the surrounding individuating accidents are required to produce the soul’s preparedness for sensory apprehension. Likewise, the requirement that individuating features accompany a form in imagination, and the requirement of stripping in intellection, indicate the conditions of preparedness. In the latter case, apprehending more than one individual is required to prepare the soul to intellect the universal quiddity, which is expressed as abstracting the universal from the individuals.

Second: taking a concept and abstracting it from its instance depends upon some connection with that instance and contact with external reality. This may be unmediated, as when the organs of sense connect with external reality in sensory knowledge; or mediated, as when imagination connects with external reality through sensation in imaginative knowledge, and as when the intellect connects with external reality in intellectual knowledge through the sensory and imaginative apprehension of particulars.

If, in apprehending a concept, the apprehensive faculty did not draw upon external reality, but instead produced the apprehension itself without any connection to external reality, the apprehended form would stand in the same relation to its own instance as to anything else. It would therefore have to be true of everything or of nothing at all. Yet it is true of its own instance rather than anything else. This is a contradiction.

You may object: there is no doubt that most acquired knowledge ultimately derives from sensation. But not all acquired knowledge is obtained through the external senses. Love, hatred, willing, aversion, and other things apprehended through the internal senses are examples; their mental forms are apprehended without connection to external reality. Moreover, the senses apprehend only accidental quiddities. No sense reaches substance insofar as it is substance. Its mental form is therefore obtained without sensation and its connection to external reality.

I reply: as for mental forms obtained through internal sensations, such as love, hatred, and the like, the soul takes them from its apprehension of those attributes through their external existence in the soul. Connection with external reality is therefore preserved in their case.

As for substance, it is undoubtedly true that no external or internal sense knows or reaches substance. But at the outset the soul has knowledge of itself by presence, through which it reaches and witnesses its own external existence. From what it knows by presence it takes a mental form, just as it takes other mental forms from things known by presence, as explained above. It then senses the attributes and accidents that subsist in it and witnesses their intrinsic need for the soul as their subject, and the soul’s subsistence in itself without need of anything in which to subsist. It subsequently finds accidental attributes that assail and befall it from outside, by which it is affected. It sees that these are like the accidents that are effects of the soul and subsist in it, and like things have the same ruling. It therefore judges that they have a subject in which they subsist, just as the soul is the subject of its accidental attributes. In this way the concept of substance is obtained: a quiddity which, when it exists, exists not in a subject.

Third: the foregoing establishes that existence divides, according to whether or not it is free of matter, into three universal realms. The first is the realm of matter and potentiality.

The second is the realm free of matter but not of its effects, such as shape, magnitude, position, and the like. It contains corporeal forms, their accidents, and their configurations of perfection, without matter bearing potentiality. It is called the “imaginal realm” and the “intermediate realm,” because it lies between the realms of matter and intellectual immateriality.

They have divided the imaginal realm into the greater imaginal realm, which subsists in its own right, and the lesser imaginal realm, which subsists in the soul. In the latter, the soul acts as it wishes according to different motives, sometimes producing true and sound forms, and sometimes arbitrary forms with which it plays.

The third is the realm free of matter and of its effects, called the “realm of intellect.”

These three realms form a vertical order. The highest in rank, strongest in manifestation, prior in existence, and nearest to the First Principle—exalted and holy—is the realm of immaterial intellects, because they are fully actual and their existence is free from any admixture of matter and potentiality. Next comes the imaginal realm, free of matter but not of its effects. Then comes the realm of matter, the abode of deficiency, evil, and possibility. Knowledge is connected to what is in this realm only through what corresponds to it in the imaginal and intellectual realms, as was indicated above.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 243–246 (canvases 247–250); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 164–168. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XI-04 · Another sense in which acquired knowledge divides into universal and particular
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 304–305
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=304&type=c_fbook

In this sense, universal knowledge is knowledge that does not change when the external known changes. An example is the form of a building that a builder conceives and according to which he builds. It remains as it is before the building, while the building exists, and after it, even if the building ceases to exist. This is called “knowledge prior to multiplicity.”

Knowledge through causes is universal in this sense. An example is an astronomer’s knowledge that the moon will be eclipsed on a particular day, from a particular hour to another, when the celestial configuration returns to a position that requires the earth to intervene between the moon and the sun. His knowledge of this remains the same before, during, and after the eclipse.

The reason is that a complete cause does not change from what it is in its causality. Since knowledge of it corresponds to what is known, its cognitive form does not change. Likewise, knowledge of its effect does not change. It is therefore universal and constant.

This makes clear that sensory knowledge cannot be universal, because sensible things change.

Particular knowledge is knowledge that changes when the external known changes. An example is our knowledge through vision of Zayd’s motion while he is moving. When he stops moving, the knowledge changes. This is called “knowledge subsequent to multiplicity.”

It may be objected: the change of knowledge, which you have acknowledged in the second division, proves that it is material. For change is a transition from one state to another; it entails potentiality, which in turn entails matter. Yet you have said that knowledge in all its divisions is immaterial.

We reply: knowledge of change is different from a change of knowledge. Change is constant in its changing, rather than itself changing. Knowledge’s connection to what changes—that is, its presence to the knower—is only in respect of its constancy, not its change. Otherwise it would not be present, and there would be no presence of one thing to another. This is a contradiction.

A clarification

The division can be generalized to include knowledge by presence. Universal knowledge would then be exemplified by a cause’s knowledge of its effect through its own essence, which possesses the effect’s perfection within itself in a higher and nobler manner. This knowledge does not change if the effect passes away, supposing that it can pass away. Particular knowledge would be exemplified by a cause’s knowledge of its perishing effect that is identical with the effect itself. Such knowledge passes away with the passing away of the effect.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 246–247 (canvases 250–251); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 169–170. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.
