1428 p.169 n.1; p.170 n.1: Explanation of prior to multiplicity and long authorial Asfar gloss quoted by the editor. This elaborates bodily manifestations and stable cognitive forms but is not a Nihaya authorial footnote.

N-XI-05 · The kinds of intellection
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 305–306
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=305&type=c_fbook

They have stated that intellect is of three kinds.

The first is intellect in potentiality: it is not actually any of the intelligibles, nor does it actually possess any of the intelligibles, since it is devoid of all intelligibles.

The second is for it actually to intellect one intelligible or many intelligibles, distinguishing them from one another and arranging them. This is detailed intellection.

The third is for it actually to intellect many intelligibles without distinguishing them from one another. This is a simple, comprehensive intellection containing all the details. They illustrate it by the case in which someone asks you several questions about matters you know, and the answer immediately comes to you. At the very first moment when you begin to answer, you actually know them all with certain knowledge, yet without distinguishing some from others or setting out their details. Distinction and detail arise only in the answer, as though the simple knowledge you possess were a spring from which the details issue and flow. This is called “comprehensive intellection.”

They presented this division as a division of acquired knowledge.

But since you have learned above that every acquired knowledge ultimately rests upon knowledge by presence, you must understand the discussion in a manner that also applies to knowledge by presence. Do not overlook this.

The same applies to the discussions of acquired knowledge that follow this one.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 247–248 (canvases 251–252); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 171–172. N-XI-06 · The degrees of intellect
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 306–307
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=306&type=c_fbook

They have stated that intellect has four degrees.

The first is the material intellect: the degree at which the soul is devoid of all intelligibles. It is called “material intellect” because it resembles prime matter in being devoid of all actualities.

The second is intellect in habitu: the degree at which the soul intellects self-evident matters, whether conceptions or assents. Self-evident knowledge precedes all other knowledge, because theoretical knowledge depends upon it.

The third is actual intellect: the degree at which the soul intellects theoretical matters by deducing them from self-evident matters.

The fourth is the soul’s intellection of all the self-evident and theoretical intelligibles it has acquired that correspond to the realities of the higher and lower worlds. It brings them all to presence and attends to them without any material distraction. It thus becomes a world of knowledge corresponding to the world of concrete reality. This is called “acquired intellect.”

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 248–249 (canvases 252–253); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 173–174. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XI-07 · That which bestows these cognitive forms
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 307–308
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=307&type=c_fbook

The bestower of universal intellectual forms is an intellectual substance separate from matter, possessing all universal intellectual forms.

For, as established above, these cognitive forms are immaterial and are bestowed upon the soul. They therefore have a bestower. Their bestower is either the soul, which both produces and receives them, or something outside it, whether material or immaterial. That the soul should itself bestow and produce them is impossible, because this would require one and the same thing to be both agent and recipient; its falsity was established above. That the bestower should be material is refuted by the fact that a material thing is weaker in existence than an immaterial one. It is therefore impossible for it to be their agent, since an agent is stronger in existence than its act. Moreover, the action of material causes is conditional upon position, whereas an immaterial thing has no position. It is therefore established that the bestower of these intellectual forms is an immaterial intellectual substance, the nearest of the immaterial intellects to the substance receiving the bestowal. It contains all intellectual forms, intellected through comprehensive intellection. The soul prepared for intellection is united with it to the extent of its preparedness, and receives from it those intellectual forms for which it is prepared.

You may object: let us grant that universal cognitive forms are bestowed by a separate substance, on the basis of the demonstration already given. But why are all of them attributed to a single individual intellect? Why not ascribe them to many intellects of different quiddities, attributing each form to a separate substance different from the one to which another is attributed, or attributing each group of forms to an intellect different from the one to which another group is attributed?

I reply: the reason is what was established in earlier discussions: every immaterial species is confined to a single individual. It follows that each link in the chain of intellects, which demonstration establishes and to which it establishes that the existence of material things and material effects is attributable, is a species confined to a single individual. Their multiplicity is a vertical multiplicity, ordered and arranged as efficient causes, beginning with the first of them to issue from the First Principle and ending with the intellect nearest to material things and material effects. Material things and material effects must therefore be ascribed to the intellect nearest to them, which the Peripatetics call the “Agent Intellect.”

The Illuminationists, to be sure, also establish horizontal intellects beyond and below the vertical intellects: these are the lords of material species. They hold, however, that the existence of each species, with its material individuals and their perfections, is attributable to that species’ lord and archetype.

Reasoning similar to that given for universal cognitive forms applies to particular cognitive forms. It establishes that the bestower of particular cognitive forms is a separate imaginal substance, containing all particular forms in the manner of comprehensive knowledge. The soul is united with it to the extent of its preparedness, and it bestows the appropriate forms upon the soul.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 249–250 (canvases 253–254); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 174–176. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XI-08 · The division of acquired knowledge into conception and assent
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 308–310
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=308&type=c_fbook

Acquired knowledge is either a mental form obtained from a single known, without affirmation or negation, such as knowledge of a human or of the antecedent of a conditional proposition; this is called “conception.” Or it is a mental form consisting of items of knowledge accompanied by affirmation or negation, as in categorical and conditional propositions; this is called “assent.”

Since a proposition contains affirmation or negation, it is composed of more than one part.

The prevalent view is that an affirmative categorical proposition consists of the subject, the predicate, the predicative relation—which is the relation of the predicate to the subject—and the judgment that the subject is united with the predicate. This applies to compound existential propositions, whose predicates are other than the subject’s existence, as when we say, “The human is capable of laughter.” Simple existential propositions, whose predicate is the subject’s existence, as when we say, “The human exists,” consist of three parts: the subject, the predicate, and the judgment. For it makes no sense for a relation, which is a relational existence, to intervene between a thing and its existence, which is the thing itself.

The prevalent view also holds that a negative categorical proposition consists of the subject, the predicate, and the negative predicative relation. It contains no judgment; it is not that it contains a negative judgment. For judgment is making something to be something, while the negation of judgment is not making it so, rather than making its nonexistence.

The truth is that a proposition requires the conception of a predicative relation only on account of judgment insofar as it is an act of the soul, rather than insofar as it is a part of the proposition. The predicative relation, assuming it is realized, is therefore external to the proposition. In other words, the proposition is the subject, the predicate, and the judgment. But the soul reaches the judgment—which consists in making the subject be the predicate—by first conceiving the predicate as related to the subject, so that it can make the judgment. The evidence for this is that simple existential propositions lack a predicative relation, yet are propositions, as stated above. A proposition, insofar as it is a proposition, therefore does not require a predicative relation for its realization.

As for judgment’s being an act of the soul within mental apprehension, its reality in our statement “Zayd is standing,” for example, is as follows. Through sensation the soul reaches one thing, namely, the standing Zayd. It then reaches the standing ʿAmr and the nonstanding Zayd. This prepares it to separate the standing Zayd into the two concepts “Zayd” and “standing.” It makes this separation and retains both concepts. Then, when it wishes to represent what it found externally, it takes the retained Zayd and standing, which are two, and makes them one. This is the judgment that we have described as an act: a making and bringing about by the soul, through which it represents external reality.

Judgment is therefore an act of the soul, while also being one of the mental forms that represent what lies beyond them. If it were a conception taken from external reality, the proposition would not convey a complete meaning on which one could stop, just as either component of a conditional proposition does not. If it were a conception produced by the soul on its own, without assistance or support from external reality, it would not represent external reality. Some further matters related to this will come to you later.

The foregoing establishes that every assent depends upon more than one conception. There is thus no assent without conception.

[Translator's analytical note · negative proposition] Both the 1404 printed chapter (pp.250–252, canvases254–256) and the 1428 edited text (vol2 pp.177–179) retain the received negative account «ولا حكم فيها، لا أن فيها حكما عدميا» and the author’s subsequent «والحق» account of conceived relation as preparation for judging. The 1428 editor objects at p.178 n.2 to denying judgment in negative propositions; that is later commentary, not an authorial revision. Preserve the distinctions and do not flatten them into one uncontested theory. The 1428 p.179 n.1 word-order correction places «أولا» with the preliminary conception, as the English already does.

The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XI-09 · The division of acquired knowledge into self-evident and theoretical
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 310–314
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=310&type=c_fbook

Self-evident knowledge, also called necessary knowledge, is that whose acquisition requires no learning or reflection. Examples include conceiving the concepts of existence, thing, and unity, and assenting that the whole is greater than its part and that four is even.

Theoretical knowledge is that which requires learning and reflection for its conception, if it is conceptual knowledge, or for assent to it, if it is knowledge by assent. Examples include conceiving the quiddity of a human or a horse, and assenting that the three angles of a triangle equal two right angles and that the human has an immaterial soul.

They have reduced self-evident propositions to six divisions: sensible propositions, propositions established through recurrent testimony, experiential propositions, innate propositions, introspectively known propositions, and primary propositions, as they have explained in logic.

The self-evident propositions most worthy of acceptance are the primary propositions. These are propositions for assent to which it suffices merely to conceive the subject and the predicate, as when we say, “The whole is greater than its part” and “A thing is established for itself”; or the antecedent and consequent, as when we say, “A number is either even or odd.”

The primary proposition most worthy of acceptance is the proposition that contradictories cannot both obtain or both be absent. This is expressed by our saying, “Either the affirmation is true and the negation false, or the negation is true and the affirmation false.”

This is a genuine disjunctive proposition. No theoretical or self-evident proposition, even among the primary propositions, can dispense with it in yielding knowledge. Our saying “The whole is greater than its part,” for example, yields knowledge only if it excludes its contradictory and that contradictory is false.

It is therefore the first proposition to which assent attaches, and all theoretical and self-evident knowledge ultimately rests upon it in an exceptive syllogism through which the knowledge is completed.

If doubt about it were supposed, this would spread to every proposition and invalidate knowledge at its very root.

The following consequences ensue.

First: for every proposition supposed, we have a true proposition, either that proposition itself or its contradictory.

Second: the contradictory of one proposition is one, and there is no intermediary between contradictories.

Third: contradiction between two conceptions ultimately amounts to contradiction between two assents. Thus the contradiction between human and nonhuman amounts to the existence and nonexistence of the human, which in turn amount to our saying, “The human exists” and “The human does not exist.”

A clarification

The sophist, who denies the existence of knowledge altogether, does not concede the first of first principles. If he conceded it, this would be his acknowledgment that of every two contradictory propositions, one is true and correct; and this would acknowledge the existence of some knowledge.

Now the sophist, in displaying doubt about every judgment, either acknowledges that he knows he is doubting, or does not acknowledge this.

If he acknowledges that he knows he is doubting, he has acknowledged some knowledge. To this is added his concession of the first of first principles, followed by knowledge that of every two contradictory propositions one is true and correct. Further items of knowledge follow upon this.

If he does not acknowledge that he knows he is doubting, but instead declares that he doubts everything and doubts his own doubt, without being certain of anything, then arguing with him is futile and no demonstration will succeed with him. Such a person is either afflicted by a disorder that has impaired his apprehension, in which case he should consult a physician; or he obstinately opposes the truth and displays what he does in order to refute the truth and escape its consequences. In that case, let him be beaten and tormented, deprived of what he loves, and compelled to undergo what he hates, since for him everything and its contradictory are equal.

Some of those who display doubt, however, have turned to the rational sciences without being equipped with logical principles or trained in the art of demonstration. They have witnessed investigators disagreeing on questions through affirmation and denial, and have seen the arguments they offer for the two sides of a contradiction. Because of their limited resources, they have been unable to distinguish truth from falsehood, and so have accepted contradictory positions in question after question. They have consequently come to distrust logic, supposing that there is no path to attaining reality that secures thought against error, and no way to know anything as it is. As you see, however, this is itself a decisive judgment about many things: that the investigators’ ideas and arguments diverge without any having a preponderance over others, and that this entails the incapacity of argument as such to attain reality. Such a person may therefore abandon his supposition when alerted to this. He should be treated by explaining the laws of logic and showing him self-evident propositions that admit no doubt under any circumstances, such as the necessity that a thing be established for itself and the impossibility of its being denied of itself. Every effort should be made to explain the meanings of the parts of propositions to him, and he should be instructed to learn the mathematical sciences.

There are two groups of doubters short of those just mentioned. One group concedes the human being and his apprehensions while expressing doubt about everything beyond that. Another group has noticed that saying “we and our apprehensions” acknowledges that each of them knows the existence of other human beings and their apprehensions. There is no difference between this knowledge and other apprehensions in their property of disclosing what is external. They have therefore changed the formulation to “I and my apprehensions.”

The answer is that a human sometimes errs in his apprehensions, as in errors of sight and touch and other errors of thought. Unless there were external realities that apprehension either corresponds to or fails to correspond to, this would not be coherent. Moreover, to classify apprehension of the self and of its apprehensions as knowledge, while classifying apprehensions beyond this as doubts, is plainly arbitrary.

Another form of sophistry is the claim: “Empirical investigation shows that sensible things, in their external existence, do not correspond to their forms in sensation. Since knowledge ultimately derives from sensation, nothing known corresponds to external reality in a way that discloses a truth.”

The answer is that if sensation does not disclose the reality of the sensible thing as it is externally, and all other knowledge ultimately derives from sensation and shares its ruling, then from where has it become evident that external realities differ from what sensation reaches? The supposition is that every apprehension is sensory or ultimately derives from sensation, and that sensation has no access to external reality. The claim thus ends in sophistry, just as the claim that mental forms are mere likenesses of external things ends in sophistry.

Another form of sophistry is the claim: “What we count as knowledge consists of opinions, having nothing of knowledge that excludes its contradictory.”

The answer is that this very statement, “What we count as knowledge consists of opinions,” is itself a proposition claiming knowledge. If it were a matter of opinion, it would not establish that knowledge consists of opinions. It would establish only the opinion that it consists of opinions. Reflect upon this and take heed.

The same applies to the claim: “Our knowledge is relative, varying with the conditions of existence. There is knowledge relative to each condition, but no absolute knowledge, no permanent knowledge, no universal knowledge, and no necessary knowledge.”

These statements refute themselves. If “knowledge is relative” is itself a relative statement, it establishes that there is an absolute statement and thus refutes itself. If it is an absolute statement, an absolute statement is thereby established, and it again refutes itself. The same holds for “there is no absolute knowledge.” As for “there is no universal knowledge,” if it is itself universal, it refutes itself; if it is not universal, it establishes a universal statement and thus refutes itself. Likewise, “there is no permanent knowledge” and “there is no necessary knowledge” refute themselves however they are supposed.

There is, to be sure, an admixture of relativity in practical knowledge, to which reference will be made later, God willing.

[translation] “Innate propositions” renders fiṭriyyāt, the logical class of propositions whose supporting inference is immediately available to the mind when their terms are conceived.

N-XI-10 · The division of acquired knowledge into real concepts and concepts formed by intellectual consideration
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 314–317
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=314&type=c_fbook

A real concept is a concept that sometimes exists externally, where its effects follow upon it, and sometimes exists in the mind, where its external effects do not follow upon it: the concept of human, for example. It follows that it stands in an equal relation to existence and nonexistence. This is the quiddity predicated of a thing in answer to “What is it?”

A concept formed by intellectual consideration is the opposite of a real concept. It is either one of those concepts whose instance is characterized precisely by being external, with its effects following upon it, and therefore cannot enter the mind, whose character is that external effects do not follow there. Otherwise it would be transformed from what it is. Existence and its real attributes, such as unity, necessity, and the like, are examples. Or its instance is characterized precisely by not being external, as with nonexistence. This too cannot enter the mind; otherwise it would be transformed into something capable of external existence, whereas there is no mental existence for that which has no external existence. Or it is one of those concepts whose instance is characterized precisely by being mental, such as the concepts of universal, genus, and differentia. Such a thing does not exist externally, since otherwise it would be transformed from what it is.

These are known mental concepts, but in respect of their instances they are either purely external and do not enter the mind, as with existence and what accompanies it; or pure nullity, as with nonexistence; or purely mental, with no access to external reality. They are therefore not abstracted from external reality, and are not quiddities that exist at one time with external existence and at another with mental existence. They are, however, abstracted from instances, as is shown by their being acquired items of knowledge upon which the effects do not follow. They must therefore be abstracted from instances in the mind.

As for meanings whose instances are characterized precisely by being mental, our minds can take something abstracted from external reality—which is a concept—as an instance and consider it. The intellect is then compelled to consider properties appropriate to it. Thus it abstracts the concept of human from several individuals, such as Zayd, ʿAmr, Bakr, and others, then takes this concept and sets it up as an instance. Examining the properties surrounding it, the intellect finds it to be the entire quiddity of the instances, in which case it is a species; or a part of their quiddity, in which case it is a genus or differentia; or something external to their quiddity, equal in extension or broader, in which case it is a proprium or common accident. It finds that the concept admits of being true of many, which is universality; and it proceeds in this manner.

As for concepts whose instances are characterized precisely by being external or not being external, they appear to be abstracted from the judgment in affirmative propositions and its absence in negative propositions.

To explain: when the soul first reaches some sensible quiddity through sensation, it takes what it has reached and stores it in imagination. When it reaches it a second time, or at the next instant, and takes it to store it, the soul finds it identical with what it reached first and corresponding to it. This is predication, which is the union of two concepts in existence. The soul then reproduces the concept repeatedly, one reproduction after another, and makes them one. This is a judgment and an act of the soul, while at the same time representing external reality. This act is an existential relation and a relational existence, considered as subsisting through its two terms.

The soul can then conceive the judgment, which is its own act, and consider it independently as ascribed to that of which it is an attribute, after it had been relational. It thereby conceives the existence of the concept. It then abstracts this and conceives existence on its own, without ascription. In this way, the concept of existence is abstracted from judgment and applies to its external instance, even though that instance is characterized precisely by being external. Such things are instances of existence, rather than individuals containing its concept in the way that a quiddity is contained in its individuals. The soul then abstracts from the instances of existence its distinctive attributes, such as necessity, unity, multiplicity, potentiality, actuality, and others.

Next, when the soul reaches a sensible quiddity and stores it, then reaches another quiddity different from it, it does not find the second to be identical with the first and corresponding to it, as it did in the previous case. When it brings both to presence after storing them, it does not perform upon them the judgment it performed in the previous case of the repeated quiddity. Yet it considers this too an act of its own: the negation of predication, as opposed to predication. It then considers this independently and with ascription, conceiving it as the negation of the predicate from the subject. It subsequently considers it without qualification, conceiving it as negation and nonexistence. It is then compelled to consider properties belonging to it, such as the absence of distinction between nonexistences and their distinction through their ascription to existents.

The foregoing establishes the following.

First: any concept predicated of both the Necessary and the contingent, such as knowledge and life, is formed by intellectual consideration. Otherwise the Necessary would possess a quiddity—exalted is He above that.

Second: any concept predicated of more than one category, such as motion, is formed by intellectual consideration. Otherwise it would fall under more than one genus, which is impossible.

Third: concepts formed by intellectual consideration have no essential definition and are not included as a genus in the essential definition of a quiddity. The same applies to other properties distinctive of quiddities, such as universality, except by a certain extension of usage.

A clarification

In technical usage, “considered” has other meanings besides the preceding one, which lie outside our present discussion.

The first is that which stands opposed to primacy, in the sense of being intrinsically the source of effects, as discussed in the investigation of the primacy of existence and quiddity.

The second is “considered” in the sense of having no existence distinct from another, as opposed to the “real,” which has a distinct existence. An example is the considered character of the category of relation, which exists through the existence of its two terms, unlike substance, which exists in itself.

The third is a conceptual or assertoric meaning that has no realization beyond the sphere of action.

Consideration in this sense ultimately consists in borrowing objectively real concepts, with their definitions, for kinds of actions—which are different motions—and their objects, in order to attain sought-after vital ends. An example is considering the chief of a people to possess headship, so that he stands to the group as the head stands to the body in managing its affairs and directing its members toward the action required. Another is considering Zayd, for example, to possess ownership in relation to the property he has taken possession of, so that he alone may dispose of it as he wishes, as is the case with a real owner in relation to what he owns, such as the human soul that owns its faculties. Another is considering the relation between a man and a woman as a pairing, so that the two spouses share in what follows upon the pair together, as is the case with a numerical pair. Other cases follow the same pattern.

From this it is clear that these conventional meanings have neither essential definitions nor demonstrations.

They have no essential definitions because they have no quiddity falling under any category. They therefore have no genus, hence no differentia, and hence no essential definition. They do, however, have definitions borrowed from the realities whose concepts are borrowed for them.

They have no demonstrations because the premises of a demonstration must be necessary, permanent, and universal. These features are realized only in true propositions corresponding to objective reality. How could conventional premises possess them, when they do not go beyond the bounds of a claim?

It also becomes clear that reasoning about them is dialectical, composed of widely accepted propositions and conceded premises. What is accepted among them is that which has a beneficial effect in relation to the ends; what is rejected is the futile, which has no effect.

N-XI-11 · Knowledge by presence is not confined to a thing’s knowledge of itself
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 318–318
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=318&type=c_fbook

It was established above that every immaterial substance, because of the completeness of its essence, is present to itself through itself and its external identity. It therefore knows itself by presence.

Is knowledge by presence confined to a thing’s knowledge of itself, or does it include this together with a cause’s knowledge of its effect and an effect’s knowledge of its cause? The Peripatetics adopted the first view and the Illuminationists the second, which is the truth. For the existence of the effect is a relational existence in relation to the existence of its cause, subsisting through it and in no way independent of it. The effect is therefore present to its cause with its entire existence, without being veiled from it. Through its very existence it is known by its cause through knowledge by presence, if both are immaterial. Likewise, the cause is present through its existence to its effect, which is related to it, subsists through it, and is independent through its independence. The cause is therefore known to its effect through knowledge by presence if both are immaterial. This is what was sought.

It was also established above that all acquired knowledge ultimately rests upon knowledge by presence. Some acquired knowledge is such that neither knower nor known is the cause or effect of the other; rather, both are effects of a third cause.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 259–260 (canvases 263–264); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 191–192. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XI-12 · Every immaterial being is intellect, intellective subject, and intelligible
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 318–319
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=318&type=c_fbook

As for its being intellect: because its essence is complete and it is pure actuality unaccompanied by potentiality, it is possible for it to exist and be present to something. Whatever is possible for an immaterial being belongs to it actually. It is therefore actually intelligible. Since intellect is united with the intelligible, it is intellect. And since its essence exists for its own essence, it intellects its own essence. Every immaterial being is therefore intellect, intellective subject, and intelligible. Or, if you wish, say: “Intellect, intellective subject, and intelligible are three concepts abstracted from one existence.”

The demonstration just given applies not only to every immaterial being’s being intellect, intellective subject, and intelligible for itself, but also to its being intellect and intelligible for another.

It may be objected: this requires that the human soul, because it is immaterial, should intellect itself and every immaterial being supposed. But this is contrary to what is immediately evident.

We reply: this would be so if the immaterial soul were completely immaterial in both essence and act. It is, however, immaterial in essence and material in act. Because it is immaterial in essence, it actually intellects its own essence.

Its intellection of other things, however, depends upon its gradual passage from potentiality to actuality according to the different dispositions it acquires. If it became completely immaterial and the governance of the body no longer occupied it, all intellections would actually belong to it through comprehensive intellect, and it would become acquired intellect.

Note that this account applies only to immaterial essences whose existence is in itself and for itself. As for accidents, whose existence is in itself but for another, the intellective subject to which their intelligible forms occur is their subject, rather than the accidents themselves. The same ruling applies to relations and connections, whose existences are in another.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 260–261 (canvases 264–265); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 193–195. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XI-13 · Knowledge of what has a cause is obtained only through knowledge of its cause, and related matters
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 320–321
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=320&type=c_fbook

By cause here we mean the cause that necessitates the effect through the specific character of its causality, whether it is a cause through its quiddity, as four is the cause of evenness, or a cause through its external existence. It is that to which the existence of the effect is attributable, while its being attributable to anything else is impossible; otherwise a single effect would have two independent causes. Since knowledge corresponds exactly to what is known, the relation between knowledge of the effect and knowledge of the cause is the relation between the effect itself and the cause itself. It follows that knowledge of the effect depends upon and follows from knowledge of its cause. If knowledge of the effect followed from something other than knowledge of its cause, one thing would have more than one cause, which is impossible.

It is evident from this account that the ruling concerns knowledge of the caused thing itself together with knowledge of the cause itself, rather than knowledge of the two correlative attributes of causality and being caused. The latter would be of no use here, because it applies to any two correlatives supposed, without being peculiar to knowledge. Moreover, it establishes only concomitance, rather than the dependence of knowledge of the effect upon knowledge of the cause, since correlatives are together in potentiality and actuality, in the mind and externally.

You may object: we often apprehend things through sensation, judge that they are externally realized, and assent to their existence while remaining ignorant of their cause. There is thus knowledge of the effect despite ignorance of the cause. Admittedly, this does reveal in a general way that its cause exists.

We reply: what sensation reaches is the forms of external accidents, without assenting to their establishment or to the establishment of their effects. Assent belongs to the intellect. The intellect sees that what the human reaches through sensation, and which has effects outside him, is something he has not made; and whatever is such exists outside the human soul. This is a passage in knowledge from one of two concomitants to the other.

What was established above is that knowledge of what has a cause depends upon its cause. As for what has no cause, its establishment is known only through general concomitances, as has been demonstrated in the art of demonstration.

A thing’s being independent of another thing, which has no part in making it, and its being distinct from and outside that other thing are two general, mutually concomitant attributes that have no cause. Their concomitance is intrinsic, like the other subjects of divine philosophy. The existence of the sensible thing outside the soul is an instance of these two concomitants, and the intellect passes from one to the other. This is just like the intrinsic concomitance between a thing and its being established for itself. This particular thing’s being established for itself is one instance of that concomitance, and knowledge of it does not depend upon a cause.

It is therefore clear from the foregoing that investigating a conclusion sought yields knowledge of it only by proceeding to it through its cause, if it has a cause, or through general concomitances, if it has no cause. Proceeding to a cause through its effect, however, yields no knowledge at all.

[Argument scope · demonstrative knowledge] This chapter concerns the causal basis of demonstrative knowledge. Its final sentence says without qualification that proceeding from effect to cause yields no knowledge; both 1404 pp.261–263 and 1428 pp.196–199 retain it. The 1428 editor’s opening note, quoting the author’s separate Asfar gloss, explains the target as certainty, while later notes dispute the argument. Preserve the author’s statement and its explicit alternative of general concomitants; do not silently replace it with the editor’s criticism or imply this collation independently validates the proof.

N-XI-14 · Items of knowledge are not intrinsic to the soul
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 321–322
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=321&type=c_fbook

It has been said: “The items of knowledge the soul attains are intrinsic to it and actually present in it from the beginning of its being.”

When it was objected that this conflicts with the human’s observed ignorance of some items of knowledge and with their need for acquisition in order to become actual, they answered that these items are intrinsic and innate to the soul, but that its occupation with governing the body makes it heedless of its knowledge and distracts it from attending to it.

The objection is that the soul’s mode of existence, insofar as it is a soul, is that of a form governing a body. Governing the body is therefore intrinsic to it wherever it is supposed to be a soul. Combining the intrinsic character of its knowledge with the claim that governing the body distracts it from that knowledge amounts to nothing but contradiction.

This account would, to be sure, make sense on the basis of the view attributed to Plato: souls are temporally eternal, their knowledge is intrinsic to them, and their subsequent governing attachment to bodies causes this governance to make them forget the knowledge rooted in their essences.

But this is false in light of what has been established in psychology: souls originate with the origination of bodies, according to the prevalent view, or through the motion of the substances of bodies after the bodies have originated.

The claim of their eternity has sometimes been interpreted as referring to the eternity of their intellectual mode of being, which precedes their psychic mode of being. Even this, however, does not establish that knowledge is obtained by recollection rather than by the passage of thought from causes to effects, or from some general concomitants to others, as explained above.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 263 (canvases 267); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 200–201. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XI-15 · Other divisions of knowledge
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 322–323
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=322&type=c_fbook

The author of the Asfār says, in summary:

“In our view, knowledge is immaterial existence itself. Existence is not in itself a universal generic or specific nature such that it could be divided by differentiae into species, by individuators into individuals, or by accidental qualifications into classes. Rather, every item of knowledge is a simple individual identity that does not fall under an essential universal meaning.

“In one respect, therefore, the division of knowledge is identical with the division of the known, because it is united with the known as existence is united with quiddity. Accordingly, we say that some knowledge is necessary in existence through its own essence: this is the First’s—exalted is He—knowledge of His own essence, which is identical with His essence, without a quiddity. Some knowledge is contingent in existence through its own essence: this is the knowledge of everything other than Him. This divides into what is substance, such as intellectual substances’ knowledge of their own essences, and what is accident. According to the prevalent view, the latter comprises all acquired items of knowledge, because, in their view, they subsist in the mind. In our view, accidental knowledge consists in the attributes of the known things whose forms become present to the soul. We have explained that knowledge, whether intellectual or imaginative, does not consist in the known things inhering in the intellect or the soul. Rather, they stand before the knower and the soul is united with them.

“Another division: they have said that some knowledge is active, some passive, and some neither active nor passive. Active knowledge includes the Creator’s—exalted is He—knowledge of what is other than His essence, and the knowledge other causes have of their effects. Passive knowledge includes the knowledge beings other than the Creator—exalted is He—have of what is not their effect, when it is obtained only through some affection and change in the knower: in general, through the impression of forms arising in the essence of the soul or in its instruments. Knowledge that is neither active nor passive includes the knowledge intellective essences have of themselves and of things that are never absent from them. One and the same knowledge may be active in one respect and passive in another, as with originated items of knowledge that have external effects, such as the effects of estimative apprehensions upon external materials.”

He also says:

“Knowledge applies to its instances by gradation, as existence does. It therefore differs in intensity and weakness, firstness and priority and their opposites, anteriority, and so forth. Knowledge of the essence of the First—exalted is He—which is His knowledge of His own essence and identical with His essence, is more entitled to be knowledge than knowledge of anything else. It is prior to all other knowledge because it is the cause of all other knowledge, and it is the clearest and strongest in manifestation in its own essence. As for its hiddenness from us, this is due, as you have learned, to its utmost manifestation and to the weakness of our inward sight in apprehending it. The very respect in which it is hidden is the respect in which it is clear and manifest. The same holds for every knowledge of the reality of a cause in comparison with knowledge of the reality of its effect. Likewise, knowledge of the reality of any substance is more intense than knowledge of the reality of any accident. It is more entitled and prior than knowledge of the reality of the accident subsisting in that substance, because the substance is its cause—not than knowledge of the reality of other accidents that do not subsist in it.

“As for applying ‘knowledge’ to action, affection, and relation, as in teaching, learning, and being a knower, this is by equivocation or figurative usage.” End of quotation.

[Authorial source reference] The 1404 printing ends this chapter with the parenthetical Asfar reference «ج3 ص382» (p.265/c269). The 1428 editor supplies Asfar 3:382–383 for the first extract and 3:383–384 for the second (vol2 p.203 nn.2–3). These Nihaya witnesses were fully compared; the underlying Asfar passages were not independently collated in this review.

The Necessary Existent

N-XII-PRE · Introduction to Stage XII: Matters concerning the Necessary Existent
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 325–325
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=325&type=c_fbook

The inquiries concerning the Necessary Existent—exalted is His name—are in fact questions connected with the stage on necessity and contingency. They have been assigned a separate stage in recognition of their importance and out of regard for the nobility of their subject.

This stage contains twenty-four chapters.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 267 (canvases 271); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 205–206. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XII-01 · Establishing necessary existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 327–329
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=327&type=c_fbook

The demonstrations indicating His existence—exalted is He—are many and numerous.

The most reliable and firmest is the demonstration that proceeds to Him through existence. They have called it the “demonstration of the veracious,” because they make Him known through Himself rather than through something else. As you will see, it is a demonstration of fact, in which one proceeds from one concomitant of existence to another.

It has been formulated in more than one way. The briefest formulation is that the reality of existence is either necessary or entails necessary existence. The intrinsically Necessary therefore exists, which is what was sought.

To the same effect is a formulation based upon the primacy of existence: the reality of existence, which is the very reality of concrete things and the core of actuality, is an unrestricted reality that does not admit nonexistence, since no opposite admits its opposite. An unrestricted reality that does not admit nonexistence is necessary in existence through its own essence. The reality of existence in question is therefore intrinsically necessary, which is what was sought.

You may object: existence’s not admitting nonexistence does not require that it be intrinsically necessary. Otherwise the existence of every contingent thing would be intrinsically necessary, because it contradicts its own nonexistence. The contingent would therefore be necessary while still contingent, which is a contradiction.

I reply: this concerns contingent existences, which are delimited by quiddative limits that they do not surpass. Their nonexistence is abstracted from what lies beyond their limits. This is what is meant by their saying, “Every contingent thing is a composite pair.” As for the unrestricted reality of existence, which alone is fundamental, with nothing else fundamental besides it, no limit delimits it and no qualification restricts it. It is simple and pure, intrinsically excluding and contradicting nonexistence. This is intrinsic necessity.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified—formulated the demonstration in another way. He says:

“Its formulation is as follows. Existence, as stated earlier, is a single, simple, concrete reality. Its individuals differ in themselves only through perfection and deficiency, intensity and weakness, or through additional matters, as with the individuals of a specific quiddity. Its utmost perfection is that than which there is nothing more complete: that which is not dependent upon another, and than which nothing more complete can be conceived. For everything deficient is dependent upon another and needs what completes it. It was established above that completeness precedes deficiency, actuality precedes potentiality, and existence precedes nonexistence. It was also explained that the completeness of a thing is that thing together with what exceeds it.

“Existence is therefore either independent of another or intrinsically dependent upon another. The former is the Necessary Existent: pure existence, than which there is nothing more complete, unmixed with nonexistence or deficiency. The latter is everything other than Him, consisting of His acts and effects. Nothing other than Him has any subsistence except through Him, because, as explained earlier, the reality of existence has no deficiency; deficiency attaches to it only on account of being an effect. For an effect cannot equal its cause in the excellence of existence. If existence were not made, with a dominant principle bringing it into being and realizing it as that principle requires, no manner of deficiency in it could be conceived. For the reality of existence, as you have learned, is simple, possessing no limit or determination except pure actuality and realization. Otherwise it would contain composition or have a quiddity other than existentiality. It was also established above that when existence is an effect, it is itself made through simple making; its essence is intrinsically dependent upon a maker, and it is attached to its maker in its very substance and essence.

“It is therefore established and clear that existence is either complete in reality and necessary in identity, or intrinsically dependent upon that and attached to it in its very substantiality. In either case, it is established and evident that the existence of the Necessary Existent is independent in its identity of all else. This is what we intended.” End of quotation.

[translation] “Demonstration of the veracious” renders burhān al-ṣiddīqīn. “Demonstration of fact” renders burhān innī; the next chapter distinguishes this from a demonstration of the reason why (burhān limmī).

[Textual evidence · early printed reading] The 1404 print p.269 (canvas273; high-resolution detail independently inspected) reads «متعلّق الجوهر به», whereas the base transcription and 1428 digital text p.211 read «متعلّق الجوهريّة». The early reading is adopted here: existence is attached to the independent existent in its very substance. The underlying Asfar passage has not been independently collated.

[Early printed source notes] At the unrestricted-existence formulation, the 1404 printing adds «المقرّر هو السبزواري، ره، في حاشية الأسفار» (p.268 n.1): “The formulator is Sabzawārī—may God have mercy on him—in his gloss on the Asfār.” Its closing quotation locator is «الأسفار، ج6، ص16» (p.270). The 1428 edition expands these references and separately reports two variants in the underlying Asfar; those later comments are not part of the original Nihaya prose.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 268–270 (canvases 272–274); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 207–211. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «متعلّق الجوهريّة» → «متعلّق الجوهر به» [E1]. Evidence: 1404 p.269/c273 final lines, confirmed high-resolution detail; 1428 p.211. Earlier printed reading: «متعلق الجوهر به». 1404 p.269/c273 scan-backed repair; base and 1428 fused reading preserved in apparatus.

N-XII-02 · Some other demonstrations offered for the existence of the Necessary—exalted is He
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 330–333
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=330&type=c_fbook

One demonstration for Him is as follows. There is no doubt that something exists. If it, or anything included in it, is intrinsically necessary, the conclusion sought is established. If it is not intrinsically necessary, yet exists, it is necessarily intrinsically contingent. Its existence has therefore been given preponderance over its nonexistence by something outside its essence, namely, its cause. Otherwise its existence would have preponderance through itself, and it would be intrinsically necessary, though it was supposed contingent. This is a contradiction.

Its cause is either contingent like it or intrinsically necessary. In the latter case, the conclusion sought is established. In the former, the discussion transfers to its cause, and so on. Either the sequence circles back or continues infinitely, both of which are impossible, or it ends in an uncaused cause, which is the intrinsically Necessary. This is what was sought.

It has been objected that this is not a demonstrative account yielding certainty. A demonstration yields certainty only when it proceeds from cause to effect, which is a demonstration of the reason why. A demonstration of fact that proceeds from effect to cause does not yield certainty, as explained in logic.

Since the Necessary—exalted is He—is the cause of all else and is in no way the effect of anything, proceeding to establish His existence from anything whatsoever is a passage from effect to cause that does not yield certainty. This account proceeds from the contingent existent, which is His effect, to establishing His existence.

The answer is that a demonstration of fact is not confined to proceeding from effect to cause, which does not yield certainty. It may instead proceed from some general concomitants of unrestricted existents to others, and this does yield certainty, as the Shaykh explained in the Book of Demonstration in the logic of the Shifāʾ.

The preceding demonstration proceeds from a condition necessarily accompanying the concept of something existent—which is coextensive with existent insofar as it is existent—to another condition necessarily accompanying it: that among its instances there exists an uncaused cause whose existence is necessary through its own essence.

The foregoing account is therefore a demonstration of fact yielding certainty, like the other demonstrations employed in philosophy to establish the properties of existent insofar as it is existent that are coextensive with existent in general.

A clarification

The substance of the preceding account is that the realization of something existent necessarily accompanies the preponderance of its existence, either through its own essence, in which case it is intrinsically necessary, or through another, in which case it ends in something whose existence has preponderance through its own essence. Otherwise there would be a circle or an infinite regress, both of which are impossible.

The preponderance of existence can be replaced by the necessity of existence, producing a demonstration of fact along another route. It is formulated as follows. There is no doubt that something exists, and every existent is necessary, because a thing does not exist until it is necessitated. If it, or anything included in it, is necessary through its own essence, the conclusion sought is established. If it is necessary through another, namely its existent and necessary cause, then either its cause is necessary through its own essence, which establishes the conclusion, or it is necessary through another. We then transfer the discussion to the cause of its cause, and so on. Either there is a circle, an infinite regress, or a termination in something necessary through its own essence. The first two alternatives are impossible, and the third is what was sought.

Another demonstration was offered by the natural philosophers through motion and change. Its formulation is as follows. It was established above, in the discussions of potentiality and actuality, that the mover is other than the moved. Every moving thing therefore has a mover other than itself. If the mover is itself moved, it too has another mover. To avoid a circle and an infinite regress, the series of movers must end in an unmoved mover. Because it is free of matter and potentiality, exempt from change and alteration, and constant in its existence, it is either intrinsically necessary in existence or ends in such a being through the series of its causes.

Another demonstration was also offered by the natural philosophers, through the human soul. Its formulation is as follows. The human soul is intrinsically immaterial and, insofar as it is a soul, originates with the origination of the body, since differentiation without bodies and transmigration are impossible, as explained in their proper place. It is therefore contingent, dependent upon a cause that is neither a body nor corporeal. That cause is not a body, because if it were a body, every body would possess a soul, whereas this is not so. Nor is it corporeal, because if it were something corporeal—whether another soul, a corporeal form, or a corporeal accident—its influence would operate through position. Yet the soul, being immaterial, has no position. Moreover, because the soul is immaterial, it is stronger in substantiality and nobler in existence than every body and corporeal thing. There is no sense in the weaker and baser being a cause of the stronger and nobler.

The cause bringing the soul into existence is therefore something beyond the natural world. It is the Necessary—exalted is He—either without an intermediary or through an ordered series of causes ending in Him.

Another demonstration is that of the theologians through origination. Its formulation is as follows. Bodies are never devoid of motion or rest, both of which are originated; and whatever is never devoid of originated things is itself originated. All bodies are therefore originated, and everything originated needs an originator. To avoid a circle and an infinite regress, their originator is something that is neither a body nor corporeal, namely, the Necessary—exalted is He.

This argument is incomplete. The premise that whatever is never devoid of originated things is itself originated is neither evident nor demonstrated. According to their view, change in the accidents of a substance does not necessarily accompany change in the substance that is their subject. If, however, the argument is based on substantial motion, the premise is established and the argument succeeds. As you see, this argument, like the three preceding arguments, rests upon the finitude of causes and their termination in an uncaused cause, which is the Necessary—exalted is He.

[Textual evidence · cause versus actuality] The 1404 print p.271 and base transcription read a form of «فعليّته» in the necessity reformulation. The 1428 editor explicitly corrects this to «فعلّته» (vol2 p.214 n.3), “then its cause.” The English follows this disclosed correction. The early print also supports «لمفهوم موجود ما» against the extra wāw in the base.

N-XII-03 · The intrinsically Necessary has no quiddity
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 333–335
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=333&type=c_fbook

This question was discussed in the stage on necessity and contingency. It was shown there that whatever has a quiddity is contingent. By contraposition, whatever is not contingent has no quiddity. The intrinsically Necessary therefore has no quiddity, and neither does the intrinsically impossible.

There we also presented the well-known argument they have offered to deny quiddity of the Necessary—exalted and holy. If the Necessary—exalted is He—had a quiddity beyond His existence, that quiddity would in itself be neither existent nor nonexistent. It would therefore need a cause in order to be clothed with existence. That cause would be either its own essence or something outside it, and both alternatives are impossible. Its own essence cannot be the cause of its existence, because a cause necessarily precedes its effect in existence. The quiddity would thus precede its own existence through its existence, which is impossible. Nor can something else be the cause of its existence, because this would entail that the intrinsically Necessary is the effect of that other thing and is therefore contingent, although He was supposed intrinsically necessary. This is a contradiction. It is thus impossible for the intrinsically Necessary to have a quiddity beyond His existence, which is what was sought.

This is a complete demonstrative argument, free of difficulty. It is not successfully countered by appealing to the existent quiddities of contingent things as follows: if supposing the quiddity posited for the Necessary to be the efficient cause of its own existence required that quiddity to precede the existence caused by it, the same would follow for the existent quiddities of contingent things. For the quiddity of a contingent thing receives its existence, and a recipient, like an agent, must precede what depends upon it.

This counterargument is unsound. A recipient must precede what it receives in existence only when it is a recipient that is a material cause. Such a cause precedes its effect, which is the composite of form and matter. The quiddity of a contingent thing, however, is not a material cause either of its existence or of the existent quiddity. Its receptivity is only an intellectual consideration arising from the intellect’s analysis of the contingent into quiddity and existence, and its taking quiddity as the subject and existence as its predicate. In brief, quiddity is not a receptive cause of existence. But if it were supposed an efficient cause of its existence, it would be a real cause that must really precede it. For the need for a cause of existence is a real need, entailing a real cause, unlike the need for a quiddative recipient to receive existence, which is an intellectual consideration. In reality, quiddity is incidental to existence, rather than existence being incidental to quiddity.

Another argument is as follows. If existence were additional to quiddity, the quiddity would necessarily fall under one of the categories. This must be the category of substance rather than any category of accident, whether the categories are confined to a certain number, familiar or otherwise, or exceed it. For accidents, whatever they are, subsist in another. If the supposed quiddity falls under the category of substance, it must be specified by a differentia after sharing with the other substantial species. It would therefore need a specifying principle. Moreover, there is no doubt that some substantial species need a specifying and preponderating principle. If contingency is admissible for some species under a genus, it is admissible for the genus. What is possible for some species under a genus is possible for the genus; and what is impossible or necessary for a genus is impossible or necessary for every species under it. If the Necessary Existent—exalted is He—fell under a category, He would therefore have an aspect of contingency in respect of the genus. He would then be contingent rather than necessary, which is a contradiction. Since the supposed quiddity’s falling under the category of substance is impossible, the Necessary’s possessing a quiddity is impossible. This is what was sought.

It is clear from the foregoing that the necessity and obligatoriness of existence in the Necessary—exalted is He—are eternal, abstracted from the very essence, which is existence without a quiddity.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 273–274 (canvases 277–278); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 217–219. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XII-04 · The Necessary is simple, composed of neither external nor mental parts
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 335–338
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=335&type=c_fbook

It was established above that the Necessary—exalted is He—has no quiddity. He therefore has no essential definition; and having no essential definition, He has no definitional parts, genus and differentia. Having neither genus nor differentia, He has no external parts consisting of external matter and form. For matter is genus taken on condition of exclusion, and form is differentia taken on condition of exclusion. Likewise, He has no mental parts consisting of intellectual matter and form, which are genus and differentia taken on condition of exclusion in externally simple things such as accidents. In brief, He has no definitional parts consisting of genus and differentia, no external parts consisting of external matter and form, and no mental intellectual parts consisting of intellectual matter and form.

Another demonstration: if He had a part, it would precede Him in existence, and the Necessary would depend upon it for existence. For a part necessarily precedes the whole in existence, and the whole depends upon it in this respect. It is impossible for the Necessary to be preceded by and dependent upon another while being necessary in existence.

Another demonstration: if the essence of the Necessary—exalted is He—were composed of parts, either all the parts would be intrinsically necessary, some would be intrinsically necessary and others contingent, or all would be contingent. The first is impossible. If the parts were intrinsically necessary, there would be relative possibility between them, as explained above. This is incompatible with their being real parts of a real composite possessing real unity. For composition requires an intrinsic connection between the parts, through which something new arises beyond their aggregate, with an effect beyond the effects of each individual part. The second is impossible because it would require the intrinsically Necessary to depend upon the contingent. It would also require quiddity to enter into the reality of the Necessary, since, as explained in the stage on necessity and contingency, every contingent thing has a quiddity. The third is likewise impossible for reasons similar to those just given.

As they have said, these demonstrations do not suffice to deny quantitative parts, because these are potential rather than actual parts, as explained in the discussion of quantity in the stage on substances and accidents.

The following has been said in denying them: “If the Necessary had a quantitative part, that part would be either contingent or necessary. If contingent, the quantitative part would differ from its whole in reality, which is impossible. If necessary, the intrinsically Necessary would exist potentially rather than actually, which is impossible.”

Another form of composition is that arising from the negations by which a thing is characterized in its existential identity. This too is denied of the intrinsically Necessary—exalted and holy.

To explain: every identity of which something can correctly be denied, considering the limit of its existence, is constituted by affirmation and negation. The human, for example, is a human and is not a horse in the very core of its existence. Everything of this kind is composed of an affirmation, namely its own self’s being established for it, and a negation, namely another thing’s being denied of it, since these two aspects are necessarily different. Every identity of which something is denied is therefore composite. The meaning of negation’s entering into an existential identity—although existence contradicts nonexistence—is an existential deficiency in one existence compared with another. Through this are realized the degrees of gradation in the reality of existence and their specific characteristics. By contraposition, the conclusion becomes: no existential perfection is denied of any essence that is simple in reality.

The intrinsically Necessary is sheer existence, with no access for nonexistence to His essence, and no existential perfection is denied of Him. For every contingent existential perfection is an effect bestowed by a cause, and causes end in the intrinsically Necessary. The giver of a thing cannot lack it. He—exalted is He—therefore possesses every existential perfection without any admixture of nonexistence. The necessary reality is purely simple, and nothing is denied of it. This is what was sought.

It may be objected: He—exalted is He—has negative attributes established by demonstration, such as that He is neither a body nor corporeal, neither a substance nor an accident.

We reply: negative attributes amount to the negation of deficiencies and nonexistences. Negating negation is existence, and negating deficiency is a perfection of existence, as has been said.

It may be objected: the foregoing account entails that predication is valid between Him—exalted is He—and every existent and existential perfection. This entails the identity of the Necessary and the contingent—exalted is God above that—which is contrary to what is immediately evident.

We reply: not at all. If contingent existences were predicated of Him—exalted is He—by common predication, they would be true of Him in both their affirmative and negative aspects, and in both their aspects of perfection and deficiency, of which their essences are composed. The essence of the Necessary would then be composite, although it was supposed simple in reality. This is a contradiction. Rather, His possession, through His simple reality, of the perfection of every existent is possession in a higher and nobler manner. It is like a cause’s possession of the perfection of its effect, notwithstanding the distinction between them that makes predication impossible.

This is what they mean by saying, “That which is simple in reality is all things.” The predication is that of a reality and its attenuated reality, rather than common predication.

It is clear from the foregoing that the intrinsically Necessary is the completeness of every thing.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 275–277 (canvases 279–281); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 220–224. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XII-05 · The unity of the intrinsically Necessary: He has no associate in necessary existence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 338–340
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=338&type=c_fbook

It became clear in the preceding chapters that the essence of the intrinsically Necessary is existence itself, without a quiddity or any negative component. It is therefore pure existence. The pure reality of a thing is one through true unity, which cannot be doubled or repeated. For multiplicity is realized only by the distinction of its units, each possessing a meaning not present in another; and this is incompatible with purity. Whatever you suppose to be a second to it returns to being the first. The intrinsically Necessary is therefore intrinsically one, just as He exists through His own essence and is necessary through His own essence. This is what was sought. Perhaps this is what the Shaykh meant by his statement in the Taʿlīqāt: “The existence of the Necessary is identical with His identity. His being existent is therefore identical with His being Himself. Hence the existence of the intrinsically Necessary does not belong to another.” End of quotation.

Another demonstration: if the intrinsically Necessary were multiple—supposing, for example, two intrinsically necessary beings—necessity of existence would be shared by them, and their distinction would be through something beyond the meaning they share. If this were internal to the essence, composition would follow, which is incompatible with necessity of existence. If it were external to the essence, it would be accidental and caused. If it were caused by the essence, the essence would precede its own distinction in existence. But there is no essence prior to distinction, so this is impossible. If it were caused by another, the essence would depend upon another for its distinction, which is impossible. A multiplicity of necessary existents is therefore impossible under every supposition.

Against this is raised the doubt attributed to Ibn Kammūna. According to the Asfār, the first to mention it was the Illuminationist Shaykh in the Muṭāraḥāt. Ibn Kammūna, one of the commentators on his writings, then mentioned it in one of his own works, and it became known by his name. The doubt is this: why may there not be two simple quiddities, unknown in their inmost nature, distinct in the entirety of their essences, with existence predicated of them accidentally? This doubt applies both to the primacy of quiddity attributed to the Illuminationists and to the primacy of existence together with the view that existences are simple realities distinct in the entirety of their essences, attributed to the Peripatetics. The argument rests upon the primacy of existence and its being a single graded reality with different degrees.

The doubt has been answered by observing that it rests upon the abstraction of one concept from many distinct instances precisely insofar as they are many and distinct, which is impossible.

Another demonstration: if the intrinsically Necessary were multiple, so that there were, for example, two intrinsically necessary beings, there would be relative possibility between them, without an intrinsic relation of necessary implication. For such a relation is realized between two things only if one is a cause and the other an effect, or if both are effects of a third cause. Being an effect is incompatible with intrinsic necessity of existence.

Each would therefore possess a share of existence and a degree of perfection that the other did not possess. The essence of each, through its own essence, would possess something of existence and lack something of it. It has already been established that this is a composition impossible for the intrinsically Necessary.

Another demonstration is mentioned by al-Fārābī in the Fuṣūṣ: “Necessity of existence is not divided by being predicated of many things different in number; otherwise it would be caused.”

Perhaps the meaning is that, if the intrinsically Necessary were multiple, that multiplicity could not be required by its essence, since this would entail that it had no instance. For whatever was supposed to be an instance of it would be many, and the many is realized only through units. If no unit is an instance of it, there is no multiplicity; and if there is no multiplicity, it has no instance. Yet it was supposed intrinsically necessary. It remains, then, that multiplicity would be required by something else. This is impossible, because it entails dependence upon another, which cannot coexist with intrinsic necessity.

[Later editorial clarification] In the Ibn Kammūna objection, the 1404 print p. 278 and the base read قول الوجود, “predicating existence.” The 1428 editor changes this to قول واجب الوجود, “predicating Necessary Existent,” and also permits قول وجوب الوجود, “predicating necessity of existence” (vol. 2 p. 227 n. 5). Both later alternatives are recorded in the apparatus. The earlier wording and its existing English are retained because the alternatives specify the interpretation of an intelligible earlier argument.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 277–279 (canvases 281–283); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 225–229. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Retained «ويكون قول الوجود عليهما قولا عرضيّاً»; later proposal «ويكون قول واجب الوجود عليهما قولا عرضيّاً». Evidence: 1404 p.278/c282; 1428 p.227 n.5. Retain the early/base predicate existence. The later editor explicitly proposes Necessary Existent and also permits necessity of existence. The early argument is intelligible and these alternatives specify its interpretation; record both without choosing one silently for the author.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

The later editor’s objection distinguishing broad from narrow quiddity is not the author’s own reply.
