N-XII-06 · The unity of the intrinsically Necessary in lordship: there is no lord besides Him
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 340–344
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=340&type=c_fbook

Thorough investigation and precise scientific reflection show that the parts of our witnessed world—the natural world—are connected to one another: its higher and lower parts, their actions and affections, and the events resulting from these. Among them you find no existent unconnected to others in its being, influence, and being affected. What was established in the discussions of substantial motion supports this.

Every occurrence, whether a coming-to-be, an action, or an affection, therefore depends upon the world as a whole. From this we conclude that a kind of unity exists among the parts of the world, and that the comprehensive order prevailing in it is one. This is one principle.

What emerges from the preceding discussions and those to come is that this material world is the effect of a luminous realm, free of matter and exempt from potentiality. Between cause and effect there is an existential affinity through which the effect, with the existential perfection appropriate to its degree, represents the existential perfection realized in the cause in a higher and nobler manner. The same ruling applies if there are immaterial intellectual causes, one above another, until they end in the intrinsically Necessary—majestic is His remembrance.

We conclude from this that above the order prevailing in the witnessed world there is an intellectual, luminous order akin to it, which is the principle of this order. It ends in a divine order in His knowledge—exalted is He—which is the principle of the whole. This is another principle.

It is also necessary that the cause of a thing’s cause is a cause of that thing, and that the effect of a thing’s effect is an effect of that thing. Since causes end in the Necessary—exalted is He—every existent, however supposed, is His effect. In concrete reality there is nothing but the existence of substances, their effects, and the relations and connections between them. Nothing is independent in its existence except the intrinsically Necessary, and none bestows existence except Him.

It is therefore clear from the foregoing that the Necessary—exalted is He—is the one who brings about the order prevailing in our witnessed mode of being and governs through the universal governance encompassing the parts of the world. The same applies to the luminous intellectual orders above and corresponding to this order, in the manner appropriate to each according to its degree of existence. The intrinsically Necessary is thus the Lord of the world, governing its affairs through one bestowal of existence after another. Intermediate causes are merely subordinated to act as intermediaries, without independence. This is what was sought. It is therefore impossible for there to be any lord in the world other than Him, whether one or many.

Moreover, if a multiplicity of lords governing the world were supposed, as the idolaters maintain, impossibility would follow from another direction: the corruption of the order. To explain: multiplicity is realized only through units, and units only through the distinction of some from others. Distinction is accomplished only when each unit of the multiplicity possesses an intrinsic aspect that another unit lacks. Through this it differs from the other and they become distinct. All of this is necessary. The affinity between an agent and its act requires a difference between two acts corresponding to the difference between their agents. If there were separate lords, whether they jointly produced a single act or each aspect of the general cosmic order had a lord independent in its lordship—such as a lord of the heavens and earth, a lord of the human, and so forth—this would lead to corruption of the order and conflict among its parts. The unity of the order and the continuous mutual entailment among its parts rule this out.

It may be objected: the remarkable firmness and precision of the order prevailing among its parts testify that its governance proceeds from knowledge. The philosophical principles that establish the witnessed world’s dependence upon knowing immaterial causes support this. Let us grant, then, that the supposed lords have multiple, distinct essences, and that this naturally leads to differing and conflicting acts. Nevertheless, since they are rational, they might agree to peaceful cooperation and concord, attending to the good of the single order and preserving its continuance.

I reply: there is no doubt that the items of knowledge on which rational beings base their actions are cognitive forms and universal laws taken from the external order prevailing in the world. The external order has a kind of priority over these cognitive forms and universal laws, which follow it. But this external order, in its external existence, is the act of those supposed lords. It is impossible for an agent to be influenced in its act by cognitive forms abstracted from that act and posterior to it.

It may be objected: let us grant that the supposed lords producing the external order do not follow cognitive forms abstracted from their act, since these would be mental, acquired items of knowledge that follow what is known. But the supposed lords are knowing agents who know their act at the degree of their own essences, before the act. Why could they not agree to cooperation and concord in their knowledge prior to the act?

I reply: a knowing agent’s knowledge of its act before bringing it into being—as will be explained and has already been indicated—is knowledge by presence. Its basis is the cause’s possession of the effect’s perfection in a higher and nobler manner, and the affinity between the cause and its effect. Supposing the lords’ agreement and concord at the level of knowledge in this sense amounts to their cancelling the multiplicity and distinction in their existences. Yet their existences were supposed multiple and distinct. This is a contradiction.

[Authorial attribution and later editorial disagreement] The 1404 print p.281 (canvas285) and base attribute the plurality-of-lords position here to «الوثنية», idolaters. The 1428 editor substitutes «الثنوية», dualists, and defends that classification at vol2 pp.231–232 n.4. The earlier wording and its English translation are retained; the later interpretation is recorded as a disagreement, not a typographic correction. The same editor’s description of intermediate causes as merely preparatory conditions is likewise distinct from the chapter’s own qualified account of subordinated causation.

N-XII-07 · The intrinsically Necessary has no associate in any concept in respect of its instance
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 344–345
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=344&type=c_fbook

Sharing between two or more things occurs only when they are distinct and differentiated, and there is one concept by which they are characterized, as Zayd and ʿAmr are united in humanity, and the human and the horse in animality. Sharing is therefore unity in multiplicity. Multiplicity is realized only through distinct, differentiated units, each containing that by which the other units are denied of it. Each of the things sharing is therefore composed of negation and affirmation in respect of existence. Since the existence of the intrinsically Necessary is the reality of pure, simple existence, composition has no access to Him and there is no scope for negation in Him. Nothing therefore shares any meaning with Him.

Furthermore, the shared concept is either one of the quiddities or something reducible to them. Quiddities, which are intrinsically devoid of reality, have no access to the reality of the intrinsically Necessary, which is pure truth. Thus nothing is of the same genus as the intrinsically Necessary, since He has no genus; nothing is of the same species, since He has no species; nothing resembles Him, since He has no quality; nothing equals Him, since He has no quantity; nothing is congruent with Him, since He has no position; nothing stands alongside Him, since He has no place; and nothing is proportionate to Him, since no relation belongs to His essence.

Relational attributes additional to the essence—such as creation, provision, giving life, causing death, and others—are abstracted from the station of act, as will be explained, God willing. Moreover, relational attributes all reduce to His being the Sustainer. Since none brings into existence or exercises influence other than Him, He has no associate as Sustainer.

If the shared concept is one of the concepts abstracted from existence, what belongs to the intrinsically Necessary is its highest degree, infinite in intensity, unmingled with deficiency or nonexistence. What belongs to another is one of the degrees of the graded reality, not free of deficiency and composition. There is therefore no sharing.

As for predicating some concepts of both the intrinsically Necessary and other things—for example, existence predicated of Him and others with a shared meaning, disregarding the specificity of the instance, and likewise the other attributes of the Necessary considered solely as concepts, such as knowledge, life, and mercy, disregarding contingent particularities—this has nothing to do with the sharing under discussion.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 282–283 (canvases 286–287); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 234–235. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «المشاركة بين شيئين وأزيد» → «المشاركة بين شيئين أو أزيد» [L1]. Evidence: 1404 p.282/c286; 1428 p.234 n.2. Earlier printed reading: «بين شيئين وأزيد». Explicit 1428 p.234 n.2 correction; earlier conjunction preserved.

N-XII-08 · The attributes of the intrinsically Necessary in general, and their division
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 345–346
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=345&type=c_fbook

It was established above that no existential perfection is ever denied of necessary existence. Whatever perfection there is in existence—such as knowledge and power—necessary existence possesses it in a higher and nobler manner. It is predicated of Him in a way appropriate to the domain of His might and majesty. This is what is meant by His being characterized by attributes.

The primary division of attributes is into affirmative attributes, which convey a positive meaning, such as knowledge and power, and negative attributes, which convey a negative meaning. The latter can only negate the negation of a perfection, and thus return to affirming perfection, since the negation of a negation is an affirmation. Examples are our saying “one who is not ignorant” and “one who is not incapable,” which return to the knowing and the powerful. As for negating perfection, the preceding discussions have made clear that the negation of any perfection has no access to Him—exalted is He. Negative attributes therefore return in reality to affirmative attributes.

Affirmative attributes divide into real attributes, such as living, and relational attributes, such as being a knower and being powerful. Real attributes divide into purely real attributes, such as living, and real attributes involving a relation, such as creating and providing.

From another perspective, attributes divide into attributes of essence, for whose abstraction the supposition of the essence alone suffices, and attributes of act, whose abstraction depends upon supposing another. Since there is no existent other than Him—exalted is He—except His act, attributes of act are those abstracted from the station of act.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 283–284 (canvases 287–288); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 236–237. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted reading: «فالوجودالواجب يّواجدٌ له» → «فالوجود الواجبيّ واجدٌ له» [N1]. Evidence: 1404 p.283/c287; 1428 p.236. Earlier printed reading: «فالوجود الواجبي واجد له». Clear word boundaries in both external witnesses.

N-XII-09 · The attributes of essence are identical with the transcendent essence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 346–349
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=346&type=c_fbook

Investigators have differed concerning the attributes of essence abstracted from the necessary essence considered apart from anything else. Their positions are as follows.

First: these attributes are identical with the transcendent essence, and each is identical with every other. This is attributed to the philosophers.

Second: they are meanings additional to the essence and necessarily accompanying it, and are therefore eternal with its eternity. This is attributed to the Ashʿarites.

Third: they are additional to the essence and originated, according to the position attributed to the Karrāmiyya.

Fourth: the essence’s being characterized by these attributes means that the act issuing from it is the act of one possessing the attribute. Thus the transcendent essence’s being knowing means that the act issuing from it is precise, firm, and directed toward a rational end, as is the act of a knower. Its being powerful means that the act issuing from it is like the act of one possessing power. The essence therefore takes the place of the attributes.

Some appear inclined toward another position: affirming the attributes means denying their opposites. Thus affirming life, knowledge, and power, for example, means denying death, ignorance, and incapacity.

Others appear to hold that the attributes of essence are identical with the essence, but that they all have one meaning and their expressions are synonymous.

The truth is the first position. It has been established that the intrinsically Necessary is a complete cause to which every contingent existent ultimately returns, whether without an intermediary, through one intermediary, or through several. This means that the necessary reality is itself the cause. It has also been established that every existential perfection in an effect is possessed by its cause, at the station of its causality, in a higher and nobler manner. The intrinsically Necessary therefore possesses every supposed existential perfection. Moreover, He is pure existence, unmixed with nonexistence. It has been established that His existence is pure, simple, and one through true unity. There is therefore no multiplicity of aspects or difference of respects in His essence. Every existential perfection supposed in Him is identical with His essence and with every other perfection supposed of Him. The attributes of essence belonging to the intrinsically Necessary are therefore many and different in concept, but one in concrete reality and instance. This is what was sought.

The claim made by some that “the cause of bringing into existence is the will of the intrinsically Necessary, rather than His transcendent essence” has no coherent content. If, according to its proponent, this will is an attribute of essence identical with the essence, then attributing the bestowal of existence to it is identical with attributing it to the transcendent essence. Attributing it to the will while denying it of the essence is a manifest contradiction. If the will is an attribute of act abstracted from the station of act, the act precedes it. Attributing the production of the act to it would therefore assert that the effect precedes the cause, which is impossible. Moreover, attributing causality to the will of the intrinsically Necessary while denying it of the essence requires a difference between the Necessary and His will. This will is either independent of a cause, in which case it must be necessary in existence, entailing a multiplicity of necessary beings, which is impossible; or it depends upon a cause. If its cause is the Necessary, then the will is the cause of the world and the Necessary is its cause. Since the cause of a cause is a cause, the Necessary is the cause of the world. If its cause is something other than the Necessary and does not ultimately return to Him, it entails another necessary being in which it terminates, which is impossible.

As for the second position, attributed to the Ashʿarites: these attributes, which they enumerate as seven—life, knowledge, power, hearing, sight, will, and speech—are additional to the essence, necessarily accompany it, and are eternal with its eternity.

The objection is that, if these attributes are independent of a cause in their existence and subsist in their own right, there would be eight necessary beings: the essence and the seven attributes. The demonstrations of the Necessary’s oneness invalidate this and show it to be impossible.

If they depend upon a cause for their existence, and their cause is the essence, then the essence would be a cause preceding them and bestowing them while itself lacking them. This is impossible.

If their cause is other than the essence, they are necessary through another, and their necessity through another ends in a further necessary being other than the Necessary characterized by them. The demonstrations of the oneness of the intrinsically Necessary invalidate this too. It would also entail that the intrinsically Necessary needs another in order to possess the attributes of perfection. Need in any form is incompatible with intrinsic necessity of existence. Furthermore, it would entail that the Necessary lacks the attributes of perfection in His own essence, although it was established above that He is pure existence, lacking no existential perfection.

As for the third position, attributed to the Karrāmiyya, that these attributes are additional and originated: it entails that they are contingent and need a cause. Their cause is either the essence, which would entail that the essence bestows upon itself what it lacks, whose impossibility has been established; or something other than the essence, which would entail an aspect of contingency in it and the absence of existential perfections from it, whose impossibility has likewise been established.

As for the fourth position, attributed to the Muʿtazilites, that the essence substitutes for the attributes: it entails that the essence lacks perfection while bestowing every perfection, which is impossible.

This also refutes the claim that affirmative attributes of essence mean the negation of their opposites, so that life, knowledge, and power mean the negation of death, ignorance, and incapacity.

As for the claim that these attributes are identical with the essence and synonymous in a single meaning, it seems to arise from confusing the concept with its instance. What demonstration establishes is that their instance is one. Their concepts, however, are distinct and do not become one at all. Moreover, language and ordinary usage refute the claim of synonymy.

[translation] In the discussion of the Karrāmiyya, “the essence bestows upon itself what it lacks” follows the Qum 1404 witness, printed p. 287 (PDF p. 291): an tufīḍa al-dhātu li-nafsihā mā hiya fāqidatun lahu. This corrects a transcription error in the later electronic text.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 284–287 (canvases 288–291); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 238–242. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

N-XII-10 · The attributes of act are additional to the essence
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 349–350
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=349&type=c_fbook

There is no doubt that the intrinsically Necessary has attributes of act related to others, such as Creator, Provider, Giver, Generous, Forgiving, Merciful, and so forth. They are very numerous and are gathered together in the attribute of Sustainer.

Since they are related to something other than Him—exalted is He—their realization depends upon the realization of the other to which they are related. Every other thing supposed is an effect of the transcendent essence and posterior to it. The attribute depending upon that thing is therefore posterior to the essence and additional to it. It is abstracted from the station of act and ascribed to the transcendent essence.

A contingent existent, for example, has existence not through itself but through another. Considered with respect to itself, it is existence; considered with respect to another, it is a bestowal of existence by that other, of whom it is true that He brings it into existence. Under different considerations, its existence is origination without precedent, creation, making, blessing, and mercy. It is therefore true of its giver of existence that He is Originator, Creator, Maker, Benefactor, and Merciful.

Now, if the thing He brings into existence is such that its existence has some continuance, there are things available to it by which it maintains its continuance and removes its aspects of deficiency and need. When such a thing is considered in itself, the concept of provision by which it is sustained is abstracted from it. When it is considered as existing not through itself but through another, its cause that bestows it, that other is truly called its Provider. Under further different considerations, provision is truly called a gift, blessing, bestowal, generosity, and bounty; and the Provider is truly called Giver, Benefactor, Bestower, Generous, Bountiful, and so on. The other attributes of act follow the same pattern, multiplying with the multiplication of aspects of perfection in existence.

These attributes of act are truly predicated of Him—exalted is He. They are not predicated in respect of their particular characteristics of origination and posteriority to the transcendent essence, which would entail change in Him—exalted and holy—and the composition of His essence from many distinct aspects. Rather, they are predicated insofar as they have a source in the essence from which every perfection and good proceeds. He—exalted is He—is such that every possible perfection subsists through Him in its own proper locus. He is such that, when a thing is possible, it is willed by Him; when He wills a thing, He brings it into existence; when He brings it into existence, He nurtures it; when He nurtures it, He perfects it; and so forth. To the Necessary belong His necessity and eternity, and to things belong their contingency and origination.

[External comparison · recovered Stage IX, reconciled Stage X] Qum 1404 printed pp. 287–288 (canvases 291–292); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 243–244. The recovered Stage IX report records a complete comparison of this unit and its associated later notes; Stage X reconciles its base hashes, proposals and apparatus. The 1428 witness was read in a digital presentation, not as a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Later editorial commentary, kept separate from the authorial argument:

1428 p.243 nn.1–3: Full verses and both glosses read. The poems are later annotation, absent from early chapter prose.

No new Arabic or English body change is adopted from this completed review. Existing body readings and earlier adopted decisions are preserved.

N-XII-11 · His knowledge—exalted is He
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 350–356
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=350&type=c_fbook

It was established above that every immaterial being has knowledge of its own essence, because its essence, free of matter, is present to itself. Knowledge is nothing but the presence of one thing to another. The Necessary—exalted is He—is free of matter and potentiality. His essence is therefore known to His essence.

It was also established that His transcendent essence is the reality of pure, simple existence, one through true unity, into which neither deficiency nor nonexistence enters. There is therefore no existential perfection in the particulars of creation and their existential order that His essence does not possess in a higher and nobler manner. Because of its purity and simplicity, these perfections are not distinguished from one another. Everything other than Him is thus known to Him—exalted is He—at the degree of His transcendent essence, with a knowledge that is detailed in its very comprehensiveness and comprehensive in its very detail.

It was further established that all other existents are His effects, terminating in Him without an intermediary, through one intermediary, or through several. Their essences subsist through Him as the relational subsists through the independent. They are present to Him through their existences, without being veiled from Him. They are therefore known to Him at the degree of their existences through knowledge by presence: immaterial things through themselves, and material things through their immaterial forms.

The foregoing establishes that the Necessary—exalted is He—knows His essence at the degree of His essence, and that this knowledge is identical with His essence; that He knows the existents other than His essence at the degree of His essence, which is called “knowledge before bringing into existence”; that this is comprehensive knowledge in the very act of detailed disclosure; that He has detailed knowledge of the existents other than His essence at the degree of their own essences, outside the transcendent essence, which is “knowledge after bringing into existence”; and that His knowledge is knowledge by presence however it is conceived.

These are five questions.

It follows that every item of knowledge established at the degrees of contingent beings, among immaterial intellectual and imaginal causes, is knowledge belonging to Him—exalted is He.

It also follows that He is Hearing and Seeing, just as He is Knowing and Aware. For the reality of hearing and sight is knowledge of audible things and knowledge of visible things, both belonging to knowledge without qualification; and every knowledge belongs to Him—exalted is He.

Investigators have differed greatly concerning His knowledge—exalted is He—so much so that some have denied it altogether. Such a person is refuted by the demonstration establishing it.

Those who affirm it hold various positions.

First: He—exalted is He—knows His essence but not its effects, because the transcendent essence is eternal and every effect is originated.

The objection is that knowing an effect eternally does not require that it exist eternally with its own specific existence. Moreover, this position rests upon restricting knowledge by presence to a thing’s knowledge of itself and treating everything else as acquired knowledge that follows the known. This is rejected by what was established above: an immaterial cause has knowledge by presence of its immaterial effect. Demonstration has established that He—exalted is He—has knowledge by presence of His effects before bringing them into existence, at the degree of the essence, and knowledge by presence of them after bringing them into existence, at the degree of the effects.

Second: the position attributed to Plato, that His detailed knowledge—exalted is He—is the immaterial intellects and divine Forms in which the perfections of species are gathered in detail.

The objection is that this belongs to knowledge after bringing into existence, at the degree of their contingent existences. Confining His detailed knowledge of things to them entails that the transcendent essence, in itself, lacks the perfection of knowledge, whereas it is pure existence from which no existential perfection is absent.

Third: the position attributed to Porphyry, that His knowledge—exalted is He—is through union with the known.

The objection is that this suffices only to explain the realization of knowledge and that it occurs through union of the intellect with the intelligible, rather than through inherence as an accident or the like. It does not suffice to establish knowledge of things before or after bringing them into existence.

Fourth: the position attributed to the Shaykh of Illumination, followed by a group of later investigators, that things, both immaterial and material, are present to Him—exalted is He—through their concrete existence, neither absent nor veiled from Him. This is His detailed knowledge of things after bringing them into existence, while He has comprehensive knowledge of them as a consequence of His knowledge of His essence.

The first objection is that the claim that material things are present to Him—exalted is He—is rejected. Materiality does not coexist with presence, as explained in the discussions of the intellective subject and the intelligible.

The second objection is that confining detailed knowledge of things to the degree of their existences entails that the transcendent essence, which bestows every detailed perfection in things, lacks their detail. Yet it is pure existence, encompassing every existential perfection in a higher and nobler manner.

Fifth: the position attributed to al-Malaṭī, that He—exalted is He—knows the first intellect, which is the first emanated being, through its presence to Him, and knows all other things below the first intellect through the impression of their forms in the first intellect.

The objection is that this is subject to the same difficulty as the previous position: it requires that the transcendent essence lack perfection, although it possesses every perfection. Moreover, it was established in the discussions of the intellective subject and the intelligible that immaterial intellects have no acquired knowledge through impressed forms.

Sixth: the statement of some that “His transcendent essence is detailed knowledge of the first effect and comprehensive knowledge of what is below it; the essence of the first effect is detailed knowledge of the second effect and comprehensive knowledge of what is below it; and so on.”

The objection is the unacceptable consequence that the transcendent essence lacks the perfection of knowing what is below the first effect, although it is pure existence from which no perfection is denied.

Seventh: the position attributed to most later thinkers, that He—exalted is He—has detailed knowledge of His own essence, which is comprehensive knowledge of things before bringing them into existence. His detailed knowledge of things, however, comes after their existence, because knowledge follows the known and there is no known before concrete existence.

The objection is the unacceptable consequence that the transcendent essence lacks the perfection of knowledge, as in the preceding positions. Moreover, it affirms acquired knowledge through impressed forms in purely immaterial existence.

Eighth: the position attributed to the Peripatetics, that He—exalted is He—has knowledge by presence of His transcendent essence and detailed acquired knowledge of things before bringing them into existence. Their quiddities are present to His essence in the order found externally, not by entering it through identity or as parts, but by subsisting through it with mental establishment in a universal manner. “Universal” here means that the knowledge does not change when the known changes, according to the terminology of the discussions of knowledge. It is thus providential knowledge: the occurrence of the known in knowledge entails its occurrence in concrete reality.

The first objection is the same difficulty as in the preceding position: the essence would lack perfection.

The second is likewise the difficulty in the preceding position: acquired knowledge would be established in that which is immaterial both in essence and in act.

The third is that this entails the establishment of mental existence without a concrete existence to which it is compared. Consequently, it becomes another concrete existence of the quiddity before the existence proper to it, with an existence separate from Him—exalted is He. On close examination, it returns to the second position, attributed to Plato.

Know that most theologians hold this position, even though they criticize it for describing knowledge before bringing into existence as universal, supposing that “universal” means what is meant in logic’s discussion of universal and particular. For they have chosen the view that detailed knowledge before bringing into existence is acquired knowledge and remains unchanged before and after things exist.

Ninth: the Muʿtazilite position that quiddities have concrete establishment in nonexistence, and this is the object of His knowledge—exalted is He—before bringing into existence.

The objection is that the falsity of the doctrine of the establishment of nonexistent things has already been demonstrated.

Tenth: the position attributed to the Sufis, that quiddities have establishment in knowledge as a consequence of the names and attributes, and this is the object of His knowledge—exalted is He—before bringing into existence.

The objection is that the primacy of existence and the intellectually considered status of quiddity negate any supposed establishment of quiddities before their own specific concrete establishment.

[translation] “Comprehensive” renders ijmālī. In the account of divine knowledge, it denotes an undivided possession of the details, not an incomplete or merely approximate knowledge.

N-XII-12 · Providence, decree, and measure
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 356–360
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=356&type=c_fbook

They have mentioned providence, decree, and measure among the degrees of His knowledge—exalted is He—because each, through its own specific concept, applies to a particular aspect of His knowledge.

As for providence, it consists in the cognitive form’s being a necessitating cause of the known, which is the act. His detailed knowledge of things, identical with His essence, is the cause of their existence with their known particular characteristics. He—exalted is He—therefore exercises providence over His creation.

As for decree, in its familiar meaning it is to make the relation between a subject and its predicate necessary and binding. For example, when Zayd and ʿAmr dispute some property or right, refer their dispute to a judge, and present their arguments, the judge’s statement in deciding the case, “The property belongs to Zayd, and the right belongs to ʿAmr,” establishes Zayd’s ownership and ʿAmr’s right necessarily. It thereby removes the uncertainty and hesitation produced by the dispute before the judgment and the settlement of the case. In brief, the judge’s decree makes the matter necessary in knowledge, followed by making it externally necessary in a conventional sense.

When this meaning is taken through analysis as real rather than conventional, it applies to the necessity with which contingent existents are clothed in relation to their complete causes. For a thing does not exist until it is necessitated. This necessity through another, considered in relation to the complete cause, is necessitation. There is nothing in the series of contingent existence that is not necessary, necessitated through another. Causes end in the intrinsically Necessary, who is therefore the cause necessitating them and their effects.

Since contingent existents, with their best order at the degree of their concrete existences, are active knowledge belonging to the Necessary—exalted is He—the necessitation within them is a decree from Him. Above this is His essential knowledge, through which everything is disclosed to Him in detail as it is in concrete reality, in a higher and nobler manner.

Decree is therefore of two kinds: essential decree, outside the world, and active decree, within it.

This shows the weakness of what is attributed to the prevalent view: that decree is the knowledge of contingent existents and their order possessed by the separate intellects.

The same applies to Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn’s—may God have mercy on him—view that decree is essential knowledge relating to the details of creation. He says in the Asfār:

“As for decree, according to them it means the existence of the intellectual forms of all existents, bestowed by Him—exalted is He—through origination without precedent, all at once and without time. For in their view these belong to the world and to God’s acts, whose essences are distinct from His essence. In our view, they are cognitive forms necessarily accompanying His essence, without making, influence, or being affected. They are not parts of the world, since they possess neither a negative aspect nor real possibilities. Divine decree, which is the form of God’s knowledge, is therefore intrinsically eternal and endures through God’s endurance.” End of quotation.

His expression “cognitive forms necessarily accompanying His essence” should be understood as essential knowledge that is inseparable from the essence. If they necessarily accompanied it while being external to it, they would belong to the world and would not be intrinsically eternal, as he expressly states. Moreover, if they were known by presence, this would coincide with Plato’s account of knowledge, which he does not accept; and if they were acquired forms, it would coincide with the Peripatetic account, which he also does not accept.

The weakness of both positions is that the applicability of the concept of decree to one of the two degrees of knowledge—essential knowledge and active knowledge—does not deny its applicability to the other. The truth is therefore that decree is of two kinds, essential and active, as explained above.

As for measure, it is the quantity or limit attaching to a thing in its attributes and effects. Determination is the specification in knowledge of the attributes and effects attaching to it, followed by action according to what the available causes and instruments allow. A tailor, for example, determines the garment he will sew upon the cloth before him, and then sews according to what he has determined. A builder determines the building he intends upon a tract of land, according to what it accommodates and what the causes and instruments available to him require. He then constructs the building in conformity with what he has determined, owing to renewed causes that require this of him. Determination is thus to the thing determined as a mold in which a thing is cast, delimiting it by a limit or limits that it does not exceed.

When this meaning is taken as real through analysis, it applies to the limits attaching to material existents through their incomplete causes, with the cognitive forms those causes have in the mode of being above them. Each incomplete cause, with its different aspects, has an effect upon the effect that restricts its unrestricted character in its attributes and effects. When the restriction is completed through the completion of the complete cause, the thing acquires determination and individuation through the existence required by the complete cause. A human, for example, has the property of seeing, but not through his entire existence: rather, through his body. Nor through his entire body, but through an organ situated in his face. He therefore sees only what faces him; and not everything that faces him, but bodies; and not every body, but dense bodies possessing color; and not the body itself, but its surface; and not all its surfaces, but the surface facing him. Nor does he see in every position, every state, every place, or every time. If you counted the conditions surrounding a single individual act of vision, you would find an immense multitude beyond enumeration. These are nothing but limits attached to that vision by the incomplete causes that delimit it through the effect they place in it. Some of these causes are prevented by impediments from exerting their effect.

These limits are existential aspects accompanied by negations, as was just explained. They have cognitive forms in the imaginal mode of being above the material mode, through which the attributes and effects of things are determined. None of them has access to any attribute or effect except that to which determination has directed it.

You may object: this account entails that the human is compelled and has no choice in his acts.

I reply: not at all. Choice is one of the conditions through which the human act is delimited. We have discussed the refutation of this doubt in detail in the discussions of existence and of cause and effect.

You may object: why not generalize the account of measure, which is the imposition of limits upon a thing in respect of its attributes and effects in a prior knowledge followed by concrete reality, so that it also encompasses contingent quiddities? Quiddities too are limits of their subjects, through which they are distinguished from others and accompanied by negations they do not exceed. It was established above that everything possessing a quiddity is contingent, and that the essence of the contingent is composed of affirmation and negation. Measure would therefore encompass every contingent thing, whether an immaterial intellect, a suspended imaginal form, or a material nature; and the prior knowledge through which the thing is determined would be essential knowledge.

In brief, measure in concrete reality would be the determination abstracted from concrete existence, and determining would be the specification in knowledge followed by concrete reality. Likewise, what is decreed is the necessity abstracted from concrete existence, and decree is the necessitation in knowledge that entails it, whether with respect to quiddity and essence or to attributes and effects.

I reply: there is no doubt that quiddity is an intrinsic limit of the contingent. In discussing measure, however, they have attended to the apparent meaning of its concept: the limit attaching to a thing in the attributes and effects of which it is a subject, rather than in its original essence. It therefore does not encompass what lies beyond natures that have some attachment to matter.

Their purpose in undertaking this discussion is to show that the contingent is not left unrestrained in the attributes and effects attaching to it, independently of the Necessary—exalted is He—in what characterizes it or what it does. Rather, the matter belongs to Him, and nothing occurs except what He has determined. This is close in meaning to their saying, “The cause of a thing’s cause is a cause of that thing.”

Likewise, their purpose in discussing decree is to show that the contingent occurs only through a necessity through another that ends in Him—exalted is He—in prior knowledge. This is close in meaning to their saying, “A thing does not exist until it is necessitated.”

[Source reference · restored from the early printed edition] Asfār, vol. 6, p. 292. This volume/page reference follows the quotation in the early printed edition. The later edition gives vol. 6, pp. 291–292. The Asfār source itself has not been independently collated in this review.

N-XII-13 · His power—exalted is He
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 360–364
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=360&type=c_fbook

Among the meanings we count as existential perfections is power. It concerns action rather than affection: we do not count a thing’s being affected by another, whether strongly or weakly, as power. Nor does it concern every action, but only an action of which the agent has knowledge. We do not call the agency of natural agents devoid of awareness their power. Nor does it concern every action known to its agent, but a knowledgeable act in which knowledge of the act impels its agent to perform it. A human’s agency in relation to his natural bodily activities, for example, is not power even if he knows them. Rather, it concerns an act that the agent knows to be good for him precisely insofar as he is this agent: he conceives it and assents that it is good for him insofar as he is this agent.

A necessary consequence of knowing that the act is good for the agent is that it is a perfection for him that he himself requires. For the good of every species is the perfection following upon it, and its specific nature is the principle that requires this perfection. If the agent is supposed to know it as a good and perfection for himself that he requires, he is impelled toward it through himself, rather than by the necessitation of another determining principle imposing it upon him. There is therefore no power together with compulsion. One possessing power chooses, in the sense that the act is determined for him through his own determination, rather than through determination by another.

When the knowledge that the act is good is complete, it is followed by the agent’s desire for the act. Good is loved without qualification and desired when absent. This desire is certainly a psychic quality distinct from the preceding knowledge. It is followed by will, which is certainly a psychic quality distinct both from the preceding knowledge and from desire. With its realization, the act is realized: the movement of the muscles through the active faculty diffused within them.

These are the qualifications that investigation discloses in the power found in us: being a principle of the act; knowledge that it is good for the agent, a knowledge necessarily accompanied by choice in the act; desire for the act; and will directed toward it. It has been established that every existential perfection in existence belongs to the Necessary—exalted is He—within His own essence. He is therefore necessary power itself. Desire, however, has no access to Him, because it is a psychic quality necessarily accompanied by lack; lack necessarily accompanies deficiency, and He is free of every deficiency and nonexistence.

The same applies to will as a psychic quality distinct from knowledge and desire. It is a contingent quiddity, while the Necessary—exalted is He—is free of quiddity and contingency. Moreover, will in this sense accompanies what is willed when the latter belongs to things subject to generation and corruption: it does not exist before it or remain after it. For the Necessary to be characterized by it would therefore entail change in that which is characterized, which is impossible.

It follows that power, stripped of deficiencies and nonexistences, is a thing’s being an efficient principle of an act through knowledge that it is good and through choice in giving it preponderance. The Necessary—exalted is He—is intrinsically the efficient principle of every existent. He intrinsically knows the most fitting order in things, and intrinsically chooses in His act, since no agent other than Him exercises influence upon Him. He is therefore intrinsically powerful. The account we have given applies to immaterial intellects as well.

You may object: the path you have followed to establish power for the Necessary—exalted is He—does not establish will for Him precisely as will. Their definition of power as “a thing’s being such that, if it wills, it acts, and if it does not will, it does not act” includes affirming will as an essential attribute of the Necessary constitutive of power. They have, however, explained necessary will as knowledge of the most fitting order.

I reply: their account of the meaning of power returns to the account we have given, which includes the three qualifications of being a principle, knowledge, and choice. What they say about the meaning of His power—exalted is He—is therefore true. The whole issue lies in their taking His knowledge as an instance of will. There is no way to establish this, and it is more like an act of naming.

You may object: a single existence may have different quiddities and unequal degrees according to its different modes of being. Knowledge, for example, is a psychic quality when it concerns what is outside us, but a psychic substance when it concerns our souls. An intellect’s knowledge of itself is an intellectual substance; the Necessary’s knowledge of His essence is intrinsically necessary, and a contingent being’s knowledge of itself is intrinsically contingent. That the will in us is a psychic quality therefore does not exclude the Necessary’s will for His act being His essential knowledge.

Moreover, it is conceded that a choosing agent does whatever it does only through will and volition. The Necessary—exalted is He—is a choosing agent, so He has a will for His act. But the will in us, which is a psychic quality, is not realized there. There is nothing there except knowledge and the choice necessarily accompanying it. His knowledge is therefore His will, and He is willing insofar as He is knowing through His knowledge identical with His essence.

I reply: what we concede is that a choosing animal agent does whatever it does only through knowledge of the benefit of the act and through will in the sense of a psychic quality. We also concede that the Necessary—exalted is He—does whatever He does only through knowledge of the benefit of the act. But that the knowledge found there has the existence of will and volition, even though its quiddity is not a psychic quality, is not conceded. We may, to be sure, abstract will from the station of act, like the other attributes of act, as was indicated in the discussion of attributes of act and will be explained further.

In brief, there is no proof that the concept of will applies to the Necessary’s knowledge of the most fitting order. What is intended by its concept is either what we find in ourselves, in which case it is a psychic quality distinct from knowledge, or some other concept capable of applying to knowledge that the act is good. We know of no such concept of will. For this reason, we said earlier that calling the Necessary’s knowledge of the best order His will is more like an act of naming.

Will should not be compared with knowledge, which is said to be a psychic quality and is then stripped of quiddity and made into a general existential aspect belonging to the Necessary—exalted is He—as an attribute of essence identical with the essence. For even if we concede that some instances of knowledge, namely acquired knowledge, are psychic qualities, other instances, namely knowledge by presence, are substance or something else. It has been established that a concept applying to more than one category is an existential attribute that falls under no category, abstracted from existence insofar as it is existence. Knowledge therefore has a common meaning to which analysis leads: the presence of one thing to another.

You may object: if will has no known meaning except the psychic quality found in animals, why is it abstracted from the station of act, where there is no psychic quality? This testifies that it has a meaning broader than a psychic quality and that it is an existential attribute like knowledge.

I reply: just as an expression may be used for its real meaning, it may also, by extension, be used for the necessary concomitants of that real meaning and the effects branching from it. Attributes abstracted from the station of act subsist through the act, originate with its origination, and are intrinsically posterior to the intrinsically eternal essence. It is therefore impossible for the intrinsically necessary essence to be characterized by them, whether by identity or by inherence as an accident, as explained above—unless what is intended, by extension, is the necessary concomitants of the real meaning and the effects branching from it. Mercy, for example, as we find it in ourselves, is a psychic response and affection upon witnessing a needy, afflicted person who requires some perfection, such as well-being, health, or survival. It is followed by the merciful person relieving his need and destitution, and is therefore a praiseworthy attribute of perfection. Response and affection are impossible for Him—exalted is He—so He is not characterized by mercy in its real meaning. But from the removal of need and the attainment of sufficiency, for example, mercy is abstracted, because these are among its necessary consequences. Since it is mercy related to Him, the attribute Merciful is derived from it as one of His attributes of act. Other cases follow the same pattern.

The will ascribed to Him—exalted is He—is abstracted from the station of act: either from the very act that exists externally, so that it is will, then necessitation, then necessity, then the bestowal of existence, then existence; or from the presence of the complete cause of the act, as we say, when we observe an agent assembling the causes of an act in order to perform it, “He intends to perform such-and-such an act.”

[External comparison · new Stage X review] Qum 1404 printed pp. 296–300 (canvases 300–304); 1428 edited digital text, vol. 2 pp. 258–264. Stage X compared every stored Arabic and English paragraph with the complete early printed chapter and the later digital chapter, including the chapter boundaries and all associated later editorial notes. The early witness is a facsimile; the 1428 witness is a digital presentation, not a facsimile. No manuscript or independent human specialist certification is claimed.

Adopted [E1]: «الفواعل الطبيعيّة العادمة للشهور» → «الفواعل الطبيعيّة العادمة للشعور». The early print clearly has الشعور, awareness. The base and later digital witness share الشهور, months. The source and context justify the correction; the existing English already says awareness. Evidence: 1404 p. 297 / canvas 301; compare 1428 p. 258, which still prints للشهور.

Adopted [N2]: «القدرة المجردّة عن النواقص» → «القدرة المجرّدة عن النواقص». Correct the misplaced shadda to the form المجرّدة. Consonants and philosophical content are unchanged. Evidence: 1404 p. 298 / canvas 302; 1428 p. 259.

The early text and base omit it; the context supplies the agent and no addition is needed. Evidence: 1404 p. 297 / canvas 301; 1428 p. 259.

Recorded variant; earlier body retained: «لا سبيل لتطرّق الشوق عليه»; later proposal «لا سبيل لتطرّق الشوق إليه». The early print agrees with عليه. The later agreement correction makes the antecedent action attributes explicit.
