English comparison: Every English paragraph was checked against the complete Arabic and both witnesses. Awareness already translates the intended الشعور and needs no change. The distinction between human will as a psychic quality, the unproved identification of divine essential knowledge with will, and will abstracted from the station of act is preserved. The English plural them interprets the earlier awkward به in reference to the action attributes; that interpretation and the later editor’s بها are disclosed. No English body correction is adopted.

Later editorial commentary, separate from the authorial argument:

1428 vol. 2 258 n. 1 — Choice gloss: The editor explains self-determination as what the agent’s own essence requires. This is an explanatory gloss.

1428 vol. 2 259 nn. 1–6 — Syntax, references and ontological explanation: All six notes were read: the attachment of من القيود, the reference to XII.6, power as an existential perfection, the reasoning from psychic quality through accident/quiddity/limitation to lack, the ellipsis excluding will, and the referent of إذا كان.

1428 vol. 2 260 n. 1 continued through 261 — Two definitions of power and a separate Asfar annotation: The entire lengthy note contrasts the theologians’ possibility of acting/not acting with the philosophers’ conditional definition. It questions each when understood through an added psychic will and then quotes, with abridgment, Tabatabai’s separate Asfar 6:307–308 annotation on the unconditioned efficient agent. The quote’s conclusion identifies both definitions with the agent’s freedom from necessitation imposed by act or non-act. It is not inserted into the Nihaya authorial body.

1428 vol. 2 261 nn. 1–4 — Definition sources, philosophical referent and Avicenna attribution: References for the conditional definition and knowledge of the best order, identification of the philosophers, and the attribution to Avicenna of conceptual as well as extensional identity were read. The editor’s bibliographic references have not been independently verified.

1428 vol. 2 261 n. 5 continued through 262 — Concept versus instance: The full argument distinguishes identity of instances, shared by all divine perfections, from identity of concepts. The editor argues that saying God knows and saying God wills do not convey the same concept. This reinforces the author’s objection but remains separately attributed exposition.

1428 vol. 2 262 nn. 1–4 — Orthographic emendations and references: Both explicit changes of مشيّة to مشيئة and the references to XII.10 and the end of this chapter were read. The normalized spelling is recorded as later editorial work; it is not treated as an early or manuscript reading.

1428 vol. 2 263 nn. 1–6 — References and grammatical changes: The backwards reference, philosopher/source citations, reference to the earlier editorial argument, gloss of ما بالها, addition of أن before تنتزع, and the change أن يتّصف به to أن تتّصف بها were all read. The earlier construction without أن is retained. The agreement change is disclosed as a later emendation, with the English’s plural interpretation made explicit.

1428 vol. 2 264 nn. 1–2 — Lexical preference and cross-reference: The later editor explicitly replaces العروض with العرض and refers to XII.10. The earlier العروض, conveying inherence or occurrence as an accident, is retained, consistently with the other retained loci.

N-XII-14 · The Necessary is the principle of every contingent existent: the discussion of His will’s encompassing all acts
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 364–370
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=364&type=c_fbook

The preceding principles have established that what is fundamental in every thing is its existence; that the existent divides into the intrinsically Necessary and everything else; that everything other than the intrinsically Necessary, whether substance or accident—or, in other words, essence, attribute, or act—has an intrinsically contingent quiddity equally related to existence and nonexistence; and that whatever is such needs, in becoming clothed with either existence or nonexistence, a preponderating principle that determines and necessitates that alternative, namely, a necessitating cause. Every contingent existent therefore needs, both in its origination and continuance, a cause that necessitates its existence and brings it into existence, a cause that is intrinsically necessary or ends in the intrinsically Necessary. The cause of a thing’s cause is a cause of that thing. Thus every contingent existent other than the intrinsically Necessary, even voluntary acts, is an act of the intrinsically Necessary and His effect, without an intermediary, through one intermediary, or through several.

Another route is as follows. It became clear in the discussions of cause and effect that the existence of an effect in relation to its cause is a relational, nonindependent existence, subsisting through the existence of the cause. Contingent existences, whatever they are, are therefore relations to the existence of the intrinsically Necessary, not independent of Him, and encompassed by Him in the sense of not being outside Him. In existence there is thus only one independent essence, through which these relations subsist and acquire independence. Essences, together with their attributes and acts, are His acts.

He—exalted is He—is therefore the proximate agent of every act and of its agent. The independence that appears to belong to every contingent cause in relation to its effect is the independence of the Necessary, besides which there is in reality no independence.

There is no contradiction between His being a proximate agent, as this demonstration establishes, and His being a remote agent, as established by the preceding demonstration based upon the ordered succession of causes and upon the cause of a thing’s cause being a cause of that thing. The requirement of remoteness follows from considering the existences of the quiddities of causes and effects as independently existent in themselves, according to initial consideration. Proximity is what precise consideration establishes.

It is evident that there is no conflict between attributing the act to the intrinsically necessary agent and attributing it to the agent that is its subject, such as the human. Their agency is vertical, not horizontal.

A group of theologians, namely the Muʿtazilites and their followers, have maintained that voluntary acts are created by the human and that the Necessary—exalted is He—has no part in them. His role is only to enable the human to act by creating for him the causes through which he can act, such as the faculties and limbs by which he reaches the act through his choice, which makes both acting and refraining valid for him. He can therefore refrain from the act even if the Necessary wills it, and perform the act even if the Necessary dislikes it. The Necessary has no part in producing his act. Moreover, if the act were created by the Necessary, He would be its agent rather than the human. It would then make no sense to impose obligations upon the human through command and prohibition, or to promise and threaten, or for him to deserve reward and punishment for obedience and disobedience, since no act or abstention would belong to the human. Furthermore, for Him to be the agent of voluntary acts, which include various evils and morally objectionable things such as unbelief, denial, and kinds of disobedience and sin, conflicts with the freedom of the domain of majesty and greatness from what does not befit it.

The reply is that voluntary acts are contingent things. Rational necessity judges that a contingent quiddity is equally related to existence and nonexistence and cannot leave the exact middle for either alternative without a preponderating principle necessitating it, namely a necessitating cause. The agent is one of the causes. It makes no sense for an agent whose agency is complete, accompanied by the remaining parts of the complete cause, to stand in an equal relation to acting and refraining. Rather, it necessitates the act. This necessity through another ends in the intrinsically Necessary, who is therefore the first cause of the act. The first cause is a cause of the final effect, since the cause of a thing’s cause is a cause of that thing. These are established principles demonstrated in the preceding discussions. They show that the act is related to the Necessary—exalted is He—through the bestowal of existence, and to the human, for example, as a subordinated agent who is an effect in the very act of being a cause. The Necessary’s agency is vertically ordered with the human’s agency, rather than horizontally alongside it such that they would conflict and could not coexist.

As for the necessary will’s relation to the act while the human chooses it, that will relates to the human’s performing such-and-such an act through his choice. It does not relate to the act without the qualification of choice. Choice is therefore not rendered futile, nor is the effect of human will abolished. Moreover, excluding voluntary acts from the scope of necessary power, so that He wills and it does not occur, or dislikes and it does occur, restricts the absolute power identical with the essence of the Necessary. Demonstration rejects this. Demonstration also establishes that bringing into existence and bestowing existence are peculiar to the Necessary—exalted is He—with no associate. How well Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified—said in a similar context: “There is no doubt that the doctrine of one who makes all individual human beings creators of their own acts, independent in bringing them into existence, is more reprehensible than the doctrine of one who makes idols or stars intercessors with God.” End of quotation.

As for their saying, “A voluntary act’s being created by the Necessary is incompatible with directing obligation toward the human through command and prohibition, with promise and threat regarding action and abstention, and with deserving reward and punishment, since he has no act and is not an agent”: this would hold only if attributing the act to the Necessary could not coexist with attributing it to the human. You have learned, however, that the agency is vertical. The act is related to the Necessary through acting in the sense of bringing into existence, and to the choosing human in the sense of an accident’s subsistence in its subject.

As for their saying, “The human’s voluntary acts being created by the Necessary, while including kinds of evil, disobedience, and morally objectionable things, conflicts with the purity of His domain from every deficiency and blemish”: the reply is that the evils found in the world, as will become clear, are nothing but things containing much good and little evil. Their little evil enters existence as a consequence of their much good. Evil is therefore intended by a secondary intention, while the primary intention relates only to good. Moreover, it will also become clear that existence, insofar as it is existence, is nothing but good, and evils merely attach to some existences. What the Necessary bestows of an act is its existence, intrinsically good and pure in itself. The deficiency and nonexistence accompanying it are necessary consequences of its distinction in existence. Without existential distinctions, the order of existence would be corrupted. Omitting the little evil would therefore abolish the much good found in the parts of the order.

Another group of theologians, namely the Ashʿarites and their followers, have maintained that everything existent other than the intrinsically Necessary, whether essence, attribute, or act, exists through the will of the intrinsically Necessary without an intermediary. All are His acts, and He alone is the agent.

The first consequence is the removal of causality and being caused from among things. Causes would be followed by their effects merely through custom: God’s custom would be to bring about effects after causes, without any influence of the causes upon the effects or dependence of the effects upon the causes.

The second consequence is that acts counted as voluntary would be compelled acts, in which the will and choice of their agents have no influence.

The reply is that attributing the act to the Necessary through the bestowal of existence does not conflict with attributing it to other intermediaries. The attribution is vertical rather than horizontal, as explained above. The reality of the mediation of intermediaries returns to the existence of the effect being restricted by qualifications specifying its existence. The connection of existents to one another, horizontally and vertically, makes the whole one, with some of its parts restricted by others in their existence. Bestowing one of them is therefore accomplished only by bestowing the whole. The bestowal is only one, and each receives what it has the capacity to receive.

As for denying causality and being caused among things, the demonstration given in the stage on cause and effect suffices to refute it. Moreover, if there were no relation of influence and being affected among things, and what we find of this among them were false and unreal, we would have no way to establish an agent beyond them, namely the Necessary who is the agent of all.

As for asserting compulsion and denying choice in acts on the grounds that the agency of the intrinsically Necessary and His will’s relation to the act called voluntary make that act necessary in realization and occurrence, so that it makes no sense for an act necessary in existence to be voluntary for the human, who can perform it or refrain, or for his will to influence it: the reply is that His agency is vertical and does not conflict with the agency of another when that too is vertically ordered. His will relates to the act precisely as voluntary. He wills that the human perform such-and-such an act through his own choice and will. The voluntary act is therefore necessary in realization precisely as voluntary.

Some have argued for compulsion in acts by saying that an act of disobedience is known to the Necessary—exalted is He—and is therefore necessary in realization and occurrence. If it did not occur, His knowledge would be ignorance, which is impossible. The act is thus necessary, and necessary occurrence cannot coexist with the act’s being voluntary.

Against this, the act of disobedience is known to the Necessary with the specific character of its occurrence: that it issues from the human through his choice. It is therefore necessary in realization and occurrence precisely as voluntary. If it did not occur, His knowledge would be ignorance, which is impossible. The act is therefore necessary in realization insofar as it is voluntary.

A clarification

Their argument for compulsion in acts from the Necessary’s knowledge of them and the consequent determination of their occurrence is in reality an appeal to decree in knowledge, which makes the matters it concerns inevitable. As for will, which they regard as an affirmative attribute additional to the essence, they do not regard it as a principle of the act that necessitates it. They suppose that the necessity of the act would make the agent compelled—mūjab—whereas the Necessary is a choosing agent. Rather, will’s function is to give the act preponderance through priority without necessity. Will may therefore specify whichever of the two alternatives of the act it concerns.

These are unsound views whose falsity is clear from the preceding principles. The necessity attaching to the effect is necessity through another, abstracted from its existence bestowed by its cause, and is an effect of that cause. If this necessity then returned to influence the cause by making it compelled in its agency, what is posterior in existence, precisely as posterior, would precede what is prior in existence, precisely as prior. This is impossible. Moreover, if a choosing agent became compelled through the necessity of the act, there would be no difference whether the effect’s necessity depended upon prior knowledge and a preceding decree or upon the agent’s necessitation of the act, which is the meaning of our saying, “A thing does not exist until it is necessitated.”

It has also become clear from the foregoing that preponderance through mere priority returns to the contingent’s not needing a preponderating principle to determine one of the alternatives of existence and nonexistence. For the less favored alternative remains possible even when the preferred alternative possesses priority, and the question “Why?” has still not been cut off.

Likewise, preponderance through will, while supposing its equal relation to acting and refraining, returns to the absence of any need for a preponderating principle.

N-XII-15 · His life—exalted is He
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 371–371
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=371&type=c_fbook

Life, as we find it among the kinds of animals, is a thing’s being such that it apprehends and acts.

The apprehension common to all animals is sensory apprehension additional to the essence. Their action is a limited act proceeding from knowledge and apprehension of it. Knowledge and power are therefore necessary consequences of life, rather than life itself, because we allow that knowledge may at times be absent from life, and likewise power.

The life found in an animal is thus an existential principle upon which knowledge and power follow.

Since something possessing knowledge and power additional to its essence is living, and its life is an existential perfection for it, one whose knowledge and power are identical with His essence, and who possesses every perfection and the whole of perfection, is more entitled to be called living. This is the intrinsically Necessary—exalted is He. He is therefore intrinsically living. His life is His being such that He knows and has power. His knowledge of everything proceeds from His essence, and His power is His intrinsic being a principle of everything other than Himself.

N-XII-16 · Will and speech
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 371–373
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=371&type=c_fbook

According to the prevalent view, both are counted among the attributes of essence of the Necessary—exalted is He.

As for will, it was discussed above in the investigation of power.

As for speech, it has been said:

“In our ordinary usage, speech is an expression that signifies, through conventional designation, what is inwardly intended. It is therefore a conventional existent that signifies what is in the speaker’s mind to one who knows the designation, through conventional, posited signification. For this reason, it is considered a verbal existence of the mental meaning, just as the mental meaning is counted as a thing’s mental existence and its external instance as its external existence.

“If there were a real existent signifying something through real rather than conventional signification—such as an effect signifying that which produces it, or an effect signifying through its existential perfection the perfection present in its cause in a higher and nobler manner—it would be more entitled to be called ‘speech,’ because of the fundamentality of its existence and the strength of its signification.

“If there were an existent whose essence was simple in every respect, possessing every perfection in existence in a higher and nobler manner, and disclosing through the detail of its attributes, identical with its holy essence, the comprehensiveness of its essence—as with the Necessary—then it would be speech signifying itself through itself, with comprehensiveness identical with detail.”

I say: this analyzes speech and returns the reality of its meaning to a certain sense of power. There is therefore no necessity calling for its being set apart from power. Moreover, every existential meaning, even one deeply immersed in materiality and surrounded by nonexistences and deficiencies, can be returned through analysis and the removal of deficiencies and nonexistences to one of His attributes of essence.

You may object: this also applies to hearing and sight. They are two aspects of knowledge, yet they have been set apart from power and counted as two attributes of essence.

I reply: this is because they occur in the Book and the Sunna. As for speech, nothing of it occurs in the Noble Book except as an attribute of act.

[translation] The final objection reads “set apart from power” (ufridā min al-qudra) in both the electronic transcription and the Qum 1404 witness, printed p. 308 (PDF p. 312). This reading is retained despite the contextual difficulty created by describing hearing and sight as aspects of knowledge.

N-XII-17 · Divine providence over creation: the cosmic order possesses the greatest possible goodness and precision
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 373–375
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=373&type=c_fbook

A knowing agent whose knowledge contributes to the completeness of its necessitating causality may be deficient in itself and seek completion through its act. In that case, the greater its need for the perfection it seeks through its act, the greater its concern for the act and the more thoroughly it performs it so as to include every particular feature that can be considered in perfecting its workmanship and fully realizing its benefits. This differs from the case in which the perfection sought through the act is slight and unnecessary in the agent’s view, so that its benefits may be neglected. This meaning is what is called “providence.”

The Necessary—exalted is He—is intrinsically independent and possesses every perfection in existence. He is therefore not perfected by any of His acts, and every existent is His act. He has no end in His acts outside His essence. But He has essential knowledge of everything possible that is established in it, and His knowledge, identical with His essence, is the cause of everything other than Him. His act therefore occurs as He knows it, without neglect of any of its particular features that He knows; and all are known. He thus exercises providence over His creation.

The general order witnessed throughout creation, the particular order prevailing in each species, and the arrangement and order established in the individuals of species confirm this. When we reflect upon any of these, we find benefits and advantages in its creation that fill us with wonder. The more thoroughly and deeply we examine it, the more new benefits and remarkable connections appear, astounding the intellect and revealing the precision of the matter and the perfection of the workmanship.

The foregoing account applies to the higher causes and immaterial intellects, whose essences are complete and whose existences are perfect, free of potentiality and preparedness. Their acts do not issue from them for a purpose or end returning to them from those acts that they did not possess before acting, since their essences are supposed complete. Their end in their act is therefore their own essences, which are shadows of the essence of the Necessary—exalted is He. In reality, their end in their act is the Necessary—exalted is His name.

It follows from the foregoing that the order prevailing in creation is the most precise and firmest order, because it is the attenuated reality of knowledge to which weakness and slackness have no access in any respect.

To explain: as indicated earlier, the universal realms of existence are three, with no fourth rationally possible. Existence is either marked by potentiality and preparedness, so that its possible primary and secondary perfections are not gathered together at the beginning of its being; or its possible primary and secondary perfections are gathered together at the beginning of its being, so that no perfection can be conceived as subsequently coming to it after not being. The first is the “realm of matter and potentiality.” The second is either free of matter but not of its effects, such as quality, quantity, and the other accidents occurring in material bodies, or free of both matter and all its effects. The former is the “imaginal realm,” and the latter is the “realm of intellect.”

There are therefore three universal realms, ordered according to the intensity and weakness of existence. Their order is vertical, through causality and being caused. The degree of intellectual existence is an effect of the Necessary—exalted is He—without an intermediary, and an intermediate cause of the imaginal realm below it. The imaginal degree is an effect of intellect and a cause of the degree of matter and material things. This has already been indicated and will be explained further.

The degree of intellectual existence is therefore the highest degree of contingent existence and the nearest to the Necessary—exalted is He. An intellectual species is confined to a single individual. Intellectual existence with its order is thus a shadow of the divine order in the realm of lordship, which contains every beauty and perfection.

The intellectual order is therefore the best and most precise possible order; next comes the imaginal order, a shadow of the intellectual order; then the material order, a shadow of the imaginal. The general order of the world is thus the best and most precise possible order.

N-XII-18 · Good and evil, and the inclusion of evil in divine decree
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 375–378
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=375&type=c_fbook

Good is what every thing seeks, intends, and loves, and toward which every thing naturally turns. When a choice lies among several things, what is chosen is the best of them. Good is therefore only an existential perfection upon which a thing’s existence depends, such as a cause in relation to its effect; or a primary perfection, which is the existence of the thing itself; or a secondary perfection through which the thing is perfected and a deficiency is removed from it. Evil is its opposite: the nonexistence of an essence or the absence of a perfection of an essence.

The proof that evil is the nonexistence of an essence or the absence of a perfection of an essence is as follows. If evil were something existential, it would be evil either for itself or for another. The first is impossible, because if a thing required its own nonexistence, it would never exist at all. A thing does not require its own nonexistence or the absence of any of its secondary perfections, because of the existential connection between it and them. Divine providence also requires that every thing be brought to its perfection. The second is likewise impossible. For evil, supposed to be existential, would be evil for another either by annihilating that other’s essence, by annihilating some perfection of it, or without annihilating either its essence or any of its perfections. The first and second alternatives are inadmissible, because evil would then be the nonexistence of that thing or of some perfection of it, rather than the supposed annihilating thing. This is a contradiction. The third is also inadmissible. If it annihilates nothing, neither an essence nor a perfection of an essence, it cannot be counted as evil. We necessarily know that what causes neither the nonexistence of a thing nor the absence of its perfection cannot be evil for it, since it suffers no harm from it. Evil, however it is supposed, is therefore not existential. This is what was sought.

Thorough reflection upon occurrences of evil confirms this. Careful examination of their aspects shows that the evil occurring is the nonexistence of an essence or the absence of a perfection of an essence. If, for example, a man kills another with a sword after restraining him, the effective blow undertaken by the killer is a perfection for him and is not evil; the sword’s sharpness and cutting capacity are a perfection for it and are not evil; the receptivity and softness of the victim’s neck are a perfection of his body and are not evil; and so forth. The evil is only the departure of the spirit and the cessation of life, which is privative.

The foregoing shows that an existent counted as evil because harm is suffered through it is evil incidentally, as with the killer and the sword in this example.

You may object: the apprehension involved in pain is different from the disruption of continuity produced by cutting, for example, and immediate awareness shows it to be something existential. This refutes their claim that what is intrinsically evil is privative—unless what is meant is that the source of evilness is privative even though some evil is existential.

I reply: Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified—answered that pain is the apprehension of an adverse privative condition, such as disruption of continuity and the like, through knowledge by presence. In such knowledge, what is known is present to the knower through its external existence, rather than through acquired knowledge in which the known is present by a form taken from it, not through its external existence. There are therefore not two things in pain: the disruption of continuity, for example, and a form acquired from it. Rather, the presence of that adverse condition is pain itself. Although it is a kind of apprehension, it is one of the instances of nonexistence; and although it is a kind of nonexistence, it has establishment in the manner in which privations, such as blindness, deficiency, and the like, have establishment.

In sum, because the soul is the human’s final form corresponding to the ultimate differentia, it encompasses all the perfections of the species and possesses all the bodily and other faculties. When disruption of continuity occurs as an affliction affecting a sense faculty, the soul apprehends its loss of the perfection of the faculty afflicted, at the soul’s comprehensive degree rather than at the body’s material degree.

Now, since evil is the nonexistence of an essence or the absence of a perfection of an essence, the essence affected by nonexistence must be receptive to it. Examples are material substances, which admit nonexistence through the passing away of their form, the entirety of their specific actuality. The essence whose perfection disappears through the occurrence of evil must likewise be capable of losing that perfection. That is, the nonexistence must be something that befalls it, rather than something intrinsic to its essence, like the nonexistences and deficiencies necessarily accompanying contingent quiddities. For this latter kind of nonexistence is abstracted solely from the degree of existence and its limit.

This makes clear that there is no evil in the realm of complete immateriality. Nonexistence has no access to its essences, which are established through their principle’s establishing them. Nor can nonexistences occur that oppose the perfections these essences require, which belong to them from the beginning of their existence.

The sphere and arena of evil is therefore the material world, in which opposites contend, different causes obstruct one another, and substantial and accidental motions occur, accompanied by change from one essence to another and from one perfection to another.

Evils are necessary consequences of the existence of matter receptive to different forms and diverse, conflicting perfections. Yet whatever they may be, goods prevail over them, and they are insignificant beside goods when compared with them.

For, as reported from the First Teacher, things fall into five divisions in respect of the goods and evils attributed to them: pure good, pure evil, predominance of good, predominance of evil, and equality of good and evil.

Two of the five divisions exist. The first is pure good: the Necessary—exalted is He—whose existence is necessary, who possesses every existential perfection and is the whole of perfection. Completely immaterial beings are included with Him in this division. The third is that in which good predominates. Divine providence requires its existence, because omitting much good is much evil.

As for the remaining three divisions, pure evil is pure nonexistence, sheer nullity with no access to existence. Predominance of evil and equality of good and evil are excluded by divine providence, which arranges the order of existence in the best and most precise way possible.

If you reflect upon any part of the cosmos, you will find that, were it not to occur as it has, the cosmic order, whose sides are connected to one another, would be invalidated at its root. This suffices as a predominance of evil in omitting what is predominantly good.

Since it is clear that the few evils attaching to things are necessary consequences of their many goods, intention and will relate to the goods primarily, and to the evils necessarily accompanying them consequentially and through a secondary intention.

It follows that evils enter into divine decree through a secondary intention. Or, if you wish, say “incidentally,” considering that evils are nonexistences to which intention does not attach per se.

N-XII-19 · The ordering of His acts, which is the order of creation
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 378–380
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=378&type=c_fbook

The preceding discussions have made clear that contingent existence, which is His act—exalted is He—has various divisions. Among them is its division into material and immaterial, and the division of the immaterial into intellectual and imaginal immateriality. There we indicated that the universal realms of existence are three: the realm of complete intellectual immateriality, the imaginal realm, and the realm of matter and material things. The intellectual realm is completely free, in both essence and act, of matter and its effects. The imaginal realm is free of matter but not of its effects, such as shapes, dimensions, positions, and the like. In this realm there are likenesses appearing in the characteristics of the bodies in the realm of matter and nature, in an order similar to their order in the material realm. The difference from the material order is that some imaginal things follow others through existential ordering, rather than through a form or state changing into another form or state by passing from potentiality to actuality through motion, as occurs in the material world. The condition of imaginal forms in their ordering after one another is like that of the form of motion and change in imagination. Knowledge is immaterial without qualification: imagined motion is knowledge of motion, not motion in knowledge, and knowledge of change, not change in knowledge.

No existent in the material realm is devoid of some attachment to matter. Motion and change, whether substantial or accidental, encompass it.

Since existence in its fundamental reality is a graded reality with different degrees of intensity and weakness, nobility and inferiority, and each degree subsists through what is above it and depends upon it in its very identity, the following conclusions ensue.

First: the three realms are ordered in existence through priority and posteriority. The intellectual realm precedes the imaginal realm, and the imaginal realm precedes the material realm in existence. Pure actuality, untainted by potentiality and unmixed with preparedness, is stronger and more intense in existence than what is pure potentiality, such as prime matter, or what is tainted by potentiality and mixed with preparedness, such as material natures. The intellectual and imaginal realms therefore precede the material realm.

The separate intellect is then less limited, more expansive in existence, and simpler in essence than the imaginal, which is accompanied by the effects of matter even though free of matter itself. It is clear that the less limited, more expansive, and simpler existence is, the earlier, prior, and higher its degree in the graded reality of existence, and the nearer it is to the highest degree, which is the principle of all. The intellectual realm is therefore earlier and prior in existence to the imaginal realm.

Second: this ordering of the three realms is causal, because of the priority and dependence between them. The intellectual realm is a cause of the imaginal realm, and the imaginal realm is a cause bestowing the material realm.

Third: the three realms correspond and agree in order, in a manner appropriate to the existence of each. For, as established above, every cause contains the perfection of its effect in a higher and nobler manner. The imaginal realm contains an imaginal order corresponding to the order of the material realm and nobler than it. The intellectual realm contains what corresponds to the imaginal order, but existing in a simpler, nobler, and more beautiful manner. Corresponding to it is the divine order in the knowledge of lordship.

Fourth: every contingent existent, whether material or immaterial, higher or lower, is in all respects a sign of the Necessary—exalted is He—representing through the existential perfection it possesses the perfection of the Necessary.

N-XII-20 · The intellectual realm, its order, and how multiplicity arises within it
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 380–387
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=380&type=c_fbook

It was established in the discussions of cause and effect that from one only one proceeds. Since the Necessary—exalted is He—is one and simple in every respect, admitting no aspect of multiplicity, either intellectual or external, and possessing every existential perfection in a detailed possession identical with comprehensiveness, He bestows only a single, simple existence possessing every existential perfection. This follows from the affinity between cause and effect. That existence is fully actual in every respect and free of potentiality and preparedness.

It is, however, an existence that is a shadow of necessary existence, dependent upon it, subsisting through it, and not independent apart from it. It is therefore necessarily accompanied by intrinsic deficiency and contingent limitation, through which its degree in existence is determined, and by a contingent quiddity. An existent characterized in this way is an intellect, immaterial in both essence and act, posterior in existence to the Necessary without an intermediary and prior in existential degree to all other degrees of existence.

Now a quiddity has a multiplicity of individuals only through association with matter. The reason is that multiplicity is either identical with the quiddity, a part of it, external but necessarily accompanying it, or external and separable from it. Under the first three suppositions, the quiddity would have no individual. For whenever an individual of it existed, it would have to be many. Every multiplicity is composed of units, and each unit would have to be many because it is an instance of the quiddity. This multiplicity too would be composed of units, and so on, in an infinite regress that never ends in a unit. No multiplicity would therefore be realized, and the quiddity would have no individual. Individual multiplicity must therefore be something external to the quiddity and separable from it. The attachment of a separable feature requires matter. Every quiddity with many individuals is therefore material. By contraposition, every immaterial quiddity—that is, one immaterial in existence—does not multiply through individual multiplicity. In other words, every immaterial being belongs to a species confined to a single individual. This is what was sought. Individual multiplicity in immaterial intellect is possible, to be sure, when individuals of a material species, such as the human, attain completion through intrinsic progression and substantial motion from the mode of matter and possibility to that of immateriality and pure actuality. They retain the individual distinction they possessed at the beginning of their existence in the mode of matter and potentiality.

It is therefore clear that the first emanated being proceeding from the Necessary—exalted is He—is a single intellect, the noblest contingent existent, and a species confined to a single individual. Since it is nobler and prior in existence, it is a cause of what lies below it and an intermediary in the bestowal of existence. It contains more than one aspect, so that many things can proceed from it. The many aspects in it, however, do not reach a number sufficient for the production of what lies below the intellectual mode, with its immense multiplicity. The procession of intellects must therefore be ordered in descent until a number of aspects is attained corresponding to the multiplicity in the mode that follows intellect.

This multiplicity can be conceived in one of two ways: vertically or horizontally.

The first, the occurrence of multiplicity vertically, is that an intellect exists, then another intellect, and so forth. Whenever an intellect exists, an aspect or aspects are added, until an intellect is reached through which aspects of multiplicity are realized sufficient for the procession of the mode following the intellectual mode. There are thus distinct species of intellects, each confined to a single individual, ordered in descent. Each higher intellect is more intense and nobler than the one after it and is its efficient cause, complete in its agency, because the latter’s intrinsic contingency suffices for its procession. The last of these intellects is the efficient cause of the mode following the intellectual mode. This is the view favored by the Peripatetics in their account of the ten intellects. To the last, which they call the “Agent Intellect,” they attribute the bringing into existence of the natural world.

The second, the occurrence of multiplicity horizontally, is that the vertical intellects end in horizontal intellects among which there is neither causality nor being caused. These correspond to the material species, each governing the material species corresponding to it. Through them the species in the natural world exist and its order is arranged. These intellects are called the “lords of species” and the “Platonic Forms.” This is the view favored by the Illuminationists, adopted by the Shaykh of Illumination, and chosen by Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified. Several arguments have been offered for it.

The first is that the vegetative faculties—nutritive, augmentative, and generative—are accidents inhering in a body that is their subject. They change as it changes and dissolve as it dissolves, and lack knowledge and apprehension. It is therefore impossible for them to be the principles bringing into existence the remarkable compositions of their subjects, their different activities, and the good and beautiful shapes and designs within them, together with the precise, perfected order that bewilders intellects. There must therefore be an immaterial intellectual substance exercising providence over them, governing their affairs, and guiding them to their ends in existence.

The objection is that, even if this argument were complete, it would show only that these remarkable activities and their prevailing order ultimately depend upon an intellectual substance possessing knowledge. It does not show that they depend upon a directly acting intellectual substance with no intermediary between it and the vegetative body. What they attribute to this intellectual substance could instead be attributed to the substantial form through which the species’ specific character is realized, and above it to the Agent Intellect, the last in the series of vertical intellects.

The second is that natural material species, with the order constantly prevailing in them, do not exist by chance. What occurs by chance is neither perpetual nor predominant. These species therefore have real causes. These are not what they suppose, such as temperaments and the like, since no proof indicates this. Rather, the real cause upon which each depends is an immaterial intellectual substance and universal archetype that exercises providence over it, brings it into existence, and governs its affairs. Its universality means the equality of its relation to all the material individuals that it leads from potentiality to actuality, rather than its being capable of being true of many.

The objection is that the acts and effects of each species depend upon its specific form. Without this, one substantial species would not be distinguished from another like it. The proof of the specific form is the effects peculiar to each species, which require something through which they subsist and upon which they depend as their proximate principle.

The third is that this is required by the principle of the possibility of the nobler, which is a demonstrated principle. When a contingent being exists that is inferior in existence to another contingent being, the nobler contingent being must exist before it. There is no doubt, for example, that a human actual in all human perfections is nobler in existence than the material human, who is potential in relation to most human perfections. The existence of the material human therefore proves the prior existence of his intellectual archetype. Likewise, the existence of the material individuals of every material species proves that their species’ lord exists before them. This is an individual of the species that is immaterial from the beginning of its existence, actual in all the species’ perfections, bringing the other individuals from potentiality to actuality and governing them.

The objection is that the operation of the principle of the possibility of the nobler is conditional upon the inferior and the nobler falling under one specific quiddity, so that the external existence of the inferior indicates the possibility of the nobler in respect of its quiddity. A concept’s merely being true of a thing does not show that the thing is really an individual of that concept. Every existent cause, for example, possesses all the perfections of its effect through which that effect is what it is, yet it does not follow that the cause of every thing must have the same quiddity as its effect. Thus the perfection through which the human is human, for example, belonging to a thing and applying to it does not, merely by that thing’s possessing it, reveal that it is an individual of the human quiddity. In other words, the concept of human being true of the universal human we intellect does not show that what we intellect is an individual of the human specific quiddity. Why could it not be one of the vertical intellects in the series of the human’s proximate or remote causes, because it possesses the perfection of the human and of other species? The predication would then be that of a reality and its attenuated reality, rather than common predication. If the principle’s operation is not conditional upon the inferior and nobler falling under one specific quiddity, the objection is even more forceful.

A clarification

A number of philosophers have given attention to the principle of the possibility of the nobler and based several questions upon it. Its import is that the nobler contingent being must precede the inferior contingent being in the degrees of existence: the contingent being nobler than it must have existed before it.

Ṣadr al-Mutaʾallihīn—may his secret be sanctified—formulated its proof as follows. When the inferior contingent being exists from the Creator—majestic is His remembrance—the nobler contingent being must have existed before it. Otherwise, if it could exist together with it, two things would have to exist from the intrinsically Necessary at one degree, from one essence and in one respect, which is impossible. If it could exist after the inferior and through an intermediary, the effect would have to be nobler than its cause and prior to it, which is impossible. If it could exist neither before the inferior, nor together with it, nor after it, despite being possible in the sense of possibility of occurrence—that is, its supposed occurrence entailing no impossibility—then, supposing it existed without proceeding from the intrinsically Necessary or from any of His effects, while retaining its possibility, its existence would necessarily call for a determining aspect nobler than what belongs to the intrinsically Necessary. The supposed contingent being would therefore, through its possibility, call for a cause bringing it into existence that was higher and nobler than the intrinsically Necessary. This is impossible, since the intrinsically Necessary surpasses the infinite infinitely in intensity. The conclusion is therefore established.

A clearer proof is possible. The nobility and inferiority in question are attributes of existence, returning to intensity and weakness according to existential degree. They therefore return to causality and being caused, which ultimately amount to a thing’s being independent and existent in itself, or relational, subsisting through another and existing in another. Every degree of existence subsists through what is above it, depends upon it, and is inferior to it, while sustaining what is below it, being independent in relation to it, and being nobler than it.

If two contingent beings are supposed, nobler and inferior in existence, the nobler must exist before the inferior with existential priority. Otherwise the inferior would be independent, neither relational nor subsisting through the nobler, although it was supposed relational and subsisting through it. This is a contradiction.

The two arguments yield the following conclusions.

First: whenever one existential perfection is inferior to another, the nobler exists before the inferior and the more intense before the weaker, as with two degrees of existence differing in intensity and weakness even if their quiddities differ, such as the first and second intellects.

If the inferior is a material individual of a quiddity, however, the principle establishes only that a perfection akin to it and more intense than it exists before it. It does not establish that this more intense perfection is an individual of the quiddity of the inferior. It might be one of the many aspects of perfection in a cause possessing many aspects. The human, for example, has a material individual with an inferior perfection, and above it a human perfection immaterial in every respect and nobler than it. It does not follow that this is a human by common predication, because it may be one aspect of the perfection present in its efficient cause. The resulting predication is therefore that of a reality and its attenuated reality.

The principle does, to be sure, apply to the higher immaterial ends belonging to some species attached to matter, such as the human. For demonstration establishes that they belong to their subjects by common predication, provided they encounter no natural impediment.

Second: the principle operates only beyond material things and the world of motions, among immaterial beings whose determining factors encounter no competing factor or impediment.

As for material things, the mere operation of a determining factor and the possibility of the quiddity do not suffice for the possibility of their occurrence, since an obstacle may prevent them.

The following objection therefore does not arise: if the principle were true, every material individual, such as a human individual, would have to attain the utmost intellectual and imaginative perfection proper to it, because this is nobler than its potential existence, whereas most individuals are deprived of their final perfection and barred from their ultimate existence.

[translation] “The principle of the possibility of the nobler” renders qāʿidat imkān al-ashraf. The chapter distinguishes the principle itself from the stronger claim that every material species must have a separate archetype of the same specific quiddity.

N-XII-21 · The imaginal realm
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 387–388
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=387&type=c_fbook

It is also called the “barzakh” (intermediate realm), because it lies between the intellectual realm and the realm of matter and nature. As became clear from the foregoing, it is a degree of existence free of matter but not of matter’s effects, such as quantity, quality, position, and similar accidents. The cause bringing it into existence is the last of the vertical intellects, called the “Agent Intellect” by the Peripatetics, or some of the horizontal intellects according to the Illuminationists.

It contains the images of substantial forms that are aspects of multiplicity in the intellect bestowing this realm. Some are presented to others in different configurations, without the differences of configuration impairing the individual unity of the substance concerned. An example is that many human individuals conceive someone from the past whom they have not seen, but whose name and something of whose life they have heard. Each represents him within himself in a configuration appropriate to what he supposes him to be, according to the attributes he knows, even if this differs from the configuration that person has for someone else.

Because of this point, they have divided the imaginal into separate imagination, subsisting in its own right independently of particular imagining souls, and connected imagination, subsisting in particular imagining souls.

Moreover, among the things imagined by souls are arbitrary forms that do not befit the act of a wise agent and bear a relation to the play of the imaginative faculty.

[English collation · term restored] The Arabic opening reads «ويسمّى أيضاً البرزخ». The English now retains the authorial technical term barzakh alongside the explanatory rendering 'intermediate realm', rather than silently substituting the paraphrase. Compared directly against the Qum 1417 transcription; early scan/1428 independent witness collation remains pending.

N-XII-22 · The material realm
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 388–389
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=388&type=c_fbook

This is the sensible realm, the lowest degree of existence. It is distinguished from the other two realms—the intellectual and imaginal—by its forms being attached to matter in both essence and act, or in act, and by their dependence upon preparedness. The perfections belonging to its species are potential at the beginning of existence and then gradually become actual. An obstacle may prevent them from attaining their perfection, so causes within it compete and obstruct one another.

Down to the present day, natural and mathematical investigations have discovered much about the parts of this realm, the relations between them, and the order prevailing among them. Perhaps what remains unknown is greater than what is known.

It became clear in the preceding discussions that the material realm, through the connections and continuity among its parts, is one, flowing in its essence and moving in its substance, with accidents accompanying it in this. The end at which this universal motion terminates is immateriality, as indicated in the stage on potentiality and actuality.

Since this realm is motion and moving in its substance, flow and flowing in its existence, and since its identity is renewal and change themselves rather than something upon which renewal and change supervene, its connection to an immutable cause, free of renewal and change, is valid.

The maker whose existence is immutable makes that which is intrinsically renewed and changing; it does not make a thing become renewed and changing. In this way the difficulty of the changing depending upon the immutable, and of the originated being connected to the eternal, is removed.

N-XII-23 · The origination of the world
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 389–392
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=389&type=c_fbook

It was established in the preceding discussions of eternity and origination that every existent contingent quiddity is preceded in existence by intrinsic nonexistence. It is therefore intrinsically originated. The nonexistence preceding its existence in its particular limit is abstracted from its cause that brings it into existence. Its existence is thus preceded by the existence of its cause and posterior to it.

Since the first principle of every contingent existence—whether material or immaterial, intellectual or otherwise—is the intrinsically Necessary—exalted is He—every contingent existent is intrinsically originated in relation to Him. The aggregate of contingent things, called the contingent world and everything other than the Creator, is nothing beyond its parts. Its ruling is therefore the ruling of its parts. The world with all its parts is intrinsically originated, its existence preceded by the existence of the intrinsically Necessary.

If we then disregard quiddities and confine our consideration to existence as the fundamental reality, we find existence divided into what is intrinsically necessary, subsisting through itself and independent in its realization and establishment, and what is contingent, existing in another, relational, and subsisting through another, namely the Necessary. Every contingent existence is therefore preceded by necessary existence and originated through this kind of origination. The ruling of the aggregate of contingent existences is the ruling of its parts, so the aggregate is originated through their origination.

The world of matter and nature has another origination peculiar to it: temporal origination. Its account is as follows. It was established in the discussions of potentiality and actuality that the material realm moves in its substance and in the accidents accompanying it. It flows in existence, is renewed in identity, proceeds intrinsically from deficiency to perfection, and passes out of potentiality. It is divisible into limits, each of which is an actuality for what precedes it and a potentiality for what follows it. If that same limit is divided, every limit produced through the division is again an actuality for what precedes it and a potentiality for what follows it.

This universal motion traces a quantitative extension such that every segment supposed within it divides into before and after. Likewise, every before and after divides into before and after, without stopping at any limit, as described for the motion tracing it. The difference between the two extensions is that the extension belonging to motion is indeterminate, whereas this extension occurring to motion is determinate, like the difference between the natural body and the mathematical body.

This extension traced by the substance of the world through its motion is universal time, through which motions are measured and relations among natural events are determined in terms of length, shortness, priority, and posteriority. Its priority is its being potentiality for the actuality adjoining it, and its posteriority is its being actuality for the potentiality adjoining it.

Every segment we take from the segments of this extended universal motion is found to be preceded by temporal nonexistence, because it is an actuality preceded by potentiality. It is therefore temporally originated. The aggregate of these segments and parts is nothing but the segments and parts themselves. Its ruling is therefore theirs, and it is temporally originated through their temporal origination. The world of matter and nature is thus temporally originated.

As for the theologians’ account of the temporal origination of the world—that is, everything other than the Creator, glorified is He—it is based upon the impossibility of temporal eternity for the contingent. Its substance is that contingent existences have a beginning beyond which they do not extend, so that before them no existent is found except the Necessary. Time stretches infinitely in both directions; its earlier portion is empty of the world, while its later portion is occupied by and serves as a receptacle for the world.

The objection is that time itself is a contingent existent created by the Necessary. It must therefore be included in the world that is His act. There is then nothing beyond the Necessary and His act, and no “before” in which the world’s nonexistence could be established as something contained in a receptacle. Moreover, asserting time’s infinity at both beginning and end contradicts their assertion of the impossibility of what is temporally eternal. Furthermore, time is a quantity occurring to motion subsisting in a body. Its infinity necessarily accompanies the infinity of bodies and their motions, which is the eternity of the world and contradicts their claim of its origination.

Some have attempted to escape the difficulty that time would be neither necessary nor an effect of the Necessary by saying that time is something intellectually considered, so there is no objection to saying it is neither necessary nor an effect of the Necessary.

The objection is that asserting the world’s temporal origination and its temporal eternity would then be equivalent, since time would have no reality.

Others have attempted to escape it by saying that time is something abstracted from necessary existence—exalted is He above that.

It was objected that this would entail the occurrence of change in the necessary essence.

The answer offered was: “We do not concede that what is abstracted must correspond to that from which it is abstracted.” You are well aware that this is a commitment to sophistry.

N-XII-24 · The perpetuity of emanation
Source: Arabic transcription · Qum 1417 AH, p. 392–393
https://lib.rafed.net/view.php?b_id=1642&page=392&type=c_fbook

It became clear in the preceding discussions that His power—exalted is He—is His being a principle of bringing into existence and His causality in relation to all else, and that it is identical with the transcendent essence. This necessarily entails the perpetuity of emanation, the continuity of mercy, and the uninterrupted bestowal of gifts.

This does not require the perpetuity of the natural world, because the aggregate is nothing beyond its parts, and every part is originated and preceded by nonexistence. Nor is there repetition in the existence of the world, as held by proponents of cycles and great cycles, since there is no proof for it.

It has been said: “The celestial spheres and higher bodies endure in existence as individuals, and likewise the universals of the elements and the original material species endure in existence, because their causes are separate and do not admit change.”

The reply is that there is no proof that these causes are complete and exclusive, with their influence independent of conditions and preparatory factors unknown to us. Their effects may differ as these conditions differ, so that creation need not be alike in its cycles.

Moreover, the doctrine of celestial spheres and bodies incapable of change, and other such views, were principles taken for granted from ancient astronomy and natural philosophy. These views have now been overturned.

The book was completed, praise be to God, on the sixth of the sacred month of Muḥarram in the year one thousand three hundred and ninety-five of the Prophetic Hijra. Blessings be upon Muḥammad and his family.
